FOUNDATIONS OF THE

THEORY OF KNOWLEDGE

BY

DR. MELCHIOR PALÁGYI

WITH THE SUPPORT OF THE HUNGARIAN ACADEMY OF SCIENCES

BUDAPEST PUBLISHED BY THE ATHENAEUM LITERARY AND PRINTING CO.

1904.

PDF 2

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Budapest: Athenaeum Co. Printing Office.

p. 1

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

1. The most excellent characteristic trait of our human nature is that, although we were not made for absolute, that is, perfect knowledge, its ideal image nevertheless lives in all our thirst for knowledge. Absolute, that is, perfect knowledge would be possessed by a mind that not only knew every single fact of the universe one by one, but could also survey this infinite cognition in a single timeless moment. That we cannot possess such knowledge, either now or ever in the course of time, it is wholly superfluous to prove: all the more striking, then, that the fire of such a thirst nevertheless burns unquenchably in our mind. For there is no doubt that the longing for perfect knowledge lives in the soul of each of us; otherwise we could imagine that mankind might attain such a degree of knowledge that beyond it nothing more would exist to be known. Since, however, everyone sees that human science can never develop so far that thereafter no further thing to be known should exist, it is also evident that this perpetual dissatisfaction with all human science can arise only from our measuring our limited and unfinished cognitions, secretly and involuntarily, against an unlimited and perfect knowledge.

In the comparison of our limited cognition with perfect knowledge there is revealed the excellence of our human condition just as much as its frailty. We must call the human mind excellent because it can form for itself some sort of concept of perfect knowledge; but from this same root springs the frailty of the human mind too: for of perfect knowledge we can form for ourselves only an approximate concept, but we are never able truly to realize it.

In proportion to this excellence and this frailty, a twofold danger threatens every cognition of man. For in consequence of our ardent longing for perfect knowledge we may easily

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 1

p. 2

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

deceive ourselves with the belief that in at least some narrower sphere of science we have already attained a certain completeness, a certain perfection, as a result of which we do not notice the gaps that must inevitably yawn even in that narrower sphere of our knowledge, and by such overconfidence we ourselves make it impossible, by discovering the gaps in our knowledge, to advance in cognition. That way of thinking which regards itself as finished — if only in a narrower sphere — we call dogmatism, or fixity of principle, and it is perhaps superfluous to say that this fixity has at all times been one of the chief obstacles to the advance of mankind.

On the other hand, however, our boundless yearning for absolute knowledge can also sweep us into the whirlpool of the opposite extreme, inasmuch as, discovering a deficiency, a gappiness, in all our knowledge, we fall into skepticism, or epistemological tremor, when the suspicion arises in us that we can know nothing with certainty and that perhaps all that we take for knowledge is nothing but a dreamlike illusion or self-deception. This sort of doubting itch — if it does not spring from a merely passing lyrical mood — can grow into the most dangerous intellectual disease, gnawing at the vital root of human knowledge, to such an extent that, in view of this disease alone, we need a science such as logic or theory of knowledge, which defends our knowledge against the attacks of morbid or empty doubting.

The theory of knowledge wrests the weapon from the hands of skepticism by itself laying bare the frailties of our mind and the gaps in our knowledge that follow from them. An innate frailty of our mind can become fatal to science only to the degree that we have no knowledge of it. But as soon as we have taken cognizance of some defect in our thinking or some gap in our knowledge, by this very taking of cognizance we have acquired a new cognition, a cognition of our frailty: and that is a cognition than which we can scarcely imagine a more precious. For after all, progress in science becomes possible only through our noticing some defect in our thinking, some deficiency in the system of our cognitions. In a word: the frailty of our mind is the fundamental condition of our being able to advance in science, inasmuch as it is given only to a frail mind, becoming aware of its weakness, to set out upon the path of self-perfection.

p. 3

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

The theory of knowledge sees the innate weaknesses of our mind in this light, and accordingly raises the cognition of our mind’s frailties into a power. What drives timorous minds to despair is precisely what logic transforms into the richest wellspring of our strength. This science gladly welcomes every discovery that affords a deeper insight into the limited or defective character of our knowledge, for nothing can open so immeasurable a prospect of new scientific discoveries as the perception of some theoretical limit or gap that had hitherto gone unnoticed. That skepticism nonetheless remains the most formidable enemy of human knowledge has its chief reason in this: that there exists a kind of doubting which does not really lay bare the weaknesses of our mind, but rather moves only in the province of subtle insinuation and witty calumny. This is that particular doubt which only destroys but does not enlighten, though it always performs its mind-destroying work in the name of enlightenment.

From what has just been said it will perhaps already be clear how momentous a matter it is what kind of conception we form for ourselves of the nature of the human mind: for it is evident that every one of our supposed sciences must turn into error as soon as we judge the nature of our mind either with dogmatic blindness or with skeptical faintheartedness. We therefore need a science that subjects human reason, or rather its work, science, to investigation: we need such a science if only so that doubt may not poison the natural trust we place in our mind, while on the other hand an overestimation of our mind may not blind us to the deficiencies of our knowledge and to the ever new problems that spring from them. This science is logic. Our mind can have need of nothing so much as of self-knowledge, for only this self-knowledge can teach us what we may properly expect of our mind and what tasks we must set before it on the paths of the search for truth. Logic, or the theory of knowledge, is in this sense the science in which the human mind strives for self-knowledge, so that in the ever-increasing clarity of this self-knowledge we may systematically perfect every branch of human science.

2. To get directly at the power of reason itself, or the faculty of reason, is not possible; consequently logic does not take the power of reason itself under investigation, but its work, science. Human science, however, can be investigated from two sides: either

1*

p. 4

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in such a way that we have regard to the symbols in which we express our knowledge, or else in such a way that we investigate the impressions on which science is built up. According to the testimony of history, the philosophers of different epochs have not accorded equal attention in logic to symbols and to impressions, but have striven one-sidedly to found either a symbolic or an impressionist logic. Thus, for example, it is striking that Aristotelian logic — at least as it lies before us summed up in the Organon — neglects the investigation of our knowledge from the impressionist side; whereas in modern times, under the influence of Bacon, an impressionist logic arose which wished as far as possible to disregard the symbols of speech, and in the investigation of human knowledge desired as far as possible to confine itself to the examination of sensible impressions. Hence it is that the science with which we are here concerned has chiefly two names in use: logic and theory of knowledge. (This science has, alas, still more names!) When we speak of “logic,” we are properly thinking of Aristotelian symbolic logic, but if we mention “theory of knowledge,” then it is a matter of the modern impressionist logic, which wished, so far as it was able, to trace the investigation of reason back to the investigation of impressions.

In the philosophical system whose logical foundations I intend to set forth here we shall make no distinction whatever between the names “logic” and “theory of knowledge,” for we have no reason whatever to show any partiality either toward impressions or toward symbols, and in this way to neglect either of these two conditions of human science. That without the acquisition of impressions no science can exist, it is almost superfluous to state, and if there is anything to regret, it is only that fashionable thinkers do not hold symbols to be just as indispensable to human knowledge as impressions. For whoever regards human science with an unprejudiced eye can see at first glance that without the use of signs such a science could not exist. Pray imagine for a moment what would become of mankind if it were deprived of its sign-forming faculty! Or let us not go so far, and merely try in imagination to eliminate printing from the system of present-day culture. What if we eliminate even the art of writing, so that not only printed signs, but not even written ones

p. 5

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

are at our disposal: what then will become of human science? Finally, if we imagine man deprived of the signs of speech, indeed of every sign-forming faculty at all, then we have in reality turned him out of his humanity, for we have made it impossible for him to communicate with his fellow man intellectually and to stand with him in moral relation. But I shall not spin this thought out further, for I believe that every uncorrupted mind is clear that one of the conditions of existence of human science is that sign-forming faculty by virtue of which we are able to fix our knowledge for ourselves and our descendants, to transmit it from individual to individual, from generation to generation, and by this transmission to increase and perfect it. Consequently we shall place ourselves in opposition to every philosophical conception which would, under whatever title, belittle, reduce, or altogether annihilate the importance of the use of signs in science. And perhaps we do no superfluous thing if, right at the beginning of our logical inquiries, we especially emphasize the following guiding propositions:

a) One condition of existence of all human science is formed

by those impressions which we can procure for ourselves

through the working of our sense-organs, namely through

touch, sight, hearing, etc. And since only progressing knowl‐

edge can be called truly living knowledge, we must regard it as

a condition of life of all science that it set out systematically

and untiringly to acquire ever new impressions (experiences).

b) The other condition of existence of all human science is

formed by those signs which we produce by gesture or by our

organs of speech or in any other way, and fix by notching, writ‐

ing, printing, drawing, or in any other way, and which serve to

bring our knowledge to expression through them, and to pre‐

serve it for ourselves and for others. And since, as we men‐

tioned, knowledge must progress in order to remain truly liv‐

ing knowledge, we must regard it as a condition of life of all

science that it perfect, systematically and untiringly, the sys‐

tem of signs which it uses to express knowledge.

c) The twofold condition of existence of human science stands

in close parallel with the twofold manifestation of human life,

by virtue of which our organism on the one hand possesses re‐

ceptivity for sensible impressions, and on the other hand is en‐

dowed with a self-moving power or capacity. The sensitivity or

receptivity of our organism makes it

p. 6

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

possible for us to acquire impressions; on the other hand, we need the self-moving power of our organism in order to be able to produce signs in which we express our knowledge. Without sensory nerves we should have no impressions, without motor nerves we should not be able to form signs, and thus the twofold condition of existence of all our science is already indicated in the structure of our nervous system. Just as one-sided as it would be for the biologist, or investigator of life, in studying the phenomena of life to regard merely the sensitivity of our organism or merely its self-motility, just so partial would the theorist of knowledge be if, in investigating human science, he confined himself merely to the investigation of impressions or merely of the signs used. In connection with this insight we can state that every attempt which plays impressions off against signs, or conversely signs against impressions, has a disintegrating effect upon logic and consequently a disintegrating effect upon the universality of human science.

3. But however firmly we resolve to give equal regard to the impressional and the symbolic pan of the scales of logical inquiry, by reason of our human frailty we shall now idolize our impressions, now our symbols. Individual, indeed national inclinations, or the fashionable moods of the age, play a great part in this respect. Thus, for example, the present age, if I am not mistaken, is quite able to appreciate the acquisition of new impressions from the standpoint of the progress of science, but it is questionable whether it is able to value to the same degree the importance of perfecting signs for the further development of science. One of the chief causes of this one-sided inclination I must point out here already in the introduction.

The circumstance that mankind expresses, and in the past too expressed, its knowledge in thousands and thousands of different languages and dialects readily disposes the theorist of knowledge to a certain contempt for the sign-system of languages. Look how alike our sense-organs are constructed, so that, for example, what I see as angular or round will in general be seen as such by others too, and yet these concepts “angular” and “round” are in general designated differently in every language. From this many infer that in logic it is not worth taking account of those signs either by which we express our concepts. I bring forward this mode of argument by way of example also in order to show how one can

p. 7

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

play off the uniformity of our impressions against the diversity of our linguistic sign-systems. For such is human reason that, if some secret partiality spurs it on, it can find a thousand reasons for belittling or casting suspicion on something. We readily admit, to be sure, that some great innate frailty of mankind manifests itself in its expressing its thoughts in so many different languages; but I venture to remind you of what I have already mentioned above, that our greatest resources may spring precisely from our frailties. Who has yet investigated all the advantages that may accrue to human knowledge from the multiplicity of languages? But apart from that, who says that our sense-organs are constructed quite uniformly? Are not individual divergences to be found everywhere in the working of the senses of touch, sight, hearing, etc., and do not precisely these individual divergences afford occasion for the development of a new branch of science, which sets itself the aim of characterizing every individual organization with the help of certain so-called biological constants, as, for example, physics and chemistry characterize the respective kinds of bodies by the constants of density, elasticity, specific heat, electrical resistance, atomic weight, etc.? If it is a frailty that mankind languishes in a multiplicity of tongues, then surely it is a frailty too that the sense-organs of every individual present in their working a certain individual modification. And if anyone wants to cast suspicion on the human mind because it expresses its knowledge in different languages, let him cast suspicion on it also because individual divergences show themselves everywhere in the workings of our sense-organs.

Against this it might perhaps be objected that the individual divergences appearing among the workings of the sense-organs form only a slight hindrance to mutual understanding, whereas it is a very great hindrance when two persons do not know each other’s language. From the practical point of view the existing linguistic differences may doubtless represent a very significant hindrance to intellectual intercourse, but the theorist of knowledge does not weigh the divergent character of linguistic sign-systems from this practical point of view. He raises the question whether the structural divergence of the different languages is of essential import or not. Between two languages an essential difference would exist if the one could not be translated into the other, that is, if two beings, one of whom spoke language A, the other language B, were separated from each other by a wholly unbridgeable chasm of understanding. But it is evident that between men

p. 8

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

two such unbridgeable languages, absolutely untranslatable into one another, cannot exist. And if by any chance we could meet beings, for example inhabitants of other stars, whose signs we were by no means able to interpret from our own, then we should indeed be convinced that those beings do not possess a reason of human form, or else that what we take to be signs on their part are not properly signs at all in the sense in which we use that word. In a word, we are all convinced that if we stand face to face with a being whose mind is of human form, then a way must necessarily be found of interpreting its signs from ours and translating its speech into ours. Thus, for example, no missionary has ever yet doubted that, into whatever backward savage tribe he may come, there is a way for him to master their language sooner or later; similarly no philologist or archaeologist has ever doubted that the interpretation of some ancient cuneiform script cannot meet with an absolute obstacle, that is, that the language whose remnant lies before him cannot be of such a nature that between it and our language the relation of complete untranslatability should obtain. For we do not regard as a language that to whose sense we have no access at all in our own language. Thus, for example, we do not call the sign-systems of the different animal species languages, because we hold the translation of these sign-systems into our language, or the expression of our knowledge by those animal signs, to be impossible. As soon as we succeeded in translating our knowledge into the sign-system of some animal species, that is, in making our knowledge understood by that animal species, we should not doubt for a moment that in that species we must see beings equal to us in mind, that is, in reality men.

Logically, then, there is no difference between the different languages, which we may also express by saying that the logical character of every language is the same. To see the truth of this proposition it is not necessary that we examine every single language separately and prove it to agree with the rest in logical character. For we men fix the concept of language in such a way that we acknowledge as a language only that which is translatable into our mother tongue, that is, which has the same fundamental logical character as our mother tongue. By virtue of this sovereign stipulation of ours we expect in advance of every sign-system that lays claim to being a language that our knowledge, as expressed in our mother tongue, can be expressed in the signs of that

p. 9

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

alleged language as well; for otherwise we should be compelled flatly to deny that alleged language its character as a language.

Just as the individual divergences appearing among the workings of our sense-organs, so the national differences obtaining among our sign-systems do not affect the logical character of our mind. It is true that the acquisition of a foreign language costs great labor, but from the standpoint of logical inquiry this labor does not come into consideration, for from the purely logical standpoint there is no difference among languages. Just as the physicist sees no difference in principle in whether we express temperature on the scale of Réaumur or of Celsius, so the theorist of knowledge sees no difference in principle in whether knowledge is expressed in the sign-system of this language or of that. This view is not in the least in conflict with the fact that in the different languages the difference in the mentality of the nations may manifest itself very characteristically; for we are not speaking here of these differences of mentality.

But now the reader might raise the question: in what does the common logical character of every language consist? Must not every existing language be examined, in order to establish those characteristic traits in which they all agree with one another? But where is the mortal who could become acquainted with every existing and extinct language to such an extent as to be able to single out their common logical characteristic traits and gather them into a system? And if we think besides that in the course of the future nations will arise and languages be born which do not exist today: is it not a vain striving to search in the structure of human language for any logical characteristic traits common to all of them? These questions we had to raise here already for this reason too, that in them there manifests itself the linguistic-philosophical, or rather epistemological, way of thinking of our age.

I believe that a very characteristic error is contained in the above questions. What trouble would it have been, for example, if Galileo had had to examine separately every pendulum swing that has existed and will exist, in order to establish some law concerning the motion of the pendulum. Experimentation exists so that we need not observe every case separately. Whoever wishes to establish the indispensable logical characteristics of language must step into the field of experiment, and must try artificially to

p. 10

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

construct a language in which he strives as far as possible to dispense with every characteristic of which he can even remotely suppose that it is not needed in the construction of a language. And then he will easily come upon the following elementary necessity: Every human language must of necessity be such that in it we express our knowledge in sentences, which are composed of words. A language in which no units could be formed that correspond to our sentences, or in which these sentences did not consist of units parallel to our words, we could not acknowledge as a language at all. It is an indispensable, and therefore logical, characteristic of all speech that it should be formed of sentences which consist of words: at least speech in which we wish to express science must be of this character. If someone were to remark on this that what we set up as the common logical character of every language is a “matter of course,” I could have no objection whatever to this remark, and could at most add to it that among all the wonders of nature there is no more wonderful phenomenon than that we express our knowledge in sentences which consist of words. For the realm of the true mysteries begins where men call something “a matter of course.”

2. Symbolic Logic.

4. I call that logic symbolic which — as I have said — neglects the investigation of impressions and places the chief emphasis on the investigation of the signs we use. The chief achievement, or rather the cardinal fundamental idea, of this symbolic logic may be expressed by saying that human knowledge can be resolved into elements. The elements of all knowledge would be the so-called concepts, and from these concepts our judgments would arise by composition. Of all Aristotle’s logical distinctions we ascribe the greatest importance to the definition which he repeated five times in three chapters of his Hermeneutics (1, 4, 5), and according to which we must distinguish thoughts into concepts and judgments. There are, he says, thoughts which necessarily fall under the point of view of the true and the not-true, and these are judgments; there are

p. 11

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

on the other hand, thoughts too with regard to which we cannot ask whether they are true or not, and these are concepts. If, for example, I say: “man is white,” then this is a thought that is either true or not true; but if I say merely “man” or merely “white,” then these expressions do indeed possess signification or meaning, but they do not fall under the point of view of the true or the not true, and could fall under such a point of view only if by combination we formed a judgment out of them.

“Just as in the soul there is found now a thought with which we assert neither truth nor falsehood, and now a thought which must necessarily be either true or false, so also is it in speech; for it is on combination or division that depends what makes a thought false or true. If, therefore, in a sentence the subject and the predicate are taken separately, then these correspond one by one to such thoughts as lack combination or division, as for example when I say merely ‘man’ or ‘white,’ without adding anything further: in which case such a saying contains neither truth nor falsehood. From this it is also intelligible that the ‘goat-stag’ (a fabulous animal) does indeed signify something, but does not yet say anything either true or false, unless the concept of being or of not-being is added to it, either quite generally or bound to a certain time.” (Hermeneutics 1.)

These theories of Aristotle had an immeasurable influence not only on the formation of logical science, but also on the mode of thinking of educated mankind, so that his distinctions perhaps rule over our thinking even when we no longer suspect it. Hence I hold a new founding of the theory of knowledge to be an impossibility without our entering upon a criticism of the Aristotelian fundamental concepts. From the above citation I wish especially to bring out the following factors: 1. It appears that Aristotle finds some sort of parallelism between the thoughts of the soul and the linguistic designation of these thoughts: namely, he seems to teach that to the twofold kind of thoughts appearing in the soul, namely concepts and judgments, there correspond in human speech words and sentences. 2. Aristotle makes a very original distinction between concepts and judgments, when he teaches

p. 12

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that concepts do not fall under the point of view of the true or the false, whereas our judgments are necessarily either true or false. This Aristotelian theory I shall also express by saying that our concepts, taken in themselves, would be indifferent to truth, and only with our judgments would we show sensitivity toward truth. 3. According to Aristotle, what makes our judgments judgments is always some joining or division (combination or separation). In affirmative judgments we as it were join together the concepts of subject and predicate, fit them into one another, whereas in negative judgments we separate the predicate, tear it away from the subject. For this reason Aristotle says that truth and falsity (in our judgments) are always based on some composition or decomposition. 4. From the Aristotelian mode of thinking it seems to follow that concepts are simpler thoughts than judgments, indeed that they are those elements of thought from which, by combination or separation, our judgments arise.

As regards, first of all, the theory of parallelism, by virtue of which a certain relation of mutual correspondence would obtain between the thoughts of the soul on the one hand and the signs used in speech on the other, it is developed only very faintly in Aristotle, for which reason it would be a delicate matter to attribute to the Greek sage this view or that in this direction. Nor do we wish here to interpret his views this way or that, for that would belong to the province of philology and the history of philosophy; but with regard to the theory of parallelism we have merely one observation of principle. It appears that Aristotle places in the first rank the noticing of the twofold kind of thoughts present in the soul and only in the second rank the parallel distinction of their linguistic designations; but if we had to understand this in the sense that we men first come to know that in our soul twofold thoughts, namely concepts and judgments, make their appearance, and only then discover that, corresponding to this, in our speech too words and sentences must be distinguished, then we could by no means join in such a view.

For I do not believe that a primitive man who has not learned to read and write could discover in his soul twofold thoughts, namely concepts and judgments, and at the same time notice that when some judgment appears in his soul, it has arisen from the combination of several concepts. Indeed, if someone even

p. 13

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

knew how to write, but had no notion of alphabetic writing and used only picture-writing, like, for example, the ancient Egyptian, who might designate by a little picture a thought amounting to a whole sentence, then it can hardly be supposed of such a man that he could find in his soul twofold thoughts, namely concepts and judgments; for such a man sometimes represented the single concept, but often the single judgment too, by a single little picture, a hieroglyph, a clear sign that he was not yet able to distinguish between concepts and judgments. In a word, I believe that man arrived at the distinction of concepts from judgments only by being compelled first to observe the signs of his speech more exactly, and only after he had noticed that his speech consists of certain units, which we call sentences, and that these units in turn are composed of smaller units, that is, of words, could the thought occur to him also that in his soul twofold thoughts, namely concept and judgment, may appear.

And what may have induced man to accord more heightened attention to the signs of his speech? Among the many factors that have acted upon man in this direction, perhaps none is of so great importance as the striving to fix thoughts. The more exactly we wish to set down our thoughts, the greater care must we devote to observing the signs of our speech, and the more inclined do we become to fall to thinking about our thinking, that is, the more does the idea of a scientific logic dawn within us. We may say, then, that it was in the course of developing the art of thought-fixing, that is, of writing (notching), that mankind became ever more attentive to the signs of its speech and with this also to the working of its own mind; or, in other words: it was by the development of the art of thought-fixing, that is, writing, that mankind was prepared for the founding of logic as a science. Hence in the eyes of primitive peoples “literate man” is a concept of the same meaning as “philosopher.”

And as the history of culture, so pedagogy too bears witness for our conception. To a child who is not yet able to learn reading and writing we should explain in vain that there are concepts and judgments in our soul. But if the child has already learned the reading of fixed thought, and still more, if he himself can already fix thoughts in writing, then we can gradually introduce him to the knowledge of the structure of speech (grammar),

p. 14

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

to the distinction of sentences and words. And only when he has acquired a clear concept of the difference between sentence and word can he arrive at the fundamental theory of Aristotelian logic concerning the difference between concepts and judgments. We must emphasize this lest we fall into the error of supposing that man possesses some secret “inner power of observation,” by virtue of which he need only look into his own mind in order to see concepts swarming there and clustering into judgments. For even if Aristotle himself was not given to this false opinion, his words could nevertheless give occasion, and did give occasion, to such a misunderstanding.

In the more recent theory of knowledge extraordinary mischief has been caused by the conception that the human mind is capable, avoiding the use of all signs, of sinking itself into itself and examining itself. From this curious mode of thinking there arose the concept of the so-called pure reason, which plays so notable a role in the history of modern theory of knowledge. By this pure reason we are not to understand what in ordinary life we are accustomed to call “clear reason,” but perhaps the very opposite of this, namely a mystical reason, which possesses mystical self-vision. It does not need to observe the signs of speech when it investigates the working of the mind, and it needs no impressions either in logical inquiry, for after all whoever perceived signs would thereby perceive impressions too, since of signs we can have knowledge in the first place only as impressions. Pure reason, then, is properly such a reason as can shut itself off perfectly from the world of impressions and from all remembering of this world, no less than from all self-made sensible signs and from remembering them, so that it sinks entirely into itself and thus, independently of all experience, draws out of itself pure knowledge, which consists of judgments and concepts free of experience. With this so-called rationalist (apriorist) direction of the theory of knowledge we shall have much to do, for it is dominant today as well to a great extent, even over the thinking of natural scientists and mathematicians.

The rationalist logic we may accordingly regard as a symbolic logic that has forgotten its own origin, or, if you like, has become unfaithful to its own origin. This logic deludes itself into believing that, for example, we can distinguish between concepts and judgments independently of all signs of speech or of remembering these signs. The rationalist logic we may also call

p. 15

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the logic of blue-blooded reason, for it imagines human reason as such as can deny all community with the sensible world and, hermetically sealing itself off from it, can draw out of itself the cardinal propositions of all human science. Against logic of this kind we had above all to point out that we could make no distinction between concept and judgment if we did not first become aware of the structure of our speech, that is, if we did not first discover that our speech is composed of sentences and our sentences of words. Human reason, then, has no secret and immediate power of self-perception, by which, circumventing all sensible signs and impressions, it could examine itself. A heavy and gigantic labor must human reason perform in order to struggle through to self-knowledge: it must first fix its thoughts through centuries, so that, returning again and again to its knowledge preserved in signs, it may fall to musing over the past of its own thinking, and in this way, discovering its frailties, seek paths to the perfecting of its thinking and knowledge, that is, paths to crowning with the creation of a logical science all its knowledge accumulated through the millennia. It hardly needs saying, then, that the creation of such a science is the most difficult task of all. Let us not wonder either that logical science apparently makes no progress, like the other sciences, and seems to be stuck fast in never-ending, fruitless disputes. As we do not take offense that on the clock face we see only the second hand revolving, while the hour hand seems to stand still in one place, so we ought not to wonder that the development of logic accompanies the progress of the other sciences only at a very slow pace. The experiences of centuries must accumulate, in order that mankind may look one degree deeper into the frailty of its own mind, and from this self-knowledge may draw direction and strength for the gathering of the experiences of still further centuries. And if after long historical struggles some new logical thought does at last dawn somewhere, how often do we find that it is precisely against such a thought that the whole scientific world, which holds with the older logic, rises most violently.

5. We now pass on to the core of the Aristotelian distinction, according to which only our judgments fall under the point of view of the true and the not true, while our concepts would be, so to speak, indifferent to truth. Since this conception

p. 16

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

establishes a real opposition between the nature of concepts and that of judgments, it is impossible to take note of it without the deepest uneasiness. We summarize our objections in the following points:

a) A judgment, it seems, cannot consist of any other elements

than concepts. But since concepts are allegedly indifferent in the

direction of truth, it is wholly unintelligible how from nothing

but elements indifferent in the direction of truth there can arise

something like the judgment, which is not indifferent toward

truth. This is just as unintelligible as if someone wanted to obtain,

as the sum of nothing but zeros added together, a number that

differs from zero.

b) The above objection would lose its force only if the judgment

did not consist of nothing but uniform elements, that is, of noth‐

ing but concepts, so that there would be in it, besides the ele‐

ments indifferent to truth, still other elements which are not in‐

different to truth. But in this case it is evident that for those ele‐

ments which are indifferent to truth there would be no need

whatever in the judgment. The truth or falsity of the judgment

would be decided by those elements which fall under the point of

view of the true and the false, while the remaining indifferent ele‐

ments could simply be left out of the judgment, for after all they

contribute nothing to its being true or false. If, for example, we

consider the expression x + y + z + (a—a) + (b—b) + (c—c), then the

terms a—a, b—b, c—c can simply be left out, for they are zeros,

which change nothing in the value of the expression.

c) From the two preceding points it follows that a judgment can

consist only of such elements as themselves fall under the point

of view of the true or the not true. For if the judgment consisted

of nothing but elements indifferent to truth, then in the sense of

point a) it would itself become indifferent to truth, that is, would

not be regarded as a judgment. If, however, it contained, besides

the indifferent elements, elements sensitive to truth also, then in

the sense of point b) the indifferent elements could be left out, so

that finally no other elements would remain in the judgment than

those that fall under the point of view of the true and the false.

With this we have shown that the artificial opposition which

Aristotle wishes to establish between concepts and judgments is

logically untenable.

Indeed, if we look more closely at the Aristotelian distinction, then very great obscurity, or rather ambiguity

p. 17

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

is found in it. For the Stagirite sage says that the truth or falsity of our judgments is always rooted in the composition and separation of concepts. It is evident, then, that this composition or separation would be the proper essence of the judgment. Concepts would be only, as it were, the bricks of the judgment, and would become a judgment in the true sense only by means of some thought-cement. This cement would bring it about that truth or falsity is contained in the judgment; this cement, therefore, ought not to be a concept, for concepts are only the bricks of the judgment and are indifferent to truth. Through the Aristotelian illumination, then, our judgments stand before us in two colors: at one time we believe that judgments consist of nothing but concepts and contain nothing else; at another we believe that concepts represent only one kind of element of the judgment, and that the chief role in them is played not by these concepts but by the thought-cement that holds them together. But the chief trouble is that, in whatever sense we take the ambiguous distinction, it remains in any case unintelligible. For if the judgment consists of nothing but indifferent concept-bricks, without any binding material, then it loses its character as a judgment. If, however, some binding material must be assumed among the concepts, we do not know what is to be understood by such binding material, for it must then not be a concept. From this we see in what hideous confusion Aristotelian logic leaves us on the fundamental question of logic: the relation of the concept to the judgment. This confusion has been inherited on through centuries and has made the healthy development of logic impossible. For a science into whose fundamental interpretations an inner contradiction has bored its way cannot advance.

6. We cannot join that mode of thinking which believes it can discover in our knowledge elements indifferent in the direction of truth, or which indeed traces the whole of human knowledge back to nothing but such indifferent elements (concept-bricks). For we believe that if knowledge consists of any elements at all, then they must be sensitive to truth down to the last particle. We believe that our concepts fall under the point of view of the true and the not true just as our judgments do, for we all speak of correctly or incorrectly formed concepts just as of true or false judgments. Let us not forget that we form concepts for ourselves only

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 2

p. 18

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

because we wish to judge, and that a concept never comes into being otherwise than by the activity of judging. In general, human reason can do nothing else than judge, that is, form such thoughts as appear in speech as sentences; wherefore the whole of human science consists of nothing but judgments or sentences. In the course of forming sentences or judgments there then arise words or concepts; but if man did not judge, that is, did not wish to form sentences, he would indeed never have the slightest need of concepts or words. We acknowledge that it belongs among the greatest wonders of human reason that in judging it must form concepts, or conversely that, if it wishes to form concepts, it must judge; but precisely for this reason we cannot admit that between concepts and judgments there could obtain an opposition by virtue of which judgments would be sensitive to truth while concepts, on the contrary, would show insensitivity in the fulfillment of their proper vocation.

It is true that words possess a certain apparent independence, that is, that we ascribe meaning to them even when they are mentioned singly, but this is possible only because we can insert every word into certain sentences within which its meaning can be made to count. Suppose for a moment that a word should come before us which we could not insert into any sentence at all in order that it might there unfold its meaning: what should we think of such a word? Should we not be convinced that it is properly not a word at all, but a senseless mass of sound; or else we should have to believe that it is only we who do not know the sense of that word, because it is only we who cannot make its meaning count in sentences. In this way we can convince ourselves, in the case of every concept, whether it is correctly formed or incorrectly. We have only to go back to those impressions which gave occasion for the formation of that concept and to those judgments which are indispensable for the unfolding of the sense of that concept, and then we shall know what to think of the value of that concept. Here, to be sure, we cannot yet enter upon a deeper investigation of the nature of concepts, but this much perhaps does emerge from our discussions, that our concepts live truly only within our judgments, and likewise that within our judgments it is they that bring about the truth or falsity of our thoughts, and hence cannot be insensitive to truth and falsity, as would follow from Aristotle’s interpretations.

p. 19

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

But neither can we accept Aristotle’s theory that the essence of judgment could be expressed in such words as connecting or separating. For it is well known that we can connect words in such a way that sentences do not yet arise from them; thus, for example, “the red rose” or “space and time,” although they are connections of words, are nevertheless not to be regarded as sentences. Nor, therefore, can we characterize judgments as being linkages of concepts, for no one will take an expression such as “the population of Budapest” for any sort of judgment, but at most for a compound concept. In general we may say that Aristotle’s interpretations leave us in the greatest confusion about the essence of judgment and of concepts, as well as about their relation to one another and to the signs of speech. But we have no reason to be greatly astonished at this, for the questions in point are the weightiest, and indeed among the most significant, that the human mind can raise. It is a matter of the type of our faculty of reason when we ask what it can mean that our speech is constituted as it is, that is, that it is built up out of sentences and words. The trouble is only that over such fundamental questions we are accustomed to slip by without thinking. In the system of the theory of knowledge to be presented here we shall often have occasion to touch upon questions which are not usually regarded as questions at all.

Finally, we cannot accept the theory either that our concepts are in some respect “simpler” thoughts than our judgments. It is true that our sentences are composed of words, as a result of which the semblance may arise that more composite thoughts correspond to our judgments than to our concepts: but we must also take into account that quite often, when we wish to unfold the meaning of a single word, we need a whole series of sentences. If the chemist wishes to say what he understands by the word “gold,” he must construct a whole series of sentences in which he sets forth his most various experiences relating to gold, for example, that it is a yellow metal whose density is 19, which, unlike silver, is not soluble in nitric acid, etc., etc. The word “gold,” then, serves the chemist to comprise in this one word a whole swarm of sentences, that is, a whole treatise; in his eyes Au. is only an abbreviated sign for the expression of a whole great host of sentences with which he characterizes the behavior of this metal toward

2*

p. 20

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the most various bodies in the most various circumstances. If, then, we have the right to regard the sentence as composed of a series of words, we have conversely the right to conceive the word as the abbreviated designation of the content of a whole bundle of sentences. From one point of view the word appears simpler than the sentence; from the other, precisely conversely, the sentence appears simpler than the word. From which it follows that greater simplicity or compositeness is not at all suited to designate the relation of word and sentence. We shall therefore also beware of characterizing the relation of concepts and judgments to one another by the phrases of simplicity or compositeness. If, for example, someone wishes to unfold well the meaning of the words “elliptic function,” he must write a book on elliptic functions; as a sign that sometimes a thousand sentences taken together can alone give the clear meaning of a single concept.

As regards the peculiar relation of words and sentences, modern geometry sets before us a most interesting analogy in the relation of the point and the straight line. Ancient geometry conceived the relation of point and straight line one-sidedly, in so far as it was always inclined only to regard the straight line as a continuous series of points. Only in modern times did geometry overcome this one-sided conception, by conversely conceiving the point as the bundle of straight lines or rays passing through it. Modern geometry regards now the straight line as a row of points, now the point as a bundle of rays, and with this dual mode of intuition it has cast quite a new light upon the nature of spatial figures. As with ancient geometry, so we may call ancient logic one-sided too, since it always directs our attention only to this, that the judgment is nothing other than a series of concepts, but cannot arrive at the inverted conception, according to which the concept is nothing other than a bundle of judgments. This inversion of the ancient logical standpoint is useful for the reason, among others, that it frees our mind from the error that concept and judgment stand to one another in the relation of simple element of thought and composite thought, as well as from that other error, that our concepts do not fall, just as our judgments do, under the point of view of the true and the not true. If we keep in view only these two great errors, we feel at once how much the ancient logic stands in need of rigorous examination

p. 21

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and how little we may accept any single one of its propositions without criticism.

If we inquire why ancient logic was unable to make headway with the question of the relation of concepts and judgments, and why it did not notice that great problem which lies in the structure of our speech, namely in its formation out of sentences and words, we shall find the cause of this in the circumstance that it neglected the investigation of those sensible impressions upon which our knowledge rests. For only he who sets over against his impressions the signs with which he gives an account of those impressions notices how greatly the articulation of the signs used differs from the articulation of the impressions themselves. With such a sentence as, for example, “this rose is red” we wish to express only one fact, and if we nevertheless use several signs (words) to express this one fact, we may justly raise the question how the human mind is constituted, or rather how the system of signs of speech is constituted, if one fact has to be expressed by several signs. We run into the same question if we compare some Egyptian pictorial writing with our alphabetic writing and notice that the pictorial writing articulates thoughts otherwise than the alphabetic writing, which sets down speech. Our visual images have a different articulation from our sentences, and this circumstance may rightly set us thinking about the constitution of the human mind. But the problem that lies here is noticed only by him who, in logic, lays equal weight upon the investigation of impressions and of signs. Before we turn to this logic, however, we must examine more closely that modern logic which, in the investigation of knowledge, one-sidedly lays the chief weight only upon the investigation of impressions.

3. Impressionist Theory of Knowledge.

7. The historians of philosophy are accustomed to distinguish two great parties in the field of epistemological (logical) inquiry: the party of the sensualists and that of the rationalists. The former wish to trace human knowledge one-sidedly back to sensibility, the latter, with great predilection, to the faculty of “pure reason.” We cannot join either of these parties, if only because the history of philosophy shows that there has never yet been a philosopher who could have distinguished the

p. 22

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

sensible faculty from the faculty of reason clearly and in a manner excluding all misunderstanding. Those who speak of sensibility often understand by this word an intelligent sensibility; conversely, the worshipers of “pure reason” too fall at every step into the morass of including sensibility at once in the faculty of reason. Thus both parties wage a windmill-battle against one another, for each side openly attacks what it itself unnoticed and secretly acknowledges.

In general we cannot regard as fortunate any epistemological starting point which wishes to resolve the human faculty of cognition into two separate capacities, if only because attempts of this kind all dwell in a secret self-contradiction. For whoever resolves the faculty of cognition into two capacities regards these capacities either as mutually independent and self-sufficient faculties, or else as such that singly they are not able to acquire cognition, but bring forth knowledge only by a joint co-operation. There is no third possibility. The senselessness of the first case need not be discussed at length, for the existence of two independent faculties of cognition in one person would mean that the same person lived in two worlds which have nothing whatever to do with one another, as a result of which this one person would in truth be composed of two mutually independent persons. Thus only the second possibility can still come into question, when we regard the faculty of cognition as made up of two components (componens) lacking independence, which bring forth knowledge only in their togetherness.

Just as, for example, two branches unite in one trunk, or as two elements chemically produce a compound body, so two component capacities would constitute the unified faculty of cognition. I bring up these comparisons because here an interesting opportunity opens to us of showing how easy it is to abuse comparisons, that is, how easy it is by the aid of figurative speech to exhibit a want of thought in such a light as if it concealed a profound thought.

For we can speak of two branches of a unified trunk only because these branches actually diverge, and we can recognize a compound body as consisting of two elements only because it has previously been possible to set the elements free from their chemical combination and to catch them, as for example in the electrolysis of water, in separate test tubes. But is it possible, in the case of the faculty of cognition,

p. 23

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

to separate its alleged component parts from one another in this way as well? Is it possible, for example, so to set the sensible faculty in operation that the faculty of reason is meanwhile completely and utterly excluded? Even if we grant that this might be possible, then, in consequence of the perfect exclusion of reason, we could have no cognizance whatever of what in the world the wholly reasonless sensibility is doing. An equally barren endeavor it would be so to unhitch our sensibility that it falls perfectly silent and only the non-sensible reason is left to speak. For who in the world knows a procedure by whose help we could shut our organism off completely, not only from every external impression, but even from every memory-image of any such impression, indeed from every imaginative activity relating to the future? For the images of memory and the figures of fantasy belong, mark you, to the sphere of the sensuous! But supposing it were already to succeed in cutting off every sensible connection between ourselves and our surroundings, in suppressing every memory-image relating to past impressions, and in extinguishing from our soul every sensible play of imagination relating to the future: I ask, would our capacity for awareness then still remain, and if so, with what in the world could such an awareness occupy itself, which would no longer have any connection with the sensible world?

It is impossible to eliminate our sensibility so that only our mind, or to eliminate our mind so that only our sensibility, should operate, and for us to be able to have cognizance of these one-sided operations. And since this is impossible, it is evident that we cannot establish either what the mere (reasonless) sensibility and the mere (non-sensible) reason, each taken singly by itself, might contribute to our cognitions. For whoever wished to establish these contributions would first of all have to find a method for eliminating now reason, now sensibility. But to observe without reason what sensibility does, or to observe without sensibility what reason does, is alike an impossible undertaking.

Whoever doubts this may perhaps be convinced by the following argument. Let us suppose that someone should succeed, without eliminating reason and sensibility, in establishing what each of these two capacities contributes to the production of human cognition. Let us call a the contribution with which sensibility, and b the contribution with which reason, co-operates in the construction of human science: then these contributions are either themselves

p. 24

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

already cognitions, or in themselves not yet cognitions. In the first case a and b would themselves already represent cognition, knowledge; that is, sensibility itself and reason itself would already be able, independently of one another, to bring forth cognition. We should then have two independent faculties of cognition, we should live in two disconnected worlds and be composed of two unconnected persons, which is a manifest absurdity. In the second case our knowledge would consist of two elements, neither of which is knowledge; that is, knowledge could be put together out of such elements a and b as in themselves contain no knowledge at all. In my view this would mean that knowledge would be produced, in the strict sense of the word, in factories, like sulfuric acid, beet sugar, etc.

In the Critique of Pure Reason Kant makes a most interesting attempt at an artificial isolation of sensibility and reason, and at establishing what each of these faculties contributes to the construction of human knowledge. We are compelled to regard this notable epistemological endeavor as self-contradictory in itself and consequently as impracticable. The human faculty of cognition is one and indivisible, or, to say it still more clearly: we cannot resolve our cognitions into elements which would not be cognitions. Nor may we set it as the aim of the theory of knowledge to resolve the faculty of cognition, for the task of logic cannot be to disintegrate the human mind. We therefore take our stand against every dualist theory of knowledge which would resolve into specifically different elements either the faculty of cognition or its work, knowledge.

For precisely this reason we did not classify the theorists of knowledge on the ground of whether they incline more to sensibility or to reason: whether they are sensualists or rationalists. We could not classify them so, for none of them establishes how sensibility and its operation could be isolated from reason and its operation, and so how the contribution of each to knowledge could be determined separately. But we did declare that among the conditions of knowledge we count, on the one hand, that we should possess impressions, and on the other, that we should have at our disposal signs with which we can give an account of our impressions. By this remark we gained a new basis for the classification of the theorists of knowledge, in so far as we could distinguish the symbolist school from the impressionist school. In their investigation of knowledge the symbolists involuntarily think at once, one-sidedly, of the signs

p. 25

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

(or rather of the meaning of the signs) with which we express our knowledge, whereas the impressionists, in their reflections upon human knowledge, one-sidedly lay the chief emphasis upon the investigation of impressions. With this latter school, which in history is otherwise accustomed to appear under the name of empiricist or sensualist (or perhaps realist), we must already here, in this introductory part, concern ourselves somewhat more fully.

8. As the originator of the empiricist tendency in the modern theory of knowledge we may regard chiefly Bacon of Verulam. To speak in detail of his great importance for the history of culture is almost superfluous; for it is well known what an eminent part fell to him in the modern struggle for the freedom of inquiry. For in scientific inquiry there may arise periods too in which minds, falling too greatly under the influence of tradition, turn aside from the paths of independent experience and independent thinking in order to swear blindly by the opinions of great authorities of old. Bacon’s historical merit is that he contributed in large measure to the overcoming of that school-bound (scholastic) mode of thinking which, estranged from the unprejudiced investigation of nature, believed it could arrive at the fountainhead of all truth by hair-fine exegesis of Aristotle’s philosophy. But if we thus gladly count Bacon among the liberators of modern inquiry, we cannot close our eyes to the fact either that by his impressionist thinking he drew English theory of knowledge into one-sidedness, and thereby indirectly the whole philosophic spirit of the West too.

This one-sidedness manifests itself chiefly in his skeptical remarks on the system of signs of human speech. It is well known that Bacon, in his witty, rhapsodic manner, divided the errors of mankind into four groups, which are to be traced back to the false concepts of the tribe, the cave, the market-place and the theater (“idola tribus, — specus, — fori and theatri”). Among these alleged classes of false concepts the “false concepts of the market-place” are of particular interest to us here; according to Bacon they arise from the common (market-place) but improper use of words. By way of example he adduces a few notable words to which particularly false concepts attach, such as: essence, quality, etc.; but that he objects not merely to individual words, but looks upon human speech in general with a suspicious eye (he was, after all, a politician by profession!), is evident from the fact that he distinguishes between the divine marks (properties) of things and their human designations, understanding by divine marks undoubtedly those impressions,

p. 26

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

which we receive from objects through our sense organs, and by human designations those words with which we give an account of our impressions. Behold, thus he strives to play off one condition of existence of our knowledge against the other, impressions against signs, in order to ascribe divine authority to the former, and as far as possible to make the value of the latter doubtful.

I do not believe that we can acquiesce in Bacon’s distinction above, for if one has once pronounced the impressions which things awaken in us to be of divine origin, we do not see why we should not regard the system of signs of our speech too as of divine origin, since those who use the word God understand by it the fountainhead of all things. Conversely, if we pronounce the signs of speech to be of human character, we do not see why we should not regard our sensible impressions too as of human character, since those impressions which the human organism mediates can, for that very reason, be only impressions of a human nature. In that distinction, then, which pronounces the impressions to be of divine origin and the signs to be human, we see nothing but a witty play on words, which, through the obscurity of the concepts “divine” and “human,” easily leads our thinking into error. If Bacon fights with such brilliant spirit against those dangerous false concepts which arise from the uncertain-meaning market-place use of the signs of speech, ought he not first of all to have shown particular caution in the use of such words as “divine” and “human”? Who has established, in a manner excluding all misunderstanding, what it is in us that is divine and what it is in us that is merely human? If it suits X. that the impressions should be divine and the signs of human nature, then Y. with the same right could maintain the inverse conception, according to which it is precisely in the signs of speech that we must recognize some divine gift, whereas our sensible impressions would have to be pronounced human, indeed downright animal, in character. It is evident that the one play on words is worth just as much as the other, and that it is not worth taking either of them seriously. The lesson from the matter is only how very right Bacon is that the greatest danger may threaten human knowledge from the careless use of words, but at the same time also this, that precisely because of this danger the investigation of signs belongs among the highest and most important tasks of the theory of knowledge.

p. 27

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Every endeavor that casts suspicion upon human speech in general, is in truth nothing other than a skepticism that attacks human knowledge at its root. But we now see clearly also that two kinds of skepticism must be distinguished, according as the doubt is directed against the reliability of our sense organs or against that of our speech. The two kinds of doubt were set going in the history of modern philosophy by Descartes and Bacon: the former, namely, declared our sensible impressions to be deceptive, the latter the signs of our speech. That in both kinds of doubt some truth is contained, no one can deny; on the one hand, namely, it is certain that, however carefully we observe phenomena, we can never arrive at perfect observations — and this is what we can make our own from Descartes’s doubt; on the other hand it is just as certain that we shall never establish a system of signs with which we could describe our observations with perfect precision — and this is what we too can accept from Bacon’s doubt. Both doubts taken together, however, contain only this one truth: that, being men, we cannot possess perfect knowledge. With this truth we set out upon our road, but we added at once that from the fact that we do not possess perfect knowledge we must not infer — as timorous minds are so very much inclined to do — that therefore we cannot possess any knowledge at all. Whoever asserts that we can know absolutely nothing wishes by this very assertion to express some knowledge, and so falls into contradiction with himself. There is no sense, then, in our despairing utterly of human knowledge; on the contrary, precisely because our observations are so imperfect and the signs of our speech so unreliable, we may have the prospect that we shall be able to make ever more exact observations and shall form ever more faithful signs for the description of our observations. For if we possessed a perfect faculty of observation or a perfect system of signs, we should be relieved of every endeavor of a human character. We must therefore reject both forms of skepticism, for in the end it is wholly a matter of indifference whether someone, from a rationalist inclination, holds the sensible impressions to be deceptive — as Descartes does — or, from an empiricist inclination, seeks to deprive human speech of its credit, as Bacon does, with more or less openness. We see skeptical doctrines in rationalism and empiricism alike, because whether

p. 28

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

our impressions or our signs, someone seeks to destroy the credit of, he is aiming in the final analysis at the destruction of our knowledge. It is true that from another point of view rationalism as well as empiricism may be called dogmatic doctrines, in so far as the one inclines to presumption in “pure reason,” the other to being rooted fast in the “palpable.”

For a twofold reason, then, we must take the field against rationalism and empiricism alike, for each of these tendencies has its own hobby-horse, which it idolizes in a dogmatic manner, while skeptically belittling the hobby-horse of the other. We cannot allow the importance of our sensible impressions to be emphasized at the expense of the signs of speech, or conversely our sign-forming faculty to be exalted merely in order that the frailty of our senses may stand before us in the darkest possible light. We have equal cause for dissatisfaction with our impressions and with our speech-signs, but we can equally perfect the methods of our observation and of our designation. And we must strive alike for both kinds of perfection, because impressions and signs are alike indispensable conditions of human science. As to the indispensability of impressions, it is not even necessary to insist upon it, but it is the more important to stress the indispensability of signs, because very often we meet with the false belief that our sensible impressions are already, taken by themselves, sufficient conditions of knowledge, indeed that the impression itself is already at the same time knowledge. Against this view we maintain that just as without impressions, so without expressive signs all human knowledge would be impossible.

To be confirmed in this conception, it is enough to cast a glance at the course of intellectual development of the human infant. The child could not arrive at knowledge, at cognitions, if its organism were endowed only with sensible receptivity, but remained incapable of that notable art which we may call sign-formation. If, for example, the child, from the beginnings of babbling, were never able to arrive at that stage of development in which it can find meaning in the words (or mimic movements) of others and put meaning into its own, then who in the world would believe that such a child could acquire knowledge, cognitions? In order that man may develop into a being who acquires cognitions and creates science, it is absolutely required that he be not only endowed with tactile, visual, auditory, etc. sensitivities

p. 29

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

but also able to put meaning into signs. We do not lay particular weight here on whether these signs must necessarily be audible signs, and if the reader likes to imagine human-shaped beings on other stars, whose organism possesses the power of giving light, and who express their cognitions by colored luminous signs, then we shall not stand in the way of such imaginings. Anyone, too, who in connection with these discussions should wish to point to deaf-mutes, or to some paralyzed persons who, deprived of the use of their vocal cords, their tongues, even their arms, could not produce any signs at all, although no one could deny them that they think and possess cognitions, would misunderstand us entirely. The only question here is whether the human race as such would be capable of acquiring cognitions and developing science if it were not endowed with the capacity of putting meaning into signs.

9. One of the chief reasons why such a question must even be raised, we must point to here already. Everyone knows those states of noiseless, silent or mute reflection in which a man produces no audible signs and makes no noteworthy movements either, but “meditates, absorbed in himself.” Now, it is the mystic nature of these “absorbed” states that causes men to hold the use of signs to be a quite incidental matter, and to begin to imagine that we could arrive at the profoundest knowledge without any help of the sign-forming capacity, indeed they fall straight into the false belief that the word is only an “externality,” only the “fetterer and paralyzer” of flashing reason, of the faculty of cognition. For men become eloquent at once as soon as they wish to deceive themselves completely with some half-truth.

There is no doubt that a deep mystery covers those functions of our organism which make it possible for us to meditate, absorbed in ourselves. But the veil of this mystery we may perhaps lift a little if we point to some kindred or similar phenomena. Whoever, for example, reads a newspaper or a book silently scarcely notices — while his eyes run along the lines — that he is translating the visible letters in imagination into audible sounds, although there is no doubt that this translation must take place, since the recognition of letters consists in precisely this. True, but in consequence of many years of practice we pass so quickly and so mechanically from the letters seen to the sounds corresponding to them that we no longer even

p. 30

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

notice the transition. There was a time, however, when this transition was by no means so easy and mechanical as now: I mean the time when in childhood we learned to read, and under the teacher’s supervision had to pronounce aloud, a hundred times over, the sound belonging to some letter-shape, until at last we were able to connect the letter-shape mechanically with the corresponding sound. This progress made it possible for us to pass from reading aloud to silent reading, for the first condition of silent or soundless reading is that we be able to pass so quickly and mechanically from the letters to the corresponding imagined sounds that we ourselves do not notice the transition. From this we see that certain deliberately linked functions of our organism can, through practice, become so quick and mechanical that for this reason they easily escape our attention. This physiological truth is very well suited to shed light into the mysteries of absorbed meditation.

I may leave it to the reader to draw a detailed parallel between silent meditation and silent reading, since the latter is nothing other than a silent thinking-after of a text of thought already prescribed. I wish here to bring out only two points of this parallel: 1. As in silent reading, so in silent thinking, quick and mechanical physiological functions play a part, which we do not notice. In silent reading, for example, we do not as a rule notice that we attach in imagination the corresponding sound-signs to the letter-signs seen. Similarly, in silent thinking too, imagined signs (imagined words, imagined mimic movements) that have been formed quickly and mechanically play a part, and they escape our attention. 2. As we could acquire the skill of silent reading only by first learning to read aloud, so we could acquire the wonderful art of silent thinking only by first learning to think aloud, to put meaning into audible (or visible) signs.

If, then, in childhood we had not learned to put meaning into audible signs, we should later not be able to think with silent and imperceptible signs. If our childhood lacked that stage in which we passed from babbling to meaningful sign-formation, then those periods of life could not occur either in which, absorbed in ourselves, apparently without any sign-formation, we are able to think. In a word: between thinking aloud and silent thinking there is no essential difference

p. 31

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

unless we regard as essential the greater speed and mechanical character which we attain, by many years of practice, in the imperceptible performance of certain physiological functions. Whoever, then, under the influence of the mysterious character of silent thinking, should persuade himself that for thinking we have no need of signs would fare like a conjurer who, because he can quickly make a pocket watch vanish, afterwards persuades himself that he has really annihilated that watch.

Signs, then, are not such so-called “externalities” as we could in the least dispense with in the acquisition of knowledge. The chief cause why the theory of knowledge as a science could not enter upon a healthy development I believe I find in the circumstance that thinkers were more or less inclined to regard signs as some incidental, external trimming with respect to knowledge. From this confused thought two great historical errors arose: 1. the rationalist error, which believes that true thinking is that which is signless; this dreams of a “pure reason” which shuts itself off airtight from the sensible world; 2. the empiricist or sensualist error, which, because it holds signs to be inessential for knowledge, comes to regard the impressions furnished by the sense organs as the sole condition of knowledge, to such a degree that it can no longer distinguish at all between impression and knowledge. Both errors, as we see, spring from one and the same fountainhead, for both fail to recognize the boundless importance of signs for the existence and further development of human knowledge. We can take the field successfully against both errors only if we first convince ourselves of the truth of the proposition that without signs there is no human knowledge.

We readily admit, however, that in a certain respect signs are no longer means but, alas, rather hindrances to knowledge. For if by knowledge we understand not human but absolute knowledge, then, in relation to such absolute knowledge, the use of signs can represent only a hindrance. A being which in acquiring knowledge is compelled to lean on the crutch of signs can never attain perfect knowledge. But quite similarly stands the matter with our impressions, for a being which needs impressions in order to come to knowledge is shut out from the infinite bliss of absolute knowledge. Our impressions and our signs are therefore means, or rather indispensable conditions, of our knowledge only in so far as

p. 32

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

human knowledge is in question; they at once become hindrances to our knowledge, however, if by knowledge we understand divine omniscience. I think it can now stand quite clearly before us why such great thinkers as Descartes and Bacon declared the impressions, or the speech-signs, to be deceptive. For whoever expects either from the impressions or from the signs that they should lead to omniscience will be disappointed in them. But can we set omniscience before ourselves as an attainable goal? And if we cannot, may we despise our impressions and our signs because they were not ordained to serve omniscience? Whoever is content with human knowledge will find our impressions and our signs good for this end, and, what is the chief thing, he will recognize them as improvable without limit, and so will be able to build upon them a science capable of development without limit.
