4. The Psychologistic Theory of Knowledge.

10. It is Bacon’s merit that by his bold attack upon scholastic thinking he had a liberating effect upon minds, and likewise that by his exposition of the method of the investigation of nature he gave a strong impulse to the construction of a new logic or theory of knowledge. What in him, however, was rather only exhortation, demand and at best a groping experimentation, soon begins to take on definite forms in his successors. Thus in Hobbes there already comes to very sharp expression that belittling, indeed outright contempt, of the signs of human speech which is the chief characteristic feature of every impressionist theory of knowledge. Much more important, however, is it that Locke already presents in detailed and copious elaboration the new theory of knowledge, which was born under the sign of impressionism, but by its peculiar shading may be called a “psychologistic” theory of knowledge.

Locke’s work (on the inquiry into human reason) is of epoch-making importance in the history of modern philosophy, not because some logical genius reveals itself in it, but rather because it is the first example of that new genre which we may call a “theory of knowledge.” The significance of this work is rightly appreciated only by him who takes into account how great an effort human thinking needs before it rises from the stage of childlike philosophizing about nature to epistemological inquiry. The primitive

p. 33

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

philosophy of nature raises such questions as: Out of what did the world arise, by whom or by what did it come into being, how did it come to be such as it is, for what purpose does it exist, and what will be its end? Questions of this sort usually start from a false presupposition and lead not so much to philosophizing as rather to a mythological play of imagination. In order that we may be able to raise rightly the questions that relate to the universe, our faculty of reason, or rather our knowledge, must first be subjected to investigation; that is, in other words, we must arrive at the ontological questions by the epistemological road. For this reason we must distinguish two kinds of metaphysics: 1. primitive philosophy of nature, which approaches the great questions of the universe without investigating cognition and produces rather mythology than science; of this character was chiefly the philosophizing of the ancient peoples of the East, as well as the Ionian philosophy of nature; 2. epistemological, that is, scientific metaphysics, which subjects the mind, or rather the science brought forth by it, to systematic investigation, in order to be able in this connection to discuss the questions that relate to the universe. The proper father of this epistemological inquiry we honor in Socrates, for it was he who, by the analysis of our concepts, strove to lead the mind to self-knowledge, that is, to logic. His school brought forth the ancient logic, over against which the modern age, above all since Descartes and Bacon, is endeavoring to develop a new logic. The first detailed formation of this new logic is represented by Locke’s work, for which reason we must concern ourselves with it somewhat more thoroughly already here in the introduction.

Locke sets as his direct aim the investigation of human reason, or rather of human knowledge, whereby he exercised a decisive influence upon the whole of modern philosophy, in so far as he raised the epistemological direction of thought to dominance over primitive philosophizing about nature. That in the philosophy of Berkeley and Hume, as of Leibniz and Kant, the epistemological point of view wins ever greater ground, we owe for the most part to Locke, who was able to make this point of view positively popular by the directness and raciness of his exposition, as well as by the candor and honesty of his reasoning. We must acknowledge this the more readily because in other respects we shall have to fight most steadfastly against those doctrines which flowed out of Locke’s work precisely into the whole modern scientific spirit.

Already into the program of the theory of knowledge he took up two points which became the occasion of great misunderstandings. 1. Of the theory of knowledge he demands, among

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 3

p. 34

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

other things, that it take under investigation the origin or fountainhead of knowledge. We understand it when a poet makes use of such a figurative mode of expression as the “source” of human knowledge, but we do not believe that the theory of knowledge could make the least headway with such phrases. By the “source of knowledge” everyone may understand his own faculty of cognition, but then there is no need at all of investigating the “source of knowledge,” for we can simply remain with the investigation of the faculty of cognition. If, however, someone does not understand by the source of knowledge the faculty of cognition, then he awakens the strange thought as if there were somewhere under the sun a source from which we could draw knowledge with a pitcher. Locke regards the impressions, or rather experience, as the “source” of knowledge, which easily tempts to the dangerous false belief that the impressions already, taken by themselves, represent knowledge. We shall convince ourselves that he did in fact greatly confuse the impressions with knowledge. 2. Locke demands also that the theory of knowledge examine the limits of human knowledge; chiefly in order that we may not embark on the investigation of things which surpass our human understanding. Locke still rather only plays with this demand, yet he brought thereby an enormous danger into the development of the more recent theory of knowledge, for Kant made his own this epistemological tendency of inquiring into and establishing limits, whereby — as we shall see — he turned the theory of knowledge into a science which in the greatest measure endangers the freedom of scientific inquiry.

For what does it mean to establish the limits of our knowledge? Such a demand can have only this sense, that we divide truths into two groups, an a group and a b group, in such a way that into the one there fall the truths cognizable by the son of man, into the other the truths not cognizable by the son of man. It means further that some distinguishing mark must be found for those truths which remain eternally incognizable to man, and that these incognizable truths are distinguished from the cognizable truths. But if anyone could find some distinguishing mark of the incognizable truths, he would thereby prove that those alleged incognizable truths are not wholly unknown to him. In a word, it is a logical absurdity to divide truths into the cognizable and the not cognizable, for this very division demands of us that we know some characteristic of the not cognizable truths by which they are distinguished from the cognizable

p. 35

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

truths. It is, then, a logical absurdity to set before the theory of knowledge the aim that it investigate the limits of human knowledge.

Everyone has, to be sure, the right to speak of truths which he knows and of such as he does not know. But it is quite another thing not to know something and never at all to be able to cognize something. Whoever wishes to distinguish eternally incognizable truths from cognizable truths, and sets precisely this distinction as the aim of the theory of knowledge, undertakes the task of making us acquainted with the distinguishing mark of the incognizable truths. Is not this a plain logical impossibility?

What should we say of a man who wished to mark off with stakes the ends of the world, or rather of the space of the world, as an engineer marks off an enclosed piece of ground. The realm of human knowledge has no limits that can be marked out, just as the space of the world has no marked-out limits. If some theorist of knowledge wishes to mark the limits of our knowledge, then this is an enterprise of the Háry János sort, in which a man, having wandered off to the end of the world, dangles his legs there into nothingness. It is not modesty, then, that reveals itself in the investigation of the limits of human knowledge, but rather false modesty, or rather presumption. None of us can ever get so far in knowledge that another could not get still farther, and so I confess that I can see only presumption in every enterprise that wishes to set a limit somewhere to our knowledge. Such presumption may be in good faith too, but it does not on that account cease to be in the highest degree harmful to the development of science. Human knowledge has no limit, except this one, that we cannot be omniscient. Whoever keeps this always before his eyes can never become presumptuous, but neither can he ever fall into the misery of faint-heartedness.

We do not, then, set before the theory of knowledge the logically impossible aim of investigating the limits of human knowledge, and of planting poles, as it were, at those points of the realm of truth beyond which the human mind cannot penetrate. The realm of truth is infinite, and to whatever point of it we may have advanced, we gain thereby only the prospect that we shall be able to advance still further. The theory of knowledge cannot undertake, then, the role of checking the development of human knowledge by marking out final boundaries, but on the contrary it sets itself the aim, by the investigation of the nature of our knowledge,

3*

p. 36

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of opening up ever new and fresh regions of truth, as much for its own inquiries as for the researches of all the special sciences. In this sense logic (theory of knowledge) is nothing other than the doctrine of progress of human knowledge, that is, its strategy.

11. If we already have strong objections to the points of the program of Locke’s theory of knowledge, we see all the greater dangers in the actual execution of this theory of knowledge. Above all, Locke declares war upon the doctrine of innate ideas, which is the most characteristic component of Descartes’s theory of knowledge. The great warfare which was waged between the defenders and the assailants of innate ideas casts a characteristic light upon those warfares in general which fill the philosophical literature of any given epoch. If we set these two propositions against one another: we have innate ideas; we have no innate ideas, then these are two propositions over which the suit may go on sine fine, because the assertion has as little sense as the denial. For no one can know clearly what in fact we are to understand by innate ideas. First of all, namely, it is doubtful whether we are really dealing with ideas, or only with a “disposition” to ideas. But further it is also questionable whether the so-called innate ideas, or rather their dispositions, descend into us when we come into the world, or in some stage of the embryonic state, or perhaps penetrate into our mind at the moment of conception. Or, finally, should we have to regard the human individual as existing from all eternity, and those ideas as “engraved” from all eternity in the mind of the individual, which, as opposed to “acquired concepts,” would be innate concepts? A flood of such and similar questions shows well enough that the most various and at the same time most obscure meanings attach to the expression “innate ideas.” The history of the theory of knowledge itself lays bare before us that there never arose a philosopher who was able to establish a clear sense for the expression in question. In general the pseudo-concept of innate ideas sprang from that dualistic mode of thinking which wished to resolve the faculty of cognition into two faculties: an acquired faculty (sensibility), which develops in the course of individual life, and an inherited faculty (“pure reason”), which would have passed over to the individual from the ancestors, or rather from God. I think it is not necessary to explain at greater length what an absurdity lies in this, if we wished to distinguish between ideas derived from God

p. 37

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

(that is, innate) and ideas not derived from God, that is, acquired.

There is a principle of logic according to which, if of two sentences, A and B, the one asserts what the other denies, then one of the two sentences must necessarily be true. How very carefully this principle must be handled, the struggle over innate ideas throws a most interesting light. Here are two sentences confronting one another: we have innate ideas; we have no innate ideas, of which the latter denies what the first asserts, and yet we cannot hold either of the two sentences to be true! And why? Because the asserting sentence is of wholly uncertain meaning, and so the denying sentence too can have no definite meaning. Of the disputing parties, then, the one does not know what he asserts, and the other does not know what he is contradicting. Such sorry disputes have brought philosophical dispute into ill repute in general. But we ought not to be impatient even with those disputes which begin over indefinite assertions and denials, for these very disputes may also have the result that the parties, becoming enlightened in the course of the dispute, formulate their positions intelligibly. Every philosophical dispute may be profitable, provided only that the parties really thirst for truth and do not wage war out of mere vanity.

The rationalists, who contended for innate ideas, obscurely felt the truth that our sensible impressions, taken by themselves, are not yet sufficient conditions of knowledge; they sought, then, the other condition of our knowledge too, but since they could not notice that which lay nearest, the signs of man, they ran willy-nilly after the phantom of innate ideas. Locke and the empiricists, on the other hand, felt very well that a theory of knowledge cannot be founded upon the dissolving vapor of innate ideas; only they deluded themselves with the belief that the impressions, taken by themselves, are sufficient conditions of knowledge. Locke sets out upon his epistemological researches under the spell of this thought, but what is so very characteristic of his honest inquiry is that after long reflections he begins to suspect some flaw in his original starting-point, and with endearing candor at once confesses it. This confession of Locke’s is of epoch-making importance in the history of the theory of knowledge, for it lights up like lightning the

p. 38

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the great error of the impressionist thinkers, by which they believe that the sole condition of existence of knowledge is sensible impression, and that consequently the science of knowledge, that is, the theory of knowledge, may content itself with the investigation of impressions, while it can wholly dispense with regard for speech-signs. Let Locke’s own words stand here:

“I must confess, then, that, when I first began this Discourse of the Understanding, and a good while after, I had not the least thought that any consideration of words [the signs of speech] was at all necessary to it. But when, having passed over the original and composition of our ideas, I began to examine the extent and certainty of our knowledge, I found it had so near a connexion with words [speech-signs], that, unless their force and manner of signification were first well observed, there could be very little said clearly and pertinently concerning knowledge.” . . . (Essay, Book III, ch. 9, § 21.)

Behold the impressionist theorist of knowledge, who persuades himself that the human mind can be subjected to scientific investigation without so much as mentioning the existence of human speech! Locke’s great merit is that he begins to awaken from this error and, in the middle of his work, perceives that the theorist of knowledge must treat of human speech too. What he does not see, however, is that he ought to have treated of speech-signs from the very beginning, just as he did of impressions, since human knowledge can exist just as little without signs as without impressions. Locke’s inquiry into the understanding therefore lays before us that critical situation of English impressionist theory of knowledge in which it begins to surmise the importance of the human speech-signs, without being able to draw the proper conclusions from this surmise. We may say outright that English theory of knowledge, which nevertheless represents the grandest exertion of the human mind in the interest of establishing a new logic, suffered shipwreck on the reef that, by reason of its one-sided impressionist inclinations, it was unable to recognize the significance of symbols as it should. In vain does Locke devote the whole third book of his work to reflections on words: he is nevertheless unable to appreciate the nature of speech-signs as he should, because he cannot free himself from the influence of the thought that impressions represent the sole condition of existence of our knowledge. This shows itself plainly where he confuses impressions with knowledge, or rather with concepts.

p. 39

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

By a concept we understand the meaning that attaches to our words, so that the investigation of concepts coincides with the investigation of the meaning of words. But Locke, by his impressionist inclination, strives to identify “concepts” with “impressions.” Thus, for example, he presents the “concept” of the triangle as if it were nothing else than the “impression” (or idea) of the triangle. According to him our concept of the triangle is nothing else than a general impression which unites in itself our impressions of the most various triangles. The general impression (or image) of the triangle would therefore be right-angled, acute-angled, obtuse-angled, isosceles, equilateral, scalene, and yet none of all these. Let Locke’s own words stand here: “For example, does it not require some pains and skill to form the general idea of a triangle, (which is yet none of the most abstract, comprehensive, and difficult,) for it must be neither oblique nor rectangle, neither equilateral, equicrural, nor scalenon; but all and none of these at once.” (Essay, Book IV, ch. 7, § 9.) Locke thus demands outright that we form an image of the general triangle, an image which is, for example, at once equilateral and scalene, since only such an image gives the general concept of the triangle. He himself shows us what an absurdity it is to form an image of a general concept and yet to require that the general concept itself should be only an image, that is, an impression. By this, however, he has rendered the theory of knowledge a very great service, for with almost childlike candor he exposes to us the inner contradiction of the impressionist way of thinking. He wants at any price to make images, that is, impressions, out of concepts; that is, he involuntarily wants to eliminate the signs which we use, in order to replace them with mere impressions. For, to repeat once more, concepts are nothing else than certain signs, insofar as meaning attaches to these signs. Now signs and impressions may not be exchanged for one another. The word “red” cannot be exchanged, for example, with the impression of red, just as words and their meaning in general cannot be exchanged with the facts to which we may happen to refer them.

I must remark here that Locke, already in the choice of his technical terms, proceeds in such a way that the difference between impressions and concepts (or rather

p. 40

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

signs) may vanish. The word “idea” serves him to confound the meaning of these two words, “impression” and “concept.” He calls an idea, for example, the impression of the color red, just as much as the meaning which we attach to the word red; he calls an idea, for example, the impression of “soft,” just as much as the signification that attaches to the word “doubt.” The German philosophers translated this Lockean “idea” by “Vorstellung”; and we Magyars, for our part, hastened to coin the word “képzet” for “Vorstellung.” This word “Vorstellung,” or “képzet,” is as it were the embodiment of the error that lurks in the impressionist way of thinking, for these words were used so that the difference between impression and sign (or rather between image and concept) should vanish from our thinking, and so that the false belief should arise in us that impression, taken by itself, were already knowledge. It is high time, then, that the Lockean “idea,” the German “Vorstellung,” and the Magyar “képzet” should be put on the index of serious philosophers, as expressions which have been used for centuries to confuse human thinking.

12. But the impressionist way of thinking, as we have seen, already carries within itself the seeds of inner dissolution: not only because there dawns in it the surmise that a theory of knowledge is hardly conceivable without an investigation of signs, but also because it is compelled to arrange impressions (or rather “ideas”) in two classes, of which the one class springs from outer, the other, however, from inner sensibility (or sense). This “inner sense” (internal sense), which Locke introduced into modern philosophy, exerted an immeasurable influence on the whole of modern thinking, insofar as it gave the impulse to the development of “psychology” in the modern sense (empirical psychology), and impressed upon the whole of modern inquiry into knowledge the stamp of “psychologism,” by which the theory of knowledge, that is, logic, seems to be as it were submerged in so-called psychology.

According to Locke we must distinguish from the outer senses a mysterious inner sense as well. The outer senses furnish the soul, by contact with outer objects, with impressions (ideas) such as yellow, white, cold, warm, soft, hard, bitter, sweet, etc. But the soul draws ideas, or rather impressions, from yet another wellspring besides. For by the fact that it first receives impressions from the first source, outer sensibility, in the

p. 41

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

course of time it comes to attend to its own activity as well, and receives such ideas as “perception,” “thinking,” “doubting,” “belief,” “reasoning,” “knowledge,” “willing,” etc. If anywhere, it is here that one can see what a monstrous confusion is caused by the expression “idea” as used by Locke, insofar as he calls an idea the impression “yellow” arising from outer sensibility, just as much as the concept arising from inner sensibility, that is, from self-observation, which the soul forms for itself of its own “willing,” its own “knowledge,” etc. Locke himself expresses himself as follows about inner sensibility: “This [second] source of ideas every man has wholly in himself; and though it be not sense, as having nothing to do with external objects, yet it is very like it, and might properly enough be called internal sense.” (Essay, Book II, ch. 1, § 4.)

Here too, as in many other cases, Locke exposes the logical confusion of his thinking with rare childlikeness. For on the one hand he states clearly that the inner sense is a faculty which is not sensible; on the other hand, however, by his impressionist inclinations, he nevertheless wants to qualify the inner sense as some kind of sensible faculty, so that it appears from his words that he really wants to formulate a faculty which is at once sensible and not sensible, just as above he spoke also of a triangle which would be at once equilateral and scalene.

If we consider how Locke’s outer and inner sense could divide between them the mental work to be done, we see at once that the distinction of two senses of this kind is a complete impossibility. The outer sense would indeed have impressions, but it would have no cognizance of its own activity; the inner sense, on the other hand, would procure just this missing consciousness, but then it could have no impressions itself. For if the inner sense itself had impressions, it would not need to attend to whether the outer sense disposes of such, and then it would indeed gain cognizance of its own impressions without concerning itself in the least with the activity of the outer sense. Thus the two kinds of sense could divide their role only in such a way that the one would have impressions, but would not have cognizance of them, while the other would have the taking of cognizance, but would have no impressions: which is an obvious absurdity. For how, pray, is the inner sense to gain cognizance of any impression if it is itself insensible.

p. 42

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

This whole distribution of roles has the appearance as if a blind man were to borrow eyes for himself in order to see with them, and to encourage himself in this strange loan with the following reasoning: The eye that I borrow has, to be sure, impressions, but has no consciousness of its activity; I, however, who am the fortunate possessor of an “inner sense,” shall with the help of this faculty observe attentively what sensations of color and shape the borrowed eye has. In this way my taking cognizance supplements the eye’s unconscious feelings, which together yield conscious seeing. Such is the speculatio that reveals itself in Locke’s outer and inner sense: a sense that is unconscious, and a consciousness that is insensible, produce by addition a conscious sensibility. What is chiefly characteristic of Locke’s speculation is that he names the insensible consciousness an inner sense.

After this I may perhaps be permitted to say that Locke’s “inner sense” is hardly anything else than a logically confused formulation of what we may call the self-knowledge of the mind. We human beings are able, with the help of signs, to become aware of our impressions, but at the same time we are also able to become aware of our signs by means of further signs, and so of our own activity of awareness itself. Becoming aware of awareness, knowledge of knowledge, is precisely that with which logic, or the theory of knowledge, is concerned. It was this knowledge of knowledge that Locke misunderstood when he introduced the concept of the “inner sense” into recent philosophy. For having, by his impressionist inclination, exchanged knowledge for impression, he wanted to regard knowledge of knowledge too as an impression. He was compelled, therefore, to postulate two kinds of sensibility, of which the one — outer sensibility — would be nothing else than the knowledge we possess of the “external world,” while the other — inner sensibility — would furnish us with the knowledge relating to our inner world, that is, to knowledge. The once Socratic self-knowledge thus became mere sensibility.

Later I shall set forth in detail that man can refer by signs not only to his impressions but can also refer to these signs by further signs, in consequence of which we must distinguish object-awareness, that is, direct awareness (reference by signs to impressions), from contrary awareness, that is, self-awareness (reference by signs to signs). And we shall see that logic, by developing self-awareness, strives to advance object-awareness as well, and together with this

p. 43

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the whole of human knowledge. Since, however, Locke and the empiricists were unable to appreciate the role of signs in human knowledge, they could not arrive at a proper distinction between object-awareness and self-awareness, still less at the formulation, just indicated, of the task of logic (or the theory of knowledge). With them object-awareness becomes outer sensibility, and self-awareness becomes inner sensibility; and the trouble here is not that two names are replaced by two other names, but that there could be serious talk of inner sensibility only if the empiricists could point to those inner impressions which inner sensibility commands. Of the outer senses we know that they convey such impressions as white, red, warm, cold, round, angular, etc., etc., but of inner sensibility we do not know what impressions it conveys, since Locke himself admits that the inner sensibility he has in mind is, properly speaking, not sensibility at all. In this way “inner sensibility” is rather a mere sound of a word, behind which perhaps some kind of surmise lies hidden, but which we must call an empty word (a seeming concept) so long as those inner impressions to which we are supposed to attain through that “inner sensibility” are not clearly enumerated.

By replacing self-knowledge with an enigmatic inner sensibility Locke gave occasion to a peculiar confusion: for it became obscured what the task of logic is, so that in the place of logic there could step “psychology” (in the empiricist sense), as a science which is destined to displace and replace logic. Originally, that is, logic is the science that investigates knowledge and accordingly yields knowledge about knowledge, awareness about awareness; but ever since Locke distinguished two kinds of sensibility, of which the inner sensibility was to be charged with giving an account of the activity of the soul, a new science has arisen which recalled logic to the extent that it too exercised awareness over awareness, yet differed from the ancient (symbolist) logic entirely, because it wanted to observe by means of an inner sense what takes place in the soul, and wanted to describe the mental (inner) phenomenon as meteorology, for example, describes the (outer) phenomena of the weather. This peculiar descriptive logic, or mental meteorology, then developed into that special science which has been called empirical psychology in recent times. I call this science peculiar because never

p. 44

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

has anyone been able to say clearly what kind of “sense” that “inner sense” might be, what kind of impressions the “inner impressions” might be, indeed whether such “inner impressions” exist at all.

Yes, but the word “sense” has worked with tremendous magic, and especially “inner sense,” which of course everyone was the more eager to possess because no one knew what was to be understood by it. This “inner sense” meanwhile began to work wonders, since everyone began to look into his own mental processes; to look into them in the strict sense of the word, for from an “inner sense” we can expect nothing else than that it should look sensibly into the processes of the soul. Whereas the old logic demanded of man that he reflect upon his own mental life, which was indeed a rather tiresome thing, the comfort-loving and inventive modern age suddenly found the admirable method which leads to self-knowledge without reflection. All that was needed was the “inner sense,” with the help of which the processes of the soul could be observed just as, for example, with the outer sense we observe the flight of the cranes.

I do not wish to discuss at length here what ravages the “inner sense” and the “inner observation” (innere Beobachtung) arising from it have wrought in belles-lettres, especially in the field of narrative and drama. A whole legion of modern narrative writers and dramatists considered the plot, or rather the action (which is nevertheless the proper backbone of narrative and dramatic works), negligible, and in its place came forward with ever queerer inner observations, creating heroes who, in consequence of perpetual psychologizing, arrived at no action, indeed not even at a thought, and who seemed to exist only in order to delude themselves with imagined mental processes such as never ran and never could have run their course in any human soul. This sickly psychologistic current still grows and swells in the more recent belles-lettres, so that it would be very necessary for some critical mind to set forth in detail the intellectual error that lies hidden in it, an error which stands in close connection with the psychologistic tendency of the more recent theory of knowledge. I wish here to keep in view only the influence which this tendency has exercised on the sciences, or rather on philosophy.

13. The Lockean “inner sense” was adopted by Kant too, although he stood to a certain degree in conflict with the empiricist theory of knowledge

p. 45

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

insofar as he did not identify knowledge with mere impression. He wished to mediate between the rationalist and empiricist currents of the theory of knowledge, but in this mediation he arrived at appropriating the most obscure and most dangerous concept of the empiricist (psychologistic) theory of knowledge, the inner sense, whereby German philosophy opened all its gates to the reception of English psychologism, although in truth it was precisely this tendency that he meant to check with his Critique of Pure Reason. How rapidly the psychologistic tendency gained the upper hand in his wake is shown by the philosophizing of Reinhold, Fries, Beneke and the great Herbartian school; and if for a time, in the age of feverish philosophical speculation of the Fichte- Schelling-Hegel kind, psychologism seemed to be pushed into the background, it broke forth with all the greater power after the fall of Hegelian philosophy in the systems of Lotze, Fechner and Wundt, as well as in the philosophy of the neo-Kantians. If I am not mistaken, one-sided psychologism has in our own day reached the culminating point of its vogue, from which nothing but the fate of an imperceptible decline can await it.

It is curious what a great power of attraction concepts of manifold, or rather indeterminate, meaning exert on minds. Just as the rationalists waged a gigantic battle around the pseudo-concept of innate ideas, so the mystical concept of the inner sense, which the empiricists took up, also called forth a mighty literature. It appears that such concepts of manifold or indeterminate meaning, which are born of some self-contradiction of the human mind, are destined to be rolled onward by mankind through centuries until it finally appears that they are good for nothing. Nevertheless we must not be impatient even with such concepts, for perhaps it is precisely for this reason that thinkers probe them with such great zeal, in order that in the meantime they may be able to form other and better concepts. I am therefore by no means saying that the concept of the “inner sense” does not conceal some problem within it; indeed, I have pointed out that it appeared in place of the concepts of knowledge of knowledge, of becoming aware of awareness, and I have only tried to prove that as an “inner sense” it cannot maintain itself, because Locke himself really meant by it an insensible sense.

It is true that “the inner sense” as a technical term was soon dropped by the philosophers, because it was indeed all too obvious that

p. 46

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

it was only the sort of metaphor or flower of speech that poets like to use when they speak of an “inner eye” or of the eye of the soul, wishing to indicate, as it were, that there are things which cannot be perceived by means of the senses. Moreover, it was not advisable to speak of an “inner sense” for the further reason that science could have taken this technical term seriously only if it had succeeded in pointing to a sense-organ, or to some part of the brain, that functions as an “inner sense” in contrast to the outer senses. But although the philosophers dropped the “inner sense” as a supposed organ or supposed faculty, they renounced only the word, and went on maintaining the operations of the mysterious faculty, namely inner observation and inner experience (innere Beobachtung, innere Erfahrung), as the fundamental method, or procedure, of the science of psychology. The fatal consequence of the stubborn maintenance of these confused concepts was that it now became necessary to speak of two worlds of phenomena: the world of outer phenomena, which are perceived by outer observation and with which physics*) is concerned, and the world of inner phenomena, which can be perceived by inner observation and form the object of empirical psychology. The world of phenomena in space and time, which men had previously regarded as only one world, that is, as the physical world, now became two worlds, so that only the one half, that which could be observed externally, was called physical, while the other half received the name of the psychical world on the ground that it can be observed and described from within. At first it seemed as if the philosophers spoke of the inner world and of inner phenomena only metaphorically, but the “scientific” character of our age increasingly compelled them to speak of inner phenomena not merely in some uncertain figurative, but in a direct sense, as of phenomena which, though from within, are just as much objects of observation as outer phenomena. Fashion willed that linguistics and historiography too should aspire to the title and character of natural science, and so it could not fail that psychology itself should long for the title of a descriptive natural science; for which reason, in the interest of this end, it wholly dropped the metaphorical coloring of the “world of inner phenomena” and, characterizing it as a world of phenomena in the strict sense of the word, set it directly in opposition

*) The word physics is here to be taken in the widest sense, in which it includes biology too.

p. 47

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

to the world of physical phenomena. The effort to distinguish these two kinds of phenomena strictly led to the physical phenomena being characterized as those which are in space and time, whereas the psychical phenomena, on the other hand, were supposed to occupy a place in time alone. Man’s world split asunder into two worlds: one of space and time, and one purely of time.

In vain did Auguste Comte, the positivist philosopher, raise his voice against the inner sense of the psychologicists, or rather against the supposed inner observation arising from it; in vain did he proclaim that our descendants will one day make this inner observation an object of comedy (Cours de philosophie positive, 3rd ed., vol. I, p. 32): even his greatest admirers turned away from him on this point, and John Stuart Mill speaks almost with pity of Comte’s great aversion to “psychology,” by which he simply struck psychology from the ranks of the sciences. Now it is beyond doubt that Comte overshot the mark in this question, for his discussions have the appearance as if the mind could take cognizance only of sensible objects, but could not obtain cognizance of its own taking of cognizance; in a word, Comte stands before us in such a light as if, together with “inner observation,” he wished to deny the fundamental fact of human consciousness as well.

If I, for example, take cognizance in one sentence of the lamp standing before me, then in another sentence I can take cognizance of the fact that I formerly formed a sentence in which I took cognizance of the lamp. In this way the following double sentence arises: I say that here on the table a lamp is standing. In this double sentence the second part brings to expression my object-awareness, and the first my awareness of myself. Whoever, then, would deny that we can become aware of our awareness would simply be denying human intelligence. And if something of this sort is contained in Comte’s passionate and obscure discussions, then of course we should have to regard his whole campaign against “inner observation” as an absurdity.

But here quite another matter is in question. If I, for example, see here on the table a lamp, and someone calls this table and this lamp “outer phenomena,” does he want to make me believe that I can now look through some wonderful keyhole into my own soul as well, so that I see not only the appearances of the table and the lamp but also some “inner appearances” which accompany the outer appearances, or call them forth? I believe,

p. 48

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

if we speak thus quite openly, everyone will at once see what folly it is to speak of two worlds of appearances.

That many are nevertheless inclined to distinguish the twofold world of appearances in the above sense has as one of its chief causes the fact that they set memory-images over against the impressions received from the external world, and believe that these memory-images are the inner, that is, psychical phenomena. But this is out of the question; for if by inner appearances memory-images were to be understood, then the inner sense would be nothing else than the use of the faculty of memory, and finally psychology, since it appeals to inner observation as its own special method, would be nothing else than the science which alone uses the faculty of memory, whereas the other sciences would make no use of this faculty, which, surely, is an obvious absurdity! In general we have no reason whatever to call our memory-images inner phenomena, in contrast to sensible impressions as outer phenomena, unless our very aim is to confuse ourselves with such metaphors as outer and inner. If we call “inner” that which is exclusively our own, then the “impression” is just as much inner as the “memory-image,” for my impression is just as little your impression as my memory-image is your memory-image. It would therefore have to be said what we really mean by “outer” and “inner,” and then it would appear that these words were originally borrowed from the intuition of space, and that here they would have to be used in a figurative sense, that is, as metaphors, without anyone knowing in what sense this transference is supposed to take place.

If, then, Comte attacked “inner observation” and “empirical psychology” in an improper manner as well, we must nevertheless admit that in his bold step some true inspiration revealed itself. For, having respect for factual truths, he suspected that the famous “inner observation” is a mystical procedure by whose help something can be set up as fact which no one has ever experienced. He rose in revolt against those fabricated facts with which empirical psychology simply swarms, and I confess that I regard this revolt as one of the great merits of Auguste Comte, although I could not subscribe to his argument against psychology on a single point. What I hold to be an absurdity is merely this: that there should exist two worlds of phenomena, perceptible by means of two kinds of sensibility. We have only one kind of sensibility

p. 49

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and only one world of appearances is ours; and whoever assumes yet another sensibility and thinks he can contemplate another world of appearances besides can be only the prey of fancies. It is at any rate characteristic that it is precisely the “empiricists” who want to find by experience a world of appearances which does not exist. The lesson to be drawn from this is that those who call themselves empiricists and gallop most on the watchword of experience are not always the true champions of experience. With philosophers too, alas, we are sometimes in the same position as with political parties, which very often fight under false watchwords. The empiricism that figures under this name in the history of philosophy is not the true one. It may be that sometimes it is precisely that philosopher who must be regarded as the proper champion of empirical knowledge whom the sham champions of experience call a “metaphysician.”

There are not two worlds of appearances: namely, there are no phenomena which possess a purely temporal character. This I shall prove in detail later; here in the introduction it may perhaps suffice if I restrict myself to the following remark: Suppose that some phenomenon a were only in time, but occupied no place whatever in space. If now P1, P2, P3, P4 signify four persons who are stationed, say, here in Budapest, and respectively in America, on the Moon and on the planet Mars, then it is evident that the phenomenon a can stand no nearer to P1 than to P2 or P3, or P4, since we suppose of it that it is nowhere at all in space. If, therefore, the phenomenon a appears to the person P1, there is no reason why it should not appear in just the same way to the persons P2, P3, P4 as well, since it can be neither nearer to nor farther from any one of these persons. Indeed, if the phenomenon a occupied a place in space as well, it would at once be intelligible why, for example, it appears precisely to the inhabitant of Budapest, but not at the same time to the inhabitant of the planet Mars. In a word, the phenomenon a (since it is not of a spatial character) either cannot appear to anyone, and then there can be no question of it, or else it must appear to everyone, since it cannot stand in any other relation to the one person than to the other. But of the so-called “inner phenomena” it is said that they are the inner phenomena of only one person; it is evident, therefore, that they cannot be purely temporal phenomena. Such phenomena do not exist at all.

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 4

p. 50

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

5. Formalist Theory of Knowledge.

14. If we survey the history of the development of philosophy down to our own day, we may say in general that the various epistemological or logical systems which have hitherto appeared have been more or less definitely dualistic in character. There are indeed philosophical writers who call themselves monists, but these as a rule are not thinkers about knowledge; they do not investigate knowledge, but, precisely by neglecting the epistemological standpoint, they investigate being, in order to explain all its phenomena from one principle, or at least to exhibit them in the guise of a certain uniformity. Thus those thinkers who incline to the so-called “mechanical view of the world” of today wish to conceive the whole world after the pattern of a single immeasurable machine, and because in this way they would like to make the difference between living and lifeless things vanish, they believe that they are monists, that is, that they explain all phenomena of the world from one principle.

Very close to them stand those thinkers who are adherents of the biological view of the world and who likewise labor to efface the difference between living and lifeless things, except that they believe they can attain the uniform conception by regarding all things whatever as living, and therefore ascribing life not only to animal and plant, but to the lifeless clod as well. One might therefore believe that the adherents of the biological and of the mechanical view of the world stand in the sharpest opposition to each other, but closer examination shows that the very same men who just now asserted that all things live, could do so only because they had given up the conceptual difference between living and lifeless, in consequence of which they could just as well say that all things are lifeless. For if someone resolves to attribute one and the same meaning to the word “living” and to the word “lifeless,” he will be just as entitled to say that all things are living as that all things are lifeless. Indeed, no special weight need be attached to it if someone says of the whole of nature that it is a single living being of immeasurable magnitude, for the “monist” who parades such a conception could just as well say that the whole of nature is a single lifeless machine of immeasurable magnitude. Conversely, the adherent of the mechanical view of the world may boldly call the whole universe a single machine, since in reality

p. 51

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

he too can speak thus only because, having given up from the outset the conceptual difference between living and lifeless, he could just as boldly call the whole world a living being. It is only a play with words when one party calls the world purely living, the other purely lifeless; and such a verbal play becomes possible only through the fact that the parties concerned do not subject to investigation those concepts which they have formed for themselves of the “living” and of the “not living,” that is, do not subject to investigation their stock of concepts, in other words their knowledge in general. The adherents of the mechanical as well as of the biological view of the world are only seeming monists, for a truly unified conception of the world — if we can attain one at all — can be reached only by subjecting our knowledge to investigation and approaching, with the epistemological mode of thinking thus steeled, the investigation of the great problems relating to being. Without an examination of our consciousness there can exist no serious investigation of being either. Without a theory of knowledge there is no serious natural science.

For the theory of knowledge is precisely the science which regards as its aim to teach us to place under the standpoint of unity our knowledge, which is fragmentary, broken, and consequently involved in self-contradictions. So long, that is, as we are immersed in the investigation of facts, now one, now another group of facts holds us bound, so that through the neglect of the remaining groups of facts we cannot attain a unified conception of the world. It is certain that whoever knew all the facts of the universe would by that very circumstance possess a unified knowledge, since no gap would show itself anywhere in his knowledge, and so no connection of facts would remain hidden from him. But we human beings cannot acquire a unified, that is, absolute cognition in this sense, and so unified knowledge means with us something other than absolute knowledge. It means that we human beings cannot be content with knowledge of facts (since we cannot know every fact), but must subject our knowledge of facts to investigation, in order that by knowledge obtained about knowledge we may make good, as far as lies in our power, that original weakness of ours by reason of which we cannot know every fact.

The theory of knowledge is thus the science which takes its origin directly from our striving for unified knowledge. For since the craving for unity of the human mind cannot be satisfied by the cognition of ever new facts, and that for the reason that we cannot know the totality of facts; our mind

4*

p. 52

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

seeks to help itself by taking knowledge itself under investigation, as a result of which, in place of individual facts, there come to the fore those connections in which all facts must stand to one another in order to become comprehensible. Logic therefore strives for knowledge of knowledge in order thereby to unify knowledge.

That perfect unification, however, which could be realized only in absolute knowledge, can never succeed for us human beings. We human beings can always be only on the way toward perfect unity, and we may call ourselves happy even then, if we are really pressing forward on this path. From this it evidently follows too that logic possesses just as unlimited a capacity for progress as any other branch of science, insofar as, recognizing ever more clearly and definitely the truths that relate to the connections of truths, it can mark out ever more clearly and definitely the road that leads forward in the unification of human knowledge. But from what has been said it also follows that the road of logic cannot break away from the investigations of the special sciences, but on the contrary remains in continuous, inseparable connection with them. The advancing special sciences always supply the theory of knowledge, which strives for unification, with new problems; this in turn, by its ever new unifying points of view, steers the special sciences onto the road of methodical progress and discoveries.

But although logic by its very nature is a unifying science, in its historical manifestations it has nevertheless, on the whole and in the main, taken on a more or less definite dualistic character. The theory of knowledge that Spinoza worked into his chief ethical work can perhaps be called most nearly monistic, yet he too gives only his surmises and rough sketches of his logical system, as is evident also from the fact that he did not expound it in an independent work, but used it rather only as a means in the foundation of his ethics. For the rest, however, we can certainly find the germs of a monistic theory of knowledge in the great thinkers of modern as well as of ancient times, since the striving for unity is the fundamental character of every human mind: what I wish to emphasize here, therefore, is only this, that although thinkers have always been able to strive for monism, they have nevertheless, in spite of this striving and perhaps even unconsciously, fallen into dualism, at which we can wonder all the less because man can by no means attain the perfect unification — that is, absolute knowledge.

p. 53

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

We must call the rationalist and the empiricist systems of the theory of knowledge alike dualistic, even though the latter in particular like to present themselves as monistic. That the rationalists are mired in a dualistic way of thinking is at once evident from the fact that they distinguish a knowledge which arises from experience and one which is not drawn from experience. Expressed more clearly, this means that we should have a knowledge which depends on our impressions, and a knowledge which is independent of our impressions. The one kind of knowledge would arise from a sensible faculty, the other kind, on the contrary, from a non-sensible faculty. But we saw above (ch. 3, § 7) that the faculty of cognition cannot be thought of as composed of two faculties, because that would necessarily lead to our having two worlds not connected with one another, and to our having not the least cognizance of the one while we were cognizing the other, which may be expressed briefly by saying that our personality would fall asunder into two persons independent of each other and knowing nothing of each other. Namely, if we were to divide our cognitive faculty into a sensible and a non-sensible faculty, we should possess two systems of cognitions, and indeed purely sensible cognitions and purely non-sensible ones. That no connection whatever could then exist between these two systems of cognitions is perhaps superfluous even to say. This cleavage of our cognitions finds nowhere so clear an expression as in Leibniz, who divided truths into two classes: truths of fact (vérités de fait) and truths of reason (vérités de raison).

The empiricist theorists of knowledge conceal the dualism of their thinking better, insofar as they do not divide the realm of truths into two unconnected realms of sensible and non-sensible truths. They proclaim that all truth can arise only from experience, whereby they are able to awaken a strong semblance of monism. But we convinced ourselves in the preceding chapter that they distinguish two kinds of experience, outer and inner, and accordingly two worlds of appearances. In reality, then, they are mired in a dualism like that of the rationalists. They too are not content with one world, but invent yet another, although, it is true, they call this second world too sensible. Whereas, then, the rationalists seem to believe that our world exists in two unconnected copies, a sensible and a non-sensible copy, the empiricists consider both copies

p. 54

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

sensible. Which of the two views is the queerer it would indeed be hard to decide.

From a compromise between, or rather a union of, these two views there arose the Kantian, that is, the formalist theory of knowledge, which therefore unites in itself the dualisms of the empiricist and of the rationalist way of thinking. This theory of knowledge exerted a tremendous influence on minds immediately on its appearance, and its influence has been renewed in our own days in the so-called neo- Kantian school, for which reason it is necessary that we occupy ourselves with it somewhat more thoroughly.

15. Kant too, following in the footsteps of the rationalists, divides our faculty of cognition into two faculties, namely sensibility and reason, although he cautiously adds that it may be that this twofold stem of our faculty of cognition springs from some common root. (Critique of Pure Reason, p. 29.) For he feels how awkward it would be if two mutually independent faculties produced our knowledge, for which reason he would like to regard the two faculties as such as have sprung from some unknown primal or root faculty. This wavering view is valuable to us because it shows that even he who assumes two faculties of cognition is involuntarily inclined to the insight that one ought to proceed from only one faculty of cognition. But this obscure inclination of his cannot blind us to the fact that he nevertheless wants to found his logic on two faculties of cognition. Above (ch. 3, §

We do not wish to stand in the way of anyone’s speaking of various

7) I proved that it makes no difference at all whether one divides the faculty of cognition into two independent powers, or only into two component faculties which, in an alleged dependence upon each other, would produce knowledge. For in order that we might have the right to speak of two such components, we should have to show what each component produces separately, taken by itself. But since each component, taken by itself, could produce nothing else than some kind of knowledge, it is evident that we are confronted not with two components at all, but with two independent faculties, of which the one would produce this kind of knowledge, the other that kind, which is a logical absurdity. In vain, therefore, does Kant seek to mitigate his dualistic standpoint by deriving his two faculties of cognition from some unknown common root, for by this he only confirms us the more firmly in the consciousness that logic cannot be founded on two faculties of cognition.

p. 55

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

human or animal faculties, and we object only to the distinguishing of more than one faculty of cognition. Thus, for example, everyone agrees that the faculty of sight must be distinguished from the faculty of hearing; but why? Because we can make use of our faculty of sight without having to employ our faculty of hearing too, and conversely. By closing our eyes we can as it were eliminate our seeing, and by stopping our ears our capacity for hearing, etc. It is true that such elimination does not succeed in equal measure with the different faculties, but in general we may say that man has arrived at the concept of different faculties through being able to eliminate more or less, or rather to put temporarily at rest, the operation of one organ or another. At least with regard to the biological faculties, such as seeing, hearing, smelling, tasting, etc., it is beyond doubt that we could not have arrived at distinguishing them if their operation were not bound to particular parts of our organism in such a way that through the quickening or flagging operation of these parts (the nerves) now the one, now the other faculty appears switched in or out. If now someone wishes to distinguish two faculties of cognition, a sensible one and a non-sensible one (reason), he must first of all set forth how we are to eliminate our sensibility, that is, how we are to hush it so far that with the help of our “non-sensible” faculty we can search through the non-sensible world, and conversely, how we are to dismantle our reason so far that the non-sensible world may not disturb us and we may become acquainted only with the phenomena of the sensible world. But why should I go on? Does anyone believe that with reason eliminated or dismantled we should still be capable of arriving at any knowledge whatever? Or does anyone believe that, having dismantled our sensibility, we could go wandering on adventures into a non-sensible world?

It is true that even the common view is inclined to think our faculty of cognition composed of a sensuous and a non-sensuous cognitive capacity, but the origin of this inclination and the error lying in it are easy to recognize. There exists a kind of knowledge (as when, for example, I recognize a tree as an oak, a beetle as a stag-beetle, etc., etc.) which I could not obtain without the use of my sight or other sense-organs, and it is this kind of cognition that the common view too classifies as sensuous knowledge. But there exists a kind of knowledge to which man comes by way of that silent or

p. 56

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

self-absorbed thinking, of which we have already spoken above (ch. 3, § 9); and it is this mysterious silent thinking that may appear to the superficial mind as if it ran its course without any employment of our organism, and so furnished purely non-sensuous knowledge. Here we convince ourselves once more that the mysterious fact of silent or self-absorbed thinking is the source of the most various errors. Many believe of silent thinking that it could run its course without the formation of signs, whereas silent thinking could take the place of audible thinking only by long practice, and does not differ from it essentially, as I explained above (ch. 3, § 9). He misunderstands the nature of silent thinking still more who believes that it does not require some organic operation, to say it more definitely: some brain-work. For if someone could have a thought without his brain performing some work, the question would arise on what title that person could call that thought his own. A thought that subsisted without brain-work every man could call his own on the same title, since none would have more to do with that thought than another. It is clear, therefore, that no human thought can subsist without human brain-work. All human knowledge is bound to vital functions (biological function), and to this extent all our knowledge must of necessity be sensuous. We have, therefore, no right whatever to oppose to each other two kinds of knowledge, of which the one class would depend on vital functions and the other not, since all our knowledge depends on vital functions.

The dualism which arranges our cognitions in two classes of opposite origin, namely into cognitions dependent on impressions and cognitions not dependent on impressions, I shall call classificatory dualism. Such a classificatory dualism is, for example, that of Leibniz, who, as mentioned, divided truths into two classes: the realm of truths of fact and that of truths of reason. Kant combined this classificatory dualism with a compositional dualism, according to which each of our cognitions is composed of two elements: a content element, which arises from impressions, and a formal element, which springs from the faculty of cognition itself. According to Kant there dwell in our soul, that is, in our faculty of cognition, from the beginning certain forms, and these a priori forms are those which shape our impressions into cognitions. The forms of our sensibility

p. 57

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

are space and time; the forms of our understanding, the categories. Since Kant’s whole theory of knowledge revolves around the sensible forms and the forms of the understanding that produce knowledge, we may regard him as the founder of the formalist theory of knowledge.

It is easy to see that a knowledge or cognition cannot be resolved into two elements, a material or content element and a formal element, as Kant demands. For these alleged two elements are either unrecognizable, and then there can be no question of them, or else they are recognizable, and then the recognition of the two elements would yield two cognitions. In place of one cognition we should thus have obtained two cognitions. But since, according to Kant, every cognition consists of a material and a formal element, each of the two cognitions in question would be composed of a material and a formal element. From which it is seen that it is impossible to recognize the material element and the formal element; for as soon as we should recognize, for example, the material element, this cognition itself would be composed of a material and a formal element. This proof may be presented more briefly thus. The demand that every cognition be composed of a material and a formal element makes itself impossible. For however far we resolve the cognition, we can arrive only at cognitions, and so each result of the resolution will again consist only of a material and a formal element. The material element and the formal element therefore can never be produced in isolation, and so there can be no question of such a resolution.

Kant thus starts from a double contradiction in founding his theory of knowledge. He assumes two faculties of cognition, and consequently must arrive at two unconnected classes of knowledge: this is his classificatory dualism, which he borrowed from Leibniz. Since, however, he seems to surmise that by this procedure the realm of truths would fall asunder into two unconnected realms, he is compelled to have recourse to a second dualism: he assumes, namely, every cognition to be composed of a formal and a material element, whereby there arises the semblance as if he did not divide cognitions into two classes after the manner of Leibniz, but established only one class. All cognitions would form only one class, but each single knowledge of ours would be as it were chemically composed of two elements. In this way Kant throws his readers into complete confusion, insofar as he creates now the semblance as if there existed two classes of cognitions, now the semblance as if it were not a question of two classes at all, but of some single cognition

p. 58

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

always of two elements, material and formal, welded together.

Hence it is that “a priori” and “a posteriori” bear a twofold meaning in his work. In the classificatory sense these technical terms signify that we may arrange our cognitions in two classes: cognitions that arise from experience and cognitions that are independent of experience; in the compositional (as it were chemical) sense the two terms signify that each single cognition of ours has arisen through the mystical fusion of a material and a formal element. But there is a contradiction in dividing knowledge into classes derived from two different, opposed faculties, just as there is in supposing that knowledge can be composed of a material and a formal element, and finally it is a contradiction too to believe that we can cover over the one contradiction of ours with the other.

The twofold contradiction from which Kant starts would become one contradiction if he were to make the a priori and a posteriori cognitions coincide with the formal and material cognitions: if, that is, a priori were to mean the same as formal cognition, and a posteriori the same as cognition of content, or material cognition. To this Kant would at bottom have a certain inclination, yet he needs both contradictions in order to maintain his own doctrine against that of Leibniz. For Leibniz knows only a classificatory dualism, inasmuch as he distinguishes truths of fact and truths of reason (that is, truths of an a posteriori and of an a priori character), but he is still far from thinking of every truth as composed of a formal and a material constituent. Kant may have felt that by Leibniz’s classification the realm of truths falls in two, and he believed that he could put a stop to this splitting by assuming every truth to be composed of a formal and a material constituent. He did not notice that in this way he merely heaps a new contradiction upon the self-contradiction of the Leibnizian theory of knowledge; hence it is that he was not free to take “a priori” as synonymous with the expression “formal,” and likewise he was not free to regard the expressions “a posteriori” and “material” (or “of content”) as synonymous. A priori truths are not, with him, necessarily only of a formal character, since they may have some matter as well; conversely, a posteriori truths cannot consist of mere content, but must possess form too. On the other hand, however, it is clear that Kant always regards “form” as of an a priori

p. 59

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

character, and “content,” on the other hand, as of an a posteriori character; from which it would have followed that he would have to make formal cognition coincide completely with the a priori, and material cognition with the a posteriori. Since, however, he could not do this after all, an infinitely painful struggling runs through his whole principal work, inasmuch as the a priori and the formal, like the a posteriori and the material cognition, now coincide with one another, now do not. We may also express this by saying that Kant now relapses into Leibniz, now wishes to set himself in opposition to him. It need hardly be said that this latter endeavor predominates with him, and it is clearly expressed in his ascribing forms not only to reason but to sensibility as well. “Formal” and pure-reason (or a priori) concepts do not coincide with one another in Kant, and in like manner “material” and sensibility-derived (or a posteriori) concepts do not coincide with one another in his work. He therefore broke away from Leibniz by ascribing forms to sensibility too, and this breaking away became possible only because he supplemented the Leibnizian classificatory dualism with a compositional, not to say chemical, dualism. It is to be noted, however, that he was still not able to maintain the sharpened opposition against Leibniz, for he had after all to identify the “formal” with what originates in pure reason, and to bring the “material” together with the sensible. This is the point which the German commentators touch upon, to be sure, but are not wont to develop clearly.

When Kant ascribed forms to sensibility too, it became wholly clear that he regards sensibility as a completely independent faculty of cognition, since this faculty not only disposed over cognitive matter (namely over impressions, sensations), but also possessed two innate*) forms, the forms of space and time. This procedure of Kant’s had fatal consequences for his whole theory of knowledge, for now the human faculty of cognition was openly torn in two, and, what is still more important, it was no longer possible to stop at the tearing in two, but one had to resort to new dualisms. For since he had to ascribe two forms to sensibility, Kant was compelled, in Locke’s footsteps, to divide sensibility into two faculties of cognition, outer and inner sensibility, and to conceive space as the innate form of outer, and time as that of inner sensibility

*) “Innate” means here the same as “a priori.” In place of the doctrine of “ideas born with us” Kant devised the mythology of “innate forms” dwelling in the soul.

p. 60

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

In this way he adopted the self-contradiction of the psychologists (or empiricists) as well, inasmuch as he had to distinguish two worlds of appearance, of which the one opens to outer, the other to inner experience. Indeed we may say that of that psychologistic tendency which descends from Locke, Kant became — albeit against his will — the true patron, inasmuch as it was in fact he who formulated more definitely the alleged difference between the outer and the inner world of appearance. For when he presented time as the innate form of the inner world of appearance, this inner world of appearance began to figure as one whose chief characteristic trait is that it exists only in time. From this it would have followed that, conversely, the outer world of appearance may exist only in space, since according to Kant’s theory the outer sensibility has no other innate form than space. Thus it came truly to light what self-contradiction lies hidden in the distinction between inner and outer sensibility. Two worlds would stand over against one another: 1. a purely spatial world, in which therefore nothing can happen, 2. a purely temporal world, in which only histories can run their course, without these histories having a scene anywhere. A stage without a history and a history without a stage would yawn at one another. Kant may have felt this self-contradiction — and, as I shall set forth in detail later — he thought he could resolve it by arbitrarily endowing the world of outer sensibility with temporality as well. According to him, then, a spatio-temporal and a purely temporal world would stand opposed to one another; but the attentive reader may notice that Kant could properly not have stopped even at two worlds of appearance, but that besides a purely spatial and a purely temporal world he would have had to distinguish still a spatio-temporal world as well. This psychologistic confusion, derived from Locke, penetrated through Kant into Fechner’s psycho-physics and Wundt’s physiological psychology too, whereby it became the cause of immeasurable misunderstandings.

16. In what precedes I have tried to characterize briefly that notable mediating position which Kant’s formalist theory of knowledge occupies between the rationalism of Leibniz and the empiricism of Locke. Although we take the most decided stand against such “mediation” or “reconciliation,” it is nevertheless striking that Kant at bottom undertook the greatest task that a theorist of knowledge can undertake at all, wishing to unite in the bed of one stream

p. 61

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the divergent currents of the rationalist and empiricist extremes. It is therefore very natural that so profound and great an endeavor as that which filled Kant should have had to exert an enormous influence on the whole thinking world, to such a degree that the inquiry into knowledge is in fact still today predominantly under his influence; but while we readily acknowledge this, we cannot close ourselves to the insight that Kant only apparently reconciled the opposed rationalist and empiricist tendencies, for he accepted the self-contradictions of the one party as well as of the other: he accepted Leibniz’s two worlds of knowledge (the world of truths of fact and that of truths of reason) just as he accepted Locke’s two worlds of appearance (the world of outer and that of inner sensibility).

From what has been said it is at the same time evident that Kant sought to mediate between the rationalists and the empiricists by means of a very simple formula. His formula consisted in this, that every cognition is composed of a formal element and an element of content. At first sight one would think that no mortal soul could be profoundly impressed by such a formula. For who has not already made use of such a phrase, that some intellectual content can be cast into this or that form, where by form is understood the manner of linguistic presentation. There is no more threadbare expression in the theory of style than the one which speaks of the content and the form of thoughts. Indeed we may perhaps say that among current metaphors we scarcely find more worn-out ones than those which arise from the figurative use of “content” and “form.” But it was precisely this circumstance that caused Kant’s formula to bewitch minds with irresistible force. For the expressions “content” and “form” now had to be taken in a “deeper” sense, because from technical terms of style they had become technical terms of the theory of knowledge. The more worn these metaphors were, the more were minds inclined to put some very deep meaning into them. Everyone easily persuaded himself that he understands what it is that Kant calls the form and the content of cognition. Yet it can be shown that no one at all could truly have grasped the meaning of these technical terms, for I proved above that cognitions cannot be resolved into factors which would not in turn be cognitions, and therefore not into factors such as are indicated by the words “content” and “form.” No one knows,

p. 62

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

for example, what those “forms” are, which dwell in the mind from the beginning and enclose, as it were like sheaths, the content of cognition that flows into them. Whoever falls to pondering such metaphorical speech may very easily see that it can be good for only one thing: to make ourselves believe that we have thought something very profound. It may flatter our vanity to install some mystical forms or sheaths in our mind, knowing too that this is only a sort of metaphorical talk, which is not to be taken in its literal sense; but if we do not wish to deceive ourselves, we must confess that by those forms or sheaths we really understand only some mysterious powers or laws dwelling in the mind, of which we have otherwise no knowledge whatever and never can have.

But Kant’s formula of reconciliation was very well suited to bewitching minds for yet another reason. For Leibniz’s two kinds of truths, namely the blue-blooded truths of reason and the plebeian truths of fact, stood opposed to one another, so to speak, as two hostile castes between which scarcely any unity was conceivable: Kant’s formula therefore came as a godsend, according to which every truth, or rather each single cognition, already in itself is composed of an innate formal element and a content-element of a posteriori character, for in this way the blue-blooded a priori and the plebeian a posteriori elements seemed in every single cognition to be, as it were chemically, fused with one another. But if every cognition arises through the fusion of a formal and a content-element, then no distinction could have been drawn between cognition and cognition such that the one would be of an a priori, the other of an a posteriori character, since it had been laid down that every cognition alike includes the formal component of a priori character and the content component of a posteriori character. Kant, however, as I have said, retained the classificatory dualism of Leibniz and supplemented it with his own chemical (compositional) dualism, without noticing that these two dualisms exclude one another. If there are a priori and a posteriori truths, then not every truth can consist of an a priori and an a posteriori element; and conversely, if every truth consists of an a priori and an a posteriori element, then truths cannot be divided into two classes, of which the one would be of a priori and the other of a posteriori character.

p. 63

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition
