Kant himself, however, did not notice how unbridgeable is the opposition of his chemical dualism to the classificatory dualism of Leibniz, but, in a peculiar manner, swayed to and fro now on the waves of the one conception, now on those of the other. This is one of the chief causes of the strange twilight or half-darkness that is spread over his principal work. In this twilight he himself marvels that the concepts “a priori” and “formal,” and likewise “a posteriori” and “of content,” do not wish to coincide, although he himself set up his formula in such a way that they could not coincide. He comes to the point of having to distinguish two kinds of a priori cognition: the judgments that he calls analytic and synthetic. He calls analytic a judgment whose predicate merely unfolds what could already be thought in the subject anyhow (though perhaps somewhat obscurely). Such a judgment is merely explicative in character, does not carry our knowledge forward, and its characteristic mark would be that in it subject and predicate stand in the relation of identity. To the character of the synthetic judgment, on the other hand, it belongs that its predicate was not yet thought in its subject; it therefore enlarges and advances our cognition, and its subject and predicate accordingly cannot stand in the relation of identity. It is striking that here we are really confronted with formal and content judgments, for analytic judgments are nothing other than judgments of a purely formal character, which for that reason do not advance our knowledge, because they have no content. Synthetic judgments enlarge our knowledge because they have content. From this it can be seen what really induced Kant to the famous distinction of analytic and synthetic judgments. The reason for it lay in this, that the concepts

a priori / formal

did not wish to coincide. For a priori cognitions could not all consist of mere form, but some of them had to possess content too, in order to come into account at all as cognitions. Purely formal a priori cognitions had therefore to be distinguished, which Kant calls analytic cognitions, and further a priori cognitions that also possess content, which received the epithet synthetic. But quite similarly the concepts

a posteriori / of content did not wish to coincide either, wherefore Kant would have been obliged to distinguish also two kinds

p. 64

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of a posteriori cognition: one that consists purely of content (and this would have been the synthetic a posteriori cognition), and one that consists not only of content but of form as well (and this would have been the analytic a posteriori judgment). Kant, however, thought it good to be wholly silent about these two classes, for what sense indeed would there have been in speaking of judgments that consist purely of content but possess no form (synthetic a posteriori judgments)? And again, what sense would there have been in a posteriori judgments that do not enlarge our knowledge at all (analytic a posteriori judgments), when every new experience evidently signifies an enlargement of our cognition as well?

But as great a logical impossibility as is the division of a posteriori cognitions into an analytic and a synthetic group, just so great an impossibility is the splitting of a priori cognitions into two such groups. For according to Kant, those judgments have a synthetic character in which the concept of the predicate falls outside the concept of the subject, so that in these subject and predicate cannot stand in the relation of identity. But it is wholly inconceivable how we can permit ourselves to pronounce a predicate of a subject if that predicate remains outside the concept of the subject: for the conjoining of subject and predicate in one sentence consists precisely in this, that the predicate is as it were fitted into the subject. Of negative judgments it might perhaps be said that in them the predicate falls outside the concept of the subject, for if I say, for example, “this ring is not of gold,” then the concept of “gold” here falls outside the concept of the ring. But if I say “this ring is of gold,” then I think that the concepts “ring” and “gold” are here fitted into one another, that is, that the one does not fall outside the other. When, therefore, Kant seeks to distinguish analytic and synthetic judgments by this, that in the one kind identity prevails, in the other not, we draw from this only the lesson that an immeasurable obscurity covers the relation of subject and predicate, that is, the essence of judgment, or rather the concept of the relation of identity. We shall see what a great problem the nature of the connection of subject and predicate is, and we shall be convinced that the combined labor of the greatest thinkers was not able to clarify this fundamental question of logic.

p. 65

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

17. We had to enter upon the foregoing discussions (already here in the introduction) because Kant’s theory that our cognitions are composed of a formal and a material element had a decisive influence on the conception which more recent times have formed of logic as a science. For with him logic is a purely “formal” science, that is, a science which abstracts from all the content (the matter, the objects) of our cognitions and deals exclusively with the forms of our cognition. We have Kant to thank, then, that so strange a concept as that of “formal logic” could arise. For it is incomprehensible how one can abstract from all the content of cognitions and yet continually speak of cognition, of knowledge. What indeed remains of our cognitions if we leave out their content? We believe that nothing, absolutely nothing. It would be a strange thing, too, if all the sciences dealt with the content of cognitions, and evidently with their formless content, while to logic, conversely, there fell the task of examining the contentless shape of cognitions. What special science could undertake the humiliating or, better said, impossible role of furnishing the content of cognition but without form? And is it possible to condemn logic to so ridiculous a role as to concern itself not with the content of our cognitions but to investigate their empty sheaths (if such exist!)? Surely Kant himself does not wish to understand logic as a purely contentless science, but conceives the matter so that the form of our cognitions constitutes the content of logic. But what are we to think of the forms of cognition if they can constitute the content of a science? If the form of cognition can also be content of cognition, what difference is there still between form and content? Here, then, we must once more see that the expressions “content” and “form” may perhaps do very good service in stylistics, but that cognitions are so thoroughly, in every fiber, cognitions and only cognitions, that every attempt at dissection can find nothing else in them but cognitions, wherefore it is a pity to introduce such metaphors as “content” and “form” into the theory of knowledge.

How seriously Kant took the so-called “formal” character of logic is evident above all from his regarding it as a science which cannot advance and whose essential content had already been established by Aristotle. And if we consider that according to Kant logic (at least the so-called

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 5

p. 66

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

called “general logic”) can have no regard whatever to the content of our cognitions, then we readily understand also that such a science cannot advance; indeed, what is more, we are ready to believe of such a science that it never existed and never can exist. Moreover, Kant himself has no particularly high opinion of “general logic,” for it appears more than once from his words that he regards this science as one consisting for the most part of analytic judgments, or perhaps even containing no other judgments than those in which the predicate explicatively repeats the concept of the subject. He all but says outright that this science is empty; at any rate it is characteristic that he entrusted the publication of his university lectures on general, that is, Aristotelian, logic to a private lecturer named Jäsche.

It is true that Kant set his own transcendental logic over against general or Aristotelian logic; only this transcendental logic too concerns itself with nothing else than the forms of our cognition, emphasizing more than once that these forms must in the end be capable of being exhausted. In his own logic Kant regards the “forms” as certain mystical powers which bring forth in us knowledge, experience, the picture of the world, but at the same time as powers which prevent us from being able to cognize things taken in themselves (Dinge an sich). I need not enter here into a more detailed account of them, for here I wish only to stress that Kant means to use his own theory of forms too in order to set bounds to logical (or, as he says, metaphysical) inquiry. Aristotelian logic is, according to him, an essentially completed science; transcendental logic, however, is so constructed that by means of a system of forms it throws up barriers against logical (or rather metaphysical) inquiry. This is the reason why even such thinkers as hold the depth and earnestness of Kant’s epistemological endeavors in the greatest respect are compelled to come into sharp collision with him, not only on the fundamental questions of the theory of knowledge, but also on the task of this science and so on its whole structure. Highly characteristic in this direction is the declaration of Bernard Bolzano, one of the severest of German thinkers:

“Therefore I think it one of Kant’s literary sins that he attempted to deprive us of the salutary faith in the capacity of logic to advance, through the conception, so agreeable to human sluggishness

p. 67

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of proclaiming that logic has been a finished and closed science since the time of Aristotle. In its place I believe nothing would be more justified than to set up the belief in the perfectibility not only of logic but of all science generally, directly as a kind of practical postulate indispensable to mankind. For what else is it but arrogance that tempts us to declare of any science that through all the future it will not be presentable better and more completely than we (perhaps even we ourselves) have already presented it in our own day? It would indeed be better, joining Seneca, to proclaim: “Multum egerunt, qui ante nos fuerunt, sed non peregerunt. Multum adhuc restat operis, multumque restabit: nec ulli nato post mille saecula praecludetur occasio aliquid adhuc adjiciendi.” (Bolzano: Wissenschaftslehre, Vol. I, § 9, p. 40.)

*

What the object of the theory of knowledge is, and what its task and method of inquiry are, becomes clear to us only in the measure in which we penetrate ever deeper and deeper into this science. But it is necessary that, before we plunge into the exceedingly delicate problems of the theory of knowledge, I should first give some approximate concept of the aims of this science and of its principal tendencies that have emerged in the course of history, at least to the extent that I may render account in advance, as far as possible, of my own innovating endeavors. For this purpose it may perhaps not be superfluous to summarize briefly in what follows the points of view already gained:

a) The task of the theory of knowledge cannot be to establish

the so-called limits of human knowledge, beyond which the hu‐

man mind can no longer penetrate. Human knowledge has a lim‐

it only in the sense that as human beings we can never become

omniscient; otherwise, however, it is a logical impossibility to

establish boundary-lines which the inquiring spirit could not

cross. (The proof of this important proposition is contained in

ch. 4, § 10.) Just as the astronomer does not explore the immea‐

surable depths of space in order, as it were, to stake out beyond

what boundary no further stars can be discovered, but rather

labors so that by the invention of new instruments and methods

he may open celestial horizons not yet opened, so the theorist of

knowledge too cannot mark out artificial horizons beyond

which the human spirit could not penetrate. If a son of man so

5*

p. 68

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

thinks that he has reached a limit beyond which his mind does not penetrate, he has the right only to put this down to his own individual weakness of mind, for there is no human understanding so deep that beyond it a still deeper human understanding could not arise. The characteristic trait of the human spirit is unlimited capacity for progress, which has no other final barrier than that we cannot become omniscient. Those who wish artificially to mark out some boundary for our knowledge generally represent it as if they meant to admonish immodest and overweening minds to modesty and sobriety. But true immodesty and extravagance show themselves only when someone speaks as if he were an omniscient, that is, an absolute mind. Immodesty and extravagance therefore show themselves also when someone undertakes to peg out the very last limits of human knowledge, for, after all, whoever wished to reach this ultimate unattainable limit would already have to possess an omniscient, that is, an absolute mind. In the name of free inquiry we protest against every pretentious violence which, putting on the mask of modesty and sobriety, would stand in the way of mankind’s holiest desire, the search for truth. — With this I have indicated in advance my position toward criticism (Kant) and toward positivism (Comte).

b) A science that could not advance would not even deserve

the name of science. For it is the characteristic of every truth

that other truths dwell in its immediate neighborhood, in

other words: every truth is only a transition to other truths.

And if some proposition had such a blind-alley character that

through it one could not arrive at new true propositions, the

suspicion would rightly arise that it is not a true proposition

at all. Likewise a science that was walled in, as it were, would

give rise to the suspicion that no truth dwells in it. Logic, or the

theory of knowledge, can take a place in the series of the sciences only if

it is a science possessing unlimited capacity for progress.

c) Since our knowledge of facts remains under all circum‐

stances incomplete and fragmentary, and since in spite of this

we can never give up our striving to unify knowledge, nothing

remains for us but to subject to investigation the human mind

itself as the unifier of facts. The science which, for the sake of

unifying knowledge, seeks to lead the human mind to self-

knowledge is called

p. 69

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

logic or theory of knowledge. In the historical times known to us Socrates was the first who regarded the striving for self-knowledge as the proper task of philosophy: thus everyone may regard himself as a disciple of Socrates who stands in the service of the development of self-knowledge, that is, of logic.

d) The mind itself, however, we cannot directly make the object of in‐

vestigation. The human mind can become aware of itself only in this

way, that it creates works, produces sciences; it is these works of its

that it must therefore investigate in order to know itself more and

more. In this sense logic or the theory of knowledge is the science

which presents our knowledge relating to our knowledge. For this

reason it is called the science of the sciences, or the queen of the sci‐

ences: to which latter phrase, however, soberly considered, we can as‐

cribe only this meaning, that the theory of knowledge embraces no

other endeavor than to help to advance every special science by lead‐

ing minds onto the path of self-knowledge.

e) Our knowledge of objects cannot be properly developed without a

deepening of our self-knowledge; conversely, our self-knowledge too

begins to languish if we do not duly enrich our knowledge of objects.

This proposition expresses the inseparable connection and mutual

dependence of the special sciences and the theory of knowledge (or

rather philosophy). As soon as in the name of self-knowledge we be‐

gin to look down upon knowledge of objects, or in the name of the

enlargement of knowledge of objects to rebel against self-knowledge,

we are only leveling the way for intellectual anarchy. Our age, alas,

has suffered grievously for decades from the curse of this anarchy.

e) Although all investigations of logic are directed continually to the

increase of our self-knowledge, by this very tendency it continually

opens ever new points of view to our knowledge of objects, that is, to

the special sciences. Insofar as logic or the theory of knowledge pre‐

pares, as it were, the plans of campaign for our advance in the knowl‐

edge of objects, to that extent I shall call it metaphysics. Metaphysics

is therefore nothing other than the theory of knowledge itself, inso‐

far as it sets itself the aim of marking out, by the increase of self-

knowledge, the paths of advance in the knowledge of objects. In this

sense I have called the theory of knowledge also the theory of the

progress of human knowledge.

f) The task of the theory of knowledge cannot be to resolve the facul‐

ty of cognition itself into two (or more) faculties: just as, for example,

it cannot be the task of a sword to cut itself into two (or more) parts.

Our faculty of cognition is forever one and indivisible

p. 70

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

as anyone can see at once if he tries to imagine that mental power could consist of something else than mental power, or if he attempts to conceive that two faculties, neither of which is a mental faculty, taken together should nevertheless constitute a mental faculty. Likewise it is a logical absurdity that the sciences brought into being by the faculty of cognition should consist of anything else than single cognitions, that is, that single cognitions should be capable of being resolved into constituents which taken in themselves are not cognitions. Over against the dualist theory of knowledge, which would resolve now the faculty of cognition into two faculties, now the single cognition into two so-called “elements,” I take my stand in what follows on the standpoint of the monist theory of knowledge, which can discover nothing else in our faculty of cognition than faculty of cognition, and can find nothing else in knowledge than knowledge.

g) He alone possesses knowledge who refers by signs to impres‐

sions, or who refers by new signs to these signs. Neither the

impression nor the sign taken by itself is knowledge, but only

the referring of a sign to some impression, or the referring of a

sign to another sign, can enclose some kind of knowledge. I do

not wish here to offer a “perfect definition” of knowledge, for

it is very doubtful whether we can give a perfect definition of

anything at all. Here the question is only whether what has

been said above of knowledge applies to all our knowledge. Is

it true that he possesses knowledge of objects who can refer by

signs to his impressions? Is it true, further, that he becomes

aware of his own awareness who can refer by signs to those

signs in which he has expressed his ideas? Impressionism does

not admit this, for it believes that knowledge can be traced

back purely to impressions. (The untenability of this concep‐

tion I have already discussed hitherto, but in what follows I

shall prove it in detail.) Rationalism, on the contrary, looks

with a certain disdain upon our sensible impressions, but per‐

ceives the importance of signs just as little as impressionism.

The rationalist is interested in signs only insofar as “meaning”

(or sense) is attached to them, and he believes that we could

conceive such a meaning or sense even if we had no signs.

Impressionism and rationalism therefore agree in this, that

they cannot appreciate our signs: the one because it esteems

only the impression, the other, on the contrary, because it does

not value the impression, and consequently not the sign either,

which is likewise of a sensible nature. Against both

p. 71

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

tendencies we shall unceasingly press the immeasurable significance of signs. Yet we must emphasize again and again that impressions and signs are evanescent things, and that what we call knowledge is always a referring, which, however, we could not carry out if we did not possess impressions and signs. When, therefore, in what follows we enter upon the investigation of knowledge, we shall always be investigating referrings: the referring of signs to impressions and the referring of signs to signs. 6. On the Constatation of Our Impressions.

18. Of logic we said that it occupies itself with the investigation of our cognitions, and we added that only he can possess cognitions who has signs at his disposal, with which he is able to refer either to his impressions or to his signs. By this we mean that if someone had no impressions at all, or had no signs at all at his disposal with which he could refer to his impressions or to his signs, he could not possess cognitions at all.

To lighten our task, we shall for the present say nothing of that kind of referring in which we relate our signs to our signs. For what is above all important for us is that with our signs we can refer to our impressions, and for this reason logic occupies itself in the first place with such referrings. When someone refers by a sentence to that impression which is just occupying him, we say of him that he establishes as fact (konstatálja) the impression just now occurring to him. The first question about such a sentence is whether it contains a true or a false constatation. Thus, for example, the following sentence contains a true constatation: “A book lies here on the table” — which, in order to be able to refer to it conveniently, I shall designate by A. Since, however, the reader cannot be convinced of the truth of this sentence A in the way that I myself am, let him choose from his own surroundings some impression that happens to strike him and constate it in a suitable sentence of whose truth he is himself wholly convinced. There are, to be sure, thinkers who seem to call in question the reality of every fact, or the truth of every assertion, but I believe that this celebrated so-called “universal doubt” cannot be taken seriously. For if someone tells us that he cannot believe

p. 72

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in the reality of any facts, or in the truth of any assertions of fact, we must ask him first of all whether he believes that he is speaking to us. And if he were then, continuing his speech, to tell us that he is not convinced of the reality of his speaking just now, then I ask with respect who would be the more ridiculous, this “universal doubter,” or we, who would still engage him in a philosophical dispute. There are philosophers who think it necessary to refute such a “universal doubter” at length, but I believe that by such refutations we make philosophy itself ridiculous. For let us not forget that those men who are tossed about among truly deep and great doubts have their whole life of feeling in turmoil, and that their nervous system, too, is not seldom beset by some disorder. Such doubters, however, are not to be refuted but to be healed, with the warmth of affection, and, where need be, with the means of medical science as well. Here, however, we are dealing only with a cold theoretical doubt, which, feeling not the slightest emotional agitation, says: I doubt everything, therefore also this, that I have just said so. Is it then necessary to enter into discussion with such self-contradiction? Could I not teach even a parrot, whatever is said to it, always to answer with these words: I doubt, I doubt, I doubt? Now, what certain philosophers call universal (theoretical) doubt is worth no more than the wisdom of the parrot in question.

Although, then, there are false constatations too, we should fall into self-contradiction if we called every constatation false. Since we have accordingly the right to speak also of true constatations, let us ask first of all what it means when we say of some sentence that it contains a true constatation. What does it mean, for example, that I call true the sentence A, according to which a book lies here on the table? Perhaps I can make its sense clearer by now taking the book away from the table, whereupon I can form the following true sentence: “The book is no longer on the table” — which, for brevity’s sake, I designate by B. Since the fact which I expressed in the sentence A has ceased to be a fact, the question arises whether the sentence A itself has thereby become false. Expressed generally: since our impressions pass away, do the sentences in which we have faithfully constated our impressions become lies, or errors? I believe that the answer here is difficult for no one, and everyone is ready

p. 73

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

with the answer that our impressions pass away indeed, but that the sentences in which we have faithfully constated our impressions do not thereby become errors or lies. The passing away of impressions does not touch the truth of the sentences that constate these impressions. Impressions pass away, but the truth of the sentences that constate them does not pass. If, for example, the sentence A was true at the moment of constatation, it remains true forever. The truths of constating sentences are eternal truths (aeternae veritates).

If, then, I return in memory to the impression on the basis of which I formed the sentence A, I must now too say — if I want to remain faithful to the truth — that the sentence A contains truth now as well, although the book mentioned in it is no longer on the table. Naturally I must now frame in place of the sentence A a new sentence, which I call A1, and which differs from A in that it places the lying of the book on the table in past time. Whereas, then, the sentence A expressed that the book is now on the table, the sentence A1 can only declare that there was a moment when the book lay on the table. For the rest, however, the sentence A1 refers to the same impression as the sentence A, wherefore both sentences wish to acknowledge the same truth, with only this difference, that the sentence A constated the impression in question as just occurring, whereas the sentence A1 constates the same impression as past.

After this it needs no further explanation that in the course of time I can return more than once to that impression a of mine which I constated in the sentence A, whereby I can obtain a whole series of sentences

ₙ

A1 , A2 , A3 , . . . . . A , . . . . . which were all put forth at later and later points of time, but which all agree in this, that they refer to the same impression, or rather constate the same impression a, that is, bring the same one factual truth to expression. I can imagine this series of sentences of like content continued without end, and it is in a certain respect useful to imagine it so, for thereby I can confirm myself in the conception that the sentence A expresses an eternal truth. For if I vividly imagine that some unknown power proclaims the sentence A

p. 74

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and, as it were, publishes its content to the world, unceasingly repeating it through all times, then by this fancy of mine I have given effective expression to my regarding the content of the sentence A as a truth that never passes away. It is true that the single sentence A is in itself sufficient for my having constated the impression a for all times; but whoever should perhaps not understand at once that already with the sentence A taken by itself I constated the impression a for all times may help himself a little by imagining that some power through the endless future ceaselessly repeats the content of the sentence A in new sentence after new sentence, thereby indicating, as it were, that of the truth of this sentence we can never, under any circumstances, let go.

It is true that the fact which I have expressed in the sentence A has very little practical or other significance for me, so that I do not think it worth engraving on my memory in order to be able to return to it after a time; but I chose such an example precisely in order to have the opportunity of stressing that the truth of what we are developing here depends in no way on the practical or other importance of the facts which we put into sentences. However insignificant to me the impression a may be which I constate in the sentence A, this constatation nevertheless — if it was true at all — seeks to bring to expression a truth valid for all times. The sentence A, then, in spite of all its insignificance, I can conceive as the representative of the endless series of imagined sentences A1, A2, A3, . . . . . ad inf., as a sign that already with the sentence A taken by itself I mean to say what could be said through all times by the ceaseless proclamation of infinitely many sentences of like content. Mankind, to be sure, thinks it necessary to keep repeating, and having repeated, only certain assertions of fact, handing them down from father to son — and these are the so-called facts of world history — but from the standpoint of the theory of knowledge it is good to regard every assertion of fact as one that is repeated without pause through eternity, for only so can we express clearly that we are well aware of the transitoriness of our impressions, but are just as well aware that the true sentence with which we constated our impression preserves its truth forever and ever, even though the impression to which it refers has vanished, and perhaps there is

p. 75

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

no longer any man in the world who remembers that impression, or that man, of whose impression or of whose sentence there is now question. In a word, if the sentence A is true at the moment of its utterance, then this truth does not depend on whether its memory is renewed in anyone’s memory: for it remains true without any such renewal as well.

19. From what has been said it may perhaps be evident why in laying the foundations of the theory of knowledge we must place so great weight on those signs with which we refer to our impressions. For so long as someone, looking back on his experiences, thinks of nothing but the impressions which he has lived through, he easily arrives at the conception that everything in the world is transitory, and that there is absolutely nothing permanent in the world. For whoever looks back only to his impressions may say to himself: every impression of mine which I have hitherto lived through has passed, has vanished, and there is no doubt that my future impressions too will pass away, until at last with death there will arise for me that state in which my impressions no longer exist at all. My whole past, present, future, in a word my whole world — so far as I have any knowledge of it — consists of nothing but impressions, hence of nothing but evanescent, vanishing elements in which there is nothing permanent: it is evident, therefore, that I have no right whatever to speak of anything abiding in the world. Not only my sensations of pleasure and pain are transitory, but transitory too are the impressions which hearing, sight, and even touch convey. There is nothing we are so accustomed to regard as real as that sensation of resistance which we obtain by touching, but these sensations of resistance (of hardness, of pressure) also pass away, and it is vain to speak of a so-called “matter,” which is supposed to be the eternally abiding cause of my tactile impressions; I must confess that I have no knowledge whatever of this alleged matter, but, if I wish to be honest, I know something only of those tactile impressions which the alleged “matter” arouses in me, and these tactile impressions are every bit as evanescent as the most delicate visual and auditory impressions; wherefore, if I wish to give a serious account, free from all fancifulness, of what I have experienced and of what I have knowledge of, I must confess that with the greatest effort I am not able to discover anything there but passing elements. Everything in the world is passing, and there is no permanence in it: behold, this is the fundamental thought of every

p. 76

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

consistent impressionism (sensualism, one-sided empiricism), for if nothing can be spoken of but impressions, then evidently everything can only be transitory. That this way of thinking, which haunts modern philosophy in countless variations — (appearing now under the name of subjectivism, now of phenomenalism, indeed at times straight out under that of idealism) — leads at bottom to despair of everything and to logical anarchy, it is perhaps superfluous for me to discuss at greater length.

We understand that the lyric poet, lamenting the passing of youth, power, glory, etc., in his sad rapture calls absolutely everything transitory; we understand this and do not quarrel with him, if his poem in fact makes upon us the impression of a masterpiece: we do not quarrel with him, to mention nothing else, for this reason too, that the very excellence of his poem, and therefore its abiding aesthetic value, bears witness that, although he calls everything transitory, he strives nevertheless for the realization of something that does not pass. In aesthetics we shall have occasion to see how the poet, in spite of his apparent self-contradictions, which are easily uncovered, can nevertheless stand in the service of what the philosopher calls truth. But here we are confronted not with the poet and the artist but with the theorist of knowledge, who would have us accept as truth that there is nothing abiding in the world, nothing eternal, because in the world we can refer to nothing but our impressions, and these are in every fiber fleeting, and their vanishing is as irresistible as the vanishing of time itself. Now, with this conception — as soon as it wishes to come forward as a serious theory of knowledge — we must indeed enter into dispute, pointing out that our impressions are transitory, to be sure, but that in the sentences with which we refer to our impressions a truth that does not pass away can be contained.

What I have just said we are accustomed to express in everyday life thus: what has once happened, it is impossible to make not to have happened. Or thus: there is no power that could scratch out of the past what has befallen in it. In such and similar phrases there is revealed the fundamental principle of human logic, according to which of what was we cannot declare that it was not, although we admit that it has passed. If, for example, X has killed his neighbor Y and this happened yesterday, then we cannot say today that, the event having passed, it is to be regarded as not having happened. And if

p. 77

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Y no longer lives today but still lived yesterday, we cannot say on that account that, Y being no longer alive today, it is to be taken as if he had never lived at all. These are very elementary things, and the reader may perhaps find it odd that I discuss them so emphatically, but whoever examines human thinking attentively may observe, with much pain, that it is perhaps against such an elementary truth that we sin most often. What is more, there will surely be individuals too who quite openly profess that what was undoubtedly true yesterday is no longer true today. For there is no absurdity which a man would not undertake to profess, if for no other reason, perhaps in order thereby to cause a certain sensation. For the rest, there may be very many among us who would dearly like it, if only it could be so, that what happened yesterday could be regarded as if it had not happened at all. Indeed it is questionable whether there is a single one among us who has not something in the past to repent of, and who does not at one moment or another wish to make something that has happened not to have happened. Now, whoever knows human nature from this side easily understands that men may have a million times a million reasons for longing to have what has happened not have happened, and consequently for rebelling, secretly or openly, against the fundamental principle of logic that what is once true remains true forever and ever, so that the billions of billions of aeons are powerless against what has once occurred in a fleeting moment, since there is no power that could blot out the fact of that moment, or rather eliminate it from the endless course of facts, or replace it by another fact. A distinguished philosophical poet of ours, János Vajda, has expressed this thought in many variations, each more brilliant than the last, of which I venture to cite one:

What my soul till now feared to surmise,

Stands before me, the great secret:

That only that has died which never came to be,

And that lives forever which once was. (Poems of János Vajda, Budapest, 1895, p. 172, from the poem en‐

titled “Last Song to Gina.”)

If we translate what the poet says here from the language of the passions into the language of thoughts, then there stands before us the fundamental logical proposition which I am discussing here, and which at first sight seems so “self-evident” that even to speak of it may seem superfluous

p. 78

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

one which, however, of all propositions we may most easily misunderstand. For when we think of the impressions we have lived through, we readily use such expressions as that every impression has, as it were, pushed down, put an end to, caused to vanish, annihilated the impression that preceded it, and from here it is only one step to the view that every impression has flatly given the lie to the previous impression, has turned it into something that never happened. Pain turns the previous joy into a lie, frost the previous heat, winter the summer, etc., etc. Such thoughts nestle the more easily in our minds the more we take only the present moment seriously, and the more we are at war with our past, or rather the more reason we have to break the staff over our whole life. Now with him who has in this way, for moral reasons, become incapable of understanding the fundamental principle of logic we do not argue; for he who, despairing of himself, seizes eagerly upon propositions to the effect that the present makes the past not to have happened, so that the past in reality never was at all, while the future, sinking sooner or later into the past, likewise becomes nothing — I say, he who wishes to drown the gnawing self-reproaches of his soul in such conceptions, with him it would be in vain to argue, for he thrusts correct insight away from himself, believing that only this can save him: that everything passes, everything is nothing, and that there is no truth in the world that could raise its accusing voice against him. We can enter into argument only with him who has led himself astray theoretically, by seeing the vanishing of his impressions in such a light as though this vanishing were at the same time a giving of the lie to those impressions. Such a wandering mind must be reminded that impressions do indeed vanish, but that the truth of the sentences relating to them is imperishable, that is, eternal. We may put this also by saying that in the impressions which we live through an imperishable, eternal reality presents itself to us. For it is only two modes of expression of one and the same fundamental thought when we say at one time that an eternal truth is contained in the words with which we have constated our impression, and at another that an eternal reality has presented itself to us in that vanishing impression which we have constated by a true sentence.

Whoever, then, wishes to discover the unpassing, the everlasting, in the whirl of ever-changing appearances must not think merely of his impressions, for then he will find in his experiences only what in them passes; let him think also of those sentences

p. 79

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in which he has faithfully constated his impression, and if he can find even a single sentence in which he has given himself an account of precisely the impression which he was just then living through (and how easy it is to frame such a sentence for him who, by his very humanity, has reached the stage of development at which he can speak), he will not doubt that with its signs he has given expression to an imperishable truth. It is true that the sentence itself, insofar as it stands before us as a sum of sound-impressions or of letter-sights, is just as evanescent a thing as any other impression of ours, but the meaning that dwells in these evanescent signs is nothing other than an imperishable truth. And whoever is troubled by the circumstance that an imperishable truth should dwell in evanescent sounds or letter-sights does best to imagine — as I already suggested above — that some mysterious power repeats the true sentence in question continually through endless times, as a sign that what was once true must remain true at all times.

20. After this we can lay bare the profound logical confusion in which every impressionist way of thinking is involved. He who recognizes nothing but impressions, that is, passing appearances, cannot recognize any abiding “substance,” which, as the saying goes, is the bearer of phenomena, their support, or rather the evoker of our impressions. Yet we may say outright that according to the general human conception everything that can be touched conceals within itself something that cannot be annihilated, and which remains forever. Philosophers have named substance that which is the abiding ground of transitory phenomena; the common man perhaps prefers to call the same thing matter, but let anyone name it as he will — for we have no wish to engage in barren dispute over words — this one thing is certain, that men in general are convinced that all the passing phenomena of the world have an abiding ground. What is the impressionist thinker now to do with this widespread belief? He knows, and wishes to recognize, nothing but impressions, that is, passing appearances; that alleged “substance,” however, would be something abiding, and so could be neither an impression nor a heap of impressions, wherefore the consistent impressionist would be unfaithful to his fundamental principle if he regarded that alleged “substance” or “matter” (or whatever else it may be called) as anything but a mere

p. 80

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

chimera. And yet it is precisely the impressionist thinker who is least fond of departing from the prevailing common view, for the very reason that he appeals continually to our impressions, and to our impressions alone, is that he knows that with the masses nothing can have such great authority as what can be seen with the eye, heard with the ear, or even, indeed, grasped with the hand. But what is the impressionist philosopher to do with the great herds of men, when they live in the peculiar, unbreakable belief that in what they are able to feel there is something that cannot be swindled out of the world and that remains imperishably through all passings and changes: let them call this everlasting something substance, subsistence, stuff, essence, matter, force, vigor, energy, or, for all I care, anything whatever. It is truly a most uncomfortable position into which the impressionist philosopher falls in face of millions upon millions of the masses, who by some incomprehensible enchantment speak of something that does not pass away, whereas he (the impressionist) is convinced that nothing can be experienced but what passes (that is, impressions), and that he therefore inevitably chases chimeras who dares to speak of an abiding ground of passing phenomena, of their support, their substratum, or I know not what. But if such an impressionist philosopher is about to despair altogether of mankind or of himself, let him only ask the man of the masses whence he gets this superstition of his, that passing phenomena cling to something that does not pass; for the answer will be most instructive to him. He will hear that one need only touch any body whatever, for touching convinces a man directly that there is indeed something in that body which exerts a definite resistance and which no artifice can reduce to naught in the world. Such an innocent soul is therefore convinced that what in phenomena does not pass he feels directly with his hand. The impressionist may then explain, to be sure, that, if you please, by touching we arrive at nothing but impressions of touch, and these are of a passing nature; that it is also impossible for touch, or indeed for any sense whatever, to give notice of anything but impressions and the togetherness of impressions, therefore of sheer transiencies, wherefore it ought after all to be seen that it is superstition or chimera when anyone speaks of anything other than transitory impressions.

Consistent impressionism thus comes into conflict

p. 81

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

with what we call sound reason. It may be, however, that for a time it takes its very delight in this conflict, for the trait of human vanity is well known, that even where a man converses with himself, in the world of his own secret thoughts, he seeks not what is true but that with which he might practice a sort of spiritual dandyism. In the end, however, even the impressionist is compelled to ask whence the masses can have got that ingrained belief, according to which there is in passing phenomena something that does not pass, and indeed, horribile dictu, this very thing that does not pass might even be felt with the hand. Now, in face of this question, whence the masses obtained that ingrained belief, according to which passing phenomena cling to an abiding subsistence, impressionism is utterly helpless. At most it might come forward with the suggestion that someone has instilled the superstition of substance into the masses, but then it would also have to name who that someone is. This could not be laid to the charge of the servants of religion, for the natural sciences too do nothing else than point continually to what in the changing phenomena of nature never passes away (see the principles of the conservation of matter and the conservation of energy that figure in the fashionable physical handbooks). It is the highest pride of modern natural science too that it seeks the unpassing in all the mutability of appearances. Hence the question indicated above cannot be solved by any sort of imputations or suspicions, from which it merely appears that impressionism does not understand human nature, being unable to explain what can induce the son of man to believe that he finds something that does not pass in the passing phenomena which his sense-organs disclose to him. But he who does not understand human nature does not understand nature at all either, for to understand man and to understand nature are properly one task.

The logical confusion of the impressionist way of thinking, which I touched upon in passing a moment ago, has nowhere found such a striking and, one might say, such a brilliant expression as in the history of English theory of knowledge, namely in the investigations of Locke, Berkeley and Hume. These thinkers are so imbued with the striving for clarity that, in spite of their errors, they furnish the best introduction to inquiry in the theory of knowledge. As regards Locke in the first place, his work becomes instructive mainly in that with rare candor of soul it lays bare the self-contradictions

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 6

p. 82

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of the amiable author. In the Introduction we have already become acquainted with Locke’s impressionist leanings, so it may be foreseen that he will be greatly entangled over the concept of substance. Since Locke fundamentally wishes to accept nothing but impressions (for the word “idea” means with him in the first place impressio), he ought flatly to have denied the existence of “substance.” But because he did not like to depart from the general human conceptions, he did not dare to deny substance, and took up toward it a wavering, floating position which in no way conceals its logical confusion. At one time he says that the general idea of substance is obscure and confused; at another he speaks of substance as though it were only a supposition, and for the rest we knew nothing of it. “The idea then we have, to which we give the general name substance, being nothing but the supposed, but unknown, support of those qualities we find existing, which we imagine cannot subsist sine re substante . . .” (Essay, Book II, ch. 23, § 2.) In his great confusion he keeps returning to the thought that we cannot conceive the properties of bodies as existing separately one by one, so that we are compelled to resort to the idea that there is something which holds the properties together, bears them, as it were, upon itself, that is, supports them within itself; and this uncertain something, of which we otherwise know nothing, is supposed to be substance. For thirty-seven sections he argues the concept of substance, and from his discussions it appears that he believes in the existence of substance, only that he calls the concept we form of it confused and obscure, and even goes so far as to regard it as a mere supposition, or flatly declares that of substance in itself we have no concept at all. “By the complex idea of extended, figured, coloured, and all other sensible qualities, which is all that we know of it, we are as far from the idea of the substance of body, as if we knew nothing at all . . .” (Essay, Book II, § 16.) Such declarations show well enough what a self-division Locke is in with respect to substance. He wholly shares the general conviction that there is indeed a substance, and even distinguishes bodily substance from mental; but on the other hand, by his assertion that of substance in itself we know nothing, he really pronounces the non-existence of substance, for of what we know nothing, of its existence

p. 83

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

we can know nothing either, and of it there can be no serious talk. Locke’s impressionism stands in sharp contradiction with his theory of substance, and this self-contradiction is so striking that the historians of philosophy too have noticed it. Locke wishes to trace all knowledge back to experience, or more exactly to impressions; but substance is not an impression, nor an aggregate of impressions, since impressions or heaps of them are passing, whereas by substance we understand that which is the imperishable foundation of all transiencies. Such a foundation impressionism cannot recognize if it does not wish to deny itself.

This was sensed by the ingenious Berkeley, who drew the extreme consequence of English impressionism, in that he denied substance, or rather bodily substance, or matter. The historians of philosophy call this matter-denying impressionism of Berkeley’s “idealism,” whereas he who recognizes the existence of nothing but that of impressions becomes, if he thinks consistently, a denier of matter. For impressions are passing, but the concept of matter contains within it the not-passing. Now the consistent impressionist can recognize the existence of nothing outside passing experiences (impressions). That abiding ground to which we relate our impressions (matter) he is compelled to regard as a “metaphysical supposition,” which must be eliminated from strictly empirical, impressionist thinking. What we experience is impression and nothing else, and every subsistence, essence, matter or the like, which is supposed to be the eternal ground of passing impressions, is a mere fictio, a remnant of the old mythological thinking, which can have no place in strictly empirical, scientific thought. Such is the doctrine of consistent impressionism, and this, as we see, leads to “immaterialism.” Berkeley’s denial of matter too is really nothing other than the extremely consistent development of the empiricist and sensualist way of thinking (which Locke founds in the footsteps of Bacon and Hobbes). Berkeley falls into contradiction with himself only by denying, of Locke’s two kinds of substance, only the material, but not the spiritual. Here, at this point, the theological and metaphysical thinker breaks out of him; his denial of matter, however, is only the carrying of the principle of sensuousness to an extreme. Modern investigators of nature (such as Mach, for example) have sensed that Berkeley’s immaterialism is really the clear development of the sensuous way of thinking. Only

6*

p. 84

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in this do they go beyond Berkeley’s doctrine, that they deny not only material substance but the soul too, which Berkeley (in contrast with his impressionist standpoint) had retained. Only our own age has attempted to carry through in this way the extreme impressionism, which knows no matter but only clusters of impressions floating in the air, and which does not recognize even the “I,” because in it too it sees nothing but a few decades’ stretch of clusters of impressions. That this impressionism (or phenomenalism) is really only a disguise of universal doubt, which will recognize no reality and no truth, it is superfluous to explain at greater length.

21. If we wish to understand whence the son of man gets that irresistible inclination to relate his passing sensible impressions to some abiding foundation, we must turn our attention from our impressions to those signs (sentences) with which we constate our impressions. This is just what the impressionist thinkers neglect to do with due attention. Yet that we form signs by which we refer to our impressions is also a fact among the other facts, and indeed a fact of which it is well worth taking notice. Look, of those impressions which Locke, Berkeley and Hume themselves personally lived through we know, fundamentally, not much, and we are not unduly interested in them, but those signs which convey their thoughts to us we can still study now, and shall surely go on studying through many hundreds of years. Had they had only impressions, and had they really concentrated their being only on enjoying their experiences, who would speak of them now? But because they convey their thoughts to us by signs, from which we feel — even if we disagree with them — that they sought the truth with a warm heart, we mention their names with gratitude and respect, even when we declare war on their method of inquiry. It is a pity that they did not esteem signs in the same measure as their impressions, for philosopher, scholar, poet, artist are all of them just such men as thirst for impressions only in order to be able to give an account, by signs, of the truths to which their impressions have enabled them to attain . . . We so-called “modern men” are filled with our inventions, the steamship, the telegraph, the telephone, etc., which, as we are wont to say, annihilate every distance on earth; but what, after all, are all these inventions

p. 85

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

taken together beside our words, which annihilate the distance of the ages between us, binding the generations of millennia to one another by the electric current of the spirit? If we could call our speech an invention, then it would indeed be the most important of all our inventions, for it is this that makes it possible for us to have inventions at all.

That men are nevertheless inclined to disparage the signs they use is indeed very characteristic of them. They think that the word is “only a means of communication” between man and man, and that man, if we regard him as a solitary thinker, can do without words. They make a difference in principle between solitary and social thinking, believing that the one is essentially different in nature from the other. Yet if they considered that the six-year-old child, when he meditates silently, is just as childish as when he speaks aloud, and that the girl thinks like a girl, the woman like a woman, the man like a man, whether they spin the thread of their thoughts silently or aloud, then without any deeper inquiry they could come to see that silent human thinking cannot differ essentially from thinking aloud. Yes, if it could be shown, for example, that Galileo understood nothing of the theory of motion when he thought silently and understood it only when he spoke aloud, and likewise that Leibniz was ignorant in the differential calculus so long as he thought silently and understood his own discoveries only when he spoke of them aloud: then I should believe that an essential difference exists between the two kinds of thinking. But such simple considerations are not wont to affect minds, for it is in vain: it is only what they do not understand that impresses the masses (I speak of the cultivated masses). Fortunately the investigation of nature too has by now taken up the question in point, and is on the way to pulling aside the veil from the secret of silent or inward-turned thinking, at least so far that it becomes clear that in silent thinking physiological functions of a similar nature come into play as in thinking aloud, and that the divergence between them cannot establish a difference in principle. To use a figure: silent thinking may be compared to the physicist’s experiment in which the phenomenon that arises is observed only by himself, and thinking aloud to the case when, by the help of a projecting apparatus, the physical phenomenon becomes visible all at once to the whole audience present. Now it is evident

p. 86

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that the physical experiment remains essentially the same, whether a projecting apparatus is used or not. In like manner the process of our thinking too remains essentially the same, whether we think silently within ourselves or project our thoughts in audible words for others.

If, starting from this point of view, we begin to lay stress not only on our impressions but also on the sentences that constate them, we may take up two positions with regard to these sentences: 1. we may declare that absolutely every sentence in which we constate our impressions is false, which would mean that we men are altogether incapable of giving an account of any experience of ours. This is the standpoint of universal doubt, which, however, is untenable. For he who declares to us that every sentence in which we constate our impressions is false secretly presupposes of us that we have heard his words and have constated them at least as sounds, and likewise that we are convinced of the truth of this constatation. Since, then, universal doubt contains a self-contradiction, nothing remains for us but: 2. to acknowledge that among the sentences in which men constate their impressions there are to be found not only erroneous or false but also true sentences.

If we have now led the impressionist thinker to the point of acknowledging that there is such a thing as true constatation, all that remains is to throw a little light for him on the meaning of the word “true.” For with regard to this word one may again take up two positions: 1. someone might assert that a true constatation is true only for a certain term, for example for a second, a year, a century, etc., but beyond that becomes a lie, which, however, is a patent self-contradiction, for it expresses that only a sufficient time need elapse for truth to cease to be truth; so nothing remains but: 2. to acknowledge that the truth of an assertion cannot be tied to a term, but that what is true must remain true for all time. As soon as the impressionist thinker has seen this, he can know at once how the concept of eternity has come to be among his concepts. Everyone who is convinced of the truth of a constating sentence regards the meaning of that sentence as true for eternity. The thought of eternity, therefore, comes into play everywhere where someone faithfully gives an account of any impression of his: for the

p. 87

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

truth of such an account lays claim to eternal validity. Every true sentence, even though it relates to a passing impression, is formulated from the standpoint of eternity (sub specie aeterni): just as it is not possible at all to assert anything, that is, to formulate an assertion, otherwise than from the standpoint of eternity. Even our false assertions we formulate from the standpoint of eternity, because we hold them to be true; indeed even the liar lies only in order that his assertion may be held true: so that it is precisely lying that furnishes a decisive proof for truth, in that it would occur to no one to lie if men did not possess at all the capacity of regarding something as true. There is, then, no doubt whatever that we formulate our assertions from the standpoint of eternity, and the trouble is only that, although as soon as we use our mind we set it toward eternity, we may with our best endeavor deceive ourselves and our fellow men as well. To conceal or to gloss over this human frailty of ours is the last thing I desire; on the contrary, I regard it as the chief task of the theory of knowledge to place our humanity, with all its frailty, in the most penetrating light possible. Only we cannot place our frailty in the proper light either, unless we first see that we are beings made for truth; for he cannot speak truly even of its frailty who trembles at all lest perhaps we are capable of nothing true. Human error too can be laid bare in its true nature only by him who is convinced that his mind is set toward eternal truth. Hence every logical inquiry begins with the establishment that we are beings made for truth, and in particular that we are able to give an account of our impressions in true sentences.

If the impressionist thinker has seen this, then he must take one step more. He must see that for the very same reason for which we can put eternal truth into our words, we can relate our impressions too to an eternal reality. If we found no truth in our words, we should find no reality in our impressions either; and if the truth contained in our words, when we gave an account of an impression of ours, is eternal, then the reality too is eternal which presented itself to us in that impression. That pious soul is right, therefore, who speaks to us thus: touch any body whatever and by the

p. 88

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

feeling be convinced that in that body there is something, an indestructible reality. Yes, he is right when he says that what never passes away he feels, sees with his eyes, notices with all his sense-organs. Yes, it is the eternal itself that we feel, see, notice in those passing impressions which our sense-organs afford us: but we must add also that neither by feeling, nor by seeing, nor by any other sense-organ whatever should we be capable of finding the eternal, if we were not able to refer to our impressions by true sentences. In our passing impressions an eternal essence presents itself to us: this is the fundamental principle of logic, or rather of metaphysics, and it is as certain as it is certain that we can give an account of our impressions in true sentences.

7. Judgment and Impression.

22. In the preceding chapter we arrived at a distinction which, epistemologically, is of greater importance to us than any other: we distinguished our judgments from our impressions. If we follow attentively the thinking of the masses, indeed if we study the works of the philosophers too, we may not infrequently observe with pain that minds learned and unlearned alike are inclined to exchange their impressions for their judgments, although between these there obtains a difference such as that between transience and eternity. Our impressions without exception are all fleeting; they emerge in the course of time and sink down again in it: but the truth that is contained in a judgment of ours lays claim to eternal validity. Our experiences we call impressions insofar as they are transitory, and we may call them experiences of eternity insofar as they are judgments containing truth. A graver difference than this cannot be imagined: our impressions are experiences of transience, our judgments, on the contrary, experiences of eternity.

This is the distinction to which impressionist thinkers do not attain, and hence they exchange their impressions for their judgments, their sensible experiences for their experiences of eternity. They do not, to be sure, say openly that by judgment they mean the impression itself, for against so definite an avowal sound reason would at once protest; but they seek formulas by which they may covertly reduce judgment

p. 89

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

to impressions. Such a formula is, for example, that judgment is nothing other than a connection of impressions. If I say, for example, that a book lies here on the table, then this sentence would contain nothing but the connection of two impressions (that of the table and that of the book). They do not reflect that I can take the book from the table, and that consequently (or for some other reason) the connection of the two impressions in question ceases, whereas the content of that assertion (that the book lies on the table) is a truth that never passes away. Judgment can be neither a single impression nor a connectedness of single impressions, for “connected” impressions are just as transitory as single impressions, whereas judgment seeks to express a truth that does not pass away. Some believe that they can come closer to the concept of judgment if they speak of memories instead of impressions and present judgment as the connection of memory-images. But what I have said of impressions holds also for memory-images, namely that, whether we speak of single or of “connected” memory-images, they are in any case passing, whereas the truth of judgment does not depend on whether we still remember the impressions we lived through or have forgotten them, for the truth of judgment is eternal truth. Finally there are theorists of knowledge who like to present judgment as the connection neither of impressions nor of memories but rather of “ideas” (Vorstellungen). These take refuge behind the uncertain meaning of the word “idea.” For by idea I may understand impressions, also memory-images, also their connection, and, what is the chief thing, I may understand “concepts” as well. Those, then, who call judgment a connection of ideas are really returning to Aristotle’s definition, except that in place of “connection of concepts” they use “connection of ideas,” because in this way they think they can smuggle the Aristotelian definition into the impressionist view. As for the Aristotelian definition, we have already criticized it in the Introduction.

To avoid every misunderstanding I must stress that judgment indeed cannot be formed without impressions and memories, or, if you will, without “connected” impressions and “connected” memories; but it by no means follows from this that we are to exchange our judgments for the connected occurrence of our impressions or memories. Myriads of impressions and memories might occur connected together without there lurking in them the faintest spark of judgment. For only such a being judges as, in its passing

p. 90

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

impressions and memory-images finds an eternal reality, or in its constating signs an eternal truth (or at least believes it finds one). I am far from supposing that I have thereby established the essence of judgment exhaustively, for if we succeeded in defining judgment completely and exhaustively, then the essence of knowledge (and at the same time of existence!) would be established too, that is, the degree of perfect knowledge would have been reached. But we may well be content here also with asserting of the essence of judgment something that really fits judgments, even if we are not able to define the essence of judgment exhaustively. Of every judgment, or at least of every constating judgment, it holds that to judge is to regard our passing impressions (or our memory-images) as the manifestation of an eternal reality. Or if, instead of impressions, we think of our signs, then to judge is to express in passing symbols an eternal truth (or what we take to be one).

In this way we have arrived at another important distinction of ours, in that we distinguish between the passing symbols with which we express our judgments and the eternally valid truths which we are accustomed to call the sense or content of those symbols. To confirm ourselves in this distinction it is expedient to return to the infinite series of sentences

A, A1, A2, A3 . . . . ad inf. of which we have already spoken in the preceding chapter. For if by A we understand a sentence in which we constate an impression of ours that presents itself immediately, then such a sentence already, taken by itself, lays claim to eternal truth; but in order to give this claim the most effective expression, it is expedient to conceive the sentence A as though it were the representative or substitute of infinitely many sentences of identical content, which sound forth through the endless future in an unbroken series, constating continually the same impression which we had already constated in the sentence A. By the identical content of these infinitely many sentences we are admonished to distinguish between the truth which is the common content of the sentences A, A1, A2, A3 . . . . and the symbols of which these sentences are composed. Each of these sentences sounds forth at a different point of time, so that the sounds of the one cannot be exchanged for the sounds of the other; their content, however, is common, at least in so far as in each of these sentences it is the same impression that we wish to

p. 91

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

constate. It is true that in this series of sentences we always refer to the original impression a by a new and ever new memory-image, so that to the series of sentences there would correspond a series of memory-images a1, a2, a3 . . . . ad inf., but this alters nothing in the fact that we wish to bring one and the same truth to expression continually. The memory-images by which we refer to the original impression are continually different and different, likewise the symbols by which the reference is made are continually new and new, but the truth which we express in the series of sentences remains always the same. In this way anyone can clearly distinguish the identical content of the above series of sentences from the non-identical sounds of which the sentences consist. The sentence A2, for example, we think of as uttered later than the sentence A1, and thus the sounds of the sentence A2 cannot, for this reason alone, be identical with those of the sentence A1.

After this it is easy to see that every judgment of ours in which we constate some impression of ours bears the character of generality. The impression itself which we constate is not at all general, but something particular, indeed we may say something unique, for the very same impression which we once had can never occur again in the course of time. Here the reader must take care not to regard similar or equal impressions as identical. One of our impressions may agree very closely with another, so that between the two impressions we can hardly find any other difference than that they emerged at different points of time (as, for example, when the reader looks twice at these letters), but this agreement or equality of the impressions (or rather their similarity) is not to be confounded with identity. An impression can occur only once in the course of time and never again. Hence the characteristic mark of every impression is uniqueness or particularity. One and the same truth, however, we may repeat as often as we like, and by this repetition it does not become a different truth. Each member of the series of sentences A, A1, A2, A3 . . . . wishes in the strictest sense of the word to express the same truth, and the circumstance that each of these sentences expresses a truth at a different point of time alters nothing in the identity of the truth expressed by them. The memory-images a1, a2, a3 . . . . by which we refer to the original impression a are all non-identical with one another, and likewise non-identical are the sounds (or letters) which compose the sentences A, A1, A2 . . . ., but the

p. 92

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

truth which they express is indeed identical. From this we see why we call a truth general, but our impressions particular or unique. Truth is something general, because one and the same truth may present itself in any number of sentences; the impression, on the contrary, is something particular, because it occurs only once in the infinite course of time. This, moreover, is only another way of saying that truth is eternal, but the impression passing. What passes away cannot return again; it is something particular, unique. What, on the contrary, is eternal (like truth) may be repeated in countless sentences without thereby becoming a different truth; it is therefore something general.

What we have just set forth we may express also by saying that every truth bears the character of a law, even when that truth is merely the constatation of some impression of ours. By law in practical life we understand a sentence (or group of sentences) with which it is forbidden to come into conflict; in logic, however, the word law cannot have such a practical sense, for logic neither commands nor forbids anything when it establishes laws. In general the laws of science are neither commands nor prohibitions. In logic we call a law a sentence (or group of sentences) to violate which, that is, to come into conflict with which, means the same as to assert something false (erroneous). From this it appears that in logic the word law means the same as the word truth, for it is the nature of truth that to come into conflict with it means the same as to assert something false.

The true constatation of any impression of ours also bears the character of a law, for if we ever came into conflict with such a true constatation, we should unconditionally be asserting something false, even if neither we ourselves nor any other mortal should ever notice this falsity or error.

We had to emphasize that the faithful constatation of a single impression of ours bears the character of general truth, that is, of law, because the rationalist thinkers hold the opposite view. They regard as general truth only such a truth as, for example, 2 × 2 = 4. There is no doubt that the truth that I am now writing has a different character from the truth that 2 × 2 = 4; and if the rationalists examined this difference in character without any prejudice, then we should have to engage in no sort of

p. 93

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

dispute with them. It is an extraordinarily interesting and extraordinarily weighty question for the theory of knowledge in what, properly speaking, the truths of mathematics differ from truths in which we constate individual facts, and we shall regard the investigation of this difference as one of our principal tasks; but this much we can state here already, that the difference in question cannot possibly relate to “generality,” for every truth must be general, otherwise it would cease to be a truth. The rationalists, however, regard our sensible impressions with a certain disdain, not to say contempt, and are therefore inclined to disparage the sentences too which constate our impressions. This one-sided inclination shows itself in them in their denying the character of “generality” to sentences that constate sensible impressions. They do not notice that by this procedure it is not the sensible impression that they degrade, but the human mind itself, which occupies itself with that sensible impression. They do not notice that the human mind always remains the same human mind, that is, can always be set toward eternal truth, whether it constates the slightest facts or investigates the most intricate mathematical truths. The human mind is just as sublime when it ascertains the buzzing of a fly as when it says that twice two is four, and human judgment preserves human dignity in the one case just as in the other, since in the one case it strives (or can strive) after truth just as in the other. Those, therefore, who wish to deprive the constating judgment of the character of generality attack not sensibility but the human faculty of judgment at its very root. The rationalists present themselves to us as though they esteemed human reason, yet if against any party, then against them it is necessary to defend not merely the dignity of the human mind but its bare existence. If the mind cannot produce truth when it has to constate an impression, then it cannot produce any truth at all. The human mind must show above all that it is able to judge where impressions are to be constated, for if it is not capable even of this, it is best that it should give up all its labor. For if of everything that surrounds us only falsity can be asserted, then what sense would there be in our asserting anything at all? Hence no more unfortunate procedure can be imagined than that of

p. 94

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

dividing truths into two classes and setting blue-blooded (a priori = innate) truths, such as the truths of mathematics, over against plebeian (a posteriori = empirical) truths in such a way that only the first class would shine in the aureole of “generality,” whereas the second class would really only usurp the name of truth, since we cannot call truth what is not general.

Judgment, as I have said, is general under all circumstances: general even when it relates to an impression, and therefore to something particular or unique. There are, however, as we shall see later, judgments of ours which relate not to impressions but to other judgments, and of these we may say that they are general in a double sense: 1. general in themselves, since they are judgments; 2. general also in the sense that they relate to something general (a judgment).
