In this twofold sense those judgments too are general which figure with the rationalists as a priori judgments. From this it is seen that we do not wish to efface the difference between a priori and a posteriori judgments; for that there is a difference between them is obvious. We merely do not allow the one kind of judgment to be set in opposition to the other in such a way that the one kind should on that account cease to be judgment. It would be as though someone wished to distinguish the soldier from the civilian by saying that the one is a man but the other is not a man. Judgments cannot differ from one another in respect of generality, for that in which there is no generality cannot be judgment, cannot contain truth. For, to repeat: truth is eternally valid and therefore general. Judgment, therefore, together with eternal validity lays claim to generality also. And this claim of its does not cease even when it relates to something particular (an impression). For judgment retains its character as judgment even when it constates an impression.

23. To bring a certain visualizability into our concept of judgment, geometrical representation offers itself as a very suitable means. Traditional logic contented itself with representing concepts by circles, but that one might also seek some suitable geometrical symbol for judgment it did not really think of at all, the explanation of which is easily found in the fact that traditional logic starts not from the investigation of judgments but of concepts. Our starting-point

p. 95

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

differs from that of our predecessors also in that, together with impressions, we place at the head of logic the judgments that constate them. In this way we must think in the first place of a geometrical representation of judgment. If we represent a constating judgment A by a straight line, then the series of judgments A1, A2, A3 . . . ., which express the same fact only at different points of time, we may represent by nothing but parallel straight lines, as the following figure shows:

If we wish to represent a judgment differing in content from A, say K, we can do so by changing the direction of the straight line:

Here the parallel straight lines K, K1, K2, K3 again symbolize the same content of thought, only that each corresponds to a different point of time of the assertion. This mode of representation is so elementary that it hardly needs explanation, and one can at most wonder why logic has never made use of so obvious a representation. For with all its elementariness this symbolization has also its own great interest and deep significance, which, however — alas — only the mathematically trained mind can discern at once. The mathematician notices immediately that if one and the same truth is represented by a whole system of parallel straight lines, then it is really the common direction of the lines that symbolizes that truth. The mathematician, moreover, is accustomed to regard parallel straight lines as lines which meet in a point at infinity. In other words, he regards parallel straight lines as lines pointing to some point lying at infinity (as their common point of intersection). To every system of parallels there corresponds one such infinitely

p. 96

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

point at infinity, and conversely to every point at infinity a system of parallels. The point lying at infinity therefore symbolizes the direction of the whole system of parallels, since the whole system points toward it. And since the direction of the system of parallels represents, in our eyes, the truth to be represented, it is evident that we can represent the truth by the point at infinity of a system of parallels as well.

This mode of representation is so suitable because it strongly reminds us of the eternal being of truth. A point lying at infinity stands, as it were, above all space and time, for it can never be reached, and so is a fitting symbol of eternal truth standing above space and time, to which we point with our sentence as the straight line points to its point lying at infinity. And this mode of representation is also very suitable for showing that judgment has under all circumstances a general character, or that every true judgment is to be regarded as a law. Just as by the drawing of one straight line the infinite multitude of straight lines running parallel with it is also determined, so by the uttering of a single true sentence the infinitely many “parallel sentences” of identical content with it are also determined, which at different points of time proclaim the same truth. Direction we must call something general, because the same direction can be the common direction of infinitely many straight lines, just as the same truth can be the common content of infinitely many sentences. The community of direction we may regard as the law that prevails over the infinite multitude of parallel straight lines; in like manner we may call a law that content of judgment too which prevails, as their common content, over infinitely many sentences.

For the rest, we leave it wholly to the reader’s taste whether he makes friends with the above method of representation, for the truth of our propositions is completely independent of whether we represent them geometrically or renounce such means of visualization. The mathematician, however, will hardly pass the indicated method of representation by with indifference, for it will probably occur to him that some deeper and secret kinship obtains between the doctrines of logic and those of mathematics. By our representation we really wish to direct the attention of thinkers to this kinship, long suspected but never unraveled. In the course of our discussions we shall still have

p. 97

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

many an occasion to return to the close kinship of mathematical and epistemological truths. For the present our representation fully answers its purpose if it confirms us in the insight that every judgment, by the very fact of its being a judgment, is unavoidably of a general character, even when it relates to a single impression (that is, to something unique).

This is the proposition the misunderstanding of which, or rather the failure to recognize it clearly, has contributed in great measure to the decline of the science of logic. When Aristotle (in the very first chapter of his Analytics) set general and particular (limited) judgments against one another, he contributed, knowingly or not, to the rise of the dangerous semblance that generality does not belong to the essence of judgment, nay, that one of the main classes of judgments is distinguished precisely by its not being general but particular in character. I call this semblance so dangerous because nothing can so attack our whole thinking at its root as our failing to draw a proper distinction between what is transitory, that is, particular, and what lays claim to eternal validity, that is, to the character of generality. When Aristotle wishes to distinguish particular judgments from general judgments, he confuses the judgment with that to which the judgment relates. A judgment may relate to a single impression, and hence to something particular, but it does not follow from this that the judgment is particular. For what makes a judgment a judgment is not that to which it relates, but that it is the mental act of a judging being. Whatever the judgment may relate to, a grain of dust or the whole universe, that can change nothing in the judgmental character of the judgment. It is well known how far Aristotle lets himself be carried away by his classifying passion, and how happy he is when he finds one more artificial pigeonhole for storing his thoughts. In his classifying passion he makes no distinction between the judgment as judgment and that to which we relate the judgment. Our judgment may relate to one, two, three . . . many impressions, that is, to one or several particular things, but it may relate (as we have already mentioned) to something general too, for example to a single judgment, a group of judgments, all possible judgments, and heaven knows what else, which it would be superfluous to enumerate offhand. Now the question is what the sage of Stagira wanted to classify: the judgments themselves, or rather those

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 7

p. 98

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

things to which the judgments relate? But whatever he may have wished to classify, this one thing is certain, that it was a great pity that he distinguished particular and general judgments, and thereby called up the semblance that it is not things that are particular or general, but that the function of judgment itself might be something particular or general. Had Aristotle first established that every judgment can be a judgment only because it lays claim to generality, and had he then added that the things to which our judgments relate may be particular or general things, we could of course have no objection to that; but then it would at once have come to light that what is at issue is not so much a classification of judgments as of the things to be judged. And then we should at once have been cautious in classifying, for who could ever think of dividing into classes all the things of the world that we can subject to judgment? It is not necessary for me to go into the history of the fundamental logical question touched on here, and I shall mention only Kant’s modification, by which, in dividing Aristotle’s particular judgments into two classes, he arrives at the following threefold division: a) general, b) special, c) singular judgments. How much this sort of game with pigeonholes has contributed to the spoiling of interest in logic, why should I say? What thinking man would not notice at first glance what an empty verbal trifle it is when we divide judgments into two or three classes from the point of view of “quantitas.” Everyone feels the emptiness of such a game, even if he cannot give a clear account of it. In what precedes I have, I believe, shown whence comes that frightful emptiness which yawns at us from the above classification. Every judgment is general, and in the foregoing it is not judgments that are in question, but the things to be judged, of which it is said that the object of our judging may be a single thing falling under a concept, or several things, or all things. Well, I ask you, who has need of the wisdom that, if for example I wish to speak of men, either one man, or several men, or all men may come into question? Such verbosity is fit only to make logic or the theory of knowledge an object of public ridicule. For the division in question belongs not to the theory of knowledge at all, but to elementary arithmetic. To a child to whom I am explaining the operations of addition and subtraction I can say, for example: here is a bag of nuts; if someone takes nuts out of it, whether only one, or several, or all

p. 99

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the nuts he can. But I believe that even the child would rebel against such arithmetical wisdoms.

Verbosity usually brings with it, moreover, the danger that it pushes itself in the place of very useful truths, and indeed, by obscuring these, drags the mind into the quagmire of some absurdity. Behold, for example, the division of judgments into general, special and singular: it can serve only to divert our mind from the insight that every judgment is of a general character. Nay, it can even draw our thinking into the whirlpool where we may confuse the judgment with the impression itself. For if the judgment as judgment could be something particular, it could no longer be distinguished from the impression. One and the same judgment can be the common content of countless sentences A, A1, A2, A3 . . . ad inf., and so the judgment is unavoidably of a general character; the impression, on the contrary, is unavoidably something particular, for once it has passed it can never arise again as the same impression. The sentence, as a mass of sounds or a mass of letters, is likewise only something particular, but the truth that we express in a sentence is something eternally valid, that is, general. In order, then, not to confuse the sentence as a mass of sounds with the truth which it expresses, it is expedient to think of the sentence as repeated countless times in the course of the infinite future; for this procedure directly summons us to distinguish the passing impressions of sound from the eternal or general sense that lies hidden in them.

24. When we lay stress on distinguishing the judgment properly from the impression to which it relates, what we really wish is to prevent that which is everlasting from being confused with that which is transitory. Since the sounds of a sentence are transitory phenomena, we may easily fall into the error of supposing that the truth which the sentence contains is transitory too. Against this error, as we saw, the easiest defense is to regard the constating sentence A as standing in the place of the infinite series of sentences A1, A2, . . . With all these sentences A1, A2, . . . we refer to the same impression to which we had already referred with the sentence A. In this way the infinite series of the sentences in question reminds us what we must understand by the identity of a thing. We identify an impression by constating it in a judgment. For this constatation is valid not only for the moment of constatation, but

7*

p. 100

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

for all times. The constating sentence is indeed an act performed only once, but because it is an act that speaks for all times, we must think of it as infinitely repeated, so that by this infinite repetition the impression may remain identified for ever. The sentences A, A1, A2, A3, . . . all relate to the same impression, all constate the same impression; for which reason we shall also call constatation identification. We can identify an impression of ours only because we are able to refer to it by a judgment, a judgment in which an imperishable truth is contained. If the truth contained in the sentence A were of a transitory nature, then we should no longer be allowed to form a sentence A1 or A2, A3 . . . in which we refer to the same impression. Then, with the passing away of the impression itself, the truth that this impression once was would be annihilated too. Then we could no longer speak of anything that has happened, for we should have to regard what is past as though it had never been at all. But it is an obvious absurdity that we should have to regard as not having happened what has happened. The very significance of the constating sentence consists in this: that what we have once recognized as happening must be referred to, at every later time, as having happened (and not as not having happened), by anyone who wishes to remain faithful to truth.

We are able to identify an impression, then, because we are capable of referring to it in a true judgment. For the same reason we relate that impression to something that possesses an imperishable actuality. Since we find truth in our constating sentence, we find eternal actuality — reality — in the passing impression to which we refer. To identify an impression, therefore, means to relate it to an imperishable actuality. The truth which we are here expounding may be called the principle of identity (principium identitatis). By virtue of this principle only a being who is capable of judging, that is, who can find truth in his own symbols, his words, can identify anything. And only a being can identify anything who relates what passes away to some ground that does not pass away. Everything that passes away is the appearance of something that does not pass away. Everything that is sensuous is the symptom of something that is not sensuous. On this principle all constatation rests, and therefore all human science. The principle of identity is the fundamental principle of all our knowledge, and therefore we can never expound this principle sufficiently.

p. 101

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

In what follows we shall see that the whole theory of knowledge, indeed all human knowledge whatever, turns on the deepening of this principle.

8. Constatation on the Basis of Memory.

25. In our earlier discussions we distinguished the judgment A, which we make under the direct influence of the impression, from those judgments A1, A2, A3 . . . which we can form on the basis of memory, but which are wholly identical in content with the judgment A. We now direct our attention to these judgments set up out of memory, for only so can we hope to look more deeply than has hitherto been possible into the nature of judgment, and with it of knowledge (and therefore at the same time of existence). Logic, as we said, sets itself the investigation of knowledge as its aim, but because we express all our knowledge in judgments (sentences), logic can do nothing else than make our judgment the object of ever more searching judging. Hitherto our attention was directed chiefly to those judgments in which we constated our directly appearing impressions; but we can now extend it also to those judgments in which, on the basis of memory, we constate some impression we have lived through. The examination of these remembering judgments will shed new light also on the original judgments, which were made directly under the influence of the impression.

How important a factor memory is in every judgment of ours is shown by the following simple consideration. Suppose that under the direct influence of an impression a we form the constating judgment A relating to it, with the consciousness that this judgment A speaks for all times, that is, is valid for ever. But what would this consciousness of ours be worth if we were wholly unable to return once more, by way of memory, to any impression of ours? The complete absence of memory would reduce our power of judgment too to nothing, for if under no circumstances we were able to return to what we have lived through, we could never put to the test that fundamental principle of logic according to which an enduring truth is contained in our judgment. The complete absence of memory would have the consequence that not only memory-judgments but even an original judgment

p. 102

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

we should be unable to form, for where memory is lacking, any impression, however powerful, occurs in vain. A being that possessed no memory at all would not be able even to constate the directly appearing impression, for it would let the impression drop at the very moment in which it wished to grasp it. As we should try in vain to fill with water a cask that has no bottom, so the mind too would try in vain to fix a just-appearing impression in a sentence if it had no power of remembering whatever. For during the little time in which the sentence would have to be formed, the impression to be constated would already be forgotten. Thus it is easy to see that not even a directly appearing impression can be constated if memory entirely fails us; for if it took only 1/10 of a second for the mind to complete the constating sentence, then at least during this tenth of a second the impression it desires to constate must not be forgotten. Whoever considers this well will not be put off by the seemingly paradoxical statement that the original judgment too cannot be formed without the help of memory. The original judgment too is a memory-judgment, and its exceptional position rests only on its being the first memory-judgment in the series of all our possible memory-judgments.

To put the meaning of this supremely important epistemological principle in its proper light, we must emphasize above all that every impression requires a duration, and that in a mathematical point of time no impression can subsist. In other words: what we call the now is not a mathematical point of time, but a duration with blurred limits. In ordinary life we make no distinction between the mathematical and the empirical now, whereas this distinction is of immeasurable importance for the whole of human knowledge. By the mathematical now I understand the absolutely sharp, timeless boundary of the past and the future; the empirical now, on the other hand, though it be an indefinite and very short duration, is at any rate a duration, which differs from zero. But if it is a duration, then in logic it falls under the same points of view as any other duration, for example a day, a year, a millennium, a million years, etc. For the theorist of knowledge, therefore, the empirical now is just as significant a magnitude as those millions of years of which geologists like to speak. Although this empirical now means only some fractional part of a second,

p. 103

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

it nevertheless cannot be confused with the mathematical point of time, which has no duration whatever, just as the mathematical point of space has no extension.

The frailty of our human condition is revealed above all in this: that the mathematical point of time, the absolute now, we can only think, but are unable actually to establish. It is curious that theorists of knowledge are not very fond of speaking of this matter, perhaps because they do not think it very worth while to waste words on it, since everybody knows it anyhow. Yet nothing avenges itself so much as when we fail to speak of our frailties — even if they are commonly known — and glide lightly over them. How much more so when it is a question of such a frailty as in the present case, which may be called a downright fundamental frailty. Man is not able to establish a perfectly rigorous now, and whoever lays this well to heart will at once divine the whole of our human wretchedness in the cognition of facts. For if there is no now established with perfect exactness, then there are no facts at all established with perfect exactness, and then exact experience in the strict sense of the word is an impossibility. With this we have pointed to the unspeakable torments of the natural sciences, which by making our measuring instruments and methods more exact seek somewhat to alleviate the original misery of all our human observations. In my earlier discussions I deliberately kept silent about this misery, for I had first of all to bring out that man can form constating sentences containing eternal truth. Now we must add the shadow to this bright side of the human spirit. And what darker shadow can fall upon the human mind than if we say that men do indeed speak of the “now,” but that no man has ever yet strictly established a now, nor ever will be able to? In this one helplessness of ours all our other helplessness is contained. Whoever attentively examines the history of skepticism and of sophistry can convince himself that every doubt and every fallacy that ever arose in the human brain is closely connected with this weakness of ours, that men cannot exercise perfect rigor in the establishment of facts, and what comes to the same thing, in the establishment of the point of time. Hence it is that the facts themselves, singly and collectively, seem to waver, as it were, and that

p. 104

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

precisely the champions of empirical knowledge (the empiricists) can most easily become the slaves of a doubt that undermines all knowledge. For from the fact that we are unable actually to establish a mathematical now, the empiricist may straightway infer also that the concept of the mathematical now is a mere chimera! Behold, such is the son of man. First he perceives that he is a frail creature who cannot establish an absolutely exact now; then he becomes so infatuated with this helplessness of his that he afterwards finds even the very thought of an absolutely rigorous point of time an absurdity. Thus original frailty turns into sinful despair. For we are able to notice our frailty at all only because the concept of perfection lives in us. That we cannot establish an absolutely exact now could strike us only because we are filled with the concept of the absolute now. Nor should we be capable of establishing any duration, even approximately, if we did not possess the concept of the mathematical point of time. Let anyone try to think of an arbitrary duration, for example a minute, and see whether it is possible for him to think of it otherwise than as confined between a beginning and an ending point of time. If the concept of the point of time is lacking, it is impossible to form the concept of duration; if we do not think of an absolute now, there can be no question of an empirical now either.

With this premised, we return to the question of the relation of original judgments and memory-judgments, namely to the elucidation of the proposition that the original judgment itself, which relates to the directly appearing impression, could not come into being without the support of memory. At first glance it may seem odd that we should need memory even when a directly present impression is to be constated; yet some reflection shows that this oddity is only apparent, and arises from our being very little accustomed to clarify the concepts of impression and memory-image. An impression — as we have just explained — does not occupy a place in a mathematical point of time (since to think an impression without duration would be an impossibility), but fills a certain duration, which we may in thought divide into any number of parts. In a formula we can express this thus: a = a1 + a2

ₙ + a3 + . . . + a , if we denote the impression by a, and by a1, a2, a3,

ₙ . . . a the parts of the impression succeeding one another in time. The circumstance that the impression is not instantaneous in character, that is, does not

p. 105

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

occur in a mathematical point of time, but is composed of parts succeeding one another in time, has the result that our concept of the impression is exceedingly confused, and that, in consequence, the concept of the memory-image assumes a still more mystical character.

Locke set up the theory that our impressions are simple, or rather, more precisely: that if we have complex impressions as well, these are composed of partial impressions which themselves possess the character of simplicity. Locke, to be sure, speaks of ideas instead of impressions, and divides ideas into simple and complex, but, as I have already mentioned, he uses the word idea in order to be able to confuse the impression with the knowledge (judgment, concept) relating to it. This is evident also from his calling simple ideas above all our impressions of hardness, softness, heat, cold, white, red, sweet . . . etc. He speaks, to be sure, of other simple ideas as well (such as judgment, knowledge, will, power, existence, unity, etc.), but with this too he only proves that he does not draw a proper distinction between impression and concept. But what concerns us here is only that he regards the impression of red, green . . . etc. as something simple, whereby he contributed greatly to the immeasurable confusion which reigns precisely in the impressionist theory of knowledge with regard to our impressions. For it is evident that every impression, already by the very fact that it has a certain duration, must be thought of as composed of infinitely many parts succeeding one another in time. The generally prevailing psychological conception is wont to insist rather on the intensity, quality and tone of our impressions; but the first thing characteristic of every impression is that it has a certain duration, so much so that the boundlessly mysterious nature of our impressions cannot even strike our eyes so long as we do not direct our attention to the fact that any impression, however uniform it may seem, is nevertheless formed of parts succeeding one another in time.

This requires a somewhat fuller explanation. If, for example, we direct our gaze at a red patch of color, we at once notice that the red patch consists of parts in space; but we are not accustomed to think that our impression (of this red patch of color) takes up a certain duration, let us say a second, during which time that impression, in an uninterrupted process,

p. 106

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

goes on. We are not at all accustomed to think that our impressions are of a continuous character, and this carelessness of ours is one of the chief causes of our arriving at wholly absurd concepts of the nature of our impressions: such, for example, as Locke’s concept that the impressions of red, warm, hard, etc. are supposed to be simple. If, for example, we look at the red patch of color mentioned above for only half a second, this half-second is sufficient for an impression of exactly the same quality to arise in us as if we had looked at the patch for a whole second. From this it evidently follows that the impression of the red patch, if we look at it for a longer time, continually repeats itself, renews itself, although we notice nothing of this repetition and renewal. If, for example, I suppose that the impression of red renewed itself ten times in me when I looked at the patch in question for half a second, then in a look of a whole second the impression of red will certainly repeat itself twenty times. This important and interesting subject belongs to the province of physiological investigations, and we concern ourselves with it here only so far as we can know, even without physiological experiment, that every impression, being of a continuous nature, is composed of segments succeeding one another in time, and therefore can on no account be simple.

26. But although we must think of every impression of ours as consisting of boundlessly many segments succeeding one another in time, we are nevertheless not able actually to divide an impression of ours into as many segments of time as we please. Suppose, for example, that we wished to divide an impression of ours, lasting one second, into a thousand segments of time; it is certain that we could in no way succeed, for one second is so short a time for us human beings that within its duration we are not able to become aware a thousand times of the process of impression that occupies us. If, for example, we look for one second at a red patch of color, we are not able within this time to think a thousand times that we are now looking at a red patch of color. Yet that impression of a second would be actually divided into a thousand segments of time only if within this second we could think a thousand times that we are under the impression of a red patch of color, or if at least in the meantime we could become aware a thousand times of any other impression whatever. It is by the acts of our awareness or consciousness that we divide the course of time into segments, and whoever succeeded in formulating a thousand thoughts within one

p. 107

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

second would have divided this second into a thousand parts. This proposition would in any case require a far stricter formulation, but for the present it is enough if we become clear that within one second we cannot become aware of as many impressions as we please, that is, cannot establish as many facts as we like. If I call sensible awareness our capacity of being able to take cognizance of sensible impressions, then I may say that our sensible awareness has a certain speed*), which we must regard as the greater, the more facts we are able to establish within a given time (say one second). It depends on the speed of our sensible awareness how many segments we can actually distinguish in an impression lasting one second. One of the most important tasks of physiology is to subject this speed, for every sensible sphere, to the most searching investigations, and at the same time to establish between what limits the speed of sensible awareness oscillates for different individuals, ages, moods, etc. For our purpose here it is entirely sufficient if we see that we may rightly speak of the speed, or if you like the slowness, of our sensible awareness. As in running, or in beating time with our hands, however much we may practice, we cannot pass beyond a certain limit of speed, so in the constatation of facts, however nimble our sensible awareness may be, we cannot surpass a certain speed. By this insight we have only grasped more precisely that original frailty of ours, that we cannot establish a mathematical now.

And now we see how important it is not to glide over what we call the general human frailty. Nothing may seem more superfluous than the statement that we cannot actually establish a mathematical now; and yet the theory of knowledge cannot boast of many propositions more significant than this. For if we cannot actually establish a mathematical point of time, there arises the remarkable question: what is the shortest time within which we are still able to constate an impression? This question could not even arise if we did not consciously direct our attention to that original frailty of ours,

*) It would perhaps be still more correct to speak of the “density” of our acts of awareness instead of their “speed.”

p. 108

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that we do indeed demand a mathematical point of time, but cannot actually grasp it. Here we have an opportunity of confirming the proposition we already stated in the Introduction, that it is precisely from the knowledge of our frailties that we can draw our most precious knowledge. Since we cannot grasp the mathematical point of time, we must ask which is the smallest duration that we can still grasp, or, more precisely, what is the shortest time within which we can become aware of an impression? It is true that the physiologist will be able to shape this question far more rigorously still, but for us here it is already a sufficiently great gain if we see how significant a concept for the theory of knowledge the speed of sensible awareness is. For the whole world of our sensible impressions would take on another look, another aspect, if we could become aware of an impression 10, 100, 1000 times faster than we actually become aware of it. If the speed of our sensible awareness grew greater and greater, then the impression which now seems uniform would lose its uniformity, and in its place a succession of impressions would appear which would show a certain qualitative variety. Let us only try for a moment to imagine that we could become aware of an impression a trillion or a quintillion times faster than now; then we shall easily understand that this would have at least as great an effect on our whole picture of the world as if we could move a trillion or a quintillion times faster than now. To divide a second actually into a trillion parts would mean to acquire a trillion conscious experiences within one second, that is, it would mean that within one second we should experience ten thousand times as much as we can experience in a whole lifetime with our present organization. If we possessed an organization that became aware of its impressions 10, 100, 1000 . . . times faster than we do of ours, then what now seems to us a uniform quality would appear to that organization as an ever more and more complex system of impressions, and it is evident that no quality seeming simple could withstand a sufficient acceleration of awareness.

Although present-day natural science has not yet formulated this proposition clearly, it nevertheless lies latent as a presentiment in all our natural-scientific investigations. When the physicist traces sounds back to waves of air, on whose number of vibrations the pitch of the sound depends, when he likewise regards light as undulatory motion

p. 109

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and again connects the difference of colors with the number of vibrations, there lies hidden in this and similar theories always only a single striving: how to gain a glimpse into those secrets which the trillionth parts of a second hold within them, and into which, owing to the “coarseness” of our sensory organs, we cannot directly penetrate. What in the natural sciences figures as the “coarseness” of our senses, we here call the “original frailty” of our sensible awareness, and it reveals itself in this, that we need a certain duration before we can become aware of an impression. What happens within this smallest duration “runs together” or “blurs” before our senses; into its details we cannot see with mere senses. Natural science, then, is properly waging its battles against the original frailty of our sensible awareness, and, consciously or not, it always strives to construct for us a picture of the world to which we could attain directly only by an acceleration of our sensible awareness. The theorist of knowledge strives for the same end, or rather it is he who makes more conscious this thirst for knowledge lurking in the natural sciences, just as the theory of knowledge in general exists to raise all our thirst for knowledge to the highest possible degree of consciousness.

27. If we have grasped the truth that every impression of ours — even if it took up only a fraction of a second — must be thought of as consisting of countless segments succeeding one another in time, then it is impossible that we should not be deeply struck by the boundless mysteriousness of our impressions. If we denote the segments constituting the impression a, as we did above, by a1 + a2 + a3 + a4 + . . . ad inf., then it is evident that the segment a1 has already passed away by the time the segment a2 appears; further, the segment a1 + a2 has passed away by the time the segment a3 makes its appearance, and so on. In a word, every impression of which we have already become aware consists wholly of past segments. If we keep this in view, we see at once that it is impossible to become aware of an impression without the aid of the faculty of memory. In every process of impression we must distinguish two opposed (antagonistic) strivings: the one is that which ever draws our attention toward newer and newer segments of the impression, or rather toward an ever new and new impression; the other, on the contrary, is that which ever withdraws our attention from newer and newer segments or impressions, and leads it back to the segments that have just

p. 110

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

passed. The one striving binds our attention there, glues it to what is around us and is happening around us; the other striving, on the contrary, frees our attention, releases it from the influence of the immediate surroundings, in order to lead it back to what has just occurred, and in this way to make possible the taking cognizance of the appearances that have just elapsed.

The fashionable science of psychology does not notice these two antagonistic currents in our processes of impression, and consequently cannot arrive at an intelligible conception of the relation of impression and memory-image. It cannot arrive at it because it loses sight of the fact that no impression of ours is simple, as Locke and his successors imagine, but each is something boundlessly composite: namely, the sum of segments succeeding one another in time. These segments, all vanishing into the past, would be lost to us if our awareness merely pursued, without pause, ever newer and newer segments of impression, and in this pursuit never got back to what has just passed, and so never remembered it, and thus never made possible the constatation of what has just now passed away. In a word, becoming aware of impressions would be a complete impossibility if our awareness, swept along by the successive segments of impressions, rushed continually toward the future and in this rush dropped all the preceding segments. An awareness that could only rush toward the future, continually losing the segments of impression just elapsed, could not even be called awareness, for it would be wholly incapable of constating an impression (snatched, as it were, from the flood of time). In order that this constatation may become possible, it is absolutely required that the segments of our impression that have just elapsed should not be lost without trace, but should be retained in memory. While our attention, then, is occupied with what is just happening around us and is just about to appear, the activity of our memory too cannot cease, for otherwise what has just occupied our senses would at once be lost in the ocean of forgetfulness.

If, then, we constate an impression while it is acting upon us, we constate it even so only with the help of memory. Our sensible awareness has, as it were, a pulse or a breath: upon the wave of the direct impression a wave of memory follows, and only when our life-process has such an interplay of waves are we able to constate an impression. While

p. 111

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

we contemplate the things around us, we are continually rocked upon the waves of the original impressions and of the memories that follow them, even though we are not able to observe this interplay of waves directly. Yet there is no doubt that every impression which we constate must necessarily be accompanied by a memory-wave which makes the constatation of that impression possible. That we cannot separate the wave of the original impression from the memory-wave that follows it has its cause in this, that our sensible activity of awareness has a certain speed, or rather slowness, which we cannot get beyond. The relation of the original impression and the memory-image can most easily be grasped by the analogy of the ray falling upon a mirror and the ray reflected from it. In order that we may see the image of a body in the mirror, it is necessary that the body should send rays of light upon the mirror, which are regularly reflected from its surface. The incident rays correspond to the wave of the original impression, the reflected rays to the memory-wave. This, however, is only an analogy, which seeks to make more intuitive the non-intuitable fact that upon the life-wave of the original impression the life-wave of remembering follows.

At the outset the reader will perhaps find it improbable that a directly appearing impression cannot be constated otherwise than by the help of a memory-wave following upon the original impression-wave; closer consideration, however, dispels the doubts that arise against the truth of this proposition. If, for example, I meet a friend whom I have not seen for some time, then, in recognizing his face and figure, a memory-image must necessarily have arisen in me; but I do not notice the arising of this memory-image, because it at once fuses with the original impression which the face and figure of my (present) friend produce in me. This example shows that the memory-image may fuse with the original impression in such a manner and so suddenly that we do not even notice the appearance of any memory-image.

In ordinary life, and even in the fashionable psychology, we speak of a memory-image only when the impression we wish to constate has long since passed, so that our organism is no longer directly under its influence. We, however, take a standpoint very far removed from this conception, for we hold it impossible that anyone should constate an impression, whether one that is passing away, or one that has just passed, or one long past, without relying, in this judgment of his,

p. 112

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

upon memory. In order that I may notice this paper which lies here before me, a memory-image of it must arise in me, even though I keep my gaze continually fixed on the paper, and even though it seems as if the paper’s continual presence, through the direct impression it makes upon me, would render all remembering superfluous. That we do not commonly think so comes from our being accustomed to form an altogether false concept of what we call an impression. The everyday supposition that when an impression is in progress no memory-image of it can arise is quite without foundation. Indeed we can say outright that everything that lies open before our senses is nothing other than a memory; only it is a memory which is kept up in us by directly acting impressions. But we have also memories which are nourished by impressions long past, and in ordinary life it is only these latter that we are accustomed to call memories; whereas we must regard as memories those images too which directly present objects awaken in us: memories, that is, of what has just passed, thus half, a quarter, or a tenth of a second ago. Only he fails to understand this who takes the now for a mathematical now. Yes, if we were actually able to establish a mathematical now, that is, if the impression needed absolutely no time at all to arise in us, then there would be no need for a memory of it to arise, for in that mathematical point of time in which the impression appeared we should already have noticed it. Our awareness would then possess infinite speed, for no time at all would be required in order to constate something. And if one constatation requires no time, then any number of constatations could take place in a mathematical point of time. In a word, an infinitely rapid awareness could constate infinitely many facts, the whole universe, in a timeless moment. But our human frailty reveals itself precisely in this, that we need time in order to constate anything, and it is rather a matter of indifference whether we call that time a tenth of a second or ten years. It is indifferent from our standpoint, for what has passed since a tenth of a second ago has passed just as truly as if it had passed since ten years ago. And if without recalling we cannot think of what happened ten years ago, then without recalling we cannot think either of what took place a tenth of a second ago. In a word, our human nature’s

p. 113

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

frailty does not permit us to notice something at that mathematical moment in which it would begin to appear; and if we are incapable of such infinitely rapid perception, then time must elapse until we perceive something, and then we need memory too in order to perceive anything at all. It is a necessary consequence of our human frailty that even present things we can perceive only as memories. And that physiology has not yet made this proposition its own comes from the fact that philosophers have not set forth properly what our human frailty consists in, namely the finite speed of our faculty of perception.

28. The conception we have just set out of the relation of memory and impression can be made somewhat clearer if we contrast it with the prevailing empiricist conceptions. The greatest influence on these conceptions was exercised by Hume, who distinguished impressions from memories (ideas) in this way: the former he called lively, while the latter would be characterized by a lesser degree of liveliness or force. Hume had an immeasurable influence on the host of philosophers, who never tired of repeating that between the original impression and the memory there is only a certain difference of degree. Thus already Condillac speaks, in his work on impressions, of “sentiment vif ” and “sentiment faible.” (Traité des sensations, ch. II, §§ 8 and 9.) The swarm of German psychologizing philosophers too took up the Humean distinction; thus, for example, according to Platner sensation and idea differ from one another by the degree of force, and on this basis our consciousness too distinguishes them. (Philos. Aphorismen, 1793, vol. I, § 131.) Beneke likewise says that the difference between sensible percepts and ideas is preponderantly a difference of degree. This distinctio then runs, in countless stylistic modifications, through the works on the theory of knowledge and psychology down to our own days. Thus Spencer too distinguishes “actual feeling,” that is, impression, from “ideal feeling,” that is, idea, by a greater degree of intensity.

A cardinal aberration in all these distinctions shows itself at once in their readily using the term “idea” (idea = Vorstellung) in place of memory. For the “idea” can easily slip over into the “concept,” inasmuch as Hume himself calls “thoughts” those ideas which differ from the lively original impressions merely by their faintness. The fundamental aberration of the impressionist theory of knowledge

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 8

p. 114

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

becomes glaring here, if anywhere. The memory-image, just like the impression, is taken by itself a passing life-process, which must be sharply distinguished from the eternally valid truth relating to it, which we express in a judgment formed of concepts. It therefore makes a very odd impression upon us when Hume, following Locke quite closely, speaks of the “wellspring” of our ideas (thoughts), as if one actually drew thoughts out of some well. With Hume thoughts differ from impressions only by their greater faintness; from which it is seen that in essence the thought with him is nothing other than an impression, indeed only some faint, diluted edition of the impression. Against this conception I have insisted from the beginning that the impression is indeed an indispensable condition of all our knowledge, but taken by itself is by no means knowledge (not a thought). We must now add also that, just as the impression, so too the memory is an indispensable condition of all our knowledge, remarking that the memory taken by itself is just as little knowledge as the original impression itself. Of knowledge there can be question only where someone is in possession of an eternally valid truth, where, that is, he relates his passing experiences, namely his passing memory-images, as well as his passing original impressions, to a reality that does not pass away. Only in such relatings can knowledge manifest itself, but impression and memory-image are only indispensable conditions of these relatings, not the relatings themselves. In order that we may relate to the eternal, we need passing experiences; but whoever confuses our knowledge with our passing experiences wholly misunderstands it.

Having thus properly brought out that remembering is just as indispensable a condition of all our knowledge as the original impression itself, we have only to add that the original impression taken by itself could not be taken cognizance of if the corresponding memory-image did not follow upon it at once. What we call in ordinary life an impression (impressio) is composed of two components: namely, the impression proper and the memory relating to it. We need, therefore, yet a third technical term, to name the totality of the impression-wave and the memory-wave immediately following upon it. For lack of a better word I shall call this totality “sensation” [érzéklet]. Every sensation, then, is composed of an impression-wave and a memory-wave following upon it. By this I mean that it is impossible to notice any

p. 115

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

impression-wave if it does not arouse in us a memory-wave; or, in other words, every impression noticed (even if it is directly present) stands before us as a memory. This memory, which is only one half-wave of the whole sensation, must be distinguished from the memory-image which in everyday life too we call a memory-image, and which arises when by means of it we relate to a sensation already past. We must therefore distinguish the memory which is the half-wave of a direct sensation from the memory with which we refer to a past sensation. The latter I shall call “memory-replica” [emlékmás].

Moreover, very many representatives of recent psychology, even though setting out from wholly opposite basic conceptions, approach, with more or less of presentiment, the view which I have just set forth concerning the relation of memory and impression. Thus Brentano sets up the following obscure proposition: To every impression (Empfindung) an idea (Einbildungsvorstellung) is attached, that is, the activity of the faculty of memory, which modifies it in time (Emp. Psychologie, vol. I, p. 177). Hans Cornelius, again, plainly declares that together with the impression the corresponding memory-image always appears in consciousness. (Versuch einer Theorie der Existentialurtheile, 1894, p. 34.) “If I hear a tone,” Cornelius explains, “then during the duration of this tone there appears not only the impression-content of the tone, but also the memory-image of the impression that has hitherto passed, and I call the tone constant if this memory-image agrees with the one which the impression now still continuing leaves behind at every moment . . . .” Cornelius’s work just cited came into my hands only while these lines were being written, and for my part I can only rejoice in the meeting of views, although it is evident that, since we both set out from different foundations, differences going to the root of the matter remain between our conceptions. Thus, to emphasize at once the chief difference of standpoint: I regard the impression, as well as the memory, as life-processes, and not as “psychological” processes, as the investigators belonging to the psychologistic school of the theory of knowledge do. In my conception only that has a mental character which lays claim to eternal validity, whereas that which passes away, like the impression or memory-image, can be called only a life-process, that is, is of a merely biological (physiological) character. To this difference of principle there is added the fact that Cornelius believes he finds a “qualitative” difference between memories and impressions, in opposition to the Humean

8*

p. 116

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

conception, which establishes between them only a difference of degree (of vividness, of intensity). The whole dispute that goes on among psychologistic thinkers, whether there is a difference of intensity or of quality between our impressions and our memories, is a veritable model of wholly aimless argument. For the prerequisite of anyone’s being able to draw a distinction between quality and intensity is that he be able to distinguish between impression and memory, that is, that he not confuse the constating judgment which he forms on the basis of the direct impression with the judgment which he makes relying on remembering. For if someone cannot establish whether what he constates is happening now or has already happened before, he possesses no faculty of judgment. As soon as someone judges, namely as soon as he forms a constating judgment, he must necessarily know that he asserts something that is happening now; but he must also know that truth requires that he assert the same thing whenever he may return, by way of his remembering, to the present event. In a word, he cannot possess a faculty of judgment who cannot distinguish between his impressions and his rememberings. But what the difference is between “quality” and “intensity” is very hard to say, for one man may take for a “qualitative” difference many things which another is inclined to regard as “intensive.” It is ridiculous, therefore, to make the difference between impression and memory depend on the difference of “quality” and “intensity”; whereas the difference between impression and memory is the condition of the very possibility of all judging, and therefore also the prerequisite of our being able to speak of such concepts as quality and intensity. Our thinking, then, stands on its head if we first wish to distinguish between quality and intensity and on this basis to distinguish impression and memory, whereas, quite the reverse, only he can possess a faculty of judgment who does not confuse his memory-replicas with his direct sensations; so that only he who can already distinguish these may take up such questions as what the difference is between quality and intensity.

We see, then, that we can most easily disturb human thinking by raising ill-posed questions. What is at issue here? This: that man can constate something when it is still present, and can constate it when it is no longer present, and when it can be returned to only by remembering. Since we can form two such kinds of constating judgment about one and the same fact, we speak accordingly of impressions, which are the basic conditions of the one

p. 117

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

constatation, and memory-images, which are the basic conditions of the other constatation. This general conception stands in need of a certain correction. Even our immediate impressions we could not constate without memory. This is a necessary consequence of our human frailty, by virtue of which we cannot actually fix a mathematical now, but need a certain duration of time until we become aware of something. People, to be sure, call this duration “very short,” but who knows whether there may not exist, outside ourselves, other beings who might hold this very short time to be fearfully long. And one who needs a duration of time to notice anything will be able to perceive even what presents itself immediately only as a memory. This proposition is what I call the principle of the unity of impression and memory.

This principle of unity, however, by no means excludes our seeing a complete oppositeness between the impression and the memory-process: an opposition such as, for example, that between the compression and the rebounding of an elastic body, the swing of a pendulum from left to right and from right to left, or the crest and the trough of a wave. We infer this oppositeness from the fact that while we are deeply absorbed in the perception of things immediately present, we are not able to live in our memories, and conversely, when our attention has glided from an immediate sensation to some memory-replica, the pursuit of these memory-replicas draws us away from the observation of things present. We can best make the opposition of the two currents intuitable if we regard the impression as a process starting from the surface (the periphery) of the organism and moving toward the brain, and the memory, conversely, as a process starting from the brain and directed toward the surface. If an excitation has spread from the surface part of the nervous system toward the brain, it then returns (as if reflected back from there) again to the surface. The first current corresponds to the impression, the counter-current to the memory. But when a person is absorbed in the contemplation of memory-replicas, then the current, starting from the center, moves toward the surface (toward the retina) and, as if reflected back from there, returns to the center. The following figure (see page 118) renders these relations perceptible.

Here I designate by f that point of the nervous system from which the surface excitation, and by k that from which the central excitation, proceeds. When it is a question of an immediately present sensation, the excitation proceeds from f and, arriving at k, returns thence again to f, as

p. 118

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the outer arrows of the circuit show. But when our mind is occupied with memory-replicas, then the excitation, being central, proceeds from k, in order to arrive at f and return thence again to k, as the inner arrows of the circuit indicate. Our memory-replicas are as a rule pale and wavering because the central excitations are unable to cope with those surface excitations that unceasingly besiege the brain. In sleep, however — since the surface excitations are for the most part disconnected — the central excitations can assert themselves without resistance, so that our dream-images may rival immediate impressions in vividness. It is perhaps even superfluous for me to stress especially that the figure above is meant to offer a purely schematic picture of the relation of impression and memory, and that no hasty conclusions may be drawn from it concerning the actual working of the brain.

I wish here only to emphasize that in physiology, or rather in “psychophysics,” a one-sided conception as a rule prevails of what is called an impression. For people believe that it is an excitation which spreads from the surface toward the center, whereas, corresponding to the memory-wave, it consists also of a central excitation which spreads toward the surface. Equally one-sided is the conception which regards remembering only as a central excitation spreading toward the surface, whereas this central excitation has also aroused an excitation on the surface, which in turn returns to the center.

29. Since every impression must be thought of as composed of boundlessly many phases, it is evident that every impression must be inexhaustible for cognition. Of our impressions we properly know only as much as the memories relating to them permit us to cognize: for we have just explained that even the image that stands before us under the influence of an immediate impression

p. 119

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

stands before us is to be regarded only as a memory-image. If the speed of our sensible awareness, that is, of our perception, were greater than it is, then in the course of a second more impression-waves, and correspondingly more memory-waves, could arise in us, and so we should have more percepts and more judgments about what now gives occasion only to a single impression and memory, and to a single perception and judgment. But even then we should not be able to exhaust an impression to the end, since for that we should need an infinitely rapid perception. We may also express this by saying that our constating judgment can never replace the impression itself, for in order to replace it we should have to possess an infinitely rapid awareness, corresponding to the infinitely many phases of the impression. It is true, however, that an infinitely rapid awareness has need of neither impression nor memory in order to know something, and that only an awareness of finite speed stands in need of impressions, and consequently of memories as well, in order to cognize anything.

But if we need impressions in order to be able to appeal to them by a judgment, we must not fall into the fancy that we could make up for our impressions by our judgments. Every impression is irreplaceable, for its content could be made up for only by infinitely many judgments, or rather, by an infinitely rapid awareness. But it is not at all the vocation of the constating judgment to make up completely for the immediate impression. Our sensible experiences we can never exhaust by judgments, and so what immediate experience can offer can by no means be completely made up for by sentences, by words. This truth of empiricism we gladly acknowledge. If the empiricists taught nothing more than that what the immediate impression can offer cannot be made up for by constating judgments (sentences, words), it would never occur to us to take issue with them. But they teach also, with more or less concealment, that the impression can make up for the judgment, indeed that the judgment is nothing other than an impression. Against this we must now take a decided stand, for neither the impression nor the judgment is destined to make up for the other. By the judgment we refer the passing impression to the reality that does not pass away; thus the impression, taken by itself, can never make up for the constating judgment relating to it.

Conversely, of every impression we can say in our judgment only as much as our memory is able to call up from it. For

p. 120

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

even when the impression is called “present,” it too stands before us only as a memory-image. Only we are then in the agreeable position of being able to refresh ourselves with ever new impressions. If, for example, I look at the red ball that lies here on the table and say of it that it is red, then a memory-wave has followed upon the wave of the impression, on the basis of which I was able to form this sentence: that this ball is red. But owing to the presence of the ball I am in the advantageous position of being able to return at once to the intuition of it, and under the continual influence of the impression the image of the ball can hover before me lastingly; whereas if the ball were no longer here and I could say only from memory that it is red, then my memory-image would not be supported and would not be maintained for any length of time by the immediate impression. The difference between the judgment based on the immediate impression and that based on remembering can therefore be explained by the following comparison. One who constates something relying only on his memory may be likened to a man who has drawn water from a spring with his jug, but has then gone away from the spring, so that for the time being he cannot draw from it anew. But one who constates the present impression stays by the spring, so that he can, if need be, fill his jug again. Yet he too is not relieved of the labor of drawing, and from the spring there is always at his disposal only as much as he draws from it. The spring is not his, but what he scoops out of it. Likewise from the immediate impression we can constate only as much as stands before us as a memory-image. Every sensible impression may therefore be regarded as an inexhaustible ocean on whose shore we stand in order to draw from it a thimbleful of memory, but which we can never exhaust to the end.

9. The Moment of Cognition (Noticing).

30. A being which, during the whole time of its waking state, were not able for a single moment to tear itself away from the impressions of its immediate surroundings and to return to the memories of the past could not possess awareness, that is, consciousness. This proposition is a strict consequence of what we have established about the nature of judgment. For awareness, that is, consciousness, belongs only to a being that can constate its impressions and in consequence sees that the impression, to be sure, passes away, but that the truth that this impression once existed will never

p. 121

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

pass away. This, however, can be seen only by a being that is able to tear itself away from the impressions of its surroundings and to return to some experience of its past, in order to constate anew, on the basis of its memory, what it once already constated on the basis of an immediate impression. For when we constate something on the basis of an immediate impression, we constate it for all times, that is, we perceive it with the consciousness that if at any time in the course of the ages the memory of this impression should arise, we should affirm of it the same that we affirmed under the influence of the immediate impression. We know, to be sure, that we are forgetful and that our memory may lead us into error concerning the experiences of our past; we know, further, that we may have any number of experiences to which we shall perhaps never return in our remembering; we know, finally, that the span of our life is limited and that the time will come when our impressions and memories (at least in the sense in which we have them now) will no longer exist at all: but in spite of all this we clearly see that if we remembered correctly, and if we were able to look back after billions of years upon what is now happening to us, then after billions of years too we should still adhere to those constating judgments of whose truth we are convinced. We have already explained sufficiently that there can be no question of constatation where there is no consciousness that this constatation holds for all times; and so it is evident that such a consciousness cannot exist without the possibility of remembering back to past experiences. It is therefore also an evident truth that one who is, as it were, chained to the impressions of his surroundings, so that he can never become free from their influence, cannot constate a single impression either, that is, not the faintest spark of awareness or consciousness can flicker in him.

Only one who has seen this can draw the proper distinction between life and knowledge. A being may have sensations just like those we possess, or, if you please, finer and stronger sensations than ours, and yet not the slightest trace of consciousness need lurk in this being: if that being could never for a single moment tear itself away from the sensations forced upon it by its immediate surroundings. For the elementary condition of awareness or consciousness is that we be able to tear ourselves away from what surrounds us, and to occupy ourselves with the memory of what does not surround us. For only if we thus, from remembering, constate anew what we once already, on the basis of an immediate impression, constated

p. 122

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

can we awaken to the consciousness that we adhere to the truth of what once happened to us.

If a being were unable to tear itself away from the sensations of its surroundings, it might indeed live, but, as I say, it could not possess consciousness. In such a being a wave of impression might be followed by the memory-wave relating to it, and this again by a new impression and a new memory-wave: and all this alternating double wave would change nothing in the lack of awareness of the being in question. Passing experiences by themselves form no judgment, and therefore cannot form consciousness either. We now know of these passing experiences that they are double experiences, inasmuch as to every impression-wave there belongs the memory-wave that follows it: but such a double experience is so fused together, that is, the impression is so covered by the memory-wave that follows it, that together they form only one experience, one sensation. And a being possessing only such sensations could indeed have life, but not awareness or consciousness. Only a being whose memories can detach themselves from the impressions to which they relate can possess consciousness. We human beings possess not merely sensations, that is, are not merely rocked on impressions and the memory-waves relating to them, but we also command memory-replicas which have detached themselves from the original impressions, that is, which appear independently, without the original impression to which they relate being present to us. How such a detached memory, relating to the impression-wave of a past sensation, can appear is in any case a profound mystery: but we are not now concerned with the mysteriousness of this how. We emphasize only that without memory-replicas relating to the impression-waves of past sensations we could not possess consciousness.

From this, however, the false conclusion must not be drawn that we possess consciousness only when we relate by means of memory-replicas to past sensations, and that under the influence of our immediate sensations we should not possess consciousness at all. Whoever interpreted our words in this way would wholly misunderstand our foregoing discussion. What we did say is that a being which possessed sensations (impression + memory-wave), but not detached memory-replicas, could not possess awareness either. But a being such as man, who possesses not only sensations but detached memory-replicas as well, is for that very reason capable of the activity of awareness not only under the influence of its memory-replicas, but also

p. 123

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

under the influence of its immediate sensations. For if, under the influence of its immediate sensation, it did not become aware of its impression, it would not be able, under the influence of the memory-replica, to remember back to its past impression either.

On the basis of these discussions we can distinguish the awareness bound to sensations from the awareness bound to detached memory-replicas. The two kinds of awareness could not exist without each other. If we did not become aware of our immediate sensation, we could not become aware of our past experiences with the help of memory-replicas. This elementary proposition was already recognized by the impressionist theorists of knowledge too, but they are not accustomed to express it clearly. The converse of the proposition, however, they do not proclaim at all; yet it is precisely this converse that puts the nature of our human awareness in its proper light. We must state that if we had no memory-replicas appearing detached,* with the help of which we can become aware of our past experiences, then we should not be able to become aware of our present impressions either, that is, we should possess no consciousness at all. Memories must become independent in order that we may be conscious beings. In order, therefore, to possess awareness at all, we must possess at once a double awareness: 1. we must become aware of immediate impressions, 2. we must relate to past experiences by means of memory-replicas. Our awareness I shall call sensuous insofar as it is exercised within our sensations, that is, when we observe our surroundings and perceive present phenomena. Musing awareness, however, I call our consciousness when we relate not to present sensations but, by means of memory-replicas, to past experiences. Sensuous and musing awareness could not exist without each other, for it is one and the same awareness that now observes and perceives the present phenomena and that wanders off from here to the impressions of the past.

31. Since the impression and the memory-wave that follows it do not yet form a judgment, the question arises what must still be added to them in order that judgment, and with it consciousness, may come into being. In our theory of judgment the answer to this question is already contained: memory and impression must relate to some eternal reality. Judgment therefore does nothing but add to the impression and the memory, in a word, to the passing experience, its eternal validity. This is what neither the impressionist nor the rationalist theory of knowledge

* “Detachment” throughout this whole text means separation from the original impression.

p. 124

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

recognizes, indeed it seems that it is perhaps not even able to recognize it. For the rationalist is not at all inclined to ascribe eternal truth to the constating judgment: thus he cannot arrive at a correct conception of this fundamental class of judgments and of sensuous awareness. The impressionist theorist of knowledge, on the other hand, regards the sum of impression and memory-wave as already a judgment, and does not see why anything further need be added to them that would make them a judgment. But if he did suppose that something further might be added to the impression and the memory, which raises them to a judgment, he would demand above all that he be able to sense this addition itself separately. In a word, the impressionist thinker demands that at the moment when he judges he sense that he is judging. So completely is he the slave of his sensations that he would acknowledge even his act of judgment only if he had a sensation of judging.

Now the moment in which we become aware of our impression is signaled by no new impression or feeling; for we may indeed feel delight in the fact that we notice something, that we become aware of something: but this delight is by no means the noticing itself, the act of taking cognizance. In general we cannot exchange the delight that accompanies our knowledge for knowledge itself. Since, in this way, no sensation and no feeling announces to us that the moment of cognition has now arrived, this moment is, so to speak, lost for the rusted sensualist, at least to the extent that he does not acknowledge any such point of time. It is true, indeed, that it is an open self-contradiction if we acknowledge some impression of ours that presents itself, but do not acknowledge the act of acknowledging; but the sensualist would be inclined to acknowledge the moment of acknowledgment only if it made some impression on him, if it stood before him as some feeling. But that remarkable moment — precisely because it is the moment of cognition — cannot present itself as a new impression or feeling that is added to the perceived impression. For if, at the moment of noticing, to a sensation a we had to add yet a sensation b, then by this sensation b we should falsify the sensation a. Cognition cannot consist in our adding to our sensation, of our own accord, yet another sensation, for then we should have to call cognition not knowing but falsification. But what an absurdity it is, moreover, to want to cognize a sensation a by adding on our part a sensation b

p. 125

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

to it; for then to the sensation b we should have to add a new sensation c, in order to become aware of b. Likewise we should have to supplement the sensation c by a sensation d, this by a sensation e, and so on to infinity. From which it appears that we could never arrive at cognition if cognition consisted in our adding, of our own accord, some further sensation to a sensation.

By this consideration we have put in its proper light the famous doctrine of Locke, according to which, besides the outer sense, we possess also some inner sense. This exceedingly obscure theory has exerted an immeasurable influence on the whole of more recent philosophizing, for it caused the ascendancy of that psychologistic current which strives to drown logic in psychology. The inner sense is supposed to be destined to give us notice of the self-activity of our soul, and therefore of cognition too. The moment of cognition would be signaled by an inner feeling! Behold where the sensualist, who wants at any price to feel everything, is brought by this passion of his. He either calls the outer sensation a already in itself cognition, or, if in his great distress he nevertheless wishes to make a distinction between sensation and cognition, he invents an additional inner sensation b to join the outer sensation a, which would give us news of our cognizing activity. That to this inner sensation b a still more inner sensation c would then have to be added by invention, to this one a still more inner one, and that this would have to be continued thus to infinity, this theory no longer takes into account.

But if there is a moment which we may call the moment of cognition, the sensualist may ask us when this moment falls and what it is that fills the duration of this moment. These tricky questions are not, to be sure, usually put with such definiteness, but they nevertheless lurk in the theories of the theorists of knowledge.
