They are tricky because, if the moment of cognition really had a certain duration, however short, let us say 1/1000 of a second, the question would arise what fills this short interval, and then nothing would remain but to place in this short interval a passing experience, a sensation or a feeling: and then the sensualist would be right who proclaims that the act of cognition itself appears as a sensation, let us say as an “inner sensation.” Against this, however,

p. 126

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

I ask, who has ever noticed that, upon becoming aware of his surroundings, there stood before him, besides the impressions which the surroundings aroused in him, yet another impression at precisely the moment when he became aware of the surroundings? Such an additional impression, signaling the moment of cognition itself, can count only as a fairy tale: for the organism has no little bell which by its ringing would signal that now, just now, cognition has taken place. The Lockean “inner sense” would be a kind of little bell, which would perform an inner tinkling every time a man becomes aware of something. The logical contradiction that lurks in such a way of thinking I have already pointed out above, for that inner tinkling would itself be only an impression, of which one would have to become aware by a further inner tinkling, and so on to infinity. But setting aside this logical impossibility, I ask who has ever perceived that whenever his mind flashes and he has cognized something, some inner tinkling, or any sensible sign whatever, signaled this. No, no such sensible signal of the moments of cognition exists. Those moments of cognition do not even possess duration, but are to be regarded as mathematical points of time.

On this rests the immeasurable mysteriousness of the moment of cognition. In that mathematical point of time in which we become aware of something, no sensation or feeling can be present, for such passing experiences need duration, if only a brief one. Cognition, however, is an eternal experience, which takes place in a timeless moment, and which does not extend the impression and memory by so much as a trillionth part of a second. This makes intelligible and excusable that error of the sensualists by which they see cognition exhausted in the impression and the memory, in a word in sensation; and at most they invent yet an inner sensation to join the outer one, in order to distinguish cognition from sensation. They are right to this extent, that the flowing time can be filled for us by nothing other than impression and memory. Before our spirit — so long as we are in the waking state — the veil of cloud of sensations draws ceaselessly by. And this veil of cloud is never rent during the whole span of our life: that is, there never arises in it a gap through which the cognizing I itself would stand directly before us. A perpetual sensible overcast covers our soul, as it were, and there is no duration so slight,

p. 127

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that this overcast would be torn apart and through that slight time the sun of the cognizing spirit itself could shine through. Only in timeless (mathematical) moments does the light of cognition radiate through the veil of cloud of our sensations, yet without that sensible overcast which we call our world ceasing, even for the briefest while. So it is easy to understand that the sensualists acknowledge nothing but sensations. Their standpoint — let us admit it — is the most comfortable possible. They can appeal to the fact that so long as a man possesses awareness at all, the stream of impressions and memories does not cease, so that not the smallest duration occurs in which the series of sensations would be interrupted and, in the gap thus arisen, the spirit itself would appear in its eternal radiance. And since the cognizing spirit itself does not appear unveiled even for so slight a duration, it is easy to proclaim that in truth this sensible veil is cognition. It is easy to say that here are the impressions, here are the memory-images, and that nothing else is required for cognition besides them, that is, that the impressions and the memory-images make up cognition itself. It is easy to demand that one who is not of this sensualist opinion be so good as to show, show perceptibly, what can still be added to the impression and the memory that raises it to cognition. But a sober thinker cannot be lured into the trap of such a demand. Only the sensualists themselves deceive themselves with their demand: only they themselves speak of an inner sensation that would give news of the cognizing activity of the soul. It is they who become dualists, by doubling our sensation (as outer and inner sensation), in order in this way to make some distinction between sensation and cognition after all. Monistic thinking knows no such game, and cannot undertake to invent non-existent (inner) sensations. Respect factual truths: this, so to speak, is the first moral principle of monistic theory of knowledge; and from it follows that to the facts no invented fact may be added, just as no fact may be denied away from them. But the sensualist, who is proud of his impressions, that is, of the perceptible facts, falls most easily into the supposed fact of some non-existent inner sensations, because he involuntarily feels that the moment of cognition would have to be distinguished from sensation. Only this distinction must not come about by a falsification of the facts, so that we supplement our outer sensations with inner ones. The monist must adhere

p. 128

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

to the view that what takes place in time cannot be for us anything other than impression and memory, and that during the whole duration of our awareness, up to the last moment of our earthly existence, there is not a single brief instant in which the cloud-cover of our impressions and memories would be broken. Only in timeless mathematical moments does the light of the spirit break through, in order to draw our whole sensible world into the radiance of cognition.

But neither can we side with the rationalists, for they imagine that the human mind, when it sinks into itself, can abstract from all sensible impressions and memory-images, so that for a certain duration the stream of our impressions and memories can be interrupted, and the sun of the spirit itself would appear, through the duration of this gap in time, without sensible veiling, and that, looking into this sun, we could recognize truths which are wholly independent of our sensible experience (truths of an a priori character!). It is superfluous to expound at length that one who could look thus directly into the sun of truth would not be a man. It is superfluous to point out that a dualistic mode of thinking manifests itself in this rationalist theory, for it establishes an opposition between the working of sensuous and of non-sensuous awareness. I wish only to sharpen the point that dualism is the common characteristic of the sensualist and the rationalist mode of thinking. The sensualist stumbles into dualism because he wishes to avoid all “metaphysics” and wants to make the moment of cognition too perceptible; the rationalist, on the contrary, gets entangled in the morass of dualism because he wants to be a “metaphysician” in a way in which we, as men, cannot be metaphysicians. For if we could free ourselves from our sensuousness even for a single second, this would mean that our awareness had acquired infinite speed, that is, that we should cease to be men.

We must remark here that it is only an apparent contradiction when we say that the moment of cognition is a timeless mathematical moment, and that in the preceding chapters we explained that, owing to our human frailty, we need a certain duration of time for cognition. For this duration is wholly taken up by the impression and the memory, whereas cognition itself requires absolutely no time, since it is a durationless, that is, mathematical, point of time. From this we see that we may regard the impression and the memory also as hindrances to our awareness,

p. 129

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that is, its retarders, which likewise does not contradict our proposition that the impression and the memory are conditions of existence of human knowledge, that is, that it is they that make it possible for us to possess knowledge. Just as our motion would be impossible without friction, so that friction is at once a condition of existence and a retarder of our motion, in the same way we could not become aware of anything without sensations, even though precisely these sensations are to be regarded as the drag on our awareness, that is, precisely these sensations bring it about that our awareness has a certain finite speed (or, better put, slowness).

32. Since cognition takes place in a mathematical point of time, nothing perceptible is added by it to our impressions and memories. What we add to our sensations is nothing other than their eternal validity. Strictly speaking, we cannot even call this an addition, for what procures eternal validity for our sensations is already in our sensations, as their eternal reality. As the fundamental principle of logic we have stated that in our passing impressions an imperishable reality (essence) appears. This eternal essence we find in our impressions when we become aware of them. Therefore the word finding expresses what takes place in the moment of cognition better than the word adding. We find the eternal, which appears in our sensations: we find it by placing our own eternal I into our sensations. Cognition is in this way nothing other than the union of that eternally existing which appears in the impressions with that eternally existing which we call our I, as we shall expound more fully later.

By cognition, however, no change whatever befalls our sensation. For if the slightest alteration or modification took place through cognition, this would mean that the cognition itself is mixed into our sensation as a new sensation, in order to alter and falsify it. What we have expressed here might also be called the objectiveness (objectivity) of human cognition. Human knowledge is of an objective character insofar as it makes not the slightest alteration in the impression which it constates; for even the eternal validity it inserts only because it sees in it the appearance of an eternal reality. Unfortunately, however, the word “objective” is so greatly abused in philosophy that it has become an almost useless technical term. Every cognition, finally, may also be called subjective, for it is the intellectual act of some

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 9

p. 130

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

subject (person). In general one can hardly do anything any longer with the expressions “subjective” and “objective,” and one may take it as almost certain that the philosopher who throws these words about too freely is, knowingly or unknowingly, the spokesman of some sophistry. In the case of a cognition or a judgment one must not ask at all whether it is objective or subjective, but only whether it is true or false. But the theorists of knowledge very often did not dare to look the question of truth and falsity openly in the face, for which reason they preferred to speak of the objectivity and subjectivity of our knowledge. For a secret doubt gnaws at their souls, that man is perhaps not at all capable of cognizing the true and the false, and that it may therefore be more expedient to evade these dangerous words and to replace them by the mild words “objective” and “subjective.” This softening procedure is very convenient already because we cannot say of one and the same judgment that it is false and at the same time true; but we may boldly attribute to every judgment that it is at once subjective and objective: subjective because it is the intellectual act of a subject, and objective because, finally, every judgment must relate to something, which we may call the object of the judgment. If, then, we slip unnoticed the standpoints of the objective and the subjective into the place of the standpoints of the true and the false, then in the place of the incompatible truth and falsity there can step the nicely compatible objectivity and subjectivity, so that every sophistry can penetrate through open doors and windows into epistemological inquiry. If, however, someone insists at all costs on the words “subjective” and “objective,” then it is most expedient to call our passing experiences (impression, memory) subjective, but to call objective that which is eternal and manifests itself through our passing experiences. We must call the moment of cognition too objective, which is directed to the eternal reality manifesting itself in the passing appearances.

In this sense I say that in all our cognition we display an objective attitude, and that our whole human dignity rests on this admirable capacity of the objective (faithful to truth) attitude. By the objective moment of cognition we discover the eternal in the transitory, and for that very reason ascribe to it eternal validity too.

It will perhaps be appropriate to point out here the opposition of principle which

p. 131

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

separates our conception just expounded from Kant’s. Only one who sees above all that he regards all our cognition as fused together from two kinds of elements (factors) can truly penetrate Kant’s theory of knowledge: 1. an element given by experience, the impression (Empfindung), which he calls the matter of cognition, and 2. an element which the mind adds from itself and which he calls the form of cognition. This conception has for more than a hundred years exerted so incredibly deep an effect on minds that we meet it at every step among thinkers of the most various tendencies, who are otherwise opposed to Kant’s philosophy. Even among materialist thinkers we come upon statements to the effect that cognition must consist of something that is given from outside and of something that the human organism adds from its own. It is all one, they are accustomed to say, how anyone names the objective element given from without and the subjective element contributed from within: the main thing is that all cognition fuses together from two such elements. This is the theory of so-called “psychical chemistry.” For high-sounding and attractive words always offer themselves as soon as some logical absurdity has to be expressed.

In truth even Locke’s theory already recalls such a psychical chemistry. To the outer sensation, as the objective element, there is added in his theory the inner sensation as the subjective element. It is peculiar that Kant takes over this dualistic theory, but supplements it by yet another: the doctrine of the matter and form of cognition. The mind holds certain forms ready from the outset, into which the matter of cognition (the impression) pours itself, as molten ore into the form, held in readiness, of the statue that is to be cast. It can be seen that this Kantian theory marks a great advance in point of subtlety over Locke’s. For Locke adds to the outer sensation, which is given from without, once more only a sensation — and an inner one at that — as a subjective addition; Kant, on the other hand, combines the sensation originating from without with a “form” originating from the mind, and so couples the outer sensation with something which is not itself sensation but “form.” This is the fundamental proposition of Kant’s transcendentalism. But if Kant’s theory is considerably subtler than Locke’s, it can hardly be called clearer on that account: to my knowledge, at least, no philosopher has ever yet appeared who could say clearly what those “forms” are with which Kant imagined the human mind

9*

p. 132

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

to be populated from the beginning. Of the mystical nature of the Kantian forms we shall have more to say later; here I wish only to bring out especially two points from which the Kantian theory of forms is not usually considered: 1. Whatever we may understand by forms, this much is certain, that by their addition to the sensations they falsify our knowledge. For according to Kant the forms lurk neither in our impressions nor in the things themselves (Dinge an sich) which arouse the impression in us. The forms originate in the mind and represent a wholly foreign addition as against the impressions, so that these would originally be formless, and only the human mind would foist upon them certain forms that do not belong to their essence. It is evident that the mind would in this way commit systematic violence and falsification upon its impressions. Kant, however, was able to lend this peculiar procedure of the mind so extraordinarily interesting a color that by this very means he succeeded in bewitching minds. He argued, namely, that in our knowledge it is not the mind that conforms to the objects, but conversely the objects that conform to our mind, or rather to the original forms of our mind. This fundamental thought of his he himself compared with that of Copernicus. After Copernicus, he says, had seen that the explanation of the celestial motions does not succeed if we suppose that the host of stars revolves about the spectator, he tried whether the explanation would not have more effect if he regarded the spectator as revolving and left the stars at rest. Similarly, if we assume that our cognition conforms to the objects, it would remain unintelligible how we can have a priori cognitions of the objects; nothing therefore remains but, after the manner of Copernicus, to reverse the supposition and to start from the position that the whole world of phenomena conforms to the original forms of our mind, that is, that our mind forms the picture of the world into what we see it to be, because the impressions taken by themselves would be formless. This ingenious thought has greatly bewitched minds, but whoever has in view not ingenuity but truth will, I believe, see that all the activity of the mind would be mere violence and falsification if it foisted its forms, which are rooted neither in the impressions nor in the things themselves, upon the impressions. On a cube, for example, we do not foist the form of a cube, and on an octahedron we do not force the form of an octahedron. It would also be unintelligible why

p. 133

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

we should endow one body with this shape and another with that, if our impressions contained no motive whatever for it. Against Kant’s doctrine we must therefore emphasize that the task of the mind is not at all to make alteration, that is, falsification, in our impressions, but that its highest endeavor is rather to acknowledge faithfully what it finds in its impressions. In this striving for truth lies the one merit of the human mind, and if we deny it this merit, then it is not worth while to say so much as a word about the human mind. It may indeed flatter our vanity to hear that our mind harbors within itself certain mystical forms which bring forth a picture of the world out of formless impressions; for in this way we can imagine ourselves demigods, since it would be the forms of our mind that brought forth (or perhaps even created) the world: but I believe that sober thinking does not aspire to so dubious a glory. When a man cognizes something, he can place his human dignity solely in this, that he strives to add nothing to his impressions and to acknowledge in them only what can be acknowledged for all eternity. The human mind has no other glory besides this one, that it strives for truth. If a man wishes to create a world for himself, he may do so as a man of practical action or of artistic inspiration: but when it is a question of the cognition of facts, there lurks a most dangerous vanity in foisting upon our impressions some alleged “forms of reason” with which those impressions, taken in themselves, would have nothing to do.

2. If we compose cognition of two elements, forms and impressions, then we have made the impressions components of knowledge, and hence knowledge. But the impression taken by itself is not knowledge and not a component of knowledge, but a life-phenomenon, and as such a condition of existence of our knowledge. If anyone should still be inclined to exchange his impressions for his knowledge, let him think of those familiar discs which consist of sectors of different colors and, mounted on a whirling apparatus, are rotated in order thus to serve for the demonstration of the laws of color mixture. If the green follows the impression of the red color quickly enough, then both melt into each other, so that in the end a mixed color, white (gray), results in place of the two colors. Now I ask, can one do with two truths the same thing as with two colors

p. 134

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and spin them so fast that they vanish and by their mixture bring forth a new, a third truth? Surely not. Two truths we may indeed often combine, but for all that each of the two truths preserves unchanged its separateness, and one cannot mingle with the other, for otherwise each would cease to be a truth. One who exchanges his impressions for his knowledge confuses the passing with the eternally valid, that is, he misses that fundamental distinction which is the basis of all logical thinking. 10. The Twofold Articulation of the Sentence.

33. Becoming aware of our sensations takes place, as I have said, in mathematical points of time. But in order that these moments of cognition may not be lost, it is necessary that the mind rouse itself to an act by which it, as it were, erects a memorial column to the moment of cognition. This sign-forming act of the mind we call a sentence. If, then, the sensualist demands some sensible manifestation of that remarkable moment which is the point of time of cognition, we can now point to the sentence with which we constate the impression just presenting itself. The constating sentence signals in a perceptible manner the moment in which we become aware of some impression. And because the experience on which the sentence rests is double, the articulation of the sentence in which we give an account of our sensation is double too. In keeping with the impression and the memory-wave that follows it, or, if you like, with the original sensation and the memory-replica relating to it, the constating sentence itself shows a twofold articulation, that is, it is composed of two concepts, which stand to each other in the relation of subject and predicate.

In brief, the duality of the experience corresponds to the twofold articulation of the judgment relating to it. With this important proposition we take a further step forward in the understanding of the nature of judgment. Our first and most important step was that we recognized that to judge (to constate) means to refer the passing impression to an imperishable reality, that is, to refer the sensation to something non-sensible. By comparing more closely, however, the immediate constatation with the constatation from memory, a new light has fallen on the nature of our sensations, insofar as we discovered

p. 135

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that our sensations are double experiences, because they are composed of an impression-wave and a memory-wave. In accordance with this, new light falls also on the structure of our sentences, for their subject-signs and predicate-signs correspond to the twofold character of the sensation.

It is peculiar that Greek philosophy, although it recognizes it as necessary that the sentence consist of a subject-concept and a predicate-concept, nevertheless did not marvel at this necessity, but regarded it as a self-evident fact, about which it did not think it needful to ponder further. We moderns, on the other hand, stand in a very strange relation to that elementary truth that the judgment must consist of subject and predicate. For already in our early childhood we arrive at the knowledge of this truth through grammatical instruction, so that we see hardly anything in it but a grammatical rule, and because we are accustomed to regard grammar as a collection of conventional or formal knowledge, we have also lost the habit of falling into reflection over any such rules of grammar. But the theorists of knowledge too take the fact of the twofold articulation of the sentence very lightly, and even if they do not say that it is a “merely grammatical” fact, they do not proclaim that some cardinal principle of logic manifests itself in it. In this way it became a habit of thousands of years to pass indifferently by a truth with which few other truths can compete in significance. I bring this up because here a very interesting example offers itself of the fact that what we are accustomed to call increasing culture is often hardly anything but a dulling of the mind toward what is of fundamental importance and, lying nearest to us, would deserve the most thorough attention.

There is not, and never will be, a more significant fact in the intellectual life of mankind than this, that we form sentences, and that these sentences show a certain articulation. We therefore sin against the dignity of our human mind if we pass with indifferent soul by the marvelous facts mentioned, the like of which we can experience nowhere in heaven or on earth. The sentence which we utter, though a mere series of fleeting sounds, is nevertheless, among all the natural phenomena known to us, the most interesting, the most mysterious. But we must leave it to the poet to give the human word its worthy glorification: we do not undertake here the chanting of hymns, but only one boundless secret, the secret of the mind

p. 136

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

we wish to illuminate, so far as our frailty permits.

To avoid misunderstandings, we must emphasize that when we speak of sentences, then, as in our discussions hitherto, we have in mind only such sentences as constate our sensations. Only with regard to such constating sentences do we assert that they have a twofold articulation because the sensation itself, of which they give news, is to be regarded as a double experience. But if we see, with regard to such constating sentences, that they must possess a twofold articulation, then it will be easy later to convince ourselves that every sentence whatever must be of a dual character. Now it is not hard to see that the subject and predicate of the constating sentence correspond to the original sensation and to the memory-replica relating to it.

For if we remember back to some past experience of ours, is it not clear that we shall be able to assert only that for which the memory-replica (memory-image) at our disposal qualifies us? One who does not rack his brain over self-deception or the misleading of others can assert nothing whatever concerning a past experience except what his remembering places at his disposal. It needs no explanation that what we do not remember at all cannot figure at all in any true assertion of ours. It is clear, therefore, that if we constate something on the basis of remembering, then the predicate of the constating sentence is wholly bound by the memory-image that arises in us: for if we wish to be true, then the predicate of our sentence can contain neither more nor less than the memory-image in question permits us to assert. The closest connection existing between the memory-image and the predicate of the sentence is therefore striking.

It is clear, further, that we call the memory-image a memory-image only because it, as it were, points back to something: to that something to which the original sensation already related; and it perhaps need not even be said that precisely thereby the subject of our constating sentence too is determined. The subject corresponds to that something which presented itself in the immediate sensible impression, the predicate to the very same something, which now presents itself as a memory-image. In a word, it is striking that the predicate of the constating sentence relates to its subject precisely as our memory-replica relates to that which originally stood before us as an immediate sensation

p. 137

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

I believe it needs no further demonstration that, in constating from memory, the predicate of the sentence expresses what we find in the memory-image at our disposal, and that we assert this predicate of that to which our memory-image, as it were, points, and which previously stood before us as an original sensation. If, for example, I now remember the face of a deceased friend, then it is evident that on the basis of this remembering I could form one, or several, constating sentences. The predicates of these sentences would contain only what I am able to remember of my deceased friend’s face, and the common subject of these sentences could denote nothing other than that very face itself, which once made a direct impression on me and which now can arise in me only as a memory-image.

34. After this it is easy to understand why the predicate of the sentence seems to swallow up, as it were, the whole content of the sentence. For when we constate facts in sentences, we do so, relatively speaking, only rarely in the face of facts that are present, but predominantly when the facts are no longer present. In such a case the substitute experience, the memory-replica, has stepped into the place of the original experience, and then this memory-replica dictates to us everything that we can express in the predicate, indeed even that of which we are really speaking, and so the subject itself as well. In a word, because the original impression is not present, the memory-image tyrannizes over us, almost to the same degree as if the original impression had never existed, or, to put it better, as if the memory-image were itself the original impression. Indeed, whoever is deeply absorbed in his memories can become so detached from his surroundings that, on waking from it, he feels almost as if he had not been dwelling in the present at all, but in the past, among his original experiences. It follows from the nature of the matter that whoever wishes to remember correctly must inevitably come under the tyranny of the memory-image, for he must strive to have his memory replace the original impression as completely as possible. Hence it is that in constating from memory the subject is, so to speak, only a pictus masculus, whose sole role is to give support to what the predicate contains.

But if the fact that is to be constated is directly present, then, conversely, the significance of the predicate fades beside that of the subject. Hence it is that in the face of present facts we really do not feel any great inclination to form sentences,

p. 138

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and very often wish to designate by a mere noun what lies open before us, as a sign that in our thinking the subject tyrannizes over the predicate. This too follows from the nature of the matter. For under the influence of a direct impression the memory-image so coincides with the impression itself that it counts as a genuine discovery when it is successfully shown that even when the thing we are contemplating stands before us we still need memory. The direct impression, as it were, swallows memory up into itself, whereby the semblance arises as though memory played no part in it at all. In remembering, conversely, the memory-image steps into the place of the original impression to such a degree as if it were itself the original experience. To this twofold one-sidedness in our thinking and speech there corresponds the fact that at one time we concentrate the whole soul of speech in nouns (subjects), at another, quite the contrary, in adjectives and verbs (predicates). Accordingly we can distinguish among philosophers two directions of thinking: 1. the substantial mode of thinking, for which every word becomes a noun, and 2. the phenomenalist mode of thinking, which wishes to reduce everything to adjectives and verbs. That both modes of thinking are equally one-sided needs no proof.

The foregoing discussion contains fresh evidence that in our judgments we relate our transitory experiences to something that is not transitory. For I ask, what enables us to relate a memory-image a2 to an original experience a1? How is it possible that a present experience (the memory-image) should, as it were, point to what is not present (the original experience)? The answer to this question may be that a2 can point to a1 because the memory-image a2 resembles the original experience a1. But this answer, natural as it seems, stands the truth on its head to the same degree. If, for example, I remember the face of a deceased friend, it would be very odd if I began to reflect thus: Since the image which my memory now sets before me resembles the face of my deceased friend, it is therefore probable or certain that I am now remembering my friend. In general, when we remember something, we do not proceed by inferring from the resemblance of the memory-images arising in us to the original experiences what it really is that we are remembering. But indeed, how could we compare our memory-image, when the original experience,

p. 139

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

which we remember, is not even present? We can easily illuminate this situation by an analogy. If a child B resembles the father A, would it not be the greatest absurdity to say: B’s resemblance to A causes B to be A’s child; whereas the matter stands precisely the other way: for the reason that B is A’s child, there is a resemblance between B and A. What we see here of the relation of child and father we can now transfer to the relation of the memory-image and the original experience. It is not resemblance that causes the memory-image a2 to point back precisely to the original impression a1, but the reverse: because the memory-image a2 refers precisely to a1 as to the original experience, a resemblance can obtain between them. In a word, the reference does not follow from the resemblance, but the resemblance follows from the reference. Thus it makes no sense whatever to explain the memory’s reference to the original experience from resemblance, for, quite the reverse, it is from this reference that the possible resemblance must be explained.

It is wholly impossible to understand the reference or pointing of the memory-image to the original experience unless we relate both, the memory-image as well as the original experience, to the non-transitory essence that manifests itself in our experiences. The memory-image a2 can refer to the experience a1 only because both refer to their common essence a. In other words: it is the same essence a that appears in the original experience a1 and in the memory-image a2, and that can still later stand before us anew in memory-images a3, a4, . . . . . an that arise. And because the same essence appears throughout the whole series of original and substitute experiences, the substitute experiences can all refer to the original experience. But if the same essence did not appear throughout the whole series of original and substitute experiences, then memory-image and original experience would, as it were, fall apart, that is, the connection between them could not obtain. It is easy to convince oneself of the truth of this. For if the original experience a1 were not the appearance of some non-transitory essence, then the passing away of a1 would mean complete annihilation. But if something has been completely annihilated, then it is impossible to appeal to it or to point back to it: for by complete annihilation we understand precisely such a passing away as would make even the reference to it impossible. What can still be appealed to

p. 140

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

though it has passed away, was the transitory appearance of a non-transitory essence; and it can be appealed to precisely because a non-transitory essence appeared in it, which later can stand before us again in a memory-image a2. From what has been said it follows that there is no annihilation in the universe, for the whole universe must be conceived as the appearance of an eternal essence. If we did not conceive it so, then passing away would be annihilation, and the present too, since it would have to be confined to a mathematical point of time, would have to be regarded as nothing: that is, we should arrive at calling in question existence itself and, together with it, all knowledge (truth). (Universal doubt.)

It can now stand clearly before us why we need a subject-concept and a predicate-concept when we become aware of an impression. The mysticism of the sentence arises from the fact that we express by two symbols (ὄνομα and ῥῆμα) what in fact is only one. This duality in the signs arises from the fact that we find the eternal not only in the impression but also in the memory-wave that follows it, that is, we find it in the original experience just as in the substitute experience. This finding, however, takes place in such a way that we place our own I into the memory-image just as into the original impression, or, in other words, we regard memory as a manifestation of our own I just as much as the impression to which it refers. We must therefore beware of regarding memory and impression, taken by themselves, as concepts. Neither the memory-image nor the impression is a concept; they become concepts only through our finding in these transitory experiences what is eternal in them, that is, through our placing into them our own non-transitory I. It perhaps need not even be said that by this not the slightest change takes place either in the original impression or in the memory that refers to it, for the identification of our I with the impression and with the memory takes place at mathematical points of time. To the duration of time which the impression and the memory occupy, the identification with them therefore adds absolutely nothing, and so our identification with our experience brings about absolutely no change in the experience itself. By our I’s becoming identical with our impression we form the subject-concept, and by our becoming identical with the memory we bring the predicate-concept into being.*)

*) By this proposition we have established the general principle of the origin of concepts.

p. 141

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Our concepts come into being in the course of judging, and this is the reason why logic cannot proceed from the investigation of concepts, as Greek logic did. Greek logic was a symbolic logic, that is, in its inquiries it relied in the first place on the signs by which we express our knowledge. That it involuntarily laid great weight upon our speech-signs redounds only to its glory; but it is an organic fault that it forgets the sensations, which are the conditions of existence of our knowledge. But perhaps a still greater defect of ancient logic is that it is not so much a logic of judgment as a logic of concepts: that is, that it places the investigation of concepts first in the logical exposition, whereas priority in the systematic teaching of logic belongs to judgments. This cardinal proposition we can now easily prove. We can become aware of an impression-wave only if it arouses a memory-wave in us; hence a concept, taken by itself, cannot even arise in us, but concepts invariably appear in pairs, in such a way that the one refers, as predicate, to the other as subject. In a word, when we become aware of some fact and wish to express it, no single concept arises in us, but invariably a judgment, which can come into being only through the reference of two concepts to each other, because the experience too, on which our judgment rests, is composed of two waves: the waves of the original impression and of the memory. The single judgment we may call a single independent act of consciousness, but the concept is by no means an independent act of consciousness, for it comes into being only in the course of judging.

35. It is very expedient to make the relation of concept and judgment indicated here visible also geometrically. We have already seen above that the constating judgment can be represented by a straight line; we may now add that the subject and the predicate can be conceived as two points which fix that straight line:

In this figure the point a1 symbolizes the concept of the subject, the point a2 that of the predicate. If a1 and a2 are taken not as mathematical but as empirical points, they signify the original impression and the memory that refers to it, that is, that double experience on which our judgment rests. The straight line that connects the points a1 and a2 signifies the act of judgment, and indeed, if we take it as an empirical line, it symbolizes the physiological work which we must perform when we judge; but if we take it as a mathematical line, then it is nothing other than the eternal sense which we place into our act of judgment. The straight

p. 142

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

line’s direction symbolizes the direction of our mind toward truth (reality); the point of the straight line lying at infinity, however, symbolizes truth (reality) itself, which we bring to expression in our judgment, or rather in our sentence. To this point lying at infinity there sometimes corresponds in our speech a member of the sentence as well, the so-called copula (is, will be), which joins subject and predicate together. For the subject can refer to the predicate only because both refer to an eternal reality. Even the distance a1 a2 has a definite sense in this representation, for it symbolizes the duration of time which we need in order to become aware of the impression a1. The smaller, then, we imagine the distance a1 a2, the greater the speed of awareness it symbolizes. This distance can therefore represent the reciprocal of the speed of awareness at work in the constatation in question.

This method of symbolization already in itself illustrates sufficiently the sharp contrast into which our epistemological conception falls with the ancient logic of concepts. Every logic that proceeds from concepts and not from judgments is of a scholastic character, because by a single concept one cannot become aware of a fact, and so whoever begins the logical discussion with concepts thereby betrays that he is not interested in facts, but only in the sense of dependent words. Scholastic theory of knowledge we may therefore call philosophy of words, which cannot duly esteem facts, and consequently the constating judgments relating to facts either. In this sense ancient Greek logic too — at least in the form in which it lies before us in Aristotle’s Organon — must be called scholastic logic. This scholastic logic is perfectly characterized by the outline representation by which it sets concepts and their relations before us. It is characteristic that even Schopenhauer seems to take delight in the symbolization of concepts by circles, and suspects some mystery in the fact that the position of two circles to each other (one within the other, outside, intersecting) can be brought into parallel with the relation of concepts. There is no need to discuss how lame this parallel is and how untenable, for it is enough to point out that in the representation by circles the judgment has no separate symbol at all. These circles, then, speak more eloquently than the most penetrating

p. 143

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

criticism, inasmuch as they show that scholastic logic at bottom knows no judgments, but only concepts and conceptual relations, since it devises a representing symbol only for the concept, but does not symbolize the judgment at all. Scholastic logic thus breaks our thinking, as it were, into conceptual scraps and shreds, that is, it breaks up the judging power itself, as a result of which it becomes incapable of investigating the faculty of judgment. And whoever cannot investigate the faculty of judgment investigates neither the real facts nor the truths relating to them, but plays only an empty game with words. Hence it is that scholastic logic — as it stands before us in the little books that treat of “formal logic” — says quite plainly that it does not even concern itself with truth itself, but only with its so-called “form.” It was Kant who openly declared that logic is a science which abstracts from all the content of our cognitions and investigates only their form; and so it was really he who raised to a dogma the doctrine that logic is not only a contentless science, but must always remain one. It was he who made the distinction between formal and material truth, in such a way that, for example, if while writing these lines I assert that I am not writing, this is formal truth, but material falsity. Even manifest falsity can thus be declared formal truth, as soon as we break our cognitions up into form and content. Against this it must of course be stressed that truth can be neither a truth of content nor a truth of form, but only truth. It is interesting that Bolzano turns with genuine indignation against this distinction of Kant’s, and perhaps we need hardly say that in this respect we are wholly on his side.

The corruption of logic just described can be traced back to Aristotle, who, although he was one of those who over the course of millennia gave the mightiest impetus to epistemological inquiry, on the other hand also checked this development by diverting epistemological inquiry from facts and the judgments relating to them, directing all the power of his penetration to concepts. To this conceptual, that is, scholastic logic the logic of judgment must be opposed, and only in the measure in which this succeeds in the course of the centuries have we any prospect that the human mind will gradually free itself from the boundlessly heavy fetters of scholastic logic. Modern times have apparently taken up an oppositional position toward the logic of Aristotle: but this is for the most part only

p. 144

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

semblance; in place of the scholasticism of concepts there has stepped, in modern times, the scholasticism of sensations, whose characteristic trait is that it exchanges the sensation (idea) for knowledge, as we see in Locke.

To return to the symbolizing method which I tried to introduce above, I must remark that I by no means wish to lay any particular weight upon it. For our discussions of judgment can stand perfectly well without the use of any method of representation, just as my theory of judgment too matured in me without the aid of such symbolic representations. But perhaps this representation will nevertheless be of use in making the contrast of the logical system set out here with scholasticism strikingly clear. When we represent the judgment by a line and the concepts by points, I believe we emphasize with sufficient force that we are not at all inclined to confuse the peculiar nature of the judgment with that of concepts. The objection, however, can at once be raised against our representation that we designate the subject-concept and the predicate-concept with the same symbol — a point — and so do not bring out the specific difference between the subject-concept and the predicate-concept. This apparent deficiency, however, we can remedy in the following manner:

We can become aware of the impression a1 not only through the memory-image a2, but also through the memory-images a3, a4 . . . . ad inf.,

as the accompanying figure shows.*) Here the series of points a2, a3, a4 . . . . . always represents one and the same predicate-concept, in such a way that we rely each time on a different, that is, ever new memory-image when we form the same concept. The point a2, therefore, which represents the predicate, we may regard as a moving point, which can arrive at the position of a3, a4 . . . . while it always symbolizes the same predicate-concept, whereas the concept of the subject is represented by the fixed point a1. In this way we also do justice to the difference between impression and memory-image, that is, between subject and predicate, inasmuch as we symbolize the one by a fixed, the other by a flowing or moving point. Simultaneously

*) The arrow in the figure indicates the flow of time toward the future.

p. 145

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

we see also that memory-judgments of equal content need not, as we did earlier, be represented by parallel straight lines, but that we can unite all memory-judgments on one and the same straight line, by regarding the point that represents the memory as moving along the straight line.

11. The Principle of Identity.

36. At bottom all our discussions hitherto contain nothing other than the principle of identity, and I may add that all our subsequent discussions too will contain nothing other. Nevertheless we need not fear that in treating this principle we shall ever run out of new objects, thoughts, guiding Ideas, for the principle of identity is an inexhaustible fundamental principle of reason. When the mind investigates itself, the very first and chief result of this self-investigation is that it is able to constate some state of affairs, that is, that it identifies, judges. This identifying judging, however, is nothing other than the relating of our transitory impressions to an eternal essence, as we have already seen proved. We could therefore express the principle of identity by saying that everything that is transitory is the appearance of what never passes away. This principle at the same time sheds light on facts (as transitory things) and on the human mind, whose power of judgment consists precisely in being able to place transitory facts under the point of view of eternity. And since we can never run out of facts, there is always fresh occasion, through their identification, to cast new glances into the principle of identity itself, just as, conversely, through an ever deeper understanding of the principle of identity we arrive at a more unified conception of nature. All the phenomena of nature are only parables of the principle of identity,*) just as nothing else comes to expression in the principle of identity than our unifying striving in the face of the totality of facts. That this striving of ours can never lead to a final conclusion needs no discussion. And this is equivalent to the boundless capacity of the principle of identity to be deepened, or rather to the boundless capacity of logic for progress.

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 10

*) This recalls Goethe’s words: Alles Vergängliche ist nur ein Gleichniss [“All that is transitory is but a likeness”].

p. 146

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

But although in all our inquiries we shall have to do with the principle of identity, we shall nonetheless treat of it separately in this chapter, chiefly because no principle of the theory of knowledge has been so misunderstood by theorists of knowledge of the most diverse tendencies as precisely this. We must confine ourselves to bringing out two particularly characteristic misunderstandings (among the countless others of every shade): the sensualist and the rationalist identity. What all these misunderstandings have in common is that they cannot grasp why a sentence must consist of a subject and a predicate; hence it is incomprehensible to them why in identifying we should need two concepts of different character (subject-concept and predicate-concept). For if of a concept A, as subject, a concept B differing from it is asserted as predicate, it becomes incomprehensible to them how these differing concepts can be brought into a relation of identity. And if of the concept A, as subject, the concept A again must be asserted as predicate, it becomes incomprehensible what purpose this serves and how it is to advance our knowledge.

As for the sensualists, they take offense at the idea that of an A precisely this A should be asserted, for they can imagine nothing more superfluous. That this very thing should be the fundamental principle of logic provokes their downright indignation. This vexation at the (alleged) principle of identity no one has expressed so brilliantly as Locke in his chief work (Book IV, chs. 7 and 8: Of Maxims; Of Trifling Propositions). In these splendid chapters the sober-minded English philosopher rebels against the scholastic philosophers, who are so proud of their fundamental principle “what is, is,” and who set it up as the fundamental principle, indeed the very source, of all knowledge. He therefore enunciates the famous proposition “That there are universal propositions, which, though they be certainly true, yet they add no light to our understanding; bring no increase to our knowledge: these are the identical propositions, and those in which a part of a complex idea is predicated of it.” It is interesting to what lengths Locke is carried in his understandable indignation against the scholastics’ principle of identity. For to acknowledge propositions that are true, and in spite of this do not enlighten the mind, is a very strange assertion. When Locke in this way constructs the concept of trifling but true propositions, he supplies scholastic thinking, against his will, with the strongest weapon against himself. Kant, from these trifling but true propositions

p. 147

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

made his formal judgments, which he calls analytic judgments. For if even the sensualist Locke is compelled to admit that there are trifling but true propositions, this demonstrates clearly enough that we may rightly speak of merely formal, that is, “analytic judgments.” Thus Kant, in one of his fundamental distinctions (that between analytic and synthetic judgments), rests on Locke.

It is natural that Locke could by no means have accepted the peculiar scholastic transformation of his “trifling judgments” into “analytic judgments.” For the existence of judgments empty of content was a discovery for Kant, but for Locke a matter of vexation. For pages together he keeps asking what good it can be to form such sentences as “man is man,” “all whiteness is white,” “red is not blue,” etc., etc. If a monkey — so Locke says — amused himself by taking an oyster now in his right hand, now in his left, then, if he could speak, he would probably give us some such lecture as this: the oyster in my right hand is the subject, the same oyster in my left hand is the predicate, but you see all the same that this oyster is the same oyster; in general: what is, is. But just as the bulk of the said monkey’s body would certainly not increase if he knew nothing else to do with the oyster than to take it from his right hand into the left, and from there back into the right: so too our knowledge would not increase if we could say nothing else than: “a horse is a horse,” “a soul is a soul,” and more wisdom of this kind. But however much Locke ridicules identical sentences, he nevertheless cannot by any means be done with them, for he admits of them that they are true, though he adds that they are useless playthings. But how anything can be true and yet useless, when in all truth an eternal value lies hidden, Locke gives no enlightenment. Indeed he does not even seriously insist that identical sentences are useless, and is carried away into such concessions as this: “I admit as readily as anyone else that identical sentences are all true and self-evident; I admit further that the foundation of all our knowledge lies in our faculty of knowing the same idea to be the same idea” . . . Now, more than this the most passionate scholastic philosopher cannot demand, for he too says nothing other than that the principle A=A is true, and that it is the foundation of all our knowledge.

10*

p. 148

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

If, then, Locke agrees so much with the scholastics, it was a pity to attack them with such witty mockery.

Whoever has followed our discussions with attention thus far can see that by the principle of identity we understand something quite other than what either Locke or the scholastics understood by it. With us, to identify means to relate the transitory to the non-transitory, the sensible to its non-sensible ground. For the identification of a transitory fact, moreover, we need two concepts differing from each other, which we must place in the relation of identity, so that by their connection we may express one and the same fact. As two points determine only one straight line, so two concepts establish only one factual truth. This is what, in the principle of identity, neither Locke nor the scholastics put in the proper light. Identity loses all meaning with them, for if the predicate is itself nothing other than the subject, then it becomes incomprehensible why subjects and predicates should be formed at all, since then either the one or the other member of the sentence becomes superfluous.

We must therefore clearly disclose the peculiar misunderstanding that lies in the (alleged) principle of identity of the sensualists as well as of the rationalists. We have just seen that by identity Locke understands that we can recognize the same impression as the same impression. In a formula: A = A. The trouble is only that he does not give himself an account of what this means. He explains the principle of identity by the connection of the following two sentences: The impression that I have, that impression I have. This connection of sentences, however, is nothing other than the saying of one sentence twice, but with the raising of the grammatical semblance as though the one sentence were the determiner of the other. We can illuminate this by the following example: “The body that is lighter than water floats in water.” [This sentence pair is constructed grammatically just like the former, which symbolizes the (alleged) principle of identity.] Here the first sentence (the subordinate clause) is the explainer of the second (the main clause). In jest, however, I may allow myself to cast even the refusal of an explanation into an explanatory form, and if someone asks me what kind of body floats in water, I can answer him: “The body that floats in water floats in water.” I have repeated one sentence twice, but I have given the repetition such a grammatical form that the one sentence has become the explanatory subordinate clause of the other. But because in truth

p. 149

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

I have given no explanation, one must not let oneself be blinded by such a grammatical game. What confronts us here is not explanatory sentences, but merely the saying of one sentence twice.

Now I ask whether the saying of a sentence twice signifies the principle of identity. Certainly not. If, for example, I say twice that today is Saturday, I have by no means thereby given expression to the principle of identity. Whence comes it, then, that for immemorial ages philosophers have been inclined to express the principle of identity by the saying of a sentence twice? The key to this curious phenomenon is to be sought in the fact that in life we very often say one sentence twice in succession. But what does such a repetition signify? It may be that someone says a sentence twice because he takes delight in its content. It may be that he repeats it twice because he is greatly astonished at its content and by saying it over again wants to familiarize himself with its meaning. It may be that by the twofold saying he wants to give expression to his indignation that a man can assert such a thing. A good actor will find it easy to repeat a sentence even in ten different tones of voice and in this way to lend the repetition each time a different significance. From which it appears that the repetition of a sentence may have very various significance in life, and that such a repetition does not signify a logical principle which could form the foundation of all knowledge.

But then to what temptation did the theorists of knowledge yield when they named the twofold repetition of a sentence the principle of identity? This, I believe, is not hard to guess. If I say a sentence twice in succession, this, as I have just shown, can indeed have any number of significances, but it can also have the significance that I wish to draw the hearer’s attention to the circumstance that in both sentences one and the same assertion is contained. It is very natural, however, that I have by no means thereby told the hearer what he is to understand by the principle of identity; at most I have achieved so much, that I have turned his attention to this subject. If, for example, the teacher of physics sets a pendulum swinging before his hearers, he has by no means thereby explained what the laws of the pendulum’s swing are; and at most he has achieved this, that through intuition too he leads his hearers to reflect on the laws of oscillation. Similarly, if I repeat a sentence twice, then

p. 150

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

it may be that I spur my hearer to reflect on the principle of identity, but what the content of that principle is remains a fundamental philosophical problem still outstanding and to be solved. This appears from the fact that a far more effective procedure than the twofold repetition would be repetition three times, four times, five times . . . in succession, for the more often I repeat a sentence before the hearer, the more I can spur him to fall to reflecting on the principle of identity. It makes no sense, therefore, to regard the twofold repetition of a sentence as the principle of identity, for even repeating it many times cannot be regarded as that.

From this we see whence arises Locke’s false conception of the principle of identity. It arises simply from the fact that to the twofold repetition of a sentence one can lend such an outward appearance, grammatically speaking, that the repetition takes on the character of an explanation. This grammatical semblance is raised by making, of the two sentences of identical content, the one a main clause and the other an explanatory subordinate clause. It is clearer than daylight, however, that we must not be deceived by such a childish game, that is, we must not regard the twofold repetition of a sentence as an explanation. Had Locke penetrated the grammatical semblance characterized here, he could have ridiculed far more effectively that principle of identity on which scholastic logic rides. He could then have protested against someone’s setting up a juggler’s grammatical semblance as the fundamental principle of logic. Indeed, nothing has perhaps ever done so much harm to the authority of logic as this ridiculous misunderstanding of the principle of identity, as though the vain repetition of a sentence were already the principle of identity.

This comic confusion was increased still further by the formula in which the principle of identity is usually expressed: A is A (A ist A, or A = A). For in this way the twofold repetition of a sentence takes on a form as though the one sentence figured in it as subject, the other as predicate. The sense of this formula, namely, is that what is A is truly A (and not something else). Here stand before us the two sentences, which can now be disguised in the curious form A is A, when it no longer even strikes us that we are confronted with two sentences. To the repetition of a sentence, then, we can also give a grammatical form such as if we were confronted with only one sentence, and so it is easy to understand

p. 151

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

why so immeasurable a confusion could arise in the conception of the principle of identity.

Logic thus takes, as it were, the first step on the road to purification when it excludes from itself once and for all the ridiculous conception of the principle of identity, as though this principle, which touches the deepest secret of our faculty of cognition and with it of our existence, consisted of nothing other than the mechanical reeling off of a sentence twice in succession. A parrot too is able to say a sentence it has been taught a hundred times in succession, but for all that the poor creature has not the faintest notion of the principle of identity. If, then, we locate the principle of identity in the repetition of sentences, we have degraded the human condition to the condition of the parrot.

The repetition of a sentence, however, played a certain role in our own doctrine of identity too. To make it understood that the sentence A expresses a truth valid for all times, we called upon the reader to imagine its content repeated through all futures in an infinite series of sentences A1, A2, A3, . . . But we added that this is only a sensible auxiliary means of making the principle of identity intuitable. And we stressed that the sentence A by itself is perfectly sufficient to express the truth contained in it as eternally valid. It is only our human frailty that makes it necessary for us to renew truths from memory, if we wish them not to be finally lost to us in this life. But the truth of a truth does not depend in the least on whether we renew it from memory or not. It remains true, whether we can remember it or not.

In thus distinguishing the principle of identity from the twofold or manifold repetition of an assertion, we have also shown the untenability of Locke’s notion that there are trifling but true judgments. Trifling truths cannot exist in reality, for what has no (intellectual) content cannot be a truth, nor indeed a judgment at all. To be sure, if we repeat a sentence twice, this procedure may in general appear from the logical point of view as a futility, and if we begin to imagine that this twice-repeated sentence is only one sentence, we are easily carried away into the absurd assertion that there are truths which do not

p. 152

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

illuminate our mind, because they have no content. The nature of truths, on the contrary, is that they shine forever and that there is no power that could extinguish their infinite light. Of every day of the universe we can imagine that its light goes out, but over the eternal shining of the least significant little factual truth the ever-rolling flood of the ages can change nothing.

Together with Locke’s “trifling truths” there collapse Kant’s formal, or rather analytic, truths. And if there can be no talk of analytic judgments, then the concept of synthetic judgments too must be dropped. By a synthetic judgment, as we saw, Kant means a judgment whose subject and predicate do not stand in the relation of identity. Such judgments, however, as can now be easily seen, must necessarily be false judgments, for it belongs to the nature of the true judgment that in it subject and predicate stand in the relation of identity. It makes no difference that subject and predicate are two different concepts; indeed, we must necessarily relate two such different concepts to each other in order to be able to identify an impression of ours, since the experiences too on which our constating judgments rest are double experiences.

12. Sentence Pairs.

37. Now that we have roughly clarified the sense of the principle of identity, we can take a further step toward a deeper understanding of it. It is a well-known fact that whatever we may notice, in constating it we are in general content to give an account of the noticed fact, but for the most part we do not think it necessary to give an account, in a separate sentence, of the noticing itself. Whoever thinks, asserts, or says something is very often so absorbed in the object of his thought, assertion, or saying that, for this or for any other reason, he does not think it necessary to emphasize separately that he is now thinking, asserting, or saying something. In general, whoever judges of something can utter this judgment without adding: I now judge. He too who hears and understands this judgment knows that a judging has now taken place, but as a rule he thinks rather of that to which the judging refers than of the fact that he is now the ear- and understanding-witness of a judgment uttered. Instead, for example, of

p. 153

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

saying: I now hear that someone is knocking at the door, I content myself with declaring: someone is knocking at the door. Instead of saying: I see that this pendulum is swinging, I content myself with the simple declaration: this pendulum is swinging. From the sentence pair: I remember that yesterday I saw my friend, I can leave out the first sentence, confining myself to constating: yesterday I saw my friend. Finally, in place of such a sentence pair as: I know that this cubic equation has only one real root, I can simply say: this cubic equation has only one real root.

Whatever fact I may constate, I can in any case preface it by saying that I now constate, assert, say, or know such and such. Of whatever I may judge, I can preface that I now judge. In a word, to every object-judgment or sentence there belongs another sentence, in which I constate that I have now judged in a sentence. That is, every object-sentence has a subject-sentence as its counterpart. For if I have constated some fact in a sentence, then this constating sentence itself forms a new state of affairs, which I constate in a new sentence. It will be well at this point to recall what I said already in the introductory section of ch. 6, namely that for the time being we should be speaking only of those kinds of reference (sentences) in which, by our signs, we refer to our impressions; it is now high time, however, to pass over to those references in which, by our signs, we refer not to impressions, but to our signs that express these impressions. Our inquiry hitherto has been very one-sided, inasmuch as with our sentences we have referred only to our impressions, whereas we now pass from these sentences to those with which we refer to our sentences, or rather to our judgments. If we designated by A the sentence in which an impression was constated, we can now designate by B the sentence in which we constate that we have just formed a sentence A. The object of the sentence A is some direct impression (or rather the essence that appears in this impression); the object of the sentence B, on the other hand, is the sentence A itself, or rather the sense that lies hidden in the sentence A.

The concept of the object-sentence cannot obtain without the concept of the subject-sentence, and conversely the concept of the subject-sentence cannot obtain without that of the object-sentence. This proposition may be called the principle of sentence pairs. For in order that a sentence A may be called an object-sentence,

p. 154

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

it is necessary that we become aware of this sentence A, that is, that we constate this sentence A with the help of a sentence B. The sentence A which we constate is the object-sentence, and the sentence B with which we refer to the sentence A as to a state of affairs is the subject-sentence. It is evident, then, that there can be talk of an object-sentence only if we refer to it by a subject-sentence, and conversely of a subject-sentence only if there exists a sentence to which we refer as an object-sentence. That is, it is evident that the concepts of object-sentence and subject-sentence cannot subsist without each other.

On the ground thus prepared it will now be easy to form the concepts of object-awareness and self-awareness (or consciousness). We may speak of object-awareness when by a sentence A we become aware of an impression a of ours, that is, when by a sentence A we constate an impression a. Of subject-awareness or self-awareness there is question when by a sentence B we have become aware of the fact that previously, by a sentence A, we became aware of an impression a. By self-awareness, therefore, we become aware that we have just performed an act of awareness. Object-awareness refers by signs (sentences) to our impressions; self-awareness refers by signs to our signs (by sentences to sentences), or rather by acts of awareness to acts of awareness. It must never be forgotten that in every act of awareness a reference is involved, and according as we refer to an impression or to an act of awareness, this reference of ours will be an act of object-awareness or of self-awareness respectively.

Since, however, the words “object” and “subject” — as we have already discussed — easily lend themselves to misuse, so that, for example, the absurd semblance may arise that the acts of subject-awareness or self-awareness were less objective (or true) than those of object-awareness, it will be expedient to provide names that give no occasion for such a misunderstanding. I propose that we call object-awareness direct awareness, but self-awareness contrary awareness. For the relation of the two kinds of awareness recalls the relation of action and reaction. When we become aware of an impression, we relate the transitory experience to an eternal reality, that is, we then place the transitory under the point of view of the eternal; when, however, we become aware of a sentence of ours, we regard the eternally valid as fixed in transitory signs.

p. 155

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

At one time our mind passes from the transitory to the eternal that appears in it; at another, our mind passes from the eternally valid truth obtained to the transitory signs in which we express it. Or if this does not bring out clearly enough the contrast between direct and contrary awareness, let us consider that by object-awareness we point directly to a transitory phenomenon, but by contrary awareness to a content of sense that lays claim to eternal validity. Thus we shall find the recommended expressions justified. We may, however, also call self-awareness awareness raised to a higher power, if we have in view that by self-awareness we refer to our object-awareness, that is, that we, as it were, raise the latter to a higher power by the former.

But however one may settle upon the names, the essential thing is that we distinguish between awareness in so far as it refers to impressions and awareness in so far as it refers to its own references, that is, to its own acts, to itself. For this distinction is a vital question of logic. We must confess, namely, that the majority of men are friends of direct awareness and shiver at contrary awareness. To flit from impression to impression like the butterfly, and, noticing one fact after another, to form object-sentences: this is man’s primeval pastime, and the more unexpected and surprising the facts he can constate, the greater his delight. But the philosopher, above all the theorist of knowledge, has always been the spoiler of this primeval pleasure, or at least could appear in this light, because he demanded of men that they should now no longer rejoice in their impressions, but should awaken to the consciousness that they are capable of constating such impressions in sentences, that is, that they should become aware of their awareness. This is indeed a painful and fatiguing occupation for the majority, to which they give themselves only with difficulty; but for the philosopher it is positively his bread. The task of logic is the fullest possible development of contrary awareness, its elevation to the highest possible degree of perfection. To investigate consciousness with consciousness, to judge about judging: this is the logical or epistemological undertaking. Thus it is easy to understand why we must lay so great weight on the distinction between direct and contrary awareness; for it is precisely this distinction by which a clear light is shed on the task of logic.

But he is greatly mistaken who believes that the development of contrary awareness means that we suppress direct awareness. Only he can imagine this who does not understand that these

p. 156

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

two kinds of awareness differ only in their objects, but not in their nature. Direct awareness refers to impressions, that is, its object is the impression; contrary awareness, on the other hand, refers to direct awareness, that is, its object is direct awareness. Both, however, judge, that is, constate: the one constates our impressions, the other constates our constating acts. If, then, we abstract from the object to which they refer, there is no difference between the two kinds of awareness: the one judges, the other judges too.

38. In order that we may not fall into some dualistic error regarding object-awareness and self-awareness, it will be well to direct our attention to the following proposition: object-awareness and self-awareness could not develop, could not exist, without each other. We have already set forth that our object-awareness rests on a double experience: on the impression and on the memory-wave that follows it. When the sensation is just present, its two waves (the impression and the memory) form only one experience, and if our experiences consisted of nothing but such undivided sensations, we could have no awareness. The two waves of the sensation must separate from each other, and we must have memory-replicas that point back to original sensations, in order that we may become aware. (See ch. 9, § 30.) But the original sensations and the memory-replicas relating to them do not yet form awareness either. Awareness, as we have set forth, manifests itself at mathematical points of time, at which we identify our I with our memory-replicas and, through these, with our original sensations, or rather with the essence appearing in them. This union of our own essence with the essence appearing in the sensations and memory-replicas constitutes cognition, which, as I say, flashes up at mathematical points of time. But these mathematical points of time would be wholly lost to us if no sign-forming activity proceeded from them. For the mathematical point of time as such we cannot actually grasp, since to grasp it actually we should need an infinitely rapid awareness. In vain, then, would our awareness flash up at mathematical points of time, if our sentence-forming acts, which do not take place at a mathematical point of time but occupy a certain duration, did not bring us word of them. It is these formations of signs or sentences that save for us those mysterious timeless moments when we become aware of something, cognize something. Neither the sensation, therefore, nor its memory-replica, nor the becoming aware of them in the timeless moment could constitute consciousness, if there were lacking

p. 157
