original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the acts of forming signs or sentences, which report those mathematical points of time when, by means of our memory-replicas, we became aware of our sensations.

And because there is no object-awareness without signs, or rather without the understanding of signs, it is easily understood that object-awareness cannot exist without self-awareness either. For of what does self-awareness consist? Of nothing other than awareness of our signs and of the sense expressed in our signs. Object-awareness, however, also depends on such signs and on the understanding of them, only with this difference, that object-awareness uses the signs and their sense merely to refer by means of them to certain sensations; whereas self-awareness is directly interested not in that original sensation but in the sign and the sense dwelling in it, so that it takes this meaningful sign as a fact and constates, by means of new signs, that we have just apprehended signs which possess a certain sense. The existence of sentences and words is just as indispensable a condition of every object-awareness as of every self-awareness, and the difference between the two kinds of awareness is only that sentences and words are for object-awareness merely means of apprehending certain sensations, whereas for self-awareness they serve so that by means of them sentence may refer to sentence, judgment to judgment, awareness to itself.

Conversely, self-awareness could not exist without object-awareness, if only because we cannot refer by a sentence to a sentence if the signs of which sentences consist do not make a sensible impression on us. We must become aware of sensible impressions in order to take cognizance of the existence of the signs, and so self-awareness cannot exist if it does not rest on object-awareness. Object-awareness needs meaningful signs in order to function, self-awareness needs sensible impressions in order to do its work, and so our proposition, according to which object-awareness and self-awareness cannot exist without each other, is proved. This proposition is the principle of the unity of object-awareness and self-awareness.

This proposition, however, must not be so interpreted as though object-awareness and self-awareness appeared with mathematical simultaneity. It is out of the question that, becoming aware of an impression of ours, we could with mathematical simultaneity become aware of this very awareness, of our act of awareness as well. For that would mean that we are able to seize that mathematical point of time at which we became aware of some impression of ours. At the (mathematical) moment in which we perceive some

p. 158

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

fact, we cannot at the same moment become aware of the fact of perceiving, for that would mean that our consciousness is endowed with infinite speed, that is, that it needs no time in order to become aware of anything. Only if we see that we need time in order to become aware of the fact of perceiving do we understand why man is dependent on the forming of signs in order to attain consciousness and self-consciousness. That mathematical point of time at which we perceive some fact would — as I have already said — be a perfectly wasted moment if there did not proceed from it a vital movement, an act of sign-forming, which, taking up a certain duration, becomes fit to announce to us that mathematical moment at which our perception took place.

From what has been said it is evident that the nature of human consciousness can be illuminated with the help of a single proposition: the one proposition that its speed is finite, or, what comes to the same thing, that we have a concept of an infinitely fast consciousness, in comparison with which ours must be characterized as of finite speed. The finite speed of our awareness manifests itself first of all in our having impressions, that is, in our perceiving phenomena; for it is precisely these impressions that, while they make our awareness possible, at the same time delay it by the duration which they take up. But if we once need time in order to perceive something, then we depend not only on impressions but on memories too, that is, on substitute experiences, which step into the place of past experiences. From the finite speed of our awareness it follows immediately, therefore, that in our work of perceiving we must rely on double experiences. Noticing itself, to be sure, takes place in timeless moments, but these timeless moments, as such, we are unable actually to notice, which again flows only from the finite speed of our awareness. From this same finite speed it now follows also that we must perform some motion which is the indicator or sign of the timeless moment of our perceiving. We must perform a motion, I say, for our signs are nothing other than motions or movements, such as the deaf-mute makes, for example, with his hands, or he who can produce sounds, with his vocal cords, his tongue, in a word with his speaking organs. These movements are vital processes which take up time, and which can be used to report those timeless moments when we become aware of our sensations. From the finite speed of our awareness there can be derived in such

p. 159

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

a way also that frailty of ours, that we need movements for the activity of awareness, movements which serve as signs to show that we have become aware, and also of what it is that we have become aware. An infinitely fast awareness, however, needs neither impressions, nor memories, nor movements (signs) in order to be aware. If anyone perhaps objects to our deriving the characteristic features of our consciousness from the fact of the speed of awareness, we must remark in reply that we place no great weight on the “derivation.” We show here only that the characteristic features of our consciousness, such as that we need impressions, memories, movement-signs, fit so beautifully with the fact that in one second we cannot perform as many acts of awareness as we wish, that is, that our activity of awareness has a finite speed. That which is the condition of life of our consciousness, sensation and movement, is at the same time the retarder of those mathematical moments in which we are aware.

These considerations lead us at the same time to distinguish between the speed of our object-awareness and that of our self-awareness. The speed of object-awareness depends on the time by which a sensation delays the activity of awareness; the less time a sensation takes up when we take cognizance of it, the faster is our object-awareness, that is, our perception. The less time the act of sign-forming takes, which reports that we have taken cognizance of something, the faster does our contrary awareness work. Whereas, then, object-awareness is slowed by the sensations (that is, bound to certain durations), self-awareness is delayed by the labor we need in order to create symbols for our acts of awareness. Everyone knows the struggle it causes to find the right words for our thoughts. This struggle bears witness that contrary awareness needs time for its working. If we had an infinitely fast awareness, we should need no time at all to clothe our thoughts in signs; it is true that in that case we should not need signs (speech) at all.

We often hear people say that they understand or know something and merely lack the words to express it. Such declarations are sometimes not wholly without foundation. Awareness, as I have said, is always bound to certain mathematical

p. 160

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

points of time, and one in whom these moments of cognition occur frequently sometimes feels contempt as well for the speech-signs with which he ought to give an account of those notable moments. This is pardonable in individuals who are absorbed in practical action and whose moments of awareness find expression not so much in speech-signs as rather in eloquent practical deeds, in actions demanding presence of mind, in sudden swift operations, in skills, in works of the plastic arts, etc. But where theoretical insight, theoretical knowledge is at issue, there the moments of cognition must be fixed by speech-signs, and there every alleged cognition for which the corresponding symbols are lacking is mere self-deception. In men of great mind it has often been observed how marvelously they command the words that express their thoughts, and how the speech that sheds light pours from their lips, or the true thought from their pen, as though they did not produce it with effort at all and some mysterious alien power of reason were speaking through them. These inspired minds (men of genius) are in fact distinguished from their fellow men by the speed of their contrary awareness. I do not wish to enter here into a detailed discussion of the nature of genius, and point out only that if, concerning the nature of genius, we do not wish to fabricate mere mincing words and empty bombast, or to set up eccentric theories, we must first of all plunge into the investigation of the speed of human awareness. At bottom the investigation of genius is nothing other than the research into the human mind in general, so that we may say that the whole science of the theory of knowledge in fact always concerns itself only with human genius in general. If we say at times of individual men that they are distinguished by genius, we should have to understand by this that the nature of the human mind, which is common to us all, manifests itself in them with particular clarity. The great thirst for truth, which gives wings to object-perception just as to self-awareness, makes the genius. His manifestations involuntarily move us to think of a mind which by virtue of its infinitely fast consciousness unites in itself the knowledge of all things. In a word, genius reminds us of the absolute mind, toward which we all strive with all our thoughts. But perhaps precisely because we involuntarily compare genius with the absolute mind, we notice its frailties the more easily, in consequence of which some

p. 161

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

eccentric (perhaps also driven by an itch for notice) modern physiologists are inclined to represent genius as a more or less pathological mind. If they investigated the lives of average men with the same interest and attention as those of some famous man, they would probably characterize the minds of average men too as more or less pathological. As indeed it is a well-known thing that the investigator of mental derangements easily thinks he discovers derangement everywhere, naturally only not in his own theories. Whereas if anyone seeks mental disease everywhere, he ought above all to look for mental disease in this very seeking.

39. Only he looks deeper into the nature of our awareness who sees, on the one hand, that it rests on double experiences, which is why our sentences have a twofold articulation, and who notices, on the other hand, that properly speaking the sentence too cannot stand by itself, but that closer investigation always sets before us at least two sentences in place of one. Even if as a rule we suppress the subject-sentence and rather emphasize only the object-sentence, yet in the latter we regard the former as already included with more or less definiteness. If, for example, I put forward as truth that it is cold now, then I understand in this sentence also the subject-sentence that I feel and perceive it so. To every constatation — as I have already mentioned — there belongs also a subject-sentence, which expresses that I now constate (see, hear, perceive, say, assert, know, etc.) something. It is by no means a circumstance of slight importance that practical life does not permit our speech to move in paired sentences; indeed it may be said outright that this characteristic of practically minded intercourse was one of the hindrances to the development of the science of logic. It is quite easy to understand that practical life, where speech is for the most part only direction and transition to action, feels a decided antipathy to all multiplication of words (or what seems to be such). The practical man economizes so much with words that he not only leaves out one (the subject) half of the paired sentence, but even tries to shorten the object-sentence, in that he retains some important part of the sentence and simply drops the rest as guessable anyway. Sometimes, where need arises, a mimic gesture can replace the whole sentence. The narrator and the dramatist know well these abbreviations of our practical and genial intercourse,

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 11

p. 162

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

which may be very valuable from the standpoint of utility and aesthetics, but from the standpoint of logical research are by no means advantageous. If, for example, there are investigators who do not wish to acknowledge that a sentence must necessarily consist of subject and predicate, and if logic has got to the point that it has not yet clearly formulated the principle of sentence pairs, this is to be traced back to the circumstance that in everyday life economy, convenience, perhaps beauty, are the governing standpoints over our speech-signs, and that consequently the standpoint of the theory of knowledge cannot assert itself as it should.

He who, therefore, sets up the habits of everyday speech as the standard in logical inquiry wholly misjudges the significance of logical research. Thus, if in everyday life we state something as truth, we certainly do not always think that we mean our true assertion to be regarded as valid for all times. Of such things the good soul who chats for diversion, or brings forward his petty affairs, certainly does not think. But if we were to ask him whether he regards his true statements as valid for all times, he would certainly answer that this goes without saying and so requires no special emphasis. In a word, into people’s speech a great deal must be read that those concerned take for granted and do not specially emphasize. The theorist of knowledge therefore has full right to find more in everyday speech too than it seems to express expressis verbis. He finds, for example, the subject and predicate even where one of them is suppressed; he finds the paired sentence even where he is confronted with only one (e.g., the object-) sentence, etc. And it is in this sense that he formulates his logical principles. He declares boldly that a sentence must necessarily be twofold in articulation, even if the sentence ever so often appears truncated. He declares boldly too that in fact no single isolated sentence can stand in our mind, because sentences must appear at least in pairs. Subject-sentence and object-sentence inseparably belong together, and this logical truth can least of all be refuted by the fact that ever so often we suppress one of the sentences.

We are in the same case with logical propositions as, e.g., with geometrical truths. If, e.g., I say that the altitudes of a triangle necessarily pass through one point, this proposition has meaning only if I am thinking of mathematical lines and a point, which, however, can nowhere in experience be strictly

p. 163

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

found or produced. Such ideal propositions are also those which logic sets up. The ideal sentence necessarily shows a twofold articulation, that is, consists of a subject-part and a predicate-part. In ideal (logical) speech the object-sentence does not appear alone, but immediately appears accompanied by the subject-sentence. Alas, everyday speech trains our mind in such a direction that we almost lose all receptivity to such an ideal system of signs as would serve not economy with words, not convenience of speech, not euphony and melody, but solely truth that cannot be misinterpreted. And with this we have arrived at the recognition of one of the supreme frailties of human science. Our systems of signs show admirable flexibility for the purposes of practical intercourse and the solution of poetic-artistic tasks, but all the less do they satisfy the demands of inquiry into knowledge and of scientific research generally. Hence it is that Descartes and Leibniz deliberated over an ideal system of signs that would eliminate the frailties of everyday speech. Descartes, in a letter addressed to Mersenne, puts forward the ingenious idea whether we could not find for our thoughts signs of the kind that numerals are for the concepts of number. All our thinking would then be nothing other than a kind of reckoning, only not with numerals but with conceptual signs invented on the pattern of numbers. Descartes himself, however, sees that this idea of his belongs to the sphere of philosophical utopias, for he himself declares that whoever wished to devise such a system of signs would first have to arrive at a perfect philosophy. Behold the circulus vitiosus: in order to philosophize more strictly we should have to possess a strict system of conceptual signs, but conversely, in order to be able to arrive at such signs we should have to possess a perfect philosophical system of concepts. Leibniz, nevertheless, throughout a lifetime did not give up the soaring ambition of inventing a calculus philosophicus, such as Descartes had already dreamed of. Both philosophers were grand masters of mathematical sign-formation, and so it is easily understood that both brooded over a philosophical system of signs that could compete with the signs of mathematics.

But although these thoughts of Descartes and Leibniz bear the character of utopias, I nevertheless believe that they touched upon something which through millennia may incite ever new efforts

11*

p. 164

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in the theorists of knowledge, even if not precisely in the scholastic direction in which Leibniz advanced toward his goal. We must not forget that logic, precisely by subjecting our knowledge, our awareness, to investigation, does nothing other than strive to perfect our signs along with our awareness. Every true logical proposition opens a new insight into the nature of our judgment and consequently gives a fresh impulse to the development of our signs. In fact, then, every logical investigation is directed toward that calculus philosophicus of which Leibniz dreamed, only that it does not want to realize the perfecting of signs all at once and by mechanical means, but by gradual progress in logic.

We may therefore also formulate the task of logic thus: that this science wishes to perfect human speech and, generally, the systems of signs serving to express our knowledge, in the sense that they become ever more powerful factors in the cognition of truth. In so saying we have said nothing other, in fact, than that logic labors at the perfection of our awareness, with which the perfection of our systems of signs also goes hand in hand. The program of Descartes and Leibniz therefore remains an imperishable property of progressing inquiry in the theory of knowledge, naturally not in that utopian and scholastic sense in which it appeared in those bold thinkers. First, we do not deceive ourselves with the fancy that signs could be perfected in themselves, without the extension of empirical knowledge, that is, the development of object-awareness. Only the scholastic and one-sidedly rationalist philosopher can forget that our symbols multiply, grow finer and sharper to the degree that our observations, our knowledge of facts, multiply, grow finer and sharper. Anatomy, chemistry, physics, etc., are the most brilliant testimonies to how many thousands upon thousands of new technical terms, scientific symbols arise under the influence of expanding and refining object-awareness. Just as, then, our empirical knowledge can never reach a final limit, so the symbols used by us too can be developed further without limit. From which it follows that a finished system of signs valid once and for all cannot be devised for the sciences. Every new advance in the cognition of nature would burst such an artificially devised system of signs, just as the growing body bursts the garment that has grown too tight.

p. 165

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Apart from this: it is the greatest rashness to dream of a perfect system of signs to be fixed for all time, when we do not yet properly understand even the structure of the existing system of signs of everyday life. For we have seen that the theorists of knowledge did not even consider it necessary to examine why the sentence has a twofold articulation, and yet without raising this question the system of signs of our speech remains unintelligible. That to every object-sentence there belongs a subject-sentence as its complement in sense, the theory of knowledge has not yet stated (at least not clearly): I believe, for this reason, that it takes our speech as it is, and does not inquire what our system of signs ought to be like in order to become as suitable as possible for the complete expression of truth. So perhaps it is no fancy if I say that the better we strive to understand the structure of our speech, the better shall we come to know its logical frailties, and the more shall we come to be in a position to construct a philosophical (epistemological) system of signs which will make our philosophizing stricter than is possible with the exclusive use of our present customary signs. The representation of the constating sentence by a straight line perhaps means a step toward that more exact system of signs which the theory of knowledge will in the course of time certainly win for itself. Logic will then develop into a science whose authority can compete with that of mathesis. In other words, this means that logic will teach a certain strict discipline of thinking as to how we are to set out upon the discovery of ever new truths in every branch of science. If logic, as I have said once before, is the strategy of the sciences, then it is necessary that it teach us also strict methodical planning in the storming of truth. To this there leads only a single road: the investigation of our knowledge, of our judging, that is, awareness of our activity of awareness.

p. 166

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

13. On Awareness.

40. We already saw in the preceding chapter that, properly speaking, not even one judgment can exist in isolation. For if by a sentence A we become aware of some impression of ours, then it is impossible that we should not have cognizance of this sentence A, that is, that we should not become aware of this sentence A as of a new fact capable of being constated. It is true that in everyday speech we mostly omit the sentence B, which would constate that we had formed a sentence A, but this sentence B is nevertheless understood, as it were of itself, in the sentence A. If, for example, I say that this rose is red, then I understand also that I see and express this. Properly I should have to form such a double sentence: “I see that this rose is red”; but how inclined man is to take these two assertions as only one is confirmed by language in that it is quite easy to combine the two sentences into one sentence in this way: “I see this red rose.” What was formerly an object-sentence is now only the object (complementary) part of a sentence. It is known from the elements of syntax how independent sentences can be so transformed that they appear as members of an extended sentence; and conversely, how from individual members of an extended sentence we can make (attributive, adverbial, complementary) subordinate clauses. All these grammatical phenomena remind us that our sentences, as it were, play into one another, and that one can so to speak slip into the other, to figure there as a mere member of a sentence, or again slip out, to assume the character of an independent sentence.

Whatever we assert contains in all circumstances some reference (relation, reminder) to something which we have not expressed expressis verbis. It is not even necessary to point out that the object-sentence refers to a subject-sentence which complements its sense: it suffices if we remain within the circle of object-sentences, for it is not possible to set up an object-sentence in such a way that it does not contain a reference to some other object-sentence. When, in the first chapter of our theory of judgment, we brought forward as an example this constating sentence: “Here on the table lies a book,” we could not avoid that into this assertion there plays also this sentence: “Here in the room stands a table,” and so on. For

p. 167

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

if we pick some fact out of our environment, then, even if we are silent about this environment, it goes without saying that such an environment must exist. Indeed it is not at all possible to give expression to any state of affairs without other states of affairs playing into it with more or less definiteness. If I say only: “a book lies here,” even then the word “lies” refers to something on which the book rests. But the word “here” too contains a clear reference to the environment in which I am at present. Now “here” and “now” figure everywhere where someone constates his impression as it is just appearing, for elsewhere than “here” and “now” no such immediate constatation can be performed at all. “Here” and “now” designate in general terms the spatial and temporal environment in which the constatation takes place, and since these two words figure in every sentence that contains an immediate constatation, it is evident that we can never form one constating sentence in such a way that other sentences, which we do not form expressis verbis, do not play into it with more or less definiteness. In logic we speak, to be sure, of single sentences, but in fact we are never able to form a single sentence into which another sentence does not play.

The theorists of knowledge, however, are on the whole very much inclined to forget that we cannot form a perfectly isolated single sentence; indeed, even our own investigations (in the part on the theory of judgment) had this unavoidable frailty, that we spoke of some single judgment as though it could be completely isolated. Immeasurably many errors of the science of the theory of knowledge arise from the semblance as though some single judgment could be isolated in an absolute manner. Yet with our judgments we are in the same case as, e.g., with bodies or with physical and chemical processes, namely that we can lift nothing out of its environment in such a way that something of this environment does not cling to it. A body we cannot free finally from the company of the other bodies in contact with it, a chemically perfectly pure element we cannot produce, and certain foreign influences (e.g., moisture, gravitation, the presence of some slight light, etc., etc.) we can never wholly exclude in physical experiments. In logic too we cannot bring forward a judgment hermetically sealed from every other judgment, for the very bringing forward of this judgment already contains the germ of a new judgment.

Seemingly it is of very slight significance that the complete isolation of any

p. 168

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

single judgment is impossible: in fact, however, this proposition is a fundamental proposition of the science of the theory of knowledge of immeasurable importance. For it expresses that single isolated truths do not properly exist at all, that is, that all truth together forms only one truth. If we men knew all truth, that is, if we were omniscient, then the knowledge of single partial truths would cease to exist for us, for then all truth would be known to us at once. In a word, only for absolute awareness does the knowledge of every partial truth become the cognition of one truth. We men, however, are compelled to distinguish partial truths because we are not in possession of all truth, or rather: of the one absolute truth. Although, however, we do not possess absolute truth, we nevertheless know that those partial truths which we cognize are manifestations of an absolute truth embracing all truth. We can also express this by saying that we men do indeed possess a concept of an absolute truth embracing all truth, but this concept is for us only an ideal, which we strive to approach by every inquiry without ever being able to reach it. If we did not possess such a concept of absolute truth, we could not cognize partial truths either.

For, as we saw just now, it is not possible to assert some fact without keeping silent about another fact that plays into the former. If with the sentence A I aim at some single fact, then it is impossible that I should not also aim, with a sentence A1, which I have kept silent, at the spatio-temporal environment of this fact. The most skillful marksman cannot hit the center of a circle so that it be a mathematical point and that he cut out of the target nothing but a mathematical point. In the same way, if I aim at a factual truth A, it is impossible to express it without grazing the factual truth A1; conversely, if I wish to express this, I touch the truth A2 too, and so on. We can express this thus: that every factual truth is connected with every other, that is, that all factual truths form only one truth. With this we have proved that without an absolute truth partial truths could not exist for us. It is true that we do not know absolute truth itself as such, but of every partial truth we know that it cannot subsist in isolation, that is, that it is connected with every other partial truth. The mutual

p. 169

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

connection of all partial truths is precisely what we call the unity of truth. Although, then, we cannot reveal this unity of truth — for we are not omniscient, so that we could demonstrate the actual connection of all truth — we are nevertheless perfectly convinced that truth is only one.

This one, that is, absolute truth we cannot possess, because our awareness is not infinitely fast, in consequence of which it is not capable of embracing all truth at one stroke. An awareness of finite speed needs time to get from one truth to another. We men are therefore, as it were, wanderers in the realm of truth, just as our life too consists in moving from one place to another. Our knowledge is on its way from one station to another, and so we, as it were, travel from one truth toward another, unless in the meantime we fall into some error. Of truths we have — so to speak — only a dynamic possession, and the science of logic is in fact nothing other than the dynamics (dialectics) of our knowledge of truth.

On the basis of what has been said we can now distinguish the part of logic that treats of judgment and the part that treats of awareness. In the theory of judgment we deal with judgment only as a single act; but since we see that, strictly speaking, there can be no question of wholly isolated single judgments (inasmuch as the working of our mind consists in proceeding from one judgment toward another), we are compelled to supplement the theory of judgment by a theory of awareness. Strictly, these two parts of logic cannot be separated from each other; indeed we may say that the whole of logic is nothing other than the theory of awareness; but because it would be inconvenient to investigate at once the transition (that is, the awareness) from one judgment to another, in order to make the task easier we do not at first investigate the connection of judgments, but deal only with the investigation of single judgments, as though one of our judgments could really be isolated from the other. This, however, is impossible, and the nature of the single judgment itself can be made clearer only by subjecting the connection of our judgments to investigation.

In place of the theory of the connection of judgments (the theory of awareness) Aristotle developed his so-called syllogistic, whose fatal influence was felt not only in the theory of knowledge but in the development of the sciences generally. We have already seen how falsely Aristotle formulates the relation of judgment to concepts,

p. 170

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

as a result of which, instead of the investigation of judging, it actually teaches only a game with concept-pebbles; but the real game-character of this game appears wholly only in the syllogistic, for Aristotle does not formulate that general logical problem which lies hidden in the transition from one judgment to another. He concerns himself only with what is customarily called “inference,” whereas not every transition from one judgment to another can be called an inference; and yet these latter too we cannot understand if we do not investigate in general all kinds of connections of our judgments. Thus, e.g., among all the connections of our judgments the most significant is that by virtue of which one sentence (as subject-sentence) refers to another as object-sentence, that is, by virtue of which we become aware of some act of our awareness by another act, and by virtue of which our object-awareness is completed by our self-awareness in such a way that the one could not exist without the other. This statement of mine, that “twice two is four,” would have no meaning if I could not refer to it by another statement in this manner: “I say and know that twice two is four.” For in one who asserted something but did not know that he asserts something, there could be no question of assertion or judging. Only he who so judges that he can constate by another judgment that he judges can be regarded as a judging being. At least in logic, where it is a matter of developed judgment (and not of the awareness of children), by judgment we can understand only judging with self-consciousness. Since, therefore, from the standpoint of logic no object-judgment can exist without an accompanying judgment, which is the expression of our self-consciousness, it is evident that no more important connection can obtain between judgments than that which links the subject-judgment with the object-judgment. Into the investigation of this connection, however, Aristotelian logic does not enter at all, evidently because it has not properly formulated the real task of logic, the investigation of the connection of judgments, that is, of the connection of truths.

41. When we now turn to the most general task of logic, the investigation of the connection of judgments, we must emphasize that we have laid the foundation of this exceedingly difficult inquiry by the distinction of object-awareness and self-awareness. So long as logic does not make a clear distinction between those judgments by which we refer to our impressions and those judgments by which we refer to our judgments, there can be no serious talk of an investigation of the connection of judgments. For that we are able to judge and

p. 171

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

connect judgments we owe to the fact that we have self-consciousness, that is, that we become aware of our act of awareness, that we possess knowledge of our knowledge. In a word, the necessary connection of subject-awareness and object-awareness forms the foundation of every inquiry directed to the connection of judgments.

No light so penetrating can be thrown on the difference between object-awareness and self-awareness as by passing over to the investigation of mathematical, namely counting, judgments. Besides those judgments by which we constate our immediate impressions, the judgments deserving the greatest attention are those by which we count a multitude of objects. These counting judgments show something quite peculiar, a character that differs from all our other judgments, in consequence of which mathematical science too, which rests on them, occupies a wholly separate position among all the sciences. The rationalist theorists of knowledge felt this deeply, and their attention was at all times captivated by that sublime rigor which so distinguishes mathematical inquiry. It is characteristic that among the impressionist (sensualist) theorists of knowledge we find not one who penetrated deeper into mathematics and examined its spirit in order to shed light on its separate character. Neither Bacon nor his successors: Hobbes, Locke, Berkeley, Reid, Hartley, Hume, Mill, Spencer are mathematicians; indeed it may be said that several of them are thinkers very far removed from the spirit of mathematics. The one-sided impressionism of English inquiry into knowledge is perhaps explained precisely by the circumstance that among its representatives we hardly find one who showed a deep feeling for mathesis. On the other hand, the French and German school of the theory of knowledge is distinguished precisely by a great attraction to mathematics, as the investigations of Descartes, Leibniz, Kant, Bolzano, and even Comte prove. These rationalist thinkers were very deeply gripped by the exact spirit of mathematics, and more than one of them brooded on how the theory of knowledge, and thereby the whole of philosophy, could be raised to the level of an exact science such as mathematics is. And if they felt this to be a utopia, they strove at least to unravel that secret by which mathematical knowledge differs so peculiarly from all other knowledge.

Kant was positively spellbound by the fertility and rigor of mathematical thinking, so that in the very midst of his epistemological

p. 172

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

reflection we find the riddle of mathematical cognition, over which he lost himself in profound and almost mystical speculations. When he divides our cognitions into a priori and a posteriori ones, and our judgments into analytic and synthetic, what chiefly guides him in all this famous division is to establish the character of mathematical knowledge that differs from all other knowledge. It is true that neither the distinction of a priori and a posteriori nor that of analytic and synthetic is in the least suited to cast light on the nature of mathematical knowledge; nevertheless there is no doubt that Kant has the great historical merit of having directed the attention of thinkers to the investigation of the secret of mathematical cognition with such emphasis as no other philosopher before him.

It is very natural that we shall never finally solve the secret of mathematical cognition any more than that of any other cognition. For the time being, however, it is wholly sufficient if we point to a characteristic of mathematical knowledge which is not to be found in our other knowledge. I do not say that mathematical cognition will thereby be characterized exhaustively, but such an exhaustive characterization can in any case never be attained. In general, whatever we touch, we shall never exhaust it by our knowledge, and so we must always be content with casting as much light on the infinite mystery that lurks in all things as we are just able. As to the secret of mathematical cognition in particular, what is of decisive importance is whether we are able to disclose such a characteristic of counting judgments as is not to be found in other judging; for if so, we have made the first step toward the foundation of a theory of knowledge of mathematics.

If we wish to count some multitude of objects, then we certainly need such judgments as: this is one, this is one more, this is one more, etc. For only if we acknowledge of each of the objects to be counted that it is one, is it possible to count them up. I think it needs no explanation that our counting would at once be faulty as soon as we did not acknowledge of any of the objects to be counted that it too is one. So it is evident that as many times as there are objects to be counted, we must have formed such a judgment: this too is one. I do not say that this judgment: “this too is one,” must be pronounced aloud every time,

p. 173

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

indeed we know that in silent and rapid counting of objects the semblance too may arise as though one needed only to run one’s eyes quickly over the objects and it were not even necessary, stopping at each, to form such a judgment as that this too is one: but here as in countless other cases we must not let ourselves be deceived by the rapid performance of operations. The child who had never in his life thought: “this is one and this is one more,” could never have arrived at the concept of two. When he later acquires great practice in counting objects, his mind runs so swiftly over these thoughts: “this is one, this is one more, this is one more . . .” etc., that he can convince himself that his mind does not need such single acts at all, and that he can count the multitude of objects before him without these as well. But this is just as much an error as if someone who runs fast were to convince himself that he owes this speed to the fact that he does not set his feet on the ground at all. In a word, no doubt can attach to this, that in counting some multitude the mind forms as many constating judgments as there are objects it has to count. From which it follows that it is entirely indifferent whether we count the objects themselves or count those judgments by which we constate of the objects: this is one, this is one, this is one, etc.

In other words: it has no influence of any kind on the result of counting whether we count the immediate impressions received from the objects or the judgments constating these impressions. With this we have disclosed a property of counting judgments which is to be found in no other judgments. For in general it is a very different matter to constate an impression and to constate the sentence by which we constate the impression. It is a different matter, e.g., whether I constate that I feel pain or whether I constate that I have uttered this sentence. In short: the constating sentence itself is something wholly different from the impression which it constates. In general, then, we can say that the constating sentence cannot replace the impression to which it refers. With counting judgments, however, the peculiar case obtains that the constating judgment can perfectly replace the impression to which it refers. For it is perfectly indifferent whether we count our impressions or the judgments constating them.

p. 174

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

This curious character of counting judgments can lay claim to the most searching attention. Above all we must strive to express this characteristic in the most intelligible and clearest way possible. To this end I shall replace this lengthy expression “constating sentence” by a shorter expression: by this, that I say “sign.” Now we shall therefore express our proposition thus, that it is all one whether we count our impressions or the signs by which we signify the impressions. In general a sign cannot completely replace the original impression which it signifies: but in mathematical judgments, namely in counting judgments, the signs used can perfectly replace the original impressions, or rather objects, which are to be counted. Everyone can convince himself of this directly; so, e.g., if we have to count a certain multitude of balls, then every ball can be perfectly replaced by a sign (e.g., a vertical stroke: |), for whether we count the balls or these signs is wholly indifferent. If, however, we wish to form not counting and in general not mathematical but other kinds of judgments about the balls, then those signs cannot perfectly replace the balls, for if we wish to learn any properties whatever of those balls, we need sensible impressions of those balls. So, e.g., if those balls are made of some metal, we cannot learn the nature of these metals by an investigation of those signs by which we signify the metals, but we need perhaps chemical investigations too, which must be carried out on the balls themselves, not on the signs (words) made for them.

Everyone knows of mathematics that signs (symbols) play in it such an extraordinary role as in no other science. The reason of this we now see clearly. Looking through the series of the sciences, we search in vain for another in whose researches the signs can perfectly replace the impressions to which they refer. Physics, chemistry, physiology, anatomy can none of them form such signs as could perfectly replace the facts themselves, so that the investigation of the signs could substitute for the investigation of the phenomena themselves. Mathematics alone is in the peculiar position that in it signs can replace the facts. At least with respect to counting everyone sees at once that it is quite superfluous to trouble ourselves with the objects to be counted themselves, if only for each

p. 175

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of them we have made ourselves a sign. For, with respect to what we wish to know by counting, the signs representing the objects render the same service as the objects themselves.

That this must be so is very easily understood. Every task of counting demands of us above all that we acknowledge each of the objects to be counted as one. Now I ask, what does it mean to acknowledge something as one? In my view I have already acknowledged each thing as one as soon as I make it the object of my judgment. It is not necessary that I know anything else of that thing than that I judge about it, and by that very fact I can declare of it that it is one. If, then, I declare some object one, I have disregarded everything that I could assert of it, and have regard only to this, that it is at all the object of my judging. In this way it is understood why this judgment: “this is one” can step into the place of the object itself, and why, in place of the objects, I can count the judgments declaring them one. For when I declare something one, nothing in the world interests me in this object except that it is the object of my judging. But if the object too interests me only because I judge about it, and otherwise I have nothing to do with the object except that it gives me occasion for judging, then it is evident that my judgment can, from the standpoint of counting, perfectly replace the object itself.

When, therefore, we form counting judgments, our awareness becomes indifferent to the difference between object-awareness and self-awareness. Instead of counting the impressions we can count the judgments referring to them, that is, it is all one whether we perform the counting within object-awareness or within self-awareness. Hence we can rightly say that counting judgments are acts of neutralized awareness: by neutral awareness understanding an awareness which disregards the difference between direct and contrary awareness, since for it the objects of contrary awareness (the judgments) can perfectly replace the objects of direct awareness (the impressions).

42. From this it is seen that mathematics owes its peculiar character, differing from all other sciences, to the fact that it practices such an extraordinary renunciation as no other science. The world of appearances, which is the inexhaustible storehouse of ever new experiences, and whose intuition the empiricist

p. 176

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

investigator can never replace with signs, is always of interest to the mathematician only insofar as it can be replaced by signs. The mathematician always grasps phenomena only insofar as he can put his own signs in place of the phenomena as a perfect equivalent, and so he is compelled to let drop from the phenomena everything that he cannot perfectly replace with his signs. From this, however, we must by no means conclude that mathematics can detach itself from the world of impressions, that is, of experience. Leibniz, Hume and Kant indeed set forth the truths of mathesis as though they were of an “a priori” character, that is, independent of experience; but such truths, independent of experience, do not exist at all; or rather, this concept, “truth independent of experience,” is merely one of those apparent concepts around which the dispute can go on sine fine, because there is no mortal who could know what is to be understood by this concept. All our knowledge (as I have already sufficiently explained) depends without exception on our impressions; that is, if we had no impressions, we should have no knowledge either. This dependence of knowledge on our impressions the philosophers have called “dependence on experience”: rather unfortunately, for by experience we understand not only impressions but also the knowledge that relates to impressions. Now it makes no sense whatever to make our knowledge dependent not merely on our impressions but also on the knowledge relating to them, for then we should have set knowledge in a relation of dependence upon itself. It is high time to drop the ambiguous, or rather confused, term “dependence on experience” and to replace it with “dependence on impressions.”

But there is a still greater evil: the rationalists (and even so impressionist a thinker as Hume) do not make all our knowledge dependent on experience, that is, on impressions, but imagine that there is a class of our knowledge which is independent of experience, that is, of impressions. From the discussions so far, however, it is sufficiently clear that only an infinitely rapid awareness can possess a knowledge that is independent of all impressions; as a result of which it is out of the question that we should possess a knowledge independent of impressions. But now we can also explain whence arises the semblance that we possess two kinds of knowledge (a priori and a posteriori). This semblance arises from the fact that we must distinguish between the judgments of object-awareness and

p. 177

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

those of self-awareness. The judgments of object-awareness relate to our impressions, and of them it can therefore be said that they depend on our impressions; the judgments of self-awareness, however, do not relate directly to our impressions, but only to the judgments that constate these impressions, whence arises the semblance that they are independent of all impressions. But if we consider that we could not possess self-awareness if we had no object-awareness, we see at once that the judgments of self-awareness cannot be independent of the judgments of object-awareness, that is, of the impressions to which these relate. We gladly admit that the judgments which relate to judgments are of a different kind from those which relate to impressions: but one kind of judgment could not subsist without the other, that is, one kind of our knowledge is not independent of the other kind, and consequently we have no right to divide our knowledge into two such classes as a priori and a posteriori cognitions, of which the former would be valid independently of the latter. In my view, “a priori and a posteriori” are pseudo-concepts of a dualist character, which are to be eliminated from the theory of knowledge and replaced by the judgments of self-awareness and of object-awareness. The error from which the distinction between a priori and a posteriori cognitions arises is to be traced back to the fact that the theory of knowledge has not made a proper distinction between the judgments of object-awareness and those of self-awareness.

As for the judgments of mathematics, their characteristic is that they arise from neutral awareness, that is, from that kind of the activity of awareness which disregards the difference existing between object-awareness and self-awareness. To regard phenomena from the standpoint from which our signs completely replace our impressions (that is, our self-awareness completely replaces our object-awareness): this means to think mathematically. The true mathematical mind, therefore, labors continually to find ever new standpoints from which what we have hitherto been unable to replace with signs becomes replaceable by signs. The mathematician’s ultimate longing would be to make the acquisition of impressions perfectly superfluous by the discovery of signs that perfectly replace impressions. It is evident that this longing is nothing other than the striving for absolute knowledge, for a mind that no longer has need of impressions no longer has need of the crutch of signs either. But even if mathematics can never attain this ultimate goal, its striving is nevertheless clearly indicated by the fact that

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 12

p. 178

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

without pause it strives toward an ever higher development of our neutral awareness. This striving has no limit, for the difference between object-awareness and self-awareness would vanish completely only if we were to attain absolute knowledge. Mathematics is accordingly a science capable of unlimited progress, since however far we may develop our neutral awareness, it does not become absolute awareness.

From this we see at the same time that mathematical knowledge has been given to us as a kind of substitute for absolute knowledge. Absolute, that is, infinitely rapid awareness does not divide in two of itself, that is, does not split into object-awareness and self-awareness, since it has need neither of receiving impressions nor of forming signs. We men cannot attain this absolute unity of object-awareness and self-awareness, but in mathematics we strive instead to neutralize our double activity of awareness. We take objects into view only insofar as they give us occasion for judging, and otherwise the objects themselves no longer concern us. If, for example, I say of some object that it is one, then it necessarily follows from this declaration that it possesses certain properties by virtue of which we can become aware of it; only we do not trouble ourselves further about these properties, and have regard solely to the fact that the object has given us occasion to form a constating judgment. At a dear price, then, do we purchase the mathematical neutralization of our awareness, insofar as we renounce all knowledge which object-awareness can furnish in itself, and strive solely after that knowledge which arises from the fact that the activity of object-awareness gives occasion at the same time for the activity of self-awareness. He who knows a certain multitude of objects in such a way that he holds in memory the characteristic properties of each single object has scarcely any need, properly speaking, to count the objects, for it is enough for him to run his eye over the multitude of individuals and to examine whether all those individuals are present which are sufficiently familiar to him by their particular properties. He who, for example, tends a flock may know each individual of the flock so well that without counting he can tell whether the flock is short or not. But because our faculty of distinguishing and remembering objects is very weak and limited, we gladly have recourse to the exchange of impressions for corresponding signs and to the operation of counting, for which it is a matter of indifference whether we carry it out on the objects themselves or on the signs corresponding to them.

p. 179

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Having determined the concept of neutral awareness at least approximately, we can now proceed to a more thorough investigation of it. For neutral awareness manifests itself not only in an arithmetical but also in a geometrical and dynamical direction, in that we ascribe to phenomena a place in space and time. Our investigation thus leads us over to the theory of space and time.

14. Kant’s Theory of Space and Time.

43. Of the mysteriousness of the nature of space and time we are all convinced: chiefly because we all see that of world-space (cosmic void) and world-time as such no one can have a sensible impression. We can have an impression only of what is extended in space and of what happens in time, but of the cosmic void itself, whether we call it a spatial void or a temporal void, we have no separate impression. Nor would it make sense to say that we have a separate feeling of the void itself and a separate feeling of what is contained in it. But what, then, are we to think of space and time, if in themselves they cannot produce an impression? No one has felt the weight of this question more than Kant, and if his theory of knowledge still exerts a profound and guiding influence on minds today, it owes this chiefly to his theory of space and time. Schopenhauer declares this theory to be so meritorious a creation that it alone suffices to perpetuate Kant’s name. And he is not alone in this conviction, for Kant’s doctrine influences even those who wish to modify it more or less. Hence, whatever conception anyone may have of space and time, he can in no way avoid a settling of accounts with Kant’s theory.

According to Kant, space and time are the original forms of our sensible faculty of cognition. We, who do not recognize two faculties of cognition, a sensible and a non-sensible (intellectual) one, cannot for that reason alone subscribe to a doctrine of space and time which rests on a doubling of the faculty of cognition. We regard every faculty of cognition as non-sensible, that is, intellectual; both the one that constates sensible impressions and the one that constates this constatation anew. Impressions we regard simply as life-phenomena, but the knowledge that relates to them is the fact of a mathematical point of time, and thus

12*

p. 180

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

it cannot be an impression. To become an impression, it must manifest itself in a sentence. But whether we constate an impression or the sentence relating to it, this constatation always takes place through the understanding at a mathematical point of time. Object-awareness and self-awareness are manifestations of one and the same faculty of understanding, and the difference between them lies solely in the object to which they relate. And perhaps it need hardly be said that neutral awareness, too, does not establish a new faculty of cognition, for it is a manifestation of the same understanding as object-awareness or self-awareness.

Nor can we subscribe to Kant’s doctrine of space and time for this reason also, that we cannot — as I have already explained in the introduction — divide cognition into content (matter) and form. This division, however, would never have exerted so enchanting an effect on minds if Kant had spoken only of the matter and forms of the intellectual faculty of cognition. The forms of the intellectual faculty of cognition — (the doctrine of the categories) — by reason of their scholastic character won little popularity from the beginning. Kant’s formalist theory of knowledge owes its enormous effect on minds to the fact that he ascribes original forms not only to the intellectual but also to the sensible faculty of cognition, and that he presents space and time as these forms. Nothing can be more plausible than to call space and time “forms,” even if we do not know what in the world we have gained by replacing the expressions space and time with the technical term “form.” To this was added that these “forms,” according to Kant, were lodged in our mind from the beginning, which could have an all the more bewitching effect on minds because no one knew how this hidden residence of the forms in the mind was to be pictured.

At bottom, even the primitive mode of thinking regards, if not time, then at least space, as a form. We all have a primitive theory of space — (of time we shall speak later) — according to which we regard space as a kind of vessel or form in which the content of the world is contained. Of this world-vessel or world-form we are accustomed to imagine that it must have been there before everything that is contained in it; for bodies can exist only if there is first a space in which they can take up a place, just as, for example, the room must be there first and only then can we put the furniture in it. From this it is seen that even the primitive mode of thinking is inclined to regard space as a form (a vessel), and indeed as an original form

p. 181

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that is, one which precedes the content that has its place in it. If we first examine this primitive thought, to which we all incline only too readily, it will be easy to take up a position toward Kant’s higher-order theory of space as well.

The primitive theory of space in question plays a part in the thinking of even the most cultivated men, because it has penetrated the structure of our language, and, shaping our habitual phrases, exerts dominion over our thoughts as well. To convince ourselves of this, let us simply compare the following two sentences: “The liquid is in the vessel,” “The liquid is in space.” In these two sentences the locative suffix “in” plays linguistically a perfectly equal role, and yet in the two constructions a perfectly different sense attaches to it. To become aware of this difference of sense, it will be best to raise the question what can be the reason that we can pour the liquid out of the vessel but not out of space. To this question we usually receive the answer that it is not even a serious question, for it is obvious that we cannot pour the liquid out of space only because space is infinite. If only the vessel too were infinite, like world-space, then we could not pour the liquid out of it either. — From such an answer one thing at any rate is evident, namely, that we are in fact inclined to regard space as a kind of vessel.

Yet closer consideration at once reveals that an essentially different relation obtains between the vessel and the content in it than between space and the bodies in it. The vessel and its content (the liquid) stand in a relation of contact to one another, in such a way that the liquid touches the vessel along its inner surface. But between a body and that part of space in which it has its place no such relation of contact obtains at all. Where the smallest particles of the body are, there too are the smallest particles of the volume which the body fills. This peculiar relation, differing from all other relations, we can best designate by the expression “perfect mutual interpenetration.” A body can never stand in such a relation to a body. The smallest particles of two bodies cannot occupy the same place; that is, bodies stand toward one another in the relation of complete impenetrability. This relation obtains, then, between the vessel and the liquid in it as well; that is, the smallest particles of the liquid cannot occupy the same place as the vessel’s

p. 182

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

smallest particles. The smallest particles of a volume, on the other hand, coincide completely with the smallest particles of the body in it. Although, then, we use the locative suffix “in” to designate the relation of the vessel and its content as well as that of the volume and the matter in it, yet of these two relations the first has the character of complete impenetrability, the second that of complete mutual interpenetration. If we say, then, that the body is in space, the reverse manner of speaking is also justified, namely that space is in the body. And if we may use the phrase that space is, as it were, the vessel of the body, then conversely the phrase is justified that the body is, as it were, the vessel of the space in it. Or rather, we must beware of both phrases, for between body and space there obtains so perfect a mutual interpenetration that the one cannot be the vessel of the other. And if someone asks why bodies cannot be poured out of space, the answer can only be: because space cannot be poured out of bodies either.

In short, it is a thoroughly mistaken mode of thinking which regards space as a vessel or form, and fancies that space must be there first and only then can bodies take up a place in it. For if space were there first, how in the world could bodies get into it? After all, these bodies would then have had to exist before outside of space, that is, they could not have had a volume. Now what sense is there in imagining bodies first as volumeless and then stuffing them into space, where, swelling up, they acquire extension? I believe that only childish thinking is capable of such a dualist conception.

Kant’s theory of space differs essentially from the primitive conception just characterized. According to the primitive mode of thinking, world-space is something that exists independently of the human mind, whereas according to Kant space is the original form of our mind. From this it is seen that Kant calls space a form only in a figurative sense. For it would indeed be ridiculous to suppose that space and time dwelt in our mind as two real vessels. I do not wish now to press further how perverse it is that we can call space and time vessel or form only in a figurative sense; for the present it is enough to see that, since it is false to take space as a form in the literal sense, it must be false also to take it so in the figurative sense. If space

p. 183

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

is not a vessel of bodies, then the Kantian space which dwells in the mind cannot be, in a figurative sense, a vessel of the sensible impressions appearing in it.

44. At first glance it is seen that one must arrive at an altogether false conception of space if one wishes to understand the relation of space and the bodies in it after the pattern of form and content. But we now pass over to those perversities of Kant’s theory of space which arise from the fact that he regarded space as dwelling in the faculty of cognition from the beginning, whereas our impressions (sensations) he regarded as given from somewhere outside. As a result of this severance, we must regard impressions as things which, before their entry into the forms of our faculty of cognition, possess neither spatial nor temporal character. Kant’s theory compels us to speak of impressions outside of space and time, which become spatial and temporal only by entering into those forms which our sensible faculty of cognition holds ready for them. That is, we should have to distinguish “pure” impressions, which have not yet taken upon themselves the yoke of the forms of space and time, and “empirical” impressions, which have already come under the yoke of these forms. And since what stands outside space and time cannot pass away, while what enters the forms of space and time takes on the character of transitoriness, we should have to speak of impressions which by reason of their being “pure” never pass away, and of impressions which by reason of their being “empirical” have been drawn into the current of transitoriness. What can induce impressions to give up their condition outside space and time and to enter into transitoriness; what, further, those impressions are like that can never pass away; and how, finally, it is possible for them to give up their eternity: these are, of course, mere mysteries which show clearly enough that Kant’s theory harbors a kind of epistemological mythology. Impressions which stand outside space and time and from there arrive in the forms of space and time of our faculty of cognition are inevitably of a mystical nature, and recall the beings of mythology.

But perhaps still far more mystical are those forms which dwell in our sensible faculty of cognition in order to take up into themselves the matter of impressions. Of these forms one can speak in a double sense: in the sense in which these forms are at rest, that is, when they have not yet taken any impression into themselves, as, for example, when we are deeply

p. 184

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

asleep and our sensible faculty of cognition is not working; and, second, in the sense in which these forms are actually filled with the matter of impressions, that is, when we actually sense, when we intuit objects. It is clear that we can intuit the forms, namely the form of space, only when we spread it, as it were like a net, over our impressions; but it is no less clear that the form of space in itself, as it dwells in our mind, in short the empty form of space itself, we cannot intuit. For if the resting or empty form of space which dwells in our mind could be intuited, this would mean that a real vessel is hidden in our faculty of cognition, and whoever observes his faculty of cognition well could also see this vessel in it. Only one who wished to write a parody of Kant’s theory of space could present it in such a light, as though, in the sense of this theory, two real and intuitable vessels or forms were hidden in our sensible faculty of cognition. There is no doubt that Kant regards the forms dwelling in the mind only as forms in a figurative sense, that is, as forms which in themselves can never become intuitable. We see form only when we spread the form dwelling in us over our impressions like a net; that is, we see form only on bodies, but the form in itself, as it dwells in our faculty of cognition, cannot be seen. It is an invisible, incognizable form. Yet Kant presents it as though we could intuit and know this form in itself. That is why he speaks of “pure intuition” (reine Anschauung), which he opposes to empirical intuition (empirische Anschauung). Pure intuition is that which, independently of empirical intuition, establishes the system of truths relating to space, that is, the science of geometry. For Kant regards geometry as a science which establishes its truths (of an a priori character) independently of experience; and this he explains from the fact that we possess a “faculty of pure intuition,” that is, that independently of experience we can intuit the form of space which dwells in our faculty of cognition. But — as I have just shown — Kant himself would protest most vigorously against the notion that he regards the form of space as a real vessel which a man can discover as lying hidden in his own sensible faculty of cognition. At bottom, it follows from Kant’s theory that the form itself, lurking in the faculty of cognition, must be incognizable. What he calls the form of space is a

p. 185

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

mysterious power which causes us to see objects as extended. This mystical cognitive power or activity he names “form,” whereby he deceived himself too, insofar as he persuaded himself that this power itself can be intuited, whereas only its work — the extension, form and position of bodies — is what can be intuited. That original “form” which, according to Kant, lurks in our faculty of cognition must be unknowable. There is, then, no such thing as so-called “pure intuition,” and thus the theory too that the science of geometry rests on a “pure intuition” independent of experience becomes untenable. — The whole theory arose from the ambiguous use of the word “form.” “Form” is, on the one hand, what we see on bodies as extension, shape, position; “form” is, on the other hand, that mystical power which lurks in our mind and which makes it possible for us to see form on bodies. These two kinds of form Kant confuses, and he regards as intuitable also that mystical form which, preceding all experience, lurks in our mind. If we see through this error, it becomes clear to us at once that with the “form” which is supposed to lie hidden in our mind we have gained nothing, since we can know nothing about it. What should we say if someone were to explain the red color of a body by saying that a mysterious power dwells in that body which makes it red? In such an explanation no one could see anything but a multiplication of words. Similarly, no light can be thrown on the nature of space by placing in our mind a mysterious cognitive power or form which causes us to see shape or form on bodies.

Thus, for example, if from the proposition that space is the original form of our sensibility we wished to derive some truth relating to space, say the three-dimensionality of space, we could at once convince ourselves that this is utterly impossible. We can ascribe to the form of space only what we already knew before we became acquainted with the theory of form. If, for example, we already know otherwise that space is three-dimensional, then it is easy to ascribe to the form lurking in our mind that it causes the three-dimensionality of our space. But if space were four-dimensional, then we should again hold our sensible faculty’s form responsible for that. Similarly, if we know that between two points in space the straight line connecting them marks the shortest path, then again it is very easy to assert that the form lurking in our mind does not otherwise allow

p. 186

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

this; but if some curve in space marked the shortest distance between the two points, we could just as calmly declare that we owe this to the form lurking in our mind. In short, the forms lurking in our mind are the most patient creatures imaginable, for they always confirm what we know from geometry, without our having so much as thought of “forms.” It would be altogether a vain endeavor to derive from the proposition that space is a form of a priori character even the slightest new geometrical truth, for the simple reason that, as I proved above, the supposed forms, even if they existed, would be wholly unknowable to us.

From the theory of form we can derive only untenable propositions; for example, that our impressions must stand outside space and time before they enter into the forms of space and time. Further, that our sensible faculty of cognition wholly falsifies our impressions. These impressions in themselves are neither spatial nor temporal, and it is only our faculty of cognition that forces upon them the straitjacket of space and time. Yet it is precisely this conception that has had the greatest bewitching effect on minds. The thought that our impressions are in themselves formless and that only our faculty of cognition endows them with form has thrown thousands of thinkers into a fever, for nothing flatters human vanity so much as, imagining ourselves demigods, to make ourselves believe that out of a formless (spaceless and timeless) mass of impressions we create that picture of the world which appears before us. The impotent vanity of wanting to create drives us to make ourselves believe, at the price of absurdities, that the world would be a formless mass of impressions if the demigodly power of our mind did not shape it into a picture possessing form. For it is absurd to contemplate impressions that would stand outside space and time, and yet only from such impressions, with the help of the forms lurking in our mind, could a spatio-temporal picture of the world be constructed. The dignity of our faculty of cognition, however, is manifested not in our forcing upon our impressions what is not in them, but solely in our recognizing in them only what we find in them. Our mind, however, finds in every passing impression an imperishable reality: on the one hand because such an imperishable reality is truly manifested in each of our impressions, on the other because, receptive to what is eternally valid,

p. 187

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the human mind is constructed precisely so as to find eternal reality in its impressions. In short, our aptitude for truth makes up our one human pride, and from this aptitude we shall explain also — insofar as we are capable of it — why we find extension and duration in our impressions.

What I have set forth above with regard to Kant’s theory of space can be transferred to his theory of time as well. So long as we stand on the position that our knowledge is divisible into two factors (the matter and the form of cognition), we shall become entangled in contradictions, whatever epistemological problem we may treat. But although we cannot make Kant’s theory of space and time our own, we readily acknowledge that profound presentiments lurk in this theory, and that it was Kant who saw the immeasurable epistemological importance of the doctrine of space and time better than his sensualist and rationalist predecessors.

15. Neutral Awareness.

45. Whatever impression we constate, the concepts “here” and “now” must necessarily play a part in our judgments. The theory of constating judgments — and with it the principle of identity too — would remain very deficient if we did not occupy ourselves more thoroughly with the concepts of space and time. In every physical science the determinations of the place and duration of phenomena are of fundamental significance, so much so that without such determinations serious investigation of nature is not even conceivable. Yet there are scarcely any concepts about which greater confusion prevails, even among investigators of nature, than precisely the concepts of space and time. This confusion also avenges itself in every branch and chapter of the natural sciences, even where we should least suspect it.

By the concept of space and time the sensualist thinker is most hampered, for he can admit only the existence of impressions (or memories), and he does not dare to assert that space and time in themselves appear as separate impressions. He is compelled, therefore, to devise certain mysterious psychical processes which would cause the impression, which in itself would be neither

p. 188

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

extended nor possessed of duration, nevertheless somehow arrive at extension and duration. All such theories arise from the fatal error according to which the process of our cognition would be nothing other than a kind of falsifying procedure which adds to our impressions something that is not in them. Many think they can avoid this dangerous whirlpool by assuming (following Lotze) so-called “local signs” in our impressions, which would be appointed to inform us about the spatial position of our impressions. But then we should have to assume not only local but also “temporal signs,” which would be called upon to indicate the temporal position of our impressions; such temporal signs, however, hardly anyone would venture to argue into our impressions. In general, the local and temporal signs would be nothing other than finer secondary impressions, which would have their place in the primary impressions in order to give information about their spatio-temporal position. But it is evident that such a theory harbors a self-contradiction; for suppose that the local sign of some impression a1 is an impression a2 lying hidden in it, then this impression a2 itself needs a still newer impression a3 to indicate to us its place in space.
