Similarly, in the impression a3 there would have to lie an impression a4 which fixes the place of a3 in space, and so on to infinity. From this absurdity we could escape only by assuming that the impression a2 takes no place either in space or in time and is appointed solely to give information about the place or duration of the impression a1. But to assume an impression a2 which takes no place either in space or in time is sheer absurdity, as a result of which we must give up the theory of local signs, in whatever form it may appear, as logically untenable.

We have no impressions specially appointed to give information about spatial position, and precisely in this lies the mysteriousness of the nature of space and time. If we had separate impressions of the spatial and temporal position of appearances, that is, if we had a separate sense of space and a separate sense of time, then everyone would regard extension and duration as qualities of the same kind as, for example, color, sound, warmth, smoothness, sweetness, etc., and then it would occur to no one to wonder at the nature of space and time otherwise

p. 189

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

than at natural phenomena such as a red flame or a loud bang, etc. Physics and chemistry would then investigate space and time just as they investigate the electric spark or the decomposition of compounds. But there is no thinker who does not feel that space and time are something other than light, sound, electricity, etc., for everyone is clear that we can speak of light only because we have impressions of light, of sound because we have impressions of sound, etc.; but of space and time we have no separate impressions appointed to them. Precisely on this account arises the notable question how we can ascribe to appearances a spatial and temporal position, when for indicating this position we have at our disposal no separate impressions of space and no separate impressions of time.

Before we answer this question, we must first see that we apprehend spatial and temporal position by our neutral awareness. As regards, first of all, the placing of appearances in space, we can examine this spatial placing just as well on the placed objects themselves as on the drawn signs which we make of the objects. We call descriptive geometry the science which teaches us how the investigation of the spatial relations of phenomena can be perfectly replaced by those drawn signs which we make for ourselves in their place. On the basis of the explanation which we have given of neutral awareness, we can therefore declare that our intuition of space is the work of neutral awareness.

When we have regard to our impressions only insofar as they can be perfectly replaced by drawn signs, that is, when we examine them with neutral awareness, then our intuition, our attention, takes on a geometrical character, that is, becomes mere intuition of space. In general, our impressions can never be replaced completely, whether by graphic or other signs. There is no sign in the world which could perfectly replace for us the weight, smell, taste, warmth and other physical or chemical properties of bodies, for if we possessed such signs, we should no longer need immediate impressions, nor observations, nor experiments, but should investigate all kinds of properties of the existing bodily world on our own self-made wonder-signs. Or rather, we should no longer need such investigations at all, for if our self-made signs could perfectly replace the world itself, just as it

p. 190

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

appears in our impressions, then properly speaking we should be the creators of that world, and so should no longer need to investigate and cognize it. Only our frail human condition makes us rely in cognition on impressions on the one hand and on signs on the other, and indeed on signs which can never perfectly replace the impressions themselves. But if we possessed signs which perfectly replaced the impressions themselves, this would mean that the difference between impression and sign would cease. The sign which we should create would be the weight, color, scent, heat, life of the bodies themselves; in short, we should be the creating gods of the world. For this reason we regard infinitely rapid awareness as such that no impression holds it back in its awareness, and which accordingly does not make for itself frail signs incapable of replacing the impressions, but creates the world, that is, produces beings who take cognizance of it by means of impressions and signs (that is, by awareness of finite speed).

But although our signs cannot perfectly replace our impressions, there is nevertheless something in our signs which can be recognized in them just as well as in our original impressions. Thus on our drawn signs we can study spatial relations just as well as on the objects themselves, that is, on the impressions themselves which the objects arouse. To intuit objects with neutral awareness means, then, to intuit in them what we can just as well intuit on our drawn signs: that is, it means that in our impressions we disregard everything that we cannot replace with our drawn signs, and direct our attention solely to what we can perfectly replace with drawn signs.

46. We have called the work of neutral awareness our counting judgments also, which form the foundation of the science of arithmetic. It is entirely indifferent whether we count the objects themselves or the judgments that constate these objects; that is, here too our impressions are replaced by the signs (judgments) relating to them. But the signs with which we count are of a different kind from those drawn signs with which we depict spatial relations. Counting makes use of speech-signs, for it depends on the forming of sentences such as “this is one,” “this is yet one more,” etc., etc. The drawn signs, however, have an entirely different character, for these are not sentences and words, but are themselves intuitable things which stand before us as partial imitations, copies, of our original impressions. The straight

p. 191

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

line which we draw is meant to be an imitation of that edge which we see on an angular body; and similarly the drawn triangles, quadrilaterals, circles and other curved lines are imitations of those figures which the objects too present to intuition. To avoid misunderstandings, however, we must emphasize that even such geometrical signs could not play the role of signs if we did not relate sentences to them which give expression to their function. If, for example, someone, drawing a straight line, were not aware that his stroke represents a straight line, that is, if he could not form in thought the sentence “this stroke represents a straight line,” then that stroke could not serve him as a sign at all. Geometrical signs, then, can be signs only because we can form such judgments as: “this is a straight line,” “this is a circle,” “this is a spiral,” and so on. This must be particularly emphasized because otherwise the dangerous semblance arises that we have two kinds of signs: signs which arise as a result of our acts of judgment, and these are our speech-signs, and signs which originate not from judging but from the imitation of our impressions, such as, for example, the signs of geometry. Such a classification would rest on a glaring logical error, for by signs in the theory of knowledge we can understand only such perceptible actions as arise in connection with judging. The lines too which we draw as geometrical signs are signs only because they appear accompanied by judgments; namely, accompanied by such judgments as “this is a straight line,” “this is a triangle,” etc., etc. The figures which the geometer draws stand in place of sentences; that is, in other words, geometrical figures are signs only because we interpret them with sentences.

We shall not fall, then, into that logical dualism of regarding our counting signs and our geometrical signs as essentially different in nature. Whether we form counting judgments, or draw geometrical figures to make certain propositions intuitable: in both cases we form our signs by judging. And that it is one and the same judging power which forms counting sentences and which makes truths intuitable by drawings, we can show most beautifully by being able to translate our drawn signs into the language of our arithmetical (algebraic) signs. It was Descartes who taught mathematicians to express in equations (that is, in algebraic sentences) what geometry makes intuitable by lines. Thus, for example, ax + by + c = 0

p. 192

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

is the equation of some straight line (in the plane); that is, in other words: all the properties of the straight line which we can study on the drawing we can study also, disregarding the drawing, on the equation ax + by + c = 0. That beautiful parallelism which runs throughout between abstract mathematics and geometry arises simply from the fact that it is one and the same neutral awareness or judging which intuits geometrical truths on figures, or expresses abstract judgments of quantity in algebraic sentences (equations). (Instead of the word equation we may freely use the expression algebraic or, more generally, mathematical sentence, for equations are abbreviated expressions of sentences which in living speech could be set forth only at very great length.)

If someone practices himself sufficiently in depicting spatial relations by graphic signs, he can as a result of this practice come to dispense with the graphic signs to a great extent, and already sees in imagination what the less practiced can establish only by actually drawing the graphic signs. The skillful geometer can carry out in imagination such constructions as the less gifted can follow only with great difficulty on actual drawings. When we expect geometrical instruction to develop the intuition of space, what we really desire is that the pupil should more and more be able to do without actual drawing, replacing it by drawing in imagination. With constructions carried out in imagination in geometry it is as with so-called silent (inward-turned) thinking in general. Just as the silent thinker glides rapidly through sentences which he does not actually utter, so the contemplative geometer too rapidly builds his spatial figures without actually drawing them. The one thinks rather with imagined speech-signs, the other rather with imagined graphic signs, but in essence the one mode of thinking, just like the other, is nothing but judging which rests on signs, and indeed not on signs actually produced, but only on signs or movements carried out in imagination.

Silent thinking, as we have already mentioned several times, seduces men into the error that we are capable of thinking and seeing truths without the use of any signs. This is just the same error as that into which the geometer would fall if he persuaded himself that he, because in fact he casts no visible drawings upon paper,

p. 193

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

performs no constructions even in imagination, but finds his geometrical truths without the use of any signs. Yet he who never drew figures could never acquire even that practice by which he is able to dispense with drawing and to replace it by imagined signs (movements). There is, altogether, no human thinking or judging which does not rest on the forming of signs, whether signs perceptible by others, or signs not perceptible by others, or such as, because of the rapidity with which they are used, the person himself does not even deem worthy of proper attention. For this very reason we cannot accept the theory of so-called “pure reason,” for such a pure reason would have to think without the support not only of impressions but also of signs, and of this only infinitely rapid awareness would be capable. For the same reason we cannot accept the theory of “pure intuition” (reine Anschauung) either, for “pure intuition” too would not be permitted to rest on constructions carried out in imagination. For constructions carried out in imagination become possible only through our having first carried out constructions in fact: just as silent reading becomes possible only through our having first learned to read aloud.

If we wish to enter into a deeper discussion of the signs used by neutral awareness, we must first occupy ourselves still more thoroughly with object-awareness. In general, indeed, we have already examined the judgments of object-awareness, namely the constating judgments based on immediate impressions and on memory-images; but we have not yet taken into account that our impressions may belong to the sphere of different senses, as a result of which the theory of judgments relating to impressions has still remained very deficient in spite of all our efforts hitherto. We now turn, therefore, to the examination of our constating judgments from the standpoint of the sense to whose sphere the constated impressions belong.

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 13

p. 194

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

16. Object-Awareness.

47. Although it may seem an insignificant matter when we speak of five, six, seven or still more senses, this enumeration of the spheres of sense has nevertheless had an enormously disturbing effect on the development of the doctrine of the senses, and thus indirectly of the theory of knowledge as well. If we take into account that the senses of taste and smell function only when larger or smaller, or invisibly minute, particles of foreign bodies penetrate our organism and set certain of our mucous membranes in excitement, then this circumstance alone is sufficient for us not to regard taste and smell as senses of the same kind as touch, sight and hearing, for whose functioning it is by no means required that minute particles of other bodies should enter our organism. I should not at all consider it expedient to call senses those excitements which arise in our organism strictly on the ground that nutritive and reproductive processes take place in it, for then we should have to classify the phenomena arising from sexual stimulation too as separate senses. The impressions of touch, sight and hearing must be separated from all those excitements or stimulations which arise in our organism in connection with the vegetative processes going on in it (nutrition, reproduction). To these vegetative excitements belong the stimulations of taste and smell, of heat and cold, of hunger and thirst, etc., which for the theorist of knowledge are of only secondary significance. As senses properly so called we should have to regard only the so-called higher senses: touch, sight and hearing, whose wonderful cooperation forms the foundation of our object-awareness, and consequently of all our knowledge. For the theorist of knowledge only these three senses properly exist; or rather, everything that anyone might still classify as a 4th, 5th, 6th, 7th . . . and heaven knows what sense, he is inclined to regard only as a vegetative stimulation, as the following discussions will justify more fully.

Of the three senses mentioned, again, the first place belongs to touch (from the epistemological standpoint), for without it object-awareness could not even be spoken of. In general, when we speak of impressions, it is best to think first of all of the impressions of the sense of touch, for

p. 195

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

these represent, as it were, the type of what we understand by impression. One of the chief errors of the impressionist (sensualist) schools arises from the fact that, when they speak of sensations or ideas, they think first of all of the images of the sense of sight. These images easily give a very false concept of what is to be understood by impression. For the characteristic property of impressions of touch is that through them we can become aware not only of some foreign object but at the same time of our own body as well. Now by impression we originally understand a life-process on the basis of which we distinguish our own body from other objects. If, on the other hand, we think of those images which the sense of sight conveys, we easily fall into the error of supposing that by means of our impressions we have to become aware only of foreign phenomena, and not at the same time of our own organism as well. Touch, on the contrary, furnishes the complete type of the impression, for through it we can become aware not only of foreign bodies but at the same time of our own body. For this reason we distinguish two principal forms of touch, according as it conveys rather the taking cognizance of other bodies or rather that of our own organism. When we ourselves touch some object, attention to our own body for the most part recedes into the background; if, on the contrary, someone else touches our body, attention to the state of our own body usually comes to the fore. If, however, we ourselves touch one part of our body with another, then it may happen that the two principal forms of touch rapidly alternate with each other, and our attention is directed now more to the touching, now more to the touched part. In general, the impressions of the sense of touch show an immeasurable variety, so that to classify them would mean almost as much as to divide the phenomena of nature into groups. The main thing, therefore, is that in the concept of the impression of touch we should not let drop that essential factor by which touch truly becomes what it is. This essential factor consists in this, that by the sense of touch we always distinguish two bodies, one of which is our own body. For even when we press one hand with the other, the pressed hand plays the part of a foreign body in relation to the pressing hand. If some body rests quietly on our palm, it is indeed very hard to say what we feel more: that body or our own palm; but this state too we can conceive as one in which, in rapid beat, we feel in alternation now the body itself more, then, in opposition to it, our palm’s

13*

p. 196

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

resistance to it that we feel more, and the waves of this rhythm we are unable to separate properly only because the waves follow one another very quickly.

In touch we must distinguish a direct sensation and a counter-sensation, corresponding to the action and reaction that obtain between the body touched and our organism. I do not say that the direct sensation and the counter-sensation could actually be separated from each other as two successive phases of the tactile sensation; but even if this does not succeed, there is no doubt that there are tactile sensations in which the sensation of the surface of our own body predominates, and again other tactile sensations in which the sensation of the surface of the foreign body prevails. This can be explained only by supposing that in the sensation of resistance which arises in the course of touch we distinguish an influence deriving from the foreign body and a reaction proceeding from our organism. When we lift some weight, the reaction on our part manifests itself as a lifting movement; but in general the sensation of resistance contained in touch could not arise if no reaction were put forth against the influence. This reaction appears either in visible, or in very slight and barely perceptible, tensions and movements of the muscles. The counter-sensation contained in touch therefore arises from those often very slight tensions of the skin, or rather from invisible undulating movements, which the influence of the foreign body arouses as a reaction. If our body put forth no such reaction, then no tactile sensation would arise either. In short, if the counter-sensation were absent, we could have no direct sensation either. What we call a tactile impression is the inseparable unity of a direct sensation and the corresponding counter-sensation.

We must conceive our organism in general in such a way that the mechanical influences which touch its skin release, under all circumstances, involuntary, that is, reflex movements. These reflex movements are either such that they can be perceived, or so slight that they cannot even appear as motions. But if we consider that the surface of the living body is at rest only in appearance and is in reality in a rhythmic undulation, then we easily understand that our skin does not remain inactive when it comes into contact with another body, but that it is precisely its invisible compressions and invisible counter-tensions that produce the tactile impression. Only in the dead body do these slightest reflex movements fail to occur, but there the sensation itself fails to occur as well.

p. 197

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

If, then, we regard touch as the typical sense, we may boldly declare that our sensation could not arise if we were incapable of reacting to impressions with movements. With regard to the other two senses, sight and still more hearing, the truth of the physiological proposition indicated is recognized only with difficulty; but with regard to touch everyone can notice that there is no tactile impression that could arise without the accompaniment of slight movements — by these slight movements being understood those invisible undulations and vibrations which distinguish the living body from the corpse. If theorists of knowledge had always had in mind the fundamental sense, touch, in determining the concept of sense, then that fundamental truth, which countless thinkers have surmised and even expressed, would never have been obscured: that without movements no sensations can arise either, that is, that the living body, even when it appears to be wholly at rest, is in an invisible action without which it would not be capable of sensing at all. Toward impressions our organism does not behave with sheer passivity (as, for example, wax does toward the impressions that act upon it), but responds to the impression with activity, and it is precisely to these activities (that is, movements) that the organism owes its sensitivity. If this activity ceases, then sensitivity too has ceased. Only a being that can respond with movements to the impressions that affect it can possess sensitivity; but a being that would be altogether incapable, under any circumstances, of responding with movements to the influences affecting it, to that we should not ascribe sensitivity at all. Some natural scientists are inclined to ascribe sensitivity even to certain plants, because these plants display a certain mobility in relation to sunlight or other agents. In general mobility forms the criterion of sensitivity too — at least in relation to other beings — as every investigator of nature readily admits. The trouble is only that they often cannot properly distinguish mechanical motion from living movement.

If we accustom ourselves to put sensitivity into inseparable connection with motility, then we shall not form for ourselves such a false concept of impressions (sensations) as is regularly met with among the impressionists since Locke. We at once regard a certain living movement as inseparably belonging to the impression, a movement which, however,

p. 198

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

so slight that it remains invisible to us. This movement is what appears as counter-sensation in opposition to the impression that acts upon us. We therefore conceive every impression as composed of sensation and counter-sensation, because we cannot conceive sensitivity without motility, and because our movements appear as counter-sensations within our impressions.

48. The concept of counter-sensation must not be confused with the so-called “muscle sensations.” In physiology the muscle sensations figure as a separate class of sensations; but what is called counter-sensation is not a separate sensation, but forms, together with the direct sensation itself, that which we may call an impression. As in the mechanical collision of bodies there is no action without reaction, so in the physiological collision (that is, in touch) there is no sensation without counter-sensation. It is an interesting thing that we must form the concept of touch, that is, of physiological collision, on the model of mechanical collision, and carry Newton’s doctrine of actio and reactio into physiology as the principle of sensation and counter-sensation; whereas Newton conversely carried the presentiment of sensation and counter-sensation into the thought of actio and reactio, for it is evident that a man would never have had the slightest concept of any action and reaction if in our tactile sensation we did not find the action which the foreign body exerts upon us and the reaction which our organism puts forth against that body. Of the collision of two spheres we could form no correct concept whatever if we could not put our own body in the place of one of the colliding spheres and did not experience the phenomenon of the collision of the sphere and our body. From this consideration it appears that properly the thought of sensation and counter-sensation is present in us first, and only afterwards do we remold this thought into the principle of action and reaction in mechanics. In spite of this, the thought of sensation and counter-sensation has remained only an obscure presentiment, and indeed impressionist theory of knowledge discards this presentiment altogether, insofar as it does not notice that a sensation cannot subsist without counter-sensation: at least not within the sphere of touch.

Corresponding to sensation and to the counter-sensation that completes it, we must distinguish in our nerves double currents, namely direct currents and counter-currents. With this proposition I am by no means thinking to solve the great mystery of the nature of the nerve-currents; yet I believe that for the knowledge of the nature of these nerve-currents the

p. 199

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

first step is taken by distinguishing the direct currents from the counter-currents inseparably connected with them, the latter corresponding to the counter-sensation. As in the electric current we must distinguish positive and negative electricity, so too the propagated nervous excitation can be explained only by the theory of current and counter-current. The consequences of this conception for nerve physiology and nerve anatomy I cannot draw here, since this would lead far away from my subject. I hope, however, that in some forthcoming treatise I shall be able to give a detailed account of the conception which I have formed for myself of the structure and function of the nervous system.

Whereas in touch sensation and counter-sensation appear in inseparable connection, sight and hearing present sensation and counter-sensation, so to speak, in separation. As to sight in the first place, it conveys such impressions as those in which the direct sensation predominates with preponderant force over the counter-sensation. He who is absorbed in looking at objects becomes aware, by means of the images obtained, not of his own organism but of the objects. In other words, the images of sight are impressions in which the sensation so far predominates over the counter-sensation that we scarcely attend to the latter, as a result of which we hardly notice that for sight some activity of our own organism is needed. Hence arises the extraordinary mysteriousness of the sense of sight, which so greatly distinguishes it from touch. For whereas the sense of touch makes us feel our own body together with the foreign body, the sense of sight directs our attention one-sidedly to the body seen, suppressing our faculty of perception relating to our own eye, that is, to our own retina. One’s own retina is in general felt by no one when he looks at objects; that is, the visual sensations suppress the counter-sensations by means of direct sensations. Hence it is that impressionism, which places the greatest weight precisely on visual sensations, determines the concept of sensation quite one-sidedly, leaving out of it the counter-sensation, which, moreover, cannot be wholly lacking in sight either. For our eyes to be fit for sight they must be movable; and these eye-movements, together with the movements of our head and body taken as a whole, play such a part in sight as if our eye were allied with an organ of touch. For through the movements of our own parts of the body one part exerts an action upon another and puts forth a reaction against it, in consequence of which sensations and counter-sensations

p. 200

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

arise as an entire complicated system, and these always appear in connection with our visual sensations. To this is added that by the use of the two eyes we actually look at objects from two points of view, which is to some extent equivalent to our having made a movement from the one point of view toward the other. Finally it is to be taken into account that we could never have learned the use of our sense of sight if from early childhood we had not practiced the joint use of sight and touch, and the use of them in a single sense. It is in any case a very interesting problem how in general we were able to bring touch into that connection with sight in which we use them; but here it suffices, without any explanation, to appeal to the bare fact itself, that touch and sight are sensible functions so wonderfully adapted to one another as if both expressed the same thing only in two different sensible languages, and as if we could translate a tactile impression at once into the sense of sight and, conversely, a visual impression at once into the sense of touch. From this it is clear that sight can subsist without such counter-sensations as touch affords only because it stands with touch in the closest, so to speak Siamese, twinship. It is clear, further, that our sight would at once lose all its capacity for orientation if that wonderful connection which exists between it and touch were broken. I remark that this connection, which is one of the most interesting problems of the theory of the senses, is much neglected, and has hardly yet been formulated with proper clarity either in physiology or in the philosophical sciences.

As for hearing, in it the counter-sensation comes to dominate the direct sensation, so that from this point of view hearing is as it were the opposite of sight. There are those who express this opposition by calling sight objective and hearing subjective, or by saying that sight gives information rather about the order of extension, hearing rather about the order of time: but all these parallels are to be traced back to the fact that in sight the counter-sensation is suppressed, whereas in hearing it is precisely the counter-sensation that comes to dominate. It is true that through hearing we obtain some information about the qualities of sounding bodies, and even, to some extent, about their position in space: but this is possible only because our hearing develops in connection with sight and touch. Although, then, in the sounds heard there always lies some reference to that bodily

p. 201

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

world that surrounds our organism, yet it is not these references that prevail in hearing; on the contrary, the sound as sound comes truly into its own only when the visual and tactile impressions of the sounding bodies themselves do not disturb us, that is, when the whole world of appearances which touch and sight lay before us is pushed into the background and the sound appears to us as if we ourselves were sounding in it. The close connection of sounds with our world of feelings rests also on this: that in sounds the self-feeling of the organism, that is, the counter-sensation, dominates over the sensation of foreign bodies.

49. By the foregoing discussions I wish only to bring out that a certain design manifests itself in the constitution of our senses, and that without investigating this design we can understand neither the distinct character of the function of the several senses nor their joint activity. Physiology, and still more so-called physiological psychology, accustoms us to regard the spheres of the several senses in isolation, whereby our attention is diverted from the fundamental question of the theory of the senses, from the cooperation of the senses, from their organic unity, whereas without investigating this organic unity we cannot understand even the separate nature of the individual sensible spheres. If we keep in mind that we may take touch as the fundamental sense, it at once occurs to us too that the absence of all tactile capacity involves at the same time the cessation of all sensibility. An organism which could under no circumstances arrive at tactile impressions, or rather at sensations of resistance, could never obtain a basis for distinguishing its own body from foreign bodies, without which object-awareness is not even conceivable. The sense of touch by itself already furnishes us with all the most necessary data from which we can build up the world of our object-awareness, and for this very reason we called touch the typical sense. Touch is not so much one sense among the others as rather sensibility itself, because independence is found in it alone, and because with its final cessation, that is, its absolute elimination, all other sensuous qualities also vanish; hence we can say that sight and hearing are not to be regarded as separate independent senses. If we consider, further, that sensation and counter-sensation, which appear in touch in inseparable conjunction, come to dominate one-sidedly within the spheres of sight and hearing, then we begin to surmise that there lies a certain design in the fact that our touch is still

p. 202

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

supported by two such senses as sight and hearing; and then we involuntarily think that for the full development of our sensibility we really need two higher senses, and only two, besides the fundamental sense, namely sight and hearing, of which the one brings the direct sensation, and the other the counter-sensation, to the fullest possible triumph.

That this surmise of ours may assume a clearer form, however, we must subject the connection of the three senses with one another to a more searching examination. Physiology (or physiological psychology) could not illuminate this connection properly chiefly because from the beginning it kept in view only that a sensation of resistance, a sensation of color and a sensation of sound are so incomparable in their quality that it is impossible to combine them in some common fundamental sensation. Some evolutionist thinkers did indeed make a kind of desperate attempt to develop the impressions of color and sound out of the sense of touch, but such an attempt was suited only to prove what a logical absurdity it is to develop sensations of resistance gradually to the point of becoming sensations of color or sensations of sound. In general we must proceed very cautiously with the concept of “development,” for if we say of some a that it can become b, we very easily come into conflict with the principle of identity, in consequence of which we could develop anything out of anything. The evolutionary mode of thinking stands nearest to the sophist’s conjuring, which under its hand can change anything into anything. The concept of development must first be subjected to the most rigorous epistemological examination, in order that against our will we may not be swept into sophisms: but it is precisely this epistemological examination that the evolutionary doctrines usually remain in debt for.

It would be a vain labor to transform sensations of resistance, by whatever conjuring, so that they should become sensations of color. But if we recall those discussions in which we showed that in each of our sensations we must distinguish the impression-wave from the memory-wave, then perhaps we may find the way which leads step by step toward enlightenment in the great problem of the connection of the senses, or, if you will, of the unity of the senses.

Every sensation of ours — as we explained — is at the same time a memory, although that in it which is memory we cannot in fact separate from that in it which is impression. Only when the original

p. 203

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

sensation is already past and we return to it, involuntarily or willingly, there arise our substitute experiences, which we called memory-replicas, and which are suited to remind us that we should regard even the original experience itself as a memory such as is awakened and maintained in us by the immediate presence of the object. This conception of ours we can now clarify still further by examining separately the memory-replicas of the several spheres of our senses. At the same time it will also become evident that the cooperation of our different senses is possible only because their impression-waves are completed by memory-waves, and because it is these memory-waves that hold together the sensations of the different sensible spheres, which differ so greatly in quality.

If a man tries to remember a body merely on the basis of his tactile impression, he notices that this does not succeed, because his visual memories are so grown together with his tactile memories that, when the former are called up, the latter appear along with them. This state of affairs is indeed well enough known to physiologists (and psychologists), but its explanation is still very defective. We cannot properly put ourselves in the position of a man born blind, because we are not able to work with tactile memories in such a way as completely to exclude our visual memories. The shape which we imagine as felt by touch stands before us at the same time as a seen shape too, and however much we contrive, we cannot wholly suppress our visual image, so that the shape might live in our imagination, or rather in our memory, only as a shape felt by touch. In the mind of the man born blind, however, the memory of the shape of bodies can live only as a tactile memory, so that even with the greatest effort we are unable to imagine how they remember shapes never seen and merely felt by touch.

The cooperation of touch and sight is possible only because tactile and visual memories can thus grow together. We must therefore investigate how it is possible that the memories of two entirely different spheres of sense come into such inseparable connection with one another. That is, for example, how is it possible that the shape felt by touch in imagination unavoidably stands before us as the shape seen in imagination as well, and that the shape felt appears in our imagination as the very same as the shape seen: as if we thought of them not even with two different senses, but with the help of a single sense alone.

p. 204

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

A little reflection can convince anyone that the imagination of touch is essentially the same as the imagination of sight. The original tactile impression and the original visual impression — insofar as the one contains a sensation of resistance and the other a sensation of color — are indeed wholly different from one another: but as soon as we pass over to touching in imagination and seeing in imagination, we find at once that which is identical in the two kinds of imagining. For if we feel something by touch in imagination, we make imagined movements, or, better said, we imagine that we are making movements, and indeed we strive to imagine just the same movements as we need in order actually to feel the object all around. In short: our tactile memory or tactile imagination rests on our imitating in imagination the movements which play their part in actual touching. These imagined movements, however, must be carefully distinguished from actual movements. It is the actual movements that make our counter-sensation arise in touch; with imagined movements, however, since they are not carried out in reality, such counter-sensations cannot arise. From this it appears that when we pass from the sphere of actual touch to that of imagined touch, imagined movements take the place of our actual movements, so that those counter-sensations which accompany the actual movements fail to appear with the imagined movements. But if the counter-sensation drops out of touch, then the difference in principle between touch and sight has ceased. In the world of imagined movements, therefore, we need make no distinction between touch and sight, because in imagined movements the counter-sensations play just as small a part as in our visual images.

It is, then, the imagined movements that make our touch and our sight one sense. When the shape of bodies is disclosed to us by our tactile sensations, the noticing of this shape becomes possible only through those imagined movements with which we feel the body all around. But in sight too it is imagined movements that determine what we call the seen shape of bodies. Hence it is that touch and sight are capable of cooperation, and this finds its explanation in the fact that when we touch some body and at the same time see it with our eyes, we are fully convinced of the identity of the body touched and the body seen. For our tactile memories rest on imagined movements just as our visual

p. 205

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

memories do. If we wish to call up in our memory the shape of some body, whether felt by touch or seen, in both cases we shall need the same imagined movements, and the two callings-up will differ at most in this: that in visual memories imagined eye-movements can also play a part, whereas in tactile memories nothing of the kind occurs. That we nevertheless ascribe two different memories to touch and to sight rests only on the fact that imagined colors and imagined sensations of resistance already differ essentially from one another. But the imagined movements, which must unite now with imagined sensations of color, now with imagined sensations of resistance, in order to give the memory-image of an object, are identical. That is, the same imagined movements must be paired on the one hand with imagined sensations of color, and on the other with imagined sensations of resistance, in order that a visual and at the same time tactile memory of a body may stand before us.

How our hearing is connected with our senses of touch and sight is a far more complicated question. But without entering into a detailed analysis of this weighty physiological problem, we can establish in general here too that imagined movements play a decisive part in the cooperation of hearing with the other two senses. To see this it suffices to point to the fact of silent reading, when we pronounce in imagination the words which we see written or printed. This pronouncing of the sounds in imagination is possible only through imagined movements. For those movements which in reading aloud we actually carry through with our vocal organs, we carry out in silent reading only in imagination. Our sound-memory, or sound-fantasy, therefore rests on our bringing our vocal organs into play in imagination, that is, on our having imagined movements performed in these organs. These can then be associated with the imagined movements of sight and touch, and make it possible for our three senses to cooperate as if they represented only the threefold manifestation of a single sense.

Our whole object-memory and fantasy rests on imagined movements. Physiology, however, has thrown very little light on the nature of these imagined movements, because in the investigation of our sensibility it did not at once include the investigation of our movements: believing that the two subjects can be separated from one another. (I remark that I use the terms “movement” and “motility”

p. 206

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

deliberately, because I reserve the expressions “motion” and “mobility” for the change of place of inanimate bodies, whereas the change of place of living organisms is better designated by movement and motility.) Now there is no sensitivity without motility, and if we take this into account, we at once see also that our sensations are necessarily paired with counter-sensations. These counter-sensations, however, appear not only when we respond with some movement to an immediate impression, but also when we live through something in memory. What is more, that which we call memory or imagination can develop only through our impressions being paired with movements and, in consequence, with counter-sensations. For the more developed a living being is, the more capable it is not only of performing certain movements but also of checking them. These movements checked in their inception are those which then appear as imagined movements. Whatever we may remember and whatever we may picture to ourselves, such checked movements play their part in our nervous system, and these are nothing other than nerve-currents which flow in the opposite direction from those nerve-currents that arise through the immediate impression. If we call centripetal the nerve-current which, under the action of impressions, travels from the surface of the body toward the brain, then the opposite, centrifugal current appears when we carry out checked, that is, imagined, movements. These centrifugal currents make possible all remembering and, in general, all imagining.

In present-day physiology there is mention only of such centrifugal currents as, proceeding from a center (or from lower-order ganglia), flow to the muscles and cause their contraction: but here I speak of such centrifugal currents as flow back from the center to the sensible apparatus itself. These counter-currents make it possible for memory-replicas of sounds, visual images and spatial shapes to arise in us, that is, for us to be able to imagine such things.

p. 207

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

17. The Connection between the Theory of Knowledge and Physiology.

50. All our discussions hitherto give clear evidence of the close, or rather inseparable, connection of the theory of knowledge and physiology. This connection we can express briefly thus: all our cognition or knowledge rests on life-phenomena, that is, without life-phenomena we can under no circumstances arrive at knowledge or cognition. The chief defect of ancient logic was that it did not bring the investigation of knowledge into the closest connection with the investigation of our life-process. For although it connected the investigation of knowledge with the observation of our speech-signs, and these speech-signs are operations of our life-process: yet in these signs it inquired not so much into the life-phenomena as rather into the meaning expressed by them, so that it came to confound the investigation of meaning with the investigation of the audible or visible connection of signs. Thus arose that rationalist inclination which, in the observation of signs, forgets that it is observing signs and believes that it takes cognizance only of the meaning, without the mediation of any signs. From this self-deception sprang such false notions as “pure reason,” “pure thinking,” “pure cognition,” etc., in which there secretly lurks the conception that we have a cognition which does not rest on any physiological process, that is, on any life-phenomenon.

The great significance of modern theory of knowledge lies in this: that it brings the investigation of knowledge into the closest connection with the investigation of our life-process. But, led by this very correct endeavor, it let itself be drawn into an artificial opposition to ancient logic, in consequence of which it eyed the signs of human speech with suspicion, and its chief endeavor was to found the investigation of knowledge, with the greatest possible disregard of the signs of speech, on the doctrine of impressions. So it came about that the modern logical tendency became in a certain respect unfaithful to itself, in that it neglected the thorough investigation of signs as life-phenomena and as an indispensable condition of knowledge. Yet if we are guided by the thought that the investigation of knowledge must be brought into inseparable connection with the investigation of the phenomena of life, then we shall regard the study of signs as just as important a task as the scrutiny of impressions, since signs are life-phenomena in precisely the same way as

p. 208

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

impressions are, and without signs there is just as little knowledge as without impressions. The neglect of the investigation of signs has had immeasurably many harmful consequences: for example, that a separate branch arose, the so-called philosophy of language, which, in inquiring into the origin of language, did not notice that it was really dealing with the fundamental question of logic or the theory of knowledge. What belongs most closely to the theory of knowledge has dropped out of the theory of knowledge, and had there not been gifted writers and investigators of language (Herder, Wilhelm von Humboldt, etc.), we should be groping, as regards the significance of language, in the same darkness in which Bacon and his impressionist successors left the human mind.

But the neglect of the investigation of signs has also had this sad consequence, that impressionist logic lost its proper character as an inquiry into reason, that is, its logical character; for there can be talk of an inquiry into reason or understanding only so long as we bind thought to sensible signs. If we drop the investigation of signs, we have lost the sensible support without which every investigation becomes impossible, and so we either fall into the morass of the rationalists, or else we deceive ourselves with the belief that impressions already in themselves constitute knowledge — the latter being the fatal error into which impressionism has in fact fallen. The impressionist tendency therefore, on the one hand, drops the thread of properly logical investigation, insofar as it confuses knowledge with life-phenomena, namely impressions; but on the other hand it is unable to establish the physiological foundations of knowledge either, for he who believes that knowledge depends only on impressions, indeed is nothing other than impression, has thereby become incapable of correctly conceiving those life-phenomena which we call signs. Yet we cannot properly come to know even the nature of impressions if we do not bring their investigation into connection with the investigation of our capacity for forming signs. Our sensibility remains perfectly unintelligible without the study of our faculty of sign-formation. Impressionism, therefore, can produce neither a true logic nor a true physiology, but creates in their place a sort of hybrid in which logical and physiological constituents are confounded in the strangest medley. From the confusion of the two kinds of element arose that science which we call psychology. For impressionist logic, by investigating the impressions which form the basis of our knowledge and dropping the investigation of knowledge itself, was transformed into so-called “psychology.” This “psychology” drew into its sphere the

p. 209

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

investigation of feelings and the will as well, whereby it separated itself wholly from that impressionist logic from which it had sprung. Strangely, however, the original impressionist logic too remained an independent science, which received the name of theory of knowledge. What immeasurable confusion has arisen from this I need perhaps not discuss in detail. For impressionist logic had itself already slipped down from the height of the logical tasks, in confounding knowledge with impressions. In reality it became merely a doctrine of impressions, that is, a branch of physiology. If now this doctrine of impressions, united with the investigation of feelings and will, was formed into a separate science, “empirical psychology,” then it became wholly unintelligible how impressionist logic, that is, the theory of knowledge, and empirical psychology could stand side by side as separate sciences. So it came about that some practitioners of “psychology” began to proclaim that the theory of knowledge is really nothing other than psychology, and that no separate theory of knowledge is needed, or, if they expressed themselves more mildly, that they wished to acknowledge the theory of knowledge only as a chapter of psychology — as the psychology of knowledge. Behold the famous psychologistic flood which, issuing from impressionist logic, proclaims the virtual superfluity of logic itself as a separate science; and let us admit, with a certain justice. For if we confuse knowledge with impressions, then there is no need at all for an investigation of knowledge, and we may content ourselves with the doctrine of impressions, which investigators nowadays regard as belonging to psychology. One thing, however, the psychologistic thinker forgets: namely that, impressions being phenomena of life, their investigation may be said to belong to the sphere of physiology, whence a fresh rivalry may arise between the practitioners of psychology and physiology, or let us say of biology in general, in the sense that the latter lay claim to the investigation of impressions for themselves. Indeed the psychologistic current may fundamentally be called a “biologistic” current as well, since in the end it turns minds toward physiology, as the fashion of “physiological psychology,” or rather of “psychophysics,” shows. Now one can imagine what confusion may arise in minds if ancient logic appears separately as an independent science under the name of “formal logic,” if, further, modern or impressionist (English) logic likewise figures as a separate science under the name of the theory of knowledge,

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 14

p. 210

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and if, finally, this impressionist logic, dropping its logical aim altogether, presents itself now as “empirical psychology,” now as “psychophysics,” and if on top of this even this psychology melts half and half into physiology. Let the reader add that physiology in its present state likes to proclaim that the doctrine of the phenomena of life is to be traced back to mechanics, and he will readily see what a dangerous slope it is that leads over from impressionist logic through psychology and physiology into mechanics. This whole gigantic confusion of concepts, however, arises from the fact that impressionist logic confounds knowledge with the impressions to which we appeal in our knowledge.

51. While impressionist logic is thus, on the one hand, in constant danger of losing sight of the proper task of logic as a science and of molting into mere physiology (or even mechanics), on the other hand it also drives physiological inquiry in a one-sided direction, insofar as, laying the weight constantly on the investigation of impressions, it seems to forget that we are not only beings that receive impressions but at the same time beings that form signs, and that our life-process can be truly understood only if we are clear that we can become aware of an experience only with the help of another experience. This is the principle of paired or double experiences, the detailed exposition of which indeed belongs to the sphere of physiology, but without the recognition of which no scientific logic can develop either. For if we do not know those experiences on which our knowledge must rest, then we form false concepts of our knowledge itself; just as, conversely, if we form false concepts of our knowledge, then not only is our logic confused, but every science into which we carry our logic becomes confused — physiology among the others.

Physiology itself too appears to us in a different light once we have recognized the truth of the proposition that we can become aware of an experience only through another experience. This proposition rests on the fact that impressions originally release reflex movements in us, and that in our sensation not only the impression itself but also the counter-sensation aroused by the movement plays its part as an indispensable component. Hence it is that in the state of sleep our receptivity to impressions sinks deeply, and indeed the deeper, the less motility the

p. 211

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

organism retains in it. To the degree that we can eliminate reflex movements of various orders, to that degree those sensitivities too are lost which can subsist only by means of those reflex movements. On this rests the effect of anesthetics and narcotizing agents on the life-process.

Among the reflex movements belong, before all, those weak undulating movements too which in general are invisible, but which are present everywhere that some tactile sensation arises in us. It is to be remarked, further, that the checking of a movement represents just as significant a vital work as the carrying through of the reflex movement itself. If, therefore, we feel an object by touch in imagination (or remember a tactile sensation of ours), then the work of our life-process consists in checking those movements which would play their part in actual touch. This checking work appears in our nervous system in the form of counter-currents, which proceed from the center toward the terminal organs of the sense of touch. As to the effect of light on organic beings, we can indeed observe in the lower animals that they turn aside reflexively from the light directed upon them, but higher living beings check these reflex movements in themselves, so that the nerve-current aroused by the impression at once arouses a counter-current, which heads from the center toward the retina. The counter-sensation here, then, arises from a checked movement, whereas in touch it comes to be from actual movements. With this we have explained whence arises the semblance that visual impressions contain only direct sensation but no counter-sensation. For the counter-sensation here arises from checked movements, that is, has the character of an ideal counter-sensation, whereas in touch it appeared as a real counter-sensation, which originated in the carrying through of actual movements.

By these discussions I wished to indicate the close connection which exists between the theory of knowledge and physiology. By the progress of physiology new light will continually fall on those life-processes which accompany the activity of our awareness (our thinking); but conversely, by the investigation of the activity of our awareness too new illumination will flow upon the life-processes on which the activity of our awareness rests, so that the theory of knowledge will act fruitfully on the development of the physiological sciences. Thus, for example, from epistemological considerations there arises

14*

p. 212

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that physiological principle according to which we can become aware of an experience a only with the help of an inverse experience b: which principle — if I am not mistaken — will play in physiology just as notable a part as the principle of action and reaction in mechanics. For this principle will be called upon to explain why we have several senses, how they cooperate, and what physiological processes those are on which the functions of remembering and imagining rest.

18. Outer and Inner Perception.

52. On the basis of the principle of double experiences we can now also clear up the fundamental error in which impressionist logic dwells when it distinguishes an inner and an outer sense from each other, and on this basis speaks too of two kinds of perception, inner and outer perception (or inner and outer experience), and, what is still more significant, when it wishes to distinguish, corresponding to the two kinds of perception, two worlds of appearances as well, namely a physical world of phenomena, which would lie open to outer perception, and a psychical world of phenomena, which would lie open to inner perception or sensing. These two worlds of appearances would form two series more or less parallel to one another, in such a way that to individual physical appearances individual psychical appearances would correspond, although there may exist physical phenomena too which have no corresponding psychical counterpart, and conversely there would not in every case be some physical appearance corresponding to psychical phenomena. This is the theory of the so-called “psychophysical parallelism,” which appears in modern psychology, or rather in psychologistic theory of knowledge, in various shadings, and is as it were the supreme flowering of the whole psychologistic mode of thinking. The two worlds of appearances of which this theory speaks are not determined in the same way by the different authors, and perhaps there is only one point on which the overwhelming majority agree: namely, that physical phenomena possess a character in space and time, while psychical phenomena are supposed not to be spatial but to run their course only in time. But even this proposition is not usually enunciated clearly and sharply, so that it remains more or less obscure whether by psychical phenomena are really to be understood

p. 213

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

phenomena that have nothing whatever to do with space. There are thinkers who do not even wish to distinguish two separate worlds of appearances, but speak only of one world of appearances, which, however, could be observed from two sides, from without and from within; but this perception from two sides often appears to us in such a light as if there were after all question of two different worlds of phenomena. In a word, the distinction of the two worlds of phenomena is so unstable that it is very difficult to determine whether the adherents of parallelism are really speaking of two worlds of phenomena or only of the two sides of one. It seems that some authors are inclined to regard sensible impressions as physical appearances, whereas memory-images, and above all feelings and passions, they already rank in the psychical world of phenomena: but others state plainly that the sensible impressions themselves are psychical appearances, and even add that in general every phenomenon is a psychical phenomenon, and that the question is only from which point of view (from without or from within) we investigate it, for according to this point of view the same phenomenon will then be of a physical or of a psychical character. But these authors can never explain clearly how one and the same phenomenon can be observed at one time from without, at another from within, and, what is still more important, on occasion they nevertheless speak of two series of phenomena which are supposed to run their course parallel to one another. Many, as a last resort, distinguish the “stimulus” from the impression and call the stimulus physical, but the impression psychical; yet they do not explain how the stimulus can be distinguished from the impression. Is it not the impression itself that we feel as a stimulus; or, if the stimulus is something wholly different from the impression, how can we obtain cognizance of it? Have we perhaps a separate sense organ for “stimuli”? and if not, how in the world do we come to cognize them?

The foundations of this boundless confusion of concepts we can find already in Locke, who, distinguishing in his own manner the inner sense from the outer senses, seduced thinkers into the peculiar supposition that with the impressions of the outer senses we apprehend the outer world, whereas with the help of an inner sensibility we contemplate and observe our own inner world. Before him hardly any philosopher had thought of regarding the so-called “inner world” as one accessible to sensibility,

p. 214

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

but Locke, by his expression “internal sense,” which is evidently only of a metaphorical character, was able to arouse the semblance that we actually have a sense, namely the “inner sense,” with which we contemplate or observe the phenomena of our inner world just as we apprehend with touch, sight, hearing, etc., the phenomena of the “outer world.” Locke himself, to be sure, declares that he really regards the inner sense as a non-sensible faculty, but he himself too put this observation of his aside, and represented the inner sense — as its very name already shows — as if it were itself a mysterious species of human sensibility.

Modern thinkers snatched eagerly at this inner sense, because by this word they came all at once into possession of a wonderful faculty which makes possible the observation, by sensible means, of what takes place in the soul. The mental world became accessible to sensible experience just as the bodily world is to the outer senses! So arose the science which was named “empirical psychology,” and which exerted the greater attraction on minds the less they suspected what in fact is to be understood by that wonderful inner sense which opens our mental world to sensible observation. The new method pleased especially those thinkers who hated “metaphysics,” but on the other hand were glad that in place of metaphysics they had attained, as a substitute, an “inner sense,” which opens to sensible experience that which till then had been reserved as the exclusive object of investigation for the knights of the non-sensible world, the metaphysicians. So arose that half-metaphysical school which, setting “psychical” sensing against “physical” sensing, wished to found a so-called “empirical” science of the soul. That the whole foundation of this alleged science was formed by a single word, or rather a flower of speech, a metaphorical expression, the so-called “inner sense,” very few noticed.

It is true that philosophers no longer use the expression “inner sense” today, but they dropped only the word and retained in its place the phrases “inner intuition,” “inner observation,” “inner experience.” For the “inner sense” appeared as if it were a separate sensible faculty, designed to obtain cognizance of the phenomena of mental life. Of such a separate faculty, however, one could have spoken seriously only if someone had succeeded

p. 215

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in discovering some organ, in determining a part of the brain, which was set apart specially for this purpose, that it should sense the mental phenomena. As with the eye we apprehend colors and with the ear sounds, so it would have been necessary to point out, if not on the surface of the body, at least in the interior of the organism, some ganglion of the central nervous system which was qualified for the sensible noticing of mental phenomena. Naturally no serious investigator could undertake to discover such an organ, and so philosophers soon noticed that the “inner sense” is a most dangerous flower of speech, which would impose an impossible obligation on the investigators of the brain and nerves. Herbart and his school, moreover, had already declared war on the countless new and ever newer “mental faculties,” which proliferated in psychology the more luxuriantly the less mental faculty those were endowed with who undertook the investigation of the mental faculties. Along with the countless mental faculties, the peculiar inner sense invented by Locke also fell into ill repute, so that this delicate word vanished from psychological literature. But even if the psychologists gave up the inner sense, they still clung with full force to those functions which they had ascribed to that banished faculty: namely, they clung to inner experience, inner observation, inner noticing. The faculty itself they dropped, but its operations they not only acknowledged but made directly the foundation of the science of psychology, insofar as it became a generally proclaimed principle that while the other empirical sciences rest on outer observation, psychology should rest on inner observation. These plausible phrases then conquered the whole cultivated world, and first of all the belletrists and aestheticians, who piled up the more inner observation the less they knew human nature.

But gradually, and among other things chiefly in consequence of the violent attack of Comte, doubts arose against “inner observation” too. There were adherents of psychology who, taking the principle of “inner observation” seriously, wished to practice it just as conscientiously and systematically as investigators of nature practice “outer observation.” But the more seriously they took their scientific intentions, the more they had to convince themselves that a man sits down in vain with the fixed intention of observing the phenomena of his own soul inwardly just as, for example, we observe the clouds sailing across the sky.

p. 216

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

A clever German psychologist, Franz Brentano, relates with interest how young psychologists, because of the complete barrenness of their inner observing experiments, fell into despair over the weakness of their faculty for observation of this kind. Indeed, there is hardly a more comical undertaking than to retire into solitude with the intention of lying in wait (and that, moreover, by way of the senses) for the sort of phenomena that are played out on the stage of our soul: and this is all the more ridiculous because absolutely every phenomenon of which we take cognizance is played out on the stage of our soul. It could therefore not be long before it was also seen that of “inner observation,” in the sense that we deliberately and methodically lie in wait for what goes on within us, there can be no question. The most recent psychology has accordingly dropped the much-favored concept of “inner observation” (innere Beobachtung) as well, and has put in its place the accidental “inner perception” (innere Wahrnehmung). Accidentally and on occasion, against our intention, we might peep into the inner processes of our mental life; indeed, according to many (so for example Wundt) inner perception would not take place immediately, at the moment the mental process is going on, but as a rule would flash up only in our memory, to tell us what has gone on in our soul. Behold, then, of inner observation there remains by degrees only a kind of remembering; and a very singular observation it is that we are confronted with if it takes place not when our mental process is going on, but when only a remembering leads us back to our supposed mental processes. What should we say of a meteorologist who today wanted to observe the storms that blew over yesterday? A sensuous inner perception that constates mental processes when they are not present in the soul would be just as strange.

If the psychologists set an inner perception over against outer perception, and base the possibility of their whole science precisely on this contrast, then nothing could be more important for them than to establish exactly what this inner perception is supposed to be. But, as we have seen, they are far from any such determination, so that the method of “empirical psychology” is shrouded in the deepest obscurity. If there is such an inner sensing as the psychologists speak of, then this inner sensing would have to be able, above all, to sense itself inwardly too. But that anyone has sensed how he senses his own mental processes from within, of this psychology is silent. Indeed, since we could speak of perceptions

p. 217

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of the perceptions of perceptions, and so on: it is evident that inner sensible perception would have not just one but countless modes, to which, however, the psychologists are not accustomed to appeal.

53. Nevertheless the phrases relating to inner sense and inner observation are not entirely groundless; not entirely, because some presentiment makes itself felt in them, which can find expression only in a confused way. For if I have become aware of an impression of mine and have given expression to this becoming aware in a constating sentence, then this sentence itself can become the object of my activity of awareness, just as my impression did before, and I can now form a second sentence as well, by which I constate that I formed a sentence before. If I designate by a the impression to which I refer, by A1 the sentence in which I constate the impression a, then I can form an A2 sentence as well, in which I constate that I have just formed an A1 sentence. Naturally I can continue this operation further, in that I can also form an A3 sentence, in which I can express the formation of the A2 sentence that has just taken place, and so on. With an A4 sentence, then, I can refer to my A3 sentence, with an A5 sentence to the A4 sentence, and nothing prevents me from continuing these references as I please. This is the state of affairs which may have given the psychologists occasion to speak, besides outer sense, of an inner sense as well, and some of them were even inclined to assume an “innermost sense” (innerster Sinn). It is evident, however, that here there can be no question at all of two kinds of sensibility, but only of two kinds of judging. With my A1 judgment I can refer to the impression a, and I have called such judging that which flows from direct (direkt) awareness; but with the A2 judgment I can refer to my A1 judgment as well, that is, I can pass judgment on the fact that I have judged, and such judgment upon a judgment is what I have called that which flows from contrary awareness. I can, to be sure, continue the operation, in that with an A3 judgment I can refer to the A2 judgment, but we do not as a rule hold it necessary to do this any more, since we should do nothing else than again refer, with our judgment, to some judgment of ours. We are content, therefore, to distinguish two kinds of judging: 1. judging by which we refer to our impressions, that is, constate facts; 2. judging by which our judgments

p. 218

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

are referred to, that is, by which we constate that we have passed judgments, and are therefore judging beings. This operation, I say, we could indeed continue as we pleased; but however far we continued it, we should still not arrive at any new class of judging, for if we refer to the A2 judgment with an A3 judgment, we have still only judged upon our judgment. We have, then, no reason to distinguish more than two classes of judgments: 1. judgments; 2. judgments upon judgments. What sense there is in the fact that we can continue the judging upon judgment to infinity is in any case a very interesting problem; but it is a question which the inquiry into knowledge, even if it touched upon it, has been unable to raise clearly. We do surmise, to be sure, that it is a fact of great significance, and one that deserves the deepest attention of thinkers, that in judging upon judgments we can ascend to any power we please; but, as I say, there has not yet been a theorist of knowledge who has divined or elucidated the significance of this wonderful fact.

On some occasion I shall return to this problem, but perhaps I may be allowed even here to give expression to a thought of mine in passing. Those judgments by which we refer to our impressions, or to our immediate experiences generally, form the world of our direct, that is, object-awareness; those judgments, on the other hand, by which we judge upon our own judgments, establish the world of our self-awareness, for he who becomes aware that he judges has found in himself the aware or judging being, as I have already set forth. The world of our object-awareness gives, as it were, one dimension of our knowledge; the world of self-awareness adds a further dimension to it, and this further dimension, which we may also call the depth dimension of our knowledge, is just as boundless as the first, that is, the object dimension. That in the judgment upon judgment we can attain any powers we please would be, as it were, an expression of the fact that there is no limit to the deepening of our self-awareness; or, to put it popularly, that there is no thinker so deep that among men there could not turn up a still deeper one. But since logic, or the theory of knowledge, is the science of self-awareness, I see the proper task of logic in this infinite raising to higher powers of the judging upon judgment. This boundless process of raising to powers would be, as it were, an outward proof that logic itself is a science that can be developed without end.

But however one may think of the arbitrary raisability of self-awareness to higher powers, that is here a wholly incidental matter. Here

p. 219

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the question is whether, besides our outer sensibility, we have an inner sensibility, by which we peep into our own soul. I believe everyone will notice that Locke’s “inner sensibility” is only a flower of speech, coined by a man of sensualist bent, to express that we can judge upon our judgments, that is, that we are beings possessed of self-consciousness. Locke and his impressionist followers already confuse the impression itself with our taking cognizance of that impression, that is, with our (object-) judgment; they therefore also confuse our judgment upon this judgment with some sort of impression or feeling, and for this reason speak of an “inner sense,” by which we are supposed to notice what our soul is doing within when it takes note of some outer impression. Locke himself says that outer sense leads to such ideas as color, sound, warmth; whereas through inner sense we arrive at such ideas as thinking, doubting, reasoning, belief, etc., which the soul could not have drawn from without, but only from the noticing of its own inner processes. That is to say, it is clear that Locke was inclined to regard self-consciousness as a kind of inner sensibility, just as he was inclined to trace object-consciousness too to mere impressions.

But what we call self-consciousness, or self-awareness, is nothing other than becoming aware of our awareness, or rather, a judging upon our judging. When, therefore, the psychologists lay claim, for psychological inquiry, to a separate inner observation, or inner sensing, they find themselves in a twofold confusion. For on the one hand there is no inner sensing, but there certainly is a becoming aware of the acts of awareness (judgments); and on the other hand this becoming aware of awareness is not the proper, primordial method of psychology but of logic, or the theory of knowledge.
