For logic is the science that investigates our faculty of cognition, or rather its manifestations, judgments (and combinations of judgments); and to investigate judgments means nothing other than to judge upon judgments. If psychology as a separate science wishes to employ the same method, we can of course have no objection to this, provided it is clearly set forth from what standpoint psychology wishes to occupy itself with the judging upon judgments, that is, with contrary awareness. So long, however, as no such standpoint has been determined, the task of psychology as a science remains wholly obscure and uncertain. But of logic we do know that it investigates our faculty of cognition in order that

p. 220

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

it may establish whether we have a share in truth, and if we have (as we do), what sort of portion in truth is ours, and how we can increase this portion, as well as how we can avoid errors.

From the foregoing it can be seen that although we reject the confused concept which prevails in psychology concerning “inner perception,” we do not deny that behind this word some presentiment lurks, which has only to be clearly formulated for us to be freed from an immeasurable amount of error. The problem of self-awareness is what is hidden under the words “inner perception.” And in what does our self-awareness manifest itself? In this, that we have knowledge of our judgings: we know that we judge. In a judgment we pronounce that we judge. As the sovereign not only rules over his people but also expressly pronounces, perhaps even enacts in law, that the rule belongs to him and his heirs: so, too, the sovereign of all sovereigns, Reason, is not content with judging, but declares in separate judgments (or, if you like, laws) that it is the one that judges and that it judges also upon all judging. It is precisely this system of judgments, in which human reason establishes that it is the one called to judging, that it is the one that examines this calling, and that it is the one that wishes by this examination to increase our share in truth: I say, it is precisely this system of judgments, in which Reason bears witness to its own sovereignty, that is called logic, or the theory of knowledge.

But here the question may arise whether “self-consciousness” really consists in our judging also upon our judgment. Is it necessary that we should first have impressions, then express these in sentences, then again refer by sentences to the fact that we had first formed sentences: is all this necessary in order that we may boast of self-consciousness? After all, when, sunk in the deepest silence, we let our mind rest, so to speak, we are conscious beings even then, and it seems that we do not need judging, or still less a judging upon judgment, in order to regard ourselves as self-conscious. We must not let ourselves be deceived by this appearance. Man awakens to self-consciousness by learning to judge by forming signs, and by noticing that he is the one who puts meaning into the signs and finds meaning in them. If he did not form signs into which he had put meaning, if he were not able to express in further signs that he is the one who has put meaning into signs,

p. 221

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

into which he had put meaning, he would have neither object-awareness nor self-awareness. But because in forming (spoken) signs man acquires an extraordinary practice, to such a degree that, suppressing the actual utterance of these signs, he forms his signs in imagination too, and indeed with such speed that he himself cannot follow his own imagined signs with his attention (just as a man running fast cannot count how many times he lifts his feet): he comes in the end to be able, with a minimum of mental and sign-forming work, which he himself scarcely notices, to maintain his own self-consciousness for intervals of time. But that we have self-consciousness we owe solely to this, that we are able to express in meaningful signs that we form meaningful signs. We know that we know something.

54. But there lurks yet another presentiment in Locke’s inner sensing, though we must confess that the presentiment is exceedingly faint. There lurks in it the physiological principle that our experiences occur of necessity in pairs, at least whenever we take cognizance of them. Or, in other words, in order to take cognizance of an experience we must react to it with another experience. Locke’s inner feeling, taken in this sense, would signify not self-consciousness but some vital phenomenon which appears as a reaction against another vital phenomenon, as I tried to show in the preceding chapters. In Locke’s doctrine, therefore, two entirely different theories lurk, which are not distinguished from each other, so that neither the one nor the other is able to ripen into a genuine thought. The one theory is of a logical, that is, epistemological character, and would have to express that the acts of our awareness (consciousness) are judgments, either direct judgments, by which we constate our impressions, or inverse judgments, by which we constate our acts of judging. The other theory would be of a physiological character, and would establish that the phenomena of our life-processes form paired phenomena in the sense of the principle of action and reaction. By this I am far from wishing to say that either Locke or any representative of modern psychology has acquiesced in this explanation of “inner perception”: I only assert that if we wish to put something in the place of the untenable concept of “inner perception,” nothing remains but to replace it with the principle of paired judgments and paired experiences.

The principle of paired experiences is especially suited to explaining why it is that in more recent philosophy Locke and

p. 222

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

still more since Kant, the thought of a twofold world of phenomena (the physical and the psychical world of appearances) and of a supposed “parallelism” between them has been haunting us ever more strongly. After what has gone before, perhaps we need hardly say that the world of appearances is only one, and that it is a purely contrived thing, with no foundation whatever in experience, when modern psychology speaks of two worlds of phenomena. The historian of philosophy will probably record with amazement that in the age of the flowering of the natural sciences, precisely the adherents of the empirical way of thinking wished to discover two empirical worlds, two systems of phenomena, of which one would go on outside, the other inside, whereas this very outside and this inside are only a metaphor drawn from everyday speech, which has no scientific meaning. It was still understandable that at the beginning of the modern age Descartes spoke of two substances: an extended substance, which is the foundation of the material world, and a thinking substance, which is the foundation of the intellectual world, for of these two substances no one asserted that they reveal themselves to two kinds of sensibility and thus form two worlds parallel to each other. The doctrine of the two substances was only a theory, which fell under purely logical criticism, and this criticism was not wanting, for logical consistency irresistibly led thinking to the principle of one substance (Spinoza). But to speak, instead of two substances, of two worlds of phenomena, and that in the name of experience, would be perfectly incomprehensible if the principle of paired experiences did not shed light on this aberration. For that earnest and profound investigators, imbued with the love of truth, should think up, besides the existing world of phenomena, yet another world of phenomena, and set this against the first as a second world of phenomena inwardly experienceable: is that not a hair-raising thought, which would awaken in us doubt of the seriousness of scientific inquiry? But if we consider that the phenomena of our life-process may stand to one another in the relation of action and reaction, then the aberration becomes intelligible by which someone would call the phenomenon corresponding to the action physical, and the phenomenon corresponding to the reaction psychical; although better reflection leads us to see that the reaction of our organism, exactly as the action exerted upon it, is alike only physiological, that is, a life-phenomenon, and that apart from life-phenomena no other phenomena exist at all; they cannot exist, for in order that we may take cognizance of something

p. 223

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

it must exert an action on our organism, and our organism must answer that action with a reaction; otherwise there can be no question of taking cognizance, and that of which we take no cognizance at all can by no means be a phenomenon for us. But because within the life-process there obtains a great complication of actions and reactions, the error is easily understood by which one factor was classed as physical and the other as psychical. I repeat, we know nothing but life-phenomena, because every phenomenon is a phenomenon of our life-process (that is, our experience); by which, however, we have in no way excluded that some of our life-phenomena should be regarded as caused by bodies which in themselves are not living, that is, by inorganic bodies. Between body and body (between matter and matter) there may subsist the difference by virtue of which we call the one living and the other not living, and what this difference consists in is the gravest problem of applied theory of knowledge, as well as of natural science; but between phenomenon and phenomenon the contrast of life and lifelessness cannot subsist, because every phenomenon is only a factor of some life-process. From which it follows that a world of phenomena could not exist at all if living beings did not exist. This epistemological proposition is the one that is lost sight of by all those who wish to derive living beings, by some developmental or evolutionary process, from a lifeless or inorganic material world.

Since in this way there is no sense in speaking of two worlds of phenomena and of their parallelism, it is very natural that we cannot enter into a distinction either which would ascribe spatio-temporality to one class of phenomena, but to the other only temporality. Yet we see that as a result of the distinction between the inner and outer (psychical and physical) world of appearances a very notable epistemological problem has arisen, namely this: whether there are, or can be, phenomena which would go on merely in time? We must therefore be patient even with those false concepts which at times rule over minds for centuries, since human thinking arrives historically only through such false concepts at those questions whose pursuit may lead to clearer concept-formation. The dualism of the outer and inner world of appearances leads to the dualist conception of space and time: as if these were mutually independent “forms” of the

p. 224

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

human sensing. But no one can accept without being startled the notion that time should at one time enter into an alliance with space, and so make it possible for spatio-temporal (that is, physical) phenomena to arise, and at another time, severed from space, should enclose within itself a separate world of appearances (the psychical). For in this way the semblance arises as if we had to speak of two streams of time, of which the one allies itself with space, the other not. Nothing is more important, therefore, than finally to raise the epistemological question of the connection of space and time, for without this the question of the nature of space and time also remains wholly insoluble.

19. The Connection of Space and Time.

55. The doctrine of outer and inner sensibility became so greatly significant in modern theory of knowledge only because Kant, taking it up, brought it into the closest connection with his theory of space and time, in such a way that he made space the “form” of outer sensibility, and time, on the other hand, the “form” of inner sensibility. From this conception it would follow directly that outer phenomena can exist only in space, but inner phenomena only in time. Since, however, to distinguish a world of phenomena purely spatial and a world of phenomena purely temporal would be a complete absurdity, Kant had to labor to ascribe time to outer phenomena as well, though he would have reserved it only for inner sense, and hence only for inner phenomena. This is the great difficulty which, in his passage from the theory of space to the theory of time, he had to overcome. It is evident, however, that if we declare time to be the form of inner sense, then it can only be a play with words when we afterwards lend it to outer sense too; it is evident, furthermore, that as a result of this lending the phenomena of inner sense in their turn are compelled to enter into the forms of space, as I have explained in detail in my work Logic at the Crossroads (p. 113).

For the rest, we cannot say of outer sensibility in general either that space is its “form.” Thus, for example, sounds belong to outer sensibility, but we do not therefore place sounds side by side in space, as we do color-spots, for example, and consequently we cannot speak of sound-spots in the sense in which

p. 225

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

we speak of color-spots. Nor do we form spots out of the sensations of taste and smell, and so we do not build up spatial figures out of these either, as we do out of color-spots. Thus the “form of space” stands in an entirely different relation to the sense of sight from that in which it stands to the other senses, for which reason the most elementary physiological facts forbid us to bestow the “form of space” uniformly on each of the outer senses. From Kant’s theory it would follow that sounds, tastes and smells also can form spots, and that out of them we could build up spatial figures (pyramids, prisms, etc.) just as out of color-spots.

As for inner sensibility, we cannot speak of its “form” for the further reason that it is by no means settled which class or classes of phenomena we might call inner phenomena. But even if such classes were settled, we still could not speak of “their form” in any definite sense. For the word “form” designates at one time that definite duration of time during which some event actually takes place, and at another time that mysterious power dwelling in our sensibility which makes it possible for us to apprehend happenings as taking place in time. We should therefore have to distinguish the “form” which shows itself in events as their duration from the form which does not yet show itself in any event, but, lying latent in our sensibility, compels us to apprehend phenomena in temporal order. This a priori, that is, original form is what plays a significant part in Kant’s theory; but precisely this a priori form cannot be an object of intuition, as we have already shown of the a priori form of space. Thus we gain nothing by positing in our mind some original form of time, for this form would in any case be something wholly unknowable.

But the circumstance that two original forms lie latent in our sensibility, one of which would cause our experiences to arrange themselves in spatial order, the other in temporal order, would have the consequence that two mutually independent worlds of appearances would arise for us, between which no connection could subsist. Kant, then, should have raised at once, in his transcendental aesthetic, the question how the two forms of sensibility, the form of space and the form of time, come into connection with each other, and what power or law regulates their cooperation. To this question the answer would have had either to be given that

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 15

p. 226

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

there lies hidden in our sensibility no power, or rather no law of operation, which regulates the cooperation of those two forms, or else that there does lie hidden in our sensibility some power or law of operation which rules over the cooperation of the forms of space and time. Both answers alike, however, reveal the theory of forms as a logical impossibility. For if nothing regulates the cooperation of the two forms, then two worlds of appearances would arise for us, of which one would exist purely in space, the other purely in time, without standing in the slightest relation to each other; but if there is a power or law which regulates the cooperation of the two forms, then this, expressed in Kantian language, would mean that a third original form also lies hidden in our sensibility, which would determine in advance how the form of space and the form of time are to ally themselves with each other in the ordering of our experiences. Such a third, or higher, form would in fact comprehend the two forms of space and time in one form, so that it would abolish altogether the duality of the sensible forms. There could be talk only of a single space-time form, which would carry with it that the contrast of outer and inner sensibility would also be abolished, and likewise that the theory of the two worlds of appearances would dissolve into nothingness. We see, then, that two forms cannot subsist in our sensibility either independently of one another or connected with one another, for in the first case two unconnected worlds of phenomena would arise for us, in the second case there could be no talk of two forms, but only of one. Kant may have felt this, for he does not engage at all in pursuing the connection of the forms of space and time, whereas Locke, who undoubtedly had a great influence on Kant, devotes in his inquiry into the understanding separate chapters not only to space and to time, but treats their connection also in a separate chapter. What is more, at the end of this chapter Locke makes a remark on the connection of space and time which, for its profundity, would deserve to be singled out by the historians of philosophy: “…expansion and duration do mutually embrace and comprehend each other; every part of space being in every part of duration, and every part of duration in every part of expansion. Such a combination of two distinct ideas is, I suppose, scarce to be found in all that great variety we do or can conceive, and may afford matter to further speculation.” (Essay, Book II, ch. 15, § 12.) In this remark of Locke’s is contained

p. 227

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the doctrine of the unity of space and time, which I set forth years ago in my periodical Jelenkor, without my having then been aware of the remark of Locke’s just cited.

56. I do not stand alone, then, with the theory of the unity of space and time. Had Kant entered upon an examination of the connection between the “forms” of space and time, he would necessarily have discovered that these “forms” are in inseparable connection with each other; but even if he does not point to this unity, it had already been done before him by Locke, the pioneer of modern theory of knowledge, who in the sentence quoted above expresses his weighty thought with that unpretentiousness which is so characteristic of his engaging personality. Because in our concepts we are able to distinguish space from time and to separate them from each other, we need not fall into the error of tearing them apart too, as if they were beings existing separately. For such a tearing apart has the most unwelcome consequence that we must rend the world of appearances in two as well, and must distinguish two series of phenomena, whose connection becomes a mystery, or rather a logical impossibility. If, therefore, I call the doctrine of the unity of space and time fundamentally important for our whole conception of nature, I wish by this only to bring out that we cannot distinguish two worlds of appearances, unless we wish to invent, in addition to the existing world of phenomena, another which does not exist. The doctrine of the unity of space and time is worth championing only because with it we defend the unified character of the world of phenomena. For the rest, to whom would it occur to reflect on space and time, if we did not thereby wish to clarify our concept of the world of phenomena? We speak of space and time only because we ascribe extension and duration to appearances, and because it is precisely the universal fundamental law of the world of phenomena that every appearance occupies a definite position in space and in time, which cannot be appropriated from it. But precisely this fundamental law became an object of doubt in modern (psychologistic) philosophy, insofar as the conviction began to take root in minds that appearances need not take a place in both the spatial and the temporal order, and that the psychical appearances have precisely this as their characteristic feature, that they arrange themselves only in temporal order, but have nothing to do with the three dimensions of extension. And it cannot be denied that many, very many semblances speak for this conception, so that we need not

15*

p. 228

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

wonder at those thinkers who declare it absurd that anyone should doubt the twofold character of the world of phenomena. They have grown so accustomed to tearing time from space, inner sensibility from outer sensibility, that they take it almost as a matter of course that the world of inner phenomena is bound only to temporal order.

We must therefore raise with complete definiteness the question whether there can be phenomena which fill only temporal order but are not inserted into spatial order. For the sake of completeness, however, we must raise the supplementary question also, whether there can be phenomena which take a place only in spatial order and are not inserted into the order of time. For although at present I know of no thinker who asserts the existence of purely spatial phenomena, I nevertheless believe that if there are philosophers today who deny spatiality to one class of phenomena, tomorrow there may arise philosophers who in turn would deprive another class of appearances of temporality.

a) If someone asserts that a phenomenon a has a course in time but takes no place in space, such an assertion contains a certain ambiguity, which we must bring to light. That the phenomenon a does not take a place in space can be explained, 1. by saying that it is nowhere, does not happen anywhere, 2. but it can also be explained by saying that this phenomenon a does happen somewhere, but the scene of its happening is only a mathematical point of space, which has no extension at all. According to the first explanation the phenomenon a has a beginning in time and an end in time, in a word, a duration, but no scene at all. According to the second explanation the appearance a has a duration, has a scene too, but this scene is only a mathematical point of space, which has no extension.

As for the first explanation, I do not believe that anyone could find satisfaction in it. For if a phenomenon a does take its course in time, but its happening has no scene whatever, then this phenomenon a can be neither nearer to me nor farther from me than from anyone else in the world. No one would be entitled to say that this phenomenon is his impression, his experience; on the contrary, every perceiving being would feel himself equally entitled to regard that phenomenon as his own experience, for everyone could say that he has just as much

p. 229

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

to do with that phenomenon as anyone else. A being living on the planet Mars could just as well claim that phenomenon as his impression as we earthly dwellers can; nay, beings living on those constellations of the universe which have never yet become visible in any telescope could equally call the impression in question their own. Since, however, it is the characteristic of every impression that it is the impression of one being only and not at the same time of another, it is evident that an impression a to which everyone alike may lay claim cannot be an impression at all, cannot be an individual experience, that is, a phenomenon. If, however, someone believes that an impression a can be a common experience of several individuals, that is, that the same impression which takes its course in my organism can also take its course in the organism of others, then even he who is inclined to enter into such an absurdity will be obliged to admit that he knows of no impression which is the simultaneous common impression of all existing beings of the universe whatever. But even if he is inclined to assume such an impression appearing for every living being, he will still be obliged to see that what he is really speaking of is a phenomenon appearing everywhere, whereas in the sense of the first explanation the question is of a phenomenon which has no scene at all, that is, which appears nowhere.

On the strength of what has been said we must regard as a logical absurdity the assertion that there could exist phenomena which appear nowhere in the space of the world and take their course only in time. At bottom, however, there is no thinker who would assert any such thing. Those who speak of purely temporal appearances or processes are sensible enough thinkers to evade the logical absurdity of seeming to speak of appearances existing nowhere which nevertheless have being in time. For we know that human speech always allows certain so-called “mitigations,” when some all too “rigid” position collides head-on with a fundamental principle of thinking. Such a mitigation is, for example, the following: it is not a question of the purely temporal appearances having no connection whatever with space; we wish to say only that these purely temporal phenomena cannot be brought into connection with space immediately, but mediately they too must necessarily be referred to space. Mediately, because the organism of the perceiving being necessarily occupies a definite

p. 230

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

place in space. The inner phenomena themselves, it is true, take no place in space, but insofar as the organism of the perceiving man is of a spatial character, the perceived phenomenon too has some mediate relation to space.

But I believe that such evasion has no clear meaning. If something is nowhere in space, then it is wholly incomprehensible how it could nevertheless have, mediately, some relation to space. For this “relation to space” can mean nothing other than taking a place in space: but then the self-contradiction becomes manifest, for to say in one breath that the inner phenomena are not in space, and then again that they nevertheless take a place in it, is a glaring logical absurdity. Nothing remains, therefore, but to declare that inner phenomena do have a scene in space, but that this scene is only a mathematical point, which has no extension. For by means of this formula we can contrive to assign inner phenomena a place in space and yet deny them all extension. We must now, therefore, subject this formula too to criticism.

b) I believe it can easily be seen that phenomena whose scene would be the mathematical point of space without extension cannot exist. I might indeed point out that we can think a mathematical point of space by the power of our mind, but that no one among us has ever intuited such a mathematical point, nor ever will. If, then, by a phenomenon we understand something intuitable, it cannot be anything that exists only in the point without extension. But to this argument we get the reply that it is precisely the secret of inner intuition that it intuits phenomena which take their course in the mathematical point of space. Outer intuition is not capable of this, for which reason this outer intuition can perceive only phenomena or processes possessing extension, that is, only physical phenomena; but inner intuition notices precisely those inner or mental processes which have no extension and which take their course only in mathematical points of space. To show the untenability of this conception, therefore, we must resort to an entirely different kind of argument.

We must start from the proposition, of fundamental significance in the theory of knowledge, namely that human awareness in all

p. 231

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

circumstances is bound to time. This is a proposition which no one calls in question, and by which we can become aware of any experience of ours only if this experience has a certain duration. Every impression, every memory-image, every feeling or passion necessarily has some duration, however slight, during which it, as it were, holds or binds us. If such experiences had no duration at all, this would mean that they would not bind us at all, for which reason we should not even be capable of becoming aware of them. This is the principle of the finite speed of human awareness, which precisely the psychologists and the investigators of life least of all call in question, and which, besides, correct logic cannot deny.

On the basis of this proposition it is easy to understand why phenomena which possess extraordinarily slight extension are perceived only with great difficulty, or why they become altogether imperceptible to us. If, for example, the dimensions of an appearance reach only a thousandth of a millimeter, we cannot see it with the naked eye, because our awareness is not quick enough to notice it. For suppose that the speed of our awareness were so great that we became aware of an appearance in even a hundredth of a second: then in this short time the appearances lying in the spatial neighborhood of that appearance would also act upon us, so that we might perhaps not have time to separate them from one another in so short a time. The smaller the extension of an appearance a, the more quickly should we have to be able to become aware, in order to distinguish it from its spatial neighborhood, that is, from the surroundings b, c, d . . . . This we know from everyday experience. For if on a surface (a painting, for example) we wish to distinguish as many details as possible, we must examine its single parts the more attentively and for the longer time. The smaller the portion of surface on which we can fix our sight, the more rigidly and persistently we can look at precisely that small portion of surface, the more variety we can notice in it. Why? Because the appearance which occupies a very small area can assert itself in our sight only if it acts upon us for a long time and makes up, by the thousandfold repetition of the impression, for the brief duration of the single phases of the impression. In a word, everyone knows the remarkable experience that fields which seem uniform and of one pattern disclose to us, under rigid and sustained observation, ever more, new and ever new details and variations.

p. 232

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

The reason for this is that in a cursory and superficial survey not every detail had sufficient time to be noticed; that is, our awareness is not quick enough to make a thousand distinctions in a second; yet a thousand distinctions we should need, if we wished that all the varieties of a field should become noticeable even in a superficial survey. It is true that the noticeability is greatly promoted by, and its chief factor is, the intensity with which a detail acts upon us, but here it is not the intensity of the phenomena that interests us. Here we stress only that the smaller the extension of the phenomenon (assuming equal intensities), the less time falls to it in a cursory survey, and so the more it is exposed to the danger of not being noticed at all, since the possibility of noticing depends on a certain duration which was allotted to that appearance. If this duration is less than we need on account of the slowness of our awareness, then noticing has become impossible. It is clear, therefore, that the smaller the dimensions of an appearance, the quicker the awareness we need in order to single it out from the multitude of neighboring appearances. But if that appearance had no extension at all, that is, took its place only in the mathematical point of space, then we should have to possess an infinitely rapid awareness in order to notice it. Phenomena in mathematical points of space could be noticed only by an infinitely rapid awareness, that is, they would not appear at all to human awareness, since this needs time for the noticing of phenomena. But since the supposed inner intuition of which the psychologists speak is also of such a kind that it is bound to duration, it is evident that this inner intuition too is incapable of noticing appearances which take their course only in mathematical points of space. With this I have proved that appearances existing purely in the mathematical point of space, that is, purely in time, cannot exist for us. Only an infinitely rapid awareness could seize an appearance which takes its place in a mathematical point of space; but this infinitely rapid awareness has no need to run after the apprehension of appearances, since it is itself the creator of appearances.

57. After this it is easy to see that there can be no question either of appearances which take a place purely in space, but not in time. For this would mean either that those appearances

p. 233

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

exist, to be sure, but exist at no time, which is a manifest absurdity; or else that their existence manifests itself only at a mathematical point of time, but the noticing of such a manifestation again could succeed only for an infinitely rapid awareness, since an awareness of finite speed needs time in order to become aware of anything.

From what has been said it appears that every phenomenon must possess extension and duration, and that phenomena which could enter into the one order without entering into the other are not possible. It is a universal law of the phenomena of the universe that they necessarily take a place not only in time but also in space, in the sense that they are bound not only to a certain finite duration but also to a finite extension. This universal law is properly nothing other than the fundamental law of the natural sciences. Yet, strangely enough, the natural sciences are never in the habit of stating it, perhaps because they hold it “a matter of course,” perhaps because they do not wish to set themselves against “modern psychology,” which, by distinguishing outer and inner sensibility, was driven in the end to place itself in opposition to the tacitly admitted fundamental principle of all natural science, that phenomena, precisely because they are phenomena, possess at once a spatial and a temporal character.

But although we see a logical aberration in the fact that modern psychology speaks of purely temporal phenomena, we must by no means belittle the interesting labor of mind which has been performed even within this aberration. It seems to be the lot of the human race to arrive at the knowledge of great truths through great errors. Had that psychologistic conception not existed, according to which there are purely temporal phenomena, then man would not even have noticed how very remarkable, indeed I might say how infinitely far-reaching a law lies in what common sense calls, as it were, a matter of course: namely that if something happens at some time, it must also happen somewhere, and conversely, if it happens somewhere, it must also happen at some time. He who hears this law for the first time may consider it downright ridiculous, for what need is there to state something that everyone knows? But behold, modern psychologistic thinking, precisely by coming into collision with this elementary law, warns us that it is not superfluous to state what some at first

p. 234

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

hearing would call a matter of course. We should by no means know the worth of elementary truths if we were not swept into elementary errors. But he who has once seen that mankind is inclined to fall even into elementary error begins to place ever greater weight on elementary truths as well. Such an elementary truth is this too, that every phenomenon belongs not only to the temporal but at the same time to the spatial order.

And this elementary truth is not so useless as many a superficial mind would imagine, who takes a truth into cognizance in the way one squeezes a lemon and throws it away. That there are no phenomena which take a place in only one of the orders is properly the principle of the unity of space and time. What we ascribe extension to, we must ascribe duration to as well, and conversely, what we ascribe duration to, we must ascribe extension to as well; that is, extension and duration always appear only together, which is to say, stand only in inseparable connection with one another. Expressed in this way, the elementary truth in question no longer seems so banal. But what I call the unity of space and time is nothing other than the principle of the unified character of the whole world of appearances, by which I wish to say that there cannot exist two worlds of appearances (an inner and an outer). Yes, if space and time did not form a unified order, then it would be possible for a separate world of phenomena in space and a separate world of phenomena in time to arise; but space and time establish only one world-order, not two, for a phenomenon can fit into the one only if it has fitted into the other as well. The seemingly banal truth which we are pursuing is therefore by no means to be belittled, for what is at stake is whether the order of the universe is unified or not. Indeed, we shall be convinced that every secret of natural science finally flows together into the one secret, how space and time are connected with each other. But in order that we may examine this secret, we must first of all be clear that extension and duration are really in inseparable connection. This is why I say that we owe a great debt of gratitude to the psychologistic thinkers for setting up the theory of purely temporal phenomena, for without it we should perhaps never have noticed the immeasurable significance of the fundamental truth that what has duration must also possess extension.

p. 235

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

20. Rest and Motion.

58. Although we became convinced in the foregoing that duration cannot occur in any phenomenon without being bound up with extension as well, it is nevertheless beyond doubt that semblance rather favors the view that there really are purely temporal phenomena, such as our thoughts, volitions, feelings, passions, etc. No one doubts that all these experiences of ours are bound to time, but extension (extension in the strict sense of the word, such as can be measured with a meter) no one assuredly ascribes to them. If we consider this, we understand perfectly how the psychologistic theory could arise, which thought it could recognize purely temporal phenomena, since all semblance bears witness in its favor. But if we examine the cause of this semblance more closely, we are at once rid of it.

We had explained that becoming aware of any phenomenon always takes place in the mathematical point of time. When we speak of the point of time of our thoughts, volitions, feelings, impressions, we ordinarily exchange the duration which these experiences take up for the mathematical point of time at which we become aware of them. Hence arises all the conceptual confusion concerning the nature of our experiences. Becoming aware itself has no duration; it takes place in durationless points of time. In these points of time we come to know that eternal element which makes itself felt in our transitory experiences. In these mathematical points of time we human beings live eternity, that is, that which is eternally valid in our thoughts, volitions, feelings, passions, impressions, in a word, in our transitory experiences. What we call “consciousness,” therefore, flashes up only in mathematical points of time and casts its eternally valid light upon our life-phenomena, that is, our experiences. Hence it is that our “I” remains unintuitable. For our “I” manifests itself precisely in those mathematical points of time at which we become aware of our experiences: but, as I have already explained, there are no phenomena in such durationless points of time, and there cannot be. The manifestations of our I, that is, of our consciousness, are therefore not phenomena, and accordingly cannot be sensed or intuited. To become intuitable they would have to possess duration; but, as I say, they possess no duration at all, that is, they are not

p. 236

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

phenomena, not appearances. There is, then, in our experiences something that flashes up only in mathematical points of time, that is therefore no phenomenon, no appearance, and has neither extension nor duration, but which makes conscious the experience that we possess. This conscious character of our judgments, volitions, feelings, memories, impressions, which shows itself in mathematical points of time, must not be confused with the experiences themselves. Conscious character has no extension, and not even duration, since it shows itself only in mathematical points of time. Our experiences, on the other hand, have not only duration but extension also, inasmuch as they are nothing other than processes taking place in our organization, or rather in our nervous system. Of these, surely, no one doubts that they possess not only duration but extension as well. Now, it is precisely these phenomena possessing extension and duration which, from another point of view, namely insofar as they are conscious, we must call possessed of neither extension nor duration, since they become conscious only in mathematical points of time.

Thus we do not dispute away from our experiences that which is intellectual in them; on the contrary, we emphasize their intellectual nature in the fullest measure by ascribing to them—insofar as they are intellectual—neither duration nor extension; on the other hand, however, we grant our experiences in full also that which invests them with the character of sensibility, which qualifies them as phenomena, appearances: together with duration, extension too. Only we do not enter into that hybrid theory which believes it sees the intellectual nature of experiences in their being purely temporal phenomena, and the sensible character in their having extension. For thereby two worlds of appearances arise, each of which, being of sensible character, leaves us, on the one hand, unable to do justice to the intellectual nature of our experiences, while on the other we falsify the sensible world of appearances too, insofar as we add a nonexistent world to an existing one.

If, then, someone says that the conscious character which lies hidden in our sensations, memory-replicas, feelings, will and judgments cannot be something that has extension and could be measured with a meter, he expresses precisely what we too profess as our conviction, only that we add that this conscious character, as such, takes effect only in mathematical

p. 237

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

points of time, that is, has no duration either, and is therefore in no way phenomenon-like, nothing sensuous. Apart from this conscious character, however, all our experiences, under whatever name they may figure, possess not only duration but extension also, since they are nothing other than those space-time processes which take place in our organization and belong to the sphere of physiology (physiologia). We know no phenomena other than life-phenomena, for consciousness itself is no phenomenon, no appearance. For in consciousness that manifests itself which lives eternally, that is, the I.

So-called “psychical” phenomena we therefore do not know, for what is “psychical” (consciousness) is not a phenomenon, and what is a phenomenon is already no longer psychical but of physical character (that is, possessing duration and extension). If anyone nevertheless wishes to cling to the expression “psychical phenomenon,” then we are compelled to call absolutely every phenomenon psychical, that is, to eliminate the word psychical. For every phenomenon could be called psychical insofar as in every phenomenon either our own consciousness, or that of other beings, or at least that of their producer (the infinitely rapid awareness) manifests itself. So even then it is not the phenomenon but the consciousness manifesting itself in it that we call psychical.

59. Semblance, as I say, nevertheless speaks in favor of there being purely temporal phenomena. This semblance arises because in the case of many phenomena we can designate the place where they actually take place only approximately or very imperfectly. Thus, for example, we are often scarcely able to determine from which spatial direction sound-waves reached our eardrum. Many kinds of unpleasant bodily feelings we can localize only very uncertainly, and in the case of the so-called mental excitements we are positively at a loss as regards localization. But from the fact that we can determine the spatial position of certain phenomena only very imperfectly, it by no means follows that they have no extension at all. Logic, on the contrary, demands that every phenomenon have a local course.

It belongs to the original frailty of our organization that we often remain, to a great degree, uninformed about the spatial position of phenomena, as I shall explain in more detail later. Here I wish to point only to that other frailty of ours,

p. 238

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that, conversely, there are phenomena which throw us into the greatest confusion as regards their temporal course: these are the so-called standing or resting phenomena, which produce the marvelous semblance as if there were in them no temporal continuity. Thus, for example, if someone looks at a colored patch (say, on the wall), he does not in the least notice that this colored patch is in reality continually renewed in his seeing. For if we suppose, for example, that a twentieth of a second sufficed for us to become aware of the colored patch, then in the next twentieth of a second a new image has already arisen in us, that is, within one second the vision of that colored patch has been renewed in us twenty times, whereas of this renewal we have noticed absolutely nothing. Every vision must be a continuous process of renewal, for otherwise we should suddenly notice nothing more. But what we call rest consists precisely in this, that no renewal, and hence no temporal occurrence, appears in it. That patch of color there spreads out on the wall as if it had nothing to do with time, and as if the same colored phenomenon stood before us in the first second as in the second, whereas there is no doubt that the impression of the second second cannot be the same as that of the first. In short, resting phenomena produce the semblance as if they did not run their course in time, just as certain phenomena taking place in the interior of the organism, again, awaken the semblance as if they were not bound to a place. Whoever accepts the one semblance must accept the other too, and is then compelled to speak not only of purely temporal but also of purely spatial phenomena. But just as it is absurd to speak of phenomena which are only spatial but do not run their course in time (but stand), so it is absurd to assume phenomena which indeed run their course in time but occupy no place in space.

Besides, we must never lose sight of the fact that we can never determine the position of phenomena with perfect exactitude, either in space or in time. This is a truth which a person not occupied with the natural sciences is especially inclined to leave out of account. Yet the real frailty of our human nature manifests itself precisely in this, that neither our determinations of place nor our determinations of time can ever possess perfect exactitude. What immeasurable significance this inexactness has, of that, apart from investigators of nature,

p. 239

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

few other mortals have a true inkling. Who would bother about millimeters or millionths of a second? Those are negligible quantities! He who thinks so has never yet thought seriously either about natural phenomena or about his own frailty. From a certain practical point of view, under given circumstances not only the millimeter but the meter and the kilometer are negligible; similarly there may be practical circumstances in which someone sets no store by differences not only of seconds but even of a few hours or days. But when it is a matter of cognizing phenomena as completely as possible, then we might suddenly realize that perfect cognition is impossible simply because we cannot determine perfectly the extension and duration of any phenomenon whatever. We should become omniscient if we could determine perfectly the spatial and temporal position of even a single phenomenon.

This I must set out somewhat more fully. In order, namely, to measure a straight line segment with perfect exactitude, we should have to determine for that purpose the distance between its two mathematical end-points. But mathematical points we can only think, not actually mark out, since such marking out would require infinitely rapid awareness (that is, we should have to be omniscient). Nevertheless, without the concept of the mathematical point we could not think clearly about spatial relations, for if we merely wish to conceive the definite concept of a distance, we are compelled even so to regard it as enclosed between two points which themselves possess no extension. Hence all the effort that some thinkers expend on eliminating the concept of the mathematical point from our thinking is in vain. That would mean abolishing geometry, and with it mathematical science. Exactitude exists in our thinking only so long as we cling to the concepts of the mathematical point of space and point of time, and truth (mathematical truth) too can exist, at least in our thoughts, only so long as we do not give up the idea of perfect exactitude, even though we cannot realize it in our actual measurements. For as soon as we gave up the idea of perfect exactitude, the idea of gradual approach to it would cease too, for if there is no perfect exactitude, how then could one approach it in countless degrees? If there are in human thinking

p. 240

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

great achievements of eternal value, then among these surely the concepts of the mathematical point of space and of time also have a place. Without these concepts not only the fundamental sciences of the intuitable world (mathematics and dynamics) but logic itself becomes impossible. For the principle of identity, which is after all the foundation of all our knowledge, has a meaning only if we refer it to mathematical points of space and of time. Phenomena that flow apart, which have no definite, that is, exact boundary at all, cannot be identified. By eliminating the concepts of the mathematical point of space and of time, therefore, the concept of identity, and with it that of truth, would cease to exist.

This is in no way altered by the circumstance that, not possessing infinitely rapid awareness, we are unable actually to grasp or touch points without extension and duration. That we nevertheless stand in unconditional need of such concepts is only a sign that our finitely rapid awareness stands in unconditional need of the concept of infinitely rapid awareness. As certain as the principle of identity is, so certain is it that finitely rapid awareness leans upon infinitely rapid awareness, that is, so certain is it that truths can have validity only if they are rooted in infinitely rapid awareness. If the concept of infinitely rapid awareness (the deity) is eliminated, the meaning of all our words, the truth of all our thoughts, the real existence of all phenomena cease as well. Thus the fundamental concepts of logic hang together: whichever of them we attack, we come into conflict with the whole boundless system of truths.

For the actual marking out of a mathematical point of space, as I say, we should need infinitely rapid awareness. Since, however, we do not possess infinitely rapid awareness, the actual marking out of a mathematical point of space is for us an impossibility. It follows from this that the actual identification of a mathematical point of space is likewise impossible for us. We do speak of identical places in space, but whether the place which we marked out a second ago is really that same place a second later, that we can never decide. Indeed, we may be certain that the place which a phenomenon occupies is never, even for the duration of a second, the same place which it occupied before. Identical places exist only in our thinking, but never in our observations and measurements, for to that it would first of all be necessary

p. 241

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that we be able actually to set up a mathematical point of space as such. If we could actually set up such a mathematical point of space, we should then have actually identified this point as such with itself, and then nothing would stand in the way of our identifying a second such point as well, whereby we should already have identified a straight line segment of space. By the setting up of three points the identification of a triangle, by the setting up of four points that of a tetrahedron, would succeed; in a word, the perfect identification of any arbitrary portions of space depends purely on the identification of the mathematical point of space. But since this latter is something we human beings cannot achieve, we are unable actually to identify any portion of space whatever.

What we have deduced here logically, astronomy also proves empirically. If our earth rotates, its parts of surface, which we call identical, cannot occupy the same place in cosmic space in two successive seconds. If the sun in fact stood firmly in one place, then in relation to this absolutely resting body we could indeed know that our earth, in revolving about it, might graze certain absolutely identical places after the lapse of a year; but because we cannot say of the sun either that it does not change its place, we cannot assert of the earth either that by the repetition of its orbit it could return to absolutely identical positions in cosmic space. It would be quite superfluous to discuss here in detail all those motions which present-day astronomy ascribes to the earth, and even to the sun of our planetary system: for the only point is that there is no astronomer who would believe that our earth ever, in the course of its motions, appears at the same place in cosmic space at which it once appeared before.

But supposing that the earth or any heavenly body did appear twice (or more often) at the absolutely identical place in cosmic space, even then one thing is certain, namely that we human beings could never establish this absolute identity of place: for the simple reason that mathematical points of space as such we are unable to set up and identify. We can always know something only of the position of phenomena, but mathematical points of space are not phenomena, but only indispensable instruments of our judgment in the approximate determination of the position of phenomena. In order to determine the position of a mathematical point of space as such, we should have to survey cosmic space completely in its threefold-dimensional boundlessness, for the position of a math. point of space would be truly determined only by knowledge of the position of all

Dr. Palágyi: The Foundations of the Theory of Knowledge. 16

p. 242

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the other points of space. For any mathematical point of space can be conceived as the comprehension of the whole boundless cosmic space: modern geometry, too, in fact conceives it so when it regards the point as the center of a pencil of rays passing through it. This pencil of rays includes within itself every straight line that passes through that point, and so embraces every point of cosmic space. The determination of the absolute spatial position of one point would therefore be equivalent to the determination of the absolute position of every point, that is, to complete mastery over infinite space. That is why I say that only infinitely rapid awareness would be capable of actually setting up a mathematical point of space.

After this it is clear whence it comes that we relate the world of phenomena at all to space, or rather to points of space, although this space or these points of space are not appearances, that is, arouse no impressions in us. This comes from our identifying appearances, that is, regarding every phenomenon as distinguished from every other phenomenon. Identification, however, is nothing other than the relating of transitory phenomena to some non-transitory ground. When we constate the transitory, we find the eternal in it by the power of our judgment; and to find this eternal is the same as not to permit that transitory thing to be exchanged for any other transitory thing: that is, the same as to posit an eternal order in which every phenomenon has the place due to it, which no other phenomenon can appropriate from it. It is therefore a vain endeavor to explain from the transitoriness of our transitory experiences what space and what time are. For we relate our experiences to space and time only because we find something non-transitory in them. There is no need for anything sensibly perceptible to be contained separately in an impression of ours which should mark its position or its point of time: for the mere existence of sensed phenomena is sufficient for us to regard them as belonging to an eternal order, that is, to bind them to space and time. Since, however, it suffices that a phenomenon —disregarding all its special qualities—be related to the eternal merely by virtue of its existence and so be inserted into an eternal order, it is very natural that whenever we judge of phenomena (whether when they are present or when they are already past) we always have it in our power to relate them to the eternal and

p. 243

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

insert them into an eternal order. In a word, whenever we become aware, whenever we judge, we always have at our disposal the so-called “intuition of space and time.” For whenever we judge, we always have sensations, memory-replicas, etc., which we relate to the eternal and so insert into the eternal order. Hence it is that we can also form signs which, from the point of view of apprehending space and time, completely replace the original phenomena themselves, as I explained in an earlier chapter. For these signs are themselves phenomena, which, disregarding all their qualities, can be related by their mere existence to the eternal and to the eternal order. Since, then, our judgment finds space-timeness in phenomena by their mere existence, it finds this same space-timeness also in the signs formed by it, that is, it can (from the point of view of space-timeness) replace the phenomena themselves completely by signs. With this I have explained why the apprehension of space and time belongs to the sphere of neutral awareness. It belongs here because the mere existence of phenomena suffices to give occasion for a constating judgment, hence for insertion into an eternal order. From the point of view of mere existence, however, our signs can completely replace our original impressions. That is, our sign-forming awareness can completely replace (in this respect!) our sensing awareness. Our inverse consciousness can replace our direct consciousness. On this rests the character of the arithmetical and geometrical sciences, which distinguishes them from all others.

60. The truth that we cannot set up, or rather identify, a mathematical point of space, that therefore we are altogether unable to cognize identical places of cosmic space, is usually expressed by saying that between motion and rest there exists only a relative difference. This mode of expression, however, is very obscure, for it leaves us in complete confusion about the difference between motion and rest. The principle of the relativity of motion wishes above all to inform us that we are unable actually to mark out identical places in cosmic space. For if it were possible to mark out such identical places actually, then the thing with which we carried out the marking would enjoy absolute rest, so that in relation to it we could establish whether some other body had performed motion in the absolute sense or not. It is a pity, then, to use such a veiled mode of expression as “the relativity of motion and rest,” when we ought to admit openly that we are not able

16*

p. 244

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

to mark out identical places of cosmic space. If we state it so openly, we are at once in a position also to see into the cause of the helplessness in question. For we are unable to mark out identical volumes or areas because that which would be the basic condition of this, namely that we set up a mathematical point of space in cosmic space, is an impossibility for us human beings, for the reason that for the setting up of a math. point we should need infinitely rapid awareness. In this way we at least see into the nature of our own helplessness, and while on the one hand we no longer hope ever to be able to mark out identical places in space, on the other hand we are also consoled, since we see that only omniscient awareness would be capable of that.

But beyond this, the familiar proposition of natural science that “rest is only semblance” stands more clearly before us; by this it is, after all, by no means said what that “rest” then is, and above all it is not said whether we have a right to speak of it if it is only “semblance.” For if we say of something briefly that it is mere semblance, there at once arises, at the same time, the peculiar semblance as if the concept of that something, too, were to be rejected, that is, were a false concept. Now it is well known that dynamics cannot get on without the concept of rest, since with its rejection the concept of motion too would have to be dropped. If, then, someone asserts that all rest is only semblance, he cannot mean by this to say that the concept of rest also is only semblance. For we do in fact possess such a concept, and if we possess it—as this cannot soberly be called in question—then we cannot avoid subjecting it to closer examination.

Let us return to the example of the colored patch visible on the wall, of which we said that it arouses in us the semblance of rest. Of this semblance we found out above that it arises because we do not notice the renewal of the impression of that colored patch within fractions of a second. A little consideration led us to see that the impression which arises in us in the second twentieth of a second cannot be the same as that which arose in the first twentieth of a second, from which it follows that while we look at the patch the impression must necessarily have been renewed, although even the most tense attention does not notice this renewal. It belongs to experimental physiology to establish how many times the impression of a colored patch is renewed,

p. 245

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

while we look at it for a second: for us here only the fact is of interest that we cannot observe such renewal, and that this causes the semblance as if the patch were at rest. It is true that even if we could follow the renewal of the colored patch rhythmically with the eye, we should still not notice motion in the strict sense of the word, since such a renewal could not yet be called motion (that is, change of place). But neither could we say that that colored patch “rests” in the strict sense of the word, for where some renewal is present, there something happens, and this happening is what can no longer be subordinated to the concept of rest. We may therefore say that the impression that seems to rest encloses within itself a happening which we do not notice. To the extent that we can elucidate the nature of this unnoticed happening, to that extent light may fall also on the concept of rest.

For the sake of this light we must point back to our conception that every sensible effect must be thought of as composed of two waves: the impression-wave and the wave of remembering that follows immediately upon it. According to this theory, when we look at the colored patch that seems to rest, impression-waves and memory-waves follow one another in rapid succession; namely, upon the impression a1 follows the memory b1, upon this the new impression a2, then the memory b2, and so on as long as our gaze hangs upon that colored patch. Of this rhythmical alternation of impression-waves and memory-waves we notice nothing, and this causes the patch to seem at rest. Resting phenomena are therefore of such a nature that they do not permit impression-wave and memory-wave, appearing separately, to become noticeable. The memory-wave b1 so fuses with the newer impression a2, b2 with a3, and so on, that there is no way of separating them, and at most only our thinking leads us to see that such a fusion must necessarily take place. In place of “fusion” we may also use the expression “covering,” conceiving the whole process as if each wave covered up from us the preceding waves, in the way, for example, that suitably superposed congruent triangles completely cover one another, so that they seem to form only one triangle. It is true that this is only metaphorical speech and not a proper explanation, but by “fusion” or “covering” we in fact do not intend to say anything

p. 246

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

more than what we express dryly by saying that we are unable, during the duration of an impression, to separate from one another the successive wave-beats.

On this helplessness of ours rests the fact that our sensations have the character of continuity. We see, then, that what we call the continuity (continuitas) of the world of phenomena is connected with the finite rapidity of our awareness. If our awareness were infinitely rapid, we could actually divide the second into infinitely many parts, or rather, we should be able to grasp the mathematical point of time, and with it the mathematical point of space as well, which would mean, however, that we had altogether escaped from the yoke of the world of phenomena and had become wholly one with infinitely rapid awareness (that is, had become omniscient). But because our awareness is of finite rapidity, we can notice only what flows together, and it is these things flowing together that we then call series of phenomena.

By the foregoing exposition of rest it has at the same time already been established what we are to hold about motion. If the waves a1, b1, a2, b2, etc. follow one another in such a way that the impression-waves can separate from the memory-waves, then we call the phenomenon moving. If, for example, we see a ball moving, then the ever new impression-waves which proceed from the ball separate from the preceding memory-waves. That is, we are in the position of being able to distinguish to some extent the memory-image of the ball’s earlier position from the new impression-wave following it. This, surely, we knew without any explanation! But this is a sign that we gave also of rest the most natural explanation when we said that in rest successive impression-waves and memory-waves do in fact occur, only we cannot separate them. In this way, by this theory of rest and motion, we have at the same time confirmed that the fundamental theory itself, by which we resolved the sensible effect into two waves, may be regarded as correct.

But in order to see still more deeply into the nature of rest and motion, we must yet consider more closely the principle of the unity of space and time.

p. 247

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

21. Conclusion.

61. We have convinced ourselves that every phenomenon must necessarily be extended and at the same time possess a certain duration, although there are phenomena which produce the semblance as if they had no extension, and conversely there are some which seem to lack temporal continuity, and so to be standing phenomena. But in order to be strengthened in our conception that space and time constitute the unified order of the world of phenomena, it is expedient also to bring the phantasms which we are accustomed to form for ourselves of space and of time into close connection with one another, in such a way that one phantasm shall arise from the two. For it is vain to repeat that space and time, in spite of all their distinctness, establish only one order of phenomena, if we do not accustom ourselves to fold, as it were, our phantasms corresponding to space and to time into one another.

To this end we may follow two procedures. We may start from the phantasm of space and conceive it as if it were renewed without pause. If we thus think of cosmic space as continually renewing itself, we have thereby, as it were, melted the phantasm of time into that of space. The continual renewal of cosmic space then represents the flow of time. Every point of space takes part equally in the renewal, that is, in other words, the flow of time flows on at every point of space. Commonly we conceive time as a separate flow, which has nothing to do with space, and this bad habit is the chief reason why we form for ourselves very false concepts of natural phenomena. This bad habit arises from our binding space and time to separate words, and because in this way we have quite correctly separated space from time conceptually, we begin to believe that we must now also separate them once and for all in imagination. Yet time flows everywhere where cosmic space extends, that is, there is no point of space in which we should not have to imagine time as flowing. There is therefore no sense in separating time from space even in the play of our imagination; on the contrary, we need precisely to think them always connected. For this purpose, however, there is no more convenient illustrative device than to imagine cosmic space as continually renewing itself. Thereby we at once achieve also that we shall conceive every phenomenon, too, in such a way

p. 248

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that it contains continual renewal within itself, even when it seems to stand or rest.

It is not, then, an eccentric inclination that leads us to imagine cosmic space as in continual renewal, but our striving toward a faithful apprehension of phenomena that impels us to support our fantasy by such a slightly unusual means. He who has grown accustomed to imagining cosmic space as continually renewing itself does not fall into the delusion that there exist absolutely resting or standing phenomena, for he is clear that even the phenomenon that seems to stand is continually renewed in the course of time. He understands all at once Heraclitus’s famous doctrine that everything flows. This doctrine remains mystical so long as we do not conceive resting phenomena in such a way that they too exist in continual renewal, which renewal we are merely not able to notice directly. This continual renewal, however, we can raise to a clear concept only if we think of space itself, and of every phenomenon in it, as renewing itself. Naturally there at once arises also the question with what rapidity we must imagine cosmic space to renew itself. This question shows that by this dynamical conception of space we have at once connected the problem of space with the problem of human awareness. For it is human awareness that, recognizing phenomena as belonging to an eternal order, relates them to space and time: it is fitting, therefore, that the concepts of space and time be duly connected with the concept of human awareness. As often as a phenomenon under whose direct influence we stand is renewed in our awareness, so often may we regard the space also, which the phenomenon in question fills, as renewed.

This thought leads us at the same time to see that we can speak of space and time in two senses, according as we relate them to infinitely rapid or to finitely rapid awareness. In infinitely rapid awareness space is to be thought of as renewing itself with infinite rapidity, but we human beings cannot imagine such a renewal. Yet by this path we arrive at the concept of absolute space, an inkling of which dawns already on more than one philosopher. If we human beings conceive space as standing space, this expresses only our endeavor to relate phenomena to absolute space; in fact, however, we are not able to realize this endeavor, which

p. 249

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

is evident from the fact that we cannot set up an absolutely identical mathematical point of space. We human beings, properly speaking, proceed correctly only when we do not delude ourselves as if we were able to relate phenomena to an absolute space, but, true to the nature of our awareness, imagine along with the renewal of phenomena their extension too as renewing itself.

But we can accustom our imagination to the unified character of space and time in still another way. Before, we conceived duration as a supplementary aspect (the renewal) of extension; now, conversely, we shall regard extension as a supplementary aspect of duration. For this purpose it will be good to bear in mind that we in fact use the single point of time in order to comprehend in it all the points of space. The common property of all the points of space is that they are simultaneous (synchronous), that is, continually present at the same point of time, wherefore in imagination we may regard every point of space as an emanation of one and the same point of time. The one ideal dimension of time presents itself to us continually as if unfolded in the three dimensions of space. Whereas, then, before, starting from space, we conceived time as if it came into being through the continual renewal of space, so now, conversely, starting from time, we may conceive space as if it came into being through the flow of time, that is, as if it were nothing but the unfolding, the evolving, of one-dimensional time in three dimensions. I must stress, however, that all this is only figurative speech, which serves to set the fantasy in motion and to accustom it to the unified conception of space and time. I stress further that we speak of space and time always only because phenomena have extension and duration, and that by the principle of the unity of space and time we understand nothing other than the inseparable connection of the extension and the duration of phenomena. In a word, the doctrine of space and time cannot be treated for its own sake, but only for the sake of the existing world of phenomena: so that, disregarding the world of phenomena (which has extension and duration), the concepts of space and time lose their meaning completely. By this we mean that we must not raise space and time (because they figure as independent concepts) to independent beings (hypostatize them) and thereby create an absurd space-time problem. He who asks what space is and what time is has usually already hypostatized space and time by this very question, and so, quite naturally, cannot arrive at a correct conception of them.

p. 250

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

It is now entirely immaterial whether we regard space and time as “objective” beings placed “outside” the human mind, or whether, with Kant, we declare them to be “forms of sensibility,” that is, raise them to “inner” entities existing or operating in the soul. We speak of space and time only because we relate phenomena to a non-sensible ground, or, what amounts to the same thing, insert them into an eternal order. Space and time themselves are not phenomena, but they seem to be phenomena, because we ascribe extension and duration to phenomena. We ascribe them to phenomena, however, only because we cannot conceive phenomena otherwise than as the appearance of a non-sensible ground. Whoever, therefore, speaks thus: this is here, that is there, this is happening today, that happened yesterday, etc., thereby bears witness that he has related the appearances a, b, c, d . . . to a non-sensible ground, that is, inserted them into an eternal order. As the fundamental law of the whole world of phenomena we may state that every phenomenon belongs to an eternal order in such a way that no other phenomenon can appropriate its position in this order. This eternal order is indeed twofold in character, but it is nevertheless unified, in such a way that a phenomenon which occupies a place in the one order must also occupy a place in the other. This is the fundamental law of all the natural sciences.

62. Commonly the so-called law of causality (principium causalitatis*) is set up as the fundamental principle of the natural sciences; closer examination, however, reveals that philosophers have never agreed with one another about the meaning and the formulation of the law of causality. If Hume raises doubts against the principle of causality, this is very easily understood: for the principle of causality has nowhere and by no one been established in such a way that a clear meaning could attach to it. I cannot enter here into the critical history of the principle of causality, since the discussion of this principle already belongs to the special theory of knowledge (metaphysics). I shall point here to only one further point, which by itself suffices to lay bare the immeasurable confusion that reigns around the principle of causality. Although in everyday life too there is constant talk of the relation between cause and effect, there is not even agreement among minds as to whether two phenomena a and b are brought into such a connection that one of them, let us say a, is the cause of the phenomenon b, or whether a

*) Or pr. rationis sufficientis.

p. 251

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

whole group of phenomena a, b, c, d, e, f, g, h, etc. are brought into such a relation with one another that, for example, one member of the group would cause another, or one would cause all the rest, or perhaps always two would cause a third, etc., etc.? Nor does anyone establish whether, in the relation of cause and effect, simultaneity or non-simultaneity obtains between cause and effect.
