Or is it perhaps a question of the relation of several simultaneous and several non-simultaneous phenomena? But then, by the relation of cause and effect there may also be understood such a relation that the cause itself would not possess the character of a phenomenon and only its effect could be qualified as a phenomenon. In this sense we speak, for example, of forces as mysterious causes, which are not perceptible in themselves, but which would manifest themselves in all sorts of perceptible, phenomenon-like effects (in motion, color, sound, etc.). Indeed, even matter itself we may conceive as a cause, and indeed as the imperceptible cause of its own perceptible qualities. From this it is seen that the clouds of Polonius are not so changeable as the thousandfold obscure senses in which we are accustomed to take the principle of causality. It is a remarkable circumstance that precisely the natural scientists, who would be constantly concerned with it, speak least about it; the sign of this is that an exact formulation of the principle of causality is nowhere to be found. For if some philosopher had succeeded in formulating strictly what is to be understood by the relation of cause and effect, we could be sure that every textbook of physics, but every textbook of chemistry and physiology too, would begin with the strict formulation of this relation.

Among all the so-called principles of logic the principle of causality undoubtedly has the most popular sound, but let us confess that in scientific respect it is also the most obscure and the most uncertain. The explanation of this lies first of all in this, that what we call the principle of identity appears in everyday thinking very often as the principle of causality. In the sense of the principle of identity we must conceive every phenomenon as the manifestation of the eternal, which we may also express thus: the (non-sensible) eternal is the producer of every transitory phenomenon. The word cause has so wide a sense that the principle of causality in this sense would coincide entirely with the principle of identity, that is, with that fundamental thought of monistic philosophy, that in the immeasurable variety of the world of phenomena one and the same essence everywhere and at all times manifests itself.

p. 252

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

But if we do not speak of cause and effect in this sense, where we take the eternal as cause and everything that is transitory as effect, then it becomes altogether obscure what we are to understand by the principle of causality. For then nothing remains but simply to regard the causal connection as a connection of phenomena in which the essence of the phenomena (that is, their non-sensible ground) has no part. But in the sense of the fundamental principle of logic (the principle of identity), nothing else at all can bring phenomena into connection with one another except that non-sensible ground to which we relate them all, and which is nothing other than the interconnection of the whole world of phenomena. Hence it is easily understood that a thinker as impressionist as Hume had to doubt the causal law, for he who, like him, nowhere finds the eternal in the transitory appearances could not find the meaning of the causal connection binding appearances together either. Had he proceeded consistently, he would have had to raise doubts not only against the so-called causal connection, but directly to criticize the principle of identity. For the principle of causality, as I have just explained, is of quite uncertain meaning, whereas the principle of identity includes everything that is commonly called the principle of causality: namely the interconnection of the whole world of phenomena in a single non-sensible ground (primal cause).

I therefore regard the “principle of causality” merely as another word in place of the principle of identity, in the sense of which the world of phenomena is the manifestation of the eternal. In all our discussions hitherto we could properly not have dealt with anything other than the ever new illumination, the ever fuller development of the principle of identity: just as the theory of knowledge (at least the monistic theory of knowledge) can contain nothing other than the analysis of this single principle. But we hardly need say that we can never be completely finished with the building up of this principle, since this principle demands of us that we comprehend all our knowledge in a single knowledge. The actual and completed comprehension of all truth in one truth, however, can succeed only for infinitely rapid awareness, which is itself truth. We human beings can only be on the way toward this goal (the comprehension of all truth), but the goal itself we can never finally realize. The principle of identity, which is nothing other than the eternal demand of this goal, will therefore itself never become a perfectly

p. 253

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

developed principle, but always only such a central thought of the human mind, whose significance and effect grow ever greater in the course of millennia; for by striving to grasp its meaning ever more deeply we shall be able to unite by it more and more truths. Thus, for example, by the building up of the principle of identity we obtained the principle of double judgments, by which we must always check and supplement the judgment of object-awareness by the judgment of self-awareness. By the elaboration of the principle of identity we obtained also the principle of double experiences, which we also called the principle of the “unity of space and time.” For it is striking that of our double experiences in the one the reference to space, in the other the reference to time predominates, so that hence arises the semblance as if there existed standing phenomena which do not possess continuity in time, and conversely continuous phenomena which would not possess extension. Against this semblance we had to show that every phenomenon is indeed space-temporal, only that we human beings (by reason of the finite rapidity of our awareness) can always grasp the experience a only through an experience b, that is, we are always able to establish a fact, to cognize a truth only by relating paired phenomena to one another, in consequence of which in the one appearance we drop out the temporal aspect, in the other the spatial, or can establish it only very approximately. Hence arises then our endeavor to discover temporal continuity also in apparently standing phenomena, and conversely to cognize the apparently merely continuous phenomena as at the same time spatial. With this I have expressed what from the standpoint of the natural sciences we may call the causal law. For the principle of double appearances contains this, that every appearance has for us, so to speak, a hidden side. The hidden side of standing appearances is formed by their relation to time, the hidden side of merely continuous phenomena by their relation to space. What modern physics calls the principle of the transformation and conservation of energy is in fact nothing other than an intimation, starting from empirical facts, of the principle of double appearances. The detailed treatment of this, however, already exceeds the framework of the theory of knowledge in the strict sense, for which reason I have reserved it for a separate work.
